SEVENTEENTH REPORT OF THE COMMITIEE 
Having been authorised by the Department~elated Parliamentary Standing Committee on 
Industry, I, Ashok Mitra, in my capacity as Chairman of the Committee, Present its 
Seventeenth Report. 
2. The Committee has, during its tenure, striven to keep itself abreast with the emerging/ 
trends in the industrial sector and the specific problems afflicting national industrial growth. 
The issue of overall growth apart, the unevenness of industrial expansion, the Committee is 
aware, has acute political and social implications. In the current financial year, the 
Committee chose to undertake a number of field visits in different parts of the country in 
order that members could closely acquaint themselves in on the state of the major industries, 
particularly heavy and capital goods industries, and the factors instrumental for the 
persistence of industrial backwardness in several regions. 
3. For the purpose 'Of the field visits, the Committee divided itself into six Sub-, 
Committees; five of the Sub-Committees concentrated on the problems of 'individual regions, 
while the Sixth Sub-Committee was assigned the task of studying in depth the difficulties 
faced by women industrial workers as well as women entrepreneur~. . · 
•"· 
4. The reports submitted by the Sub-Committees are reproduced in full in the annexures. 
These contain a number of ·valuable" observations and recommendations. The present 
introductory section is largely based on the analysis and findings of the Sub-Committees; it 
also offers a few supplementary comments on the issues under examination. 
5. A few preliminary comments are perhaps in order. The pattern of national industrial 
growth over the last five years is somewhat mixed. While during 1991-92, 1992-93 and 1993-
94 2_verall growth _C?f_" ind~t.!l_!lputput 1 including in the capital goo~ was less than 
satisfactory, a significant improvement in performance took place in 1994-95. The first half 
of 1995-96 witnessed an accleration in the rate of growth in the manufacturing sector as well 
as in the other components of the index of industrial production. The latest reports, 
however, indicate a slackening of this tempo, so much so that the rate of growth in the 
current financial year is unlikely to exceed what it was in the preceding year. The tidings 
from the capital goods sector are specially disconcerting; the rate of growth in this sector 
here has fallen below what it was last year.

worth mentioning again in·the light of developments in the current year. The Committee had 
in that Report dwelled upon the seemingly obverse relationship fe,att!Ji.PL.!~~E:~s~.J.!l jh~

exchanges. It is conceivable that external events, incl,uding political developments, have led 
to a lowering of expectl!-tions ·on the part of foreigners and other investors regarding the 
state of health of India's financial sector. Irrespective of the validity of this hypothesis, it is 
possible to speculate on the other hypothesis, namely, that when resources are. drawn ~!!!

of conventional savings instruments, strengthening thereby the prospects of larger capital

7. A development of this nature will have an immediate impact on the prospects of several 
public sector industrial units, mostly in the capital goods sector, which the Sub-Committees 
had the opportunity to visit. The Committee would like to demarcate between two 
~
tegories of sick public sector undertakings. To the first category belong units which were 
rig!nally in the priv~-,~~ctor but were 1ak~P.-.!?~.-because of diverse considerations, 
/ ncluding the E~~i!!.!~ .P!~!_ec!_~e ~t~_of WE!!cers,. The other category consists of units, 
\ rgely specialising in the manufacture of heavy structurals and similar equipment, which 
came up in the course of the past four and a half decades, mostly on the initiative of the 
Government, in response to the challenge of self-reliant, industrial development. Invest-
ments in tl;lese units run into hundreds of thousands of crore of rupees comprised of hard­
earned savings of the Indian citizeMry. It is also not easy to brush aside such facts as that 
these units engage a huge number of workers and produce large volumes of key industrial 
commodities. Whatever criterion of efficiency may be deployed, it would need boldness to 
assert that these public undertakings do not deserve to he kept, alive merely because, on a 
one-to-one basis, their products are unable to compete with anaiogous products manufac­
tured by a foreign company and are imported from overseas. To fully open up the system 
means allowing these industrial units to go to the wall and therefore to lay waste investments 
sunk in the past. It should be one of the nation's concerns to explore whether alternative 
possibilities of survival exist for such units. The economy is extremely resource-short, as 
indicated by the recurrent crisis in the money market this year affecting fresh capital 
formation activities. In the circumstances, the endeavour ought to be to extract whatever 
output is extractable from installed industrial capacity without worrying excessively whether 
the goods produced would meet the criterion of external competitiveness. If these products 
have a ready market within the country, that should be justification enough for the process 
of production in these units not being permitted to come to a halt. The recent sharp 
deterioration in the external value of the rupee and the consequent need to curtail imports 
should lend additional support to the point of view that installed capacity in the country 
should, as far as possible, be fully utilised. 
8. Jhe problems confronting the capit!L&o.2~~~ctor.!!r~.J~m!~~h: !<?,,~~sappe~~!ernig~!_: 
In the face of unbridled globalisation_, the lifting of import controls and 31 drastic acrosS-the­
board reduction in the rates of import duties, competition from overseas can only intensify. 
The downward slide in the ext~rnal value of the national currency is unlikely to be of much 
help in restraining imports, since large profits resulting from expanding foreign trade 
concentrated in the hands of a limited section of people may re,nder a relatively stiff dollar 
rate of exchange only a minor irritant to imports. The Government and the Ministry of 
Industry in particular will therefore have to cbntinue to exercise a monitoring role vis-a-vis 
developments in these industrial units. The point made earlier, namely, that it is less than 
prudent not to avail of capacity that is already built into the system, perhaps ought not to be 
summarily brushed aside. The derating of installed capacity in a particular branch of 
industrial activity marked by old age may be carefully phased out and coordinated with 
planned imports of new capital equipment. In a totally decentralised environment, 
dovetailing of this kind is not easy. The necessity of a loose framework of industrial and 
exchange control planning therefore appears imperative even if the other features of 
industrial liberalisation are regarded as acceptable. 
9. A comment is called for on the role of the banks. The im ression the Sub-Committees 
have gathered is that, in many instances, industrial uni~ve ceased to ~-~.~~~ because <?!.

nor will budgetary funds be made available. To be fair, it is perhaps pointless to blame the 
banks for this situation; they are under injunction from the higher authorities. 
10. Ambivalence in public policy in such matters is best avoidable. A number of contrary 
forces are seemingly at work. Some sympathy apparently still lUfkS in some official quarters 
for nursing back to health industrial units that are currently experiencing difficulties but have 
excellent equipment and face prospects of a ready market. But either the banks which could 
have assisted them are themselves sick, or a genuine doubt exists whether it would be wise 
to extend the sickness of industrial units to the banks too. It is against this background that., 
the Government often announces its decision not to extend further budgetary support to the 
units concerned and also cut them off from bank finance. Because of this confusion in 
policy, units which are otherwise viable and can survive without ballistic support from the 
Government, except only marginally, also come to suffer. 
11. The mechanism for rehabilitating sick units through the m~dalities of the Board of 
Industrial and Financial Reconstruction experimented with in recent years can not be said to 
have met with unmitigated success. The BIFR was assigned the task of picking and choosing 
among the units that have gone sick; a procedure was laid down for quasi-judicial 
consideration of the conditions obtaining in the different units; supposedly hopeless cases 
were put up for purchase by private entrepreneurs, while for those units for which revival is 
agreed upon, the BIFR was to lay down a pattern of financial accommodation which the 
parties involved, including the Government, were expected to go along with. Some recent 
instances suggest that even when a certain arrangement has been finalised under the BIFR's 
auspices and with its approval, the terms laid down have not been adhered to by the banks 
or the Government itself, such as with respect to the write-off of interest charges and duties 
or the induction of fresh funds. The Sub-Committees were told that, in the case of some ( 
public undertakings that were t~rri!e>J:ies __ taken over in the past, pre-nationalisation dues 
were still pending, (attendi~g\ to the difficulties of the units . 
. ._,,!.(~ 
12. This state of ambivalenCe will perhaps continue for some while. In the meantime, it will 
be instructive if limited experiments could be tried out pursuant to various suggestions offered 
to the Committee. One such suggestion is for common production planning by a group of sick 
public sector units located in reasonable proximity to one another and specialising in a similar 
range of products: by interacting closely with one another and ensuring that the output of one 
unit is accepted as input in a neighbouring unit and vice versa, both units, it is claimed could 
significantly improve/ the prospects of their survival. Should that be the case, even banks and 
fmancial institutions might decide to change their general approach. 
13. The Sub-Committees were specially enjoined to enquire into the reasons of persistent 
industrial backwardness in several parts of the country despite periodic announcements of 
official resolve to transform the existing state of affairs. The Sub-Committees studied the 
problem with respect to the North-Eastern parts of the. country, the retarded parts of the 
Indo-Gangetic plain and similar parts in the Deccan plateau, and in the water-bound Union­
Territories. The detailed observations by the Sub-Committees can be seen in the Annexures. 
14. There are reasons to feel deeply disturbed over the economic conditions obtaining in the N·l . 
North-Eastern States. Even the rudiments of basic infrastructures are missing from the 12" ... : 
region. Not even an apology of a railway network exists over large parts, the roads are ' 'if'~ 
equally sparse, power is hard to come by. Whatever agriculture is carried on is under difficult -

,l 
4 
lS 
drcumstan<:es 8Dd basically concentrated in a single season. It is the lack of power) and the 
pervasive official disinterestedness till now to discipline the Bra}lamputra that g'primarily 
responsible for the insufficient supply of irrigation water: The assured availability of such 
water could have easily led to the cultivation bf more than one crop in the vast stretches of 
arable tracts in the region. The creation of other infrastructural facilities, including roads, 
bridges and rail links, would not only have provided additional employment during the phase 
of their construction, but would have also created the appropriate setting for full-scale 
industrialisation. 
15~ Financial resources, it wDI be stated, constitute the major stumbling block, thwarting 
the regions's aspiration to develop infrastructure and propel a~ricultural and industrial 
growth to higher plateaus. The prevalence of insurgency conditions, it is further pointed out, 
! 
implies a considerable draiuing away of funds which could have ~n otherwise deployed for 
accelerating the develepment of basic: infrastructural sei"Viees. This later statement has an 
unusual import : as if till as long as ~surgency is not totaJJy ·put down, the people of the 
North-East have to forgo the luxury of economic progress which citizenry in the other parts of 
the country are entitled to. 
16. There can be, another way of looking at this causation between insurgency and lack of 
development. Once the Union and State Governments pool their resources together and 
embark on a planned programme of economic and industrial ·growth, which. succeeds in 
transforming the economic landscape of the area and creates several sources of employment 
and earnings, that itself would conceivably be the surest guarantee against the persistance of 
insurgency conditions. Young people join the ranks of rebels bealuse of the discontent they 
tuck within themselves. Such discontent may have diverse roots, but, more often than not, is 
triggered by the lack of economic opportunities, including opportunities for gainful work. 
Viewed in this context, allocation of funds for infrastruc~ural development Jtncl industrialisa­
tion have a degree of significance which is not less than that of the allocation made for 
strengthening the defence and security arrangements. in the region. / 
17. The planning of power too, there is no question, has gone awry in the North-Eastern 
States. This region has been the victim of a strange kind of applied bureaucratic reasonin1. 
The authorities concerned, including the Planning Commission, ha~e in the past cut back on 
ambitious programmes which desired to add to the capacity for power generation in the 
North-Eastern States and Union Territories. These were invariab~ turned down; since there 
was so little of agriculture and industry in the region, it would, it was explained, be a waste 
to ~nction funds for new power projects. Such an attitude was a major stumbling block in 
the way of any growth impulses being set in motion in the region. Stagnancy of growth wa~ 
used as pretext to deny any appreciable increase in power capacity in the subsequent p~&;ud) 
and the inability to add to power capacity ensured stagnancy. in growth. The CUZ(.;~t of this 
logic has to be broken if the North-Eastern States are to register any meaninb,.ful economic 
progress. 
18. The island territories, namely Andaman and Nicobar Islands and Lakshadweep, also 
serve special attention: Although these islands are Union Territories and thus are directly 
1 administered by the Centre, yet things have practically remained neglected in as far as 
! ; industrial development is concerned. The Ministry of Industry may therefore actively involve 
]\ itself in drawing up time-bound schemes for development and promotion of appropriate 
, · small scale units in these islands. · 
~ . 
19. Two other points are worth mentioning. First, it would be risky to assume that, with 
liberalisation, the responsibility for promoting industrial growth in the backward regions, 
including in the North-East, could be left the care of private entrepreneurs. On their own 

5 
private investors are unlikely to move to territories where basic infrastructure is lacking; nor 
are they themselve going to take the initiative to promote the growth of infrastructual 
facilities in these areas since the rate of return, at least in the initial period, will be limited. 
The Government will therefore have to fill the major role for developing most of the basic I 
infrastructures, including power. 
20. The other issue to ":!!!~~-"~"~t.~~Jion needs to be dra~-2ver and over ~ain- is, once ...,. 
more, the reported indi~~l)ce Q{J?Ankst. including nat~2~al_ise&J!_anJ<.!~!!S.fi~an~al 
institutions, to the "missio_I!_EL.E!Q.!!l_9ting gr~~ .iL~.l!~!wC!T4 !~iPJll.· Both banks and 
financial institutions have concentrated their activities on half-a-dozen major States. The 
bulk of the short-term credit and long-term finance have flown to these States. Even within 
these States, the Preference has been to finance activities in urban tracts and relatively 
prosperous farming zones. In contrast, large pockets of under-developed territories have 
continued to remain so. Government funds are low, and with bank and financial institutions 
not cooperating, the situation has actually worsened in recent years. The introduction of 
schemes such as the Prime Minister's Rozgar Y ojana has not made much of a difference. In 
the view of the Sub-Committees, progress of '!YOrk under the PMRY and other projects of a 
similar nature has bogged down because of less than enthusiastic cooperation on behalf of 
the banks. In the context of the criteria of profitability indicated for the banks and the 
rigour with which these have, of late, been sought to be enforced, the banks, it is entirely 
possible, have fo\md it inadvisable to support schemes proffered by small-scale operators 
under the PMRY; the risk of default on credit advanced is adjudged to be high with respect 
to this class of borrowers. It is in the circumstances desirable that the Ministry of Finance 
evolves special stipulations and norms for facilitating the speedy implementation of public 
projects with high employment-generating and output-hosting pOtential, in particular in 
designated backward areas. 
21. In any event, the general observations on how to pull the North-Eastern States out 
of the morass of backwardness would also apply in large measure in respect of areas of 
retarded 'growth in the States of the Indo-Gangetic Plain and elsewhere. The situation as it 
has emerged in the country is as if we are dealing with two separate human conglomerations. 
In the metropolitan and near-metropolitan pockets, the phenomenon observed is of 
industrialisation on a buoyant march: most of the available supply of power is earmarked to 
cater to the needs of these advanced tracts; the power distribution network is also so 
arranged .as to serve, almost exclusively, urban and semi-urban zones. As just mentioned, . 
b~ and public financial institutions have their gaze riveted on these pockets. The rest of 
the hinterland is, it seems, hardly anybody's concern. And yet, these later tracts are where 
the bulk of the population live and subsist .only marginally. 
n. Two dimensions of the problem of inter-re.l!,~llal economic. imbalances, in the 
Committee's view need to be demarcated. Firs'f""is the uneven spreacror·resoilrces ·and basic 
facilities between the different geographical regions; the second is the simultaneous existence 
of relatively advanced and retarded tracts within the same region. Conventional prescriptions 
for narrowing both categories of disharmony have not succeeded in the past; nor can it be 
assumed that simply because the country has embarked on the road to liberalisation, 
economic inequalities of diverse types Will disappear on their own.· If anything, it could well 
be the other way round. Whatever its failings and imperfections, the State will have to play 
a crucial role in narrowing the inter-regional unevenness. There is little point in lamenting 
upon the lack of adequate perceptions in the past about such economic imbalances. 
Stereotyped recommendations made by official and quasi-official agencies and institutions 
did not work, either because these were not implemented with any degree of sincerity and 

6 
thrust; or because these were targeted on misplaced objectives. In the judgement of the 
Committee, the Ministry of Industry will have henceforth to assume the lion's share of 
responsibntty for implementing programmes of industrial growth in hitherto backward areas. It 
will have to draw up detailed blueprints of projects in coDaboration with the Planning 
Commission and of course the State Governments concerned. The preliminary task is to map the 
obvious areas of backwardness in the different States, prepare det~;tiled data on locally available 
materials and other resources, and reach a measure of consensus on the priorities which should 
guide industrial growth in such pockets. Detailed prescriptions of "!hat iS to be done in each area 
should embrace, apart from infrastructural planning, such measures as the opening of a single 
window arrangement to cater to the requlremt!nts of potential entrepreneurs, and scrutiny of 
smaD·scale activities which are to be fully integrated with the dovetaUed into the national plan, 
the State and district plans and plans and programmes of agencies at both Central and State 
levels. People's representatives, including Members of Parl~ent, should be part of this 
planning process at all levels. It will be necessary to work out priorities between ditTerent schemes 
and proposals at different levels, accompanied by corresponding resource planning at each of 
these levels. Funds earmarked for a particular district or project, it hardly needs stating, should 
not be diverted elsewhere, and such funds should be released on time. 
23. None of these ideas is new. What is however called for is sustained application of 
energy to translate them into reality. No illusion should be entertained about the magnitude of 
the task, involved. The application of energy has to be of a sustained nature, not merely because 
of recommendations made in this or that official report. It will be the product of pressure 
generated from below. This is in a sense the single most important instrumentality for propelling 
industrial growth which the Committee cannot but take cognizance of. There is a stirring of 
impulses noticeable amongst hitherto deprived sections and communities dispersed over India's 
vast countryside. The level of literacy amongst these communities is still low despite fifty years 
. of independence. That has not prevented the awakenings of a degr~e of relevant consciousness : 
millions amongst these sections are fully aware of the nature and extent of social and economic 
deprivation of which they are victims and the distance which separtttes them form other classes, 
communities and regions. 
24. Economic retardation, it can be argued, is itself an exclusive function of illiteracy and 
ck of social consciousness. Viewed in that light, perhaps the very first obligation of a 
programme which pledges to cut through industrial backwardness in the dispersed parts of the 
country would be to launch a massive literacy programme in the areas of retared growth. 
25. This, then, is the crux of the issue. The extent of economic progress achievable in a 
particular region is as much a function of the resources that are applied as of the general milieu 
in which activities take shape. It is equally dependent upon the quality of awareness of the 
populace. The development process, to really get going, awaits an impetus to be articulated in 
the hinterland. "The demand for industrialisation and for larger opportunities for work and 
employment has to originate from below. This demand still does not develop in its on, but is a 
function of environmental conditions. The importance of the social factors which give shape to 
enviroment must not be under-rated. Nor can we ignore the reality that the spread of literacy 
plays a major part in the unfolding process. Literacy assists in the absorption of newer 
technology and in the acquisition of new skills even as it makes hitherto deprived and backward 
communities better aware of their rights as free and equal members of this nation. Not that 
social and political awamesss can blossom without an adequate base of literacy. When their time 
comes, historical forces will assert themselves, however, adverse the circumstances. Literacy, 
however, imparts a greater coherence to the impulses which at such states strive to express 
themselves. 
... 

7 
26. It is, in this connection, most important to urge for the full-scale association of 
women in the on-going process of industrial aud economic development in backward regions. 
The feudal stigma perSists, women continue to be socially oppressed in several balatant ways 
in almost all parts of the country. Certain current realities are self-evident. Given the limited 
resources a poor country can garner for growth and the built-in inequalities in the system, 
the vast majority of households are in any case at the receiving end of gross social injustice 
and rough, unfair treatment. Women in these households are generally far more subjected to 
social exploitation than the other numbers. This is on account of the traditional roles 
assigned to men and women from which easy exits are not possible in an ambience marked 
by horrendous feudal legacies. On the other hand, as developments particularly in Kerala 
illustrate, once literacy and nutritional standards improve for women, social mobilisation 
gains in strength, and stirrings from below for better economic advancement assume an 
immensely superior thrust. 
27. The plight of millions and millions of women engaged in industries such as coir and 
bidi manufacture or in other small-scale activities often beggars description. Irrespective of 
the provisions of our Constitution and those of the United Nations Charter of Human 
Rights, women workers are discriminated against in industry after industry in more ways 
than one : they suffer from low wages that are paid at irregular intervals and are very often 
victims of social indignities of assorted kinds at the workplace. The ultimate remedy of 
cour-ce lies in the better mobilisation of women under the umbrella of organised trade union 
' movements. The spread of mass literacy is likely to help the process of such mobilisation, 
but this is one area where it would not be prudent to leave things to autonomous market 
forces. The administration both at the Central and State levels have to step in and eDsure that 
\ 
statutory minimum wages for women in the specified industries are not only announced 
~ regularly but are duly implemenied. The hoUsing and security arrangements for migrant 
" ( women workers should be carefully monitored and superintended. Hours of work for women 
workers also need to be strictly regulated. Not only should the law be laid down in these 
areas; where necessary, special regulatory .bodies must be set up to ensure that the statutory 
injunctions are strictly followed. 
28. While our attention cannot veer away from the problem of poor, helpless 
unorganised women engaged in cottage crafts and similar other activities, and sometimes in 
large-scale industrial undertakings as well, it would not be irrelevant to take cognizance of· 
the problems faced by women who have entered the arena of industrial entrepreneurship or 
who have ventured into the field of industrial management. It is certainly for consideration 
whether women entrepreneurs, especilly in the relatively backward regions, might not be 
encouraged to set up cooperative institutions and are helped to obtain both rmancial 
accommodation and entrepreneurial advice from rmancial agencies including the NABARD 
and the IDBI. One interesting suggestion put forward in this connection is the earmarking of 
a quota for purchases at both State and the Central levels from establishments presided over 
by women entrepreneurs. 
l9. It is far from the Committee's intention to under-emphasise the role of good roads 
or rail and communication links to propel. development, nor would it ignore such a 
worthwhile suggestion that, apart from a solid primary education base, all backward areas 
should be blanketed by technical training institutions and polytechnics so that the supply of 
skilled labour does not lag when the development process really catches on. 
3i0. A number of other suggestions deserved to be followed up. The district Panchayat 
organisation should invariably have a special cell for planning industrial growth; arrange­
ments must be made to associate technical advice of the appropriate kind with this cell. 

8 
While the task of building and expanding the basic infrastructures will devolve on higher 
authorities, a mechanism must exist whereby the felt urges of the people are enabled to 
reach the . upper echelons in the decision-making proces$. 
31. It should be the primary responsibility of the Union and State Governments to 
earmarks funds to ensure that projects formulated at the local level on the basis of local 
resources are not allowed to die out. State Finance Commissions can be generally expected 
to allocate funds for implementing such local level programmes; it would still be desirable if 
resources are additionally made available, on a matching basis, by the Union Government 
for such purposes. 
32. As mentioned above, the development of basic infrastructures will largely depend 
on the contents and contours of planning at the national level; the role of the Union 
Government will necessarily loom large here. In the view of the Committee, while planning 
at the national level for infrastructural growth, the priorities hitherto followed need to be 
reversed, and project which would help enlarge the growth potential in hitherto retarded and 
deprived areas should be lifted to the top of the ageuda. That is to say, where the choice is 
between allocating funds at the margin on a project either in a backward region or in an 
area already r~~atively economically advanced, the decision should, as far as possible, be in 
favour of the hitherto retarded area. This is in fact the outstanding challenge the nation will 
have to face at the level of national decision-making in the coming years. In case, because of 
resource constraint, a temporary halt is ordered in capacity expansion of telecommunications 
or rail or road links in a metropolitan or semi-metropolitan region, it may have 
repercussions on the pace of growth in. the region for some while. On the other hand, such 
an embargo on the expansion of basic infrastructures might conceivably also lead to a more 
rational use and elimination of waste from out of existing capacity. True, such slackening of 
the pace of expansion of infrastructural facilities could inconvenience some interest groups 
and activate lobbies in their support. The authorities would do well not to yield to such 
developing pressures. That apart, the system of fiscal and monetary incentives should be so 
worked out that private entities, whether foreign or domestic, receive the appropriate signals 
at each point of time. Whatever the other compulsions, the persistence of economic 
backwardness over large stretches of the country has ominous social and political 
implications. Priorities at the national level must change in response to this challenge; the 
nature of this change must be det~rmined by impulses triggered from below. 
33. That a poor country is always hamstrung by lack of resources is a cliche. But 
because it is a cliche, its relevance is not reduced. What is essential is that Central and State 
Governments, as well as the dccentralised agencies further down the hierarchial line, must 
not -work at cross purposes and thereby fritter away resources. Political and ideological 
· positions of decision-makers at different levels will often tend to be at variance with one 
another in such a huge, big country such as ours. It would still be extraordinary if a large 
measure of agreement is not reached on the priority tasks in particularly retarded area. 
34. One point deserves separate mention. It would be neither desirable nor feasible to 
take away the executive and monitoring functions of the panchayat institutions in 
programmes aimed at eradicating industrial backwardness. No outside agency can fit into the 
format of decentralised development assigned to representative institutions the office-bearers 
of which have been elected on the basis of a democratic mandate. The panchayat bodies will 
continue to be the pivotal element for prepaiing the blueprint of growth at the local level 
and its subsequent implementation. It is for consideration whether during the on-going 
process, the panchayat agency concerned should not invite representatives of the State 
Government, the Union Government, banks and principal financial institutions to participate 

9 
in an advisory capacity. Reconciling the points of view of the different entities is not as 
difficult a task as it might seem initially, Once, through close interaction with one another, 
the realisation dawns that. their mutual roles are neither adversary nor competitive, the 
impulse to cooperate would being to gain instrength among the participating agencies, 
thereby facilitating the realisation of objectives the panchayat institution had set for itself. 
35. Some infrastructures, such as opening up rail and telecommunications links, belong 
to the exclusive domain of the Union Government. The creation of new rail connections has 
currently been reduced to a political football; priorities are more often than not determined 
on the basis of the clout of individual interest groups or individual Members of Parliament 
or particular State Governments. The time, has certainly come, the Committee feels, when 
this pattern is substituted by a more rational system of priority fixation. As argued earlier in 
this Report, the demand for industrialisation emanating from hitherto neglected regions will 
call for a shift in the agenda. In the view of the Committee, the Ministry of Industry and the 
Ministry of Railways may consider constituting a joint decision-making body in association 
with the Planning Commission to decide the aggregate sum to be set aside for railway 
development in the country's backward regions over a five-year period, indicate its region­
wise breakdown and settle the sequence according to with the projects are to be executed. 
State Governments will have the right to approach these inter-ministerial Committees with 
their suggestions and recommendations. Similarly, within each State, while deciding the 
priorities and Scheduling of infrastructure development, the State Government may consider 
taking into account representations from below and have them processed for onward 
despatch to the Centre. 
36. The Committee envisages no difficulty. in case a similar pattern were to emerge with 
respect :to the planning and implementation of other infrastructural activities, such as road 
building and creation of new telecommunications facilities. The State Government will 
express its views on the determination of priorities, but it will exercise no veto, just as no 
Central agency will have a veto either. The endeavour will be to reach a consensus on the 
basis of continuous efforts at accommodating pne another's point of view. This, after all, . 
constitutes the core of a genuinely federal arrangement. 
37. The Union Government, particularly in the Ministries of Industry and Finance, 
should, in the view of the Committee, exercise appropriate moral suasion so that State 
Governments desist from competitive soliciting of private investment in their respective 
States by, for example, lowering local taxes such as sales taxes. Such a practice reduces 
realisable revenue for all the States and thus reduces their overall capability to finance 
industrial development on their. own. It would at the same time be desirable that the Union 
Government takes a sympathetic view of representations about the desirability .of granting 
certain prerogatives to State Governments in terms of Articles 268 and 269 of the 
Constitution, a gesture which· is unlikely to alfed the revenue growth potential for the Centre. 
The nation can develop only on the basis ol endeavours shared by the Union and State 
Governments; a dog-in-the-manger policy can scarcely help the cause of balanced national 
economic growth. 
38. The Committee would like to append a further observation. The package of policies 
for promoting industrial growth in backward areas had tended to be applied in the past in 
piecemeal fashion, often sequentially, without taking into account the total picture of 
possibilities; For instance, there have been announcements from time to time of 'holidays'. 
from income and corporate taxes on behalf of the Centre and sales tax on behalf of the 
States, or of transport subsidies, of supply of inputs at subsidised rates, and so on. What bas, 
however, been missing is an intearated approach which bas also the imprimatur of approval of 

10 
banks and financial institutions. The Committee would like-to see this disarray substituted 
by a blueprint of recommendations vetted jointly by all agencies concerned for, say, a five­
year period, which will then be rolled over from year to year. Such blueprints should be 
made. prepared for all levels of administration. The blueprint at the national level should 
be dovetailed into the State and panchayat level blueprints. Requests for alterations and 
adjustments on a year-to-year basis could emanate either at the national level or the State 
level or from the panchayat bodies themselves. 
39. A measure of confusion may be inevitable in the beginning in such exercises. That 
is. only to be expected. Once the agencies and institutions operating at different levels are 
forced to interact with one another, the sheer weight of circumstances would perhaps 
persuade them to agree on a common set of goals and a common set of instrumentalities 
to attain these goaly / 
RECOMMENDATIONS 
.. 1. The Ministry of Industry should assume the main responsibility to implement 
Pfogramme of industrial growth in hitherto backward areas. It should draw up detailed 
btbeprints of projects in collaboration with the Planning Commission and the State 
Governments. Detailed prescriptions of task for each area should embrace, apart from 
infrastructural planning such measures as the opening of a single window arrangement to 
cater to the requirements of potential entrepreneurs. 
2. The Planning Commission should, in collaboration of the State Governments and the 
Union Ministry, set up a cell to monitor the availability of basic infrastructural facilities 
such as railway net-work, power, roads and water. 
3. There is need to formulate clear-cut policies with regard to the -role the Union 
Government intends to play in respect of its public sector undertakings, particularly with 
reference to disinvestment of its share holding. 
4. The Government also n~eds to r.e-derme its policy concerning the public undertakings 
which are under reference to the Bureau for Industrial and Financial . Reconstruction 
(BIFR). In this connection, possibilities may be explored for cqmmon production planning 
by a group of sick public sector units located in reasonable proximity to one another and 
specialising in a similar range of products. 
5. The banks have not been till now very enthusiastic about projects under the Prime 
Minister's Rozgar Yojana. It is necessary to ensure that time-bound measures are taken for 
examination, allocations and release of funds for such of these projects as are considered 
viable. It is desirable that the Ministry of Finance evolves special stipulations and norms for 
facilitating speedy implementation of projects with high employment generating and output· 
boosting po~ential, in particular in the designated backward are~s. 
6. In order to provide an integrated approach for the industrial development, blueprints 
of recommendations should be vetted jointly by all agencies concerned for, say, for a five 
year period. Such blueprints should be worked out at all levels of administration. The 
blueprints at the national level shoul~ be dovetailed into those for the States and panchayats. 
7. The North-Eastern States have had been long neglected. It isnecessary that a time­
bound programme is drawn up for economic development of this region, with emphasis on 
the development of basic infrastructures such as rail links, roads, air services, power and 
water. 
8. Transport and tax subsidies to backward areas and the North-Eastern regions should 

11 
continue; care should also be taken that transport subsidies are also paid in time and adjusted 
upwards when considered appropriate. 
9. The Ministry. of Industry should take special time-bound measures for development 
and promotion of smali scale units in the island territories of the country, namely, Andaman 
and Nicobar Islands; the transport subsidy prevalent here should continue without restric­
tions; the port facilities in the islands must also be improved. 
10. The Union Government should sympathetically consider representations about the 
desirability of granting certain prerogatives to the State Governments in terms of Articles 268 
and 269 of the Constitution so larger funds become available for industrial growth on the 
initiative of State Government~ 
11. Requests from State Governments for declaration their States as industrially 
backward should be considered sympathetically, and the criteria for declaring a State 
industrially backward should be reviewed from time to time so as to take into account shifting 
realities. 
12. All backward areas should be blanketed by technical and vocational training 
institutions and polytechnics so as to ensure supply of skilled labour when the development 
process catches on. 
13. District panchayats should play much greater role than at present for planning 
industrial growth. Special cells should be set up in the district panchayats and arrangements 
made to provide technical advice of the appropriate kind with such cells. 
14. The Union and State Governments should earmark funds to ensure that projects 
formulated at the local level on the basis of local resources are not allowed to die out. 
15. Panchayat institutions should be vested with executive and monitoring IJ9Wers for 
implementing programmes aimed at eradicating industrial backwardness. The responsibility 
for preparing blueprints of growth at the local level and its subsequent implementation should 
rest with the panchayats. 
16. The Ministries of Industry and Railways should constitute a joint decision-making 
body alongwith the Planning Commission to decide on the aggregate sum to be set aside for 
railway development in the country's backward regions over a five year period, indicate its 
region-wise break-down and settle the sequence according to which the projects are to be 
executed. 
17. In order to cut through industrial backwardness, massive literacy programme should 
be launched in the areas of retarded growth. · 
1 18. Women should be associated in the on-going process of industrial and economic 
development in the backward regions. The administration both at the Union and State levels 
must-ensure that statutory minimum wages for women in the specified industries are not only 
announced but also duly implemented. 
19. The housing and security arrangements for migrant women WQrkers need to be 
carefully monitored and superintended and working hours for women should be strictly 
regulated and adhered to. 
20. Women entrepreneurs, especially in the relatively backward regions, should be 
encouraged to set up cooperative institutions and help may be provided to them to obtain both 
financial accommodation and ~ntrepreneurial advice from rmancial agencies including the 
NABARD, the IDBI and other financial institutions. ,/ [.

2. The Chairman informed the Members that, as per the decision of the Committee to 
study the subjects of Industrialisation of Backward Areas in the country and the status of 
Capital Goods Industrtial. Units, the comments of the Ministry of Industry were obtained 
and have since been circulated. With a view to studying the subjects in all its dimensions, it 
was necessary that an on-the-spdt-visit of industrially under-developed regions was underta­
ken in smaller groups. These groups could also avail of the opportunity of such visits by 
going round central industrial units under the administrative control of the Ministries of 
Industry, Steel and Mines operating in those regions for a better appreciation of their 
performance. After some discussion, it was decided to constitute five Sub-Committees 
consisting of nine members each. The Sub-Committees would prepare a report on their 
studies for consideration by the Committee. The Sub-Committee may select one industrially 
backward region and the industrial units in that region for on-the-spot study. The Committee 
also decided that a member serving on a particular Sub-Committee maf associate himself or 
herself for the study with any other Sub-Committee(s) to which he or she has a background 
of knowledge and experience. The Committee authorised the Chairman of the Committee to 
nominate such Committees and appoint Convenors for each Sub-Committee. The Committee 
also authorised the Chair.man of the Committee to seek the permission of the Hon'ble 
Chairman, for the visits of the Sub-Committees to different regions. The Committee 
authorised the Sub-Committees to identify the places of their visit(s).

TWELFTH MEETING 
The Committee met at 5~00 P.M. on Wednesday, the 6th December, 1995 in Room

Study note re£ardinalst Phase visit of Sub-Committee-! of Department-related Parliamentary 
Standing Committee on Industry to Rajasthan during September 13-18, 1995 
The Sub-Committee-I of the Committee on Industry _was constituted to study the 
problems of industrialisation in backward areas and capital goods manufacturing industry in 
five States viz. Rajasthan, Gujarat, Maharashtra, Madhya Pradesh and Goa. The Sub­
Committee consisted of six Members, namely:-
1. Shri Ramdas Agarwal 
2. Shri Anil Kumar 
3. Shri Rajendra Prasad Mody 
4. Shri Chiranji Lal Sharma 
5. Shri P.G. Narayanan 
6. Shri Rasheed Masood 
Shri Ramdas Agarwal was the Convenor of the Sub-Committee. 
The Committee at first visited Rajasthan during September 13-18, 1995 for the purpose 
and held discussions with representatives of Chamber of Commerce and Industries, officers . 
of State Industry and other Departments, district authorities of Udaipur and Sirohi and 
workers and management of Hindustan Zinc Limited and Hindustan Copper Limited. The 
salient features of the outcome of study are as under:-
On 14th September, 1995, the Sub-Committee heard the views of the officers of the 
State Industry Department, district authorities and representatives of Chamber of Commerce 
and Industries of Udaipur and Workers' Unions and Management of Hindustan Zinc 
Limited. 
Officers of State Industry Department 
Giving an overview of the industrial scenario in Rajasthan, the Director of Industries 
pointed out that though Rajasthan was the second largest State in terms of area, it was 
economically backward one as compared to the other States in the country. The per capita 
income of the State was Rs. 3983 as against the National per capita income of Rs. 5598. The 
State has only 17 Kms. of railway route per thousand sq. Kms. There were only 335 large 
and medium scale industries in existence on 31.3.94. This number was much le~ in 
comparison with the neighbouring States like Maharashtra and Gujarat. Similarly there /were 
only 64437 registered SSI units in Rajasthan as on 31st March, 1993 which was just 2.88% of 
the total registered SSI units in the country: Mining and mineral based industries are th~ key 
industries in Rajasthan. Despite having great potential for automobiles, electronics and 
· petroleum based industries in Rajasthan no significant development of such industries had 
taken place in the State. He attributed inadequate road, rail and airlinks as the main reasons 
.for slow industrial developll}ent of Rajasthan. Accor,ding to him ceramics, wool based 
industries, leather industrie~. ·electronics, agro processing and mineral processing industries 
had good potential provided adequat~ thrust for development. The Sub-Committee had been 
given to understand that the Government of Rajasthan was very keen for industrial 
development. Number ·of'' effective steps had been taken such ~S- development of industrial 
areas, entrepreneurial development programme, and ade,'luate arrangement for finance. 
35 

36 
Besides, number of financial concession and facilities had been announced viz. capital 
investment subsidy, sales-tax deferment/exemption, testing equipment subsidy, subsidy on 
purchase of testing equipment for registration under -ISO 9000 series etc. State Government 
has also allowed enhanced capital iqvestment subsidy to the industries set up in .the tribal 
sub-area plan of Udaipur and Sirohi district and NIDs viz. Sirohi, Barmer and Jaisalmer. 
State Government has developed all the infrastructural facilities required for industrial 
development. One or more industrial areas with full infrastructural facilities have been 
developed by the State to encourage and assist the new industrial ventures. State investment 
subsidy upto 30% to SSI/LMI, as the case may be, has also been provided. 
Udaipur Chamber of Commerce and Industry 
The Sub-Committee was told by the representatives of the Udaipur Chamber of 
Commerce and Industry that the Southern Rajasthan was rich in natural and mineral 
resources. This region is essentially a tribal belt and has immense potential and offers 
excellent climate for all round industrial development. Unfortunately industries based on the 
minerals could not be developed for want of infrastructural facilities such as lack of (i) broad 
gauge link (Udaipur to Chittorgarh and to Ahmedabad), (ii) railway link to Banswara, 
(iii) airport of international standard, (iv) adequate air services despite the fact that 
Udaipur has immense tourism potential, (v) by-pass on the National Highway No. 8 for 
Udaipur, (vi) proper tran,sport facilitate for movements" of goods and (vii) high cost of power 
coupled with its ·shortage and poor quality. 
It had been brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee that the District Industries 
Centres (DIC) which were established for giving single window service. and to act as nodal 
agency for industrial development, after initial few yeats of establishment~ had been totally 
disintegrated. They were .unable to play any major role in the industrial development as they 
had been entrusted with various miscellaneous work. Similarly though the entire economy of . 
the region is dependent on the exploitation of the mineral wealth, the recent actions of the · 
Environment and Forest Ministry are detrimental to any development in the mining sector. 
Cases of de-reservation from the forest of the areas rich in mineral deposits are not being 
done or taking lot of time which delays the mining activities. 
The Sub-Committee was also told that though entire Southern ·Rajasthan was essentially 
tribal dominant, tax holiday scheme was not applicable to this region as was applicable to 
many other regions of the country. Imposition of TDS from this financial year on services 
including transport services was creating lo~ of pr~blems and therefore needed to be 
abOlished. Similarly Octr()i whicb has detrimental. effect Ol), th~ lo~aJ and commercial 
acti~es should be totally abolished to ensure fast ·indaisitW,··.aiut·economic growth. 
w.rk..S 1JDkHJs o1 llblt:lwiM Zlm: Llmf~«~ 
The SulrC~mmittee was infOrmed by Union's.representative of Hiridustan Zinc limited 
that they bad never allowed work ol this Public Sector Undert~~ing.to suffer on account of 
any industrial-dispute. In fact, ther~ lia,s not ·beep: a si~~ 51f~k~ · in HZL ever since its 
ineo.rpor.ation as a Public ~r Undertaking in January. 1966. Their approach has been to 
settle their problems/disputes through negotiations with the management. The workers also 
participate- in various social welfue activities for tbe· persons living around industrial units of 
HZL and have beeB contn)utiflgJrom. their welfare fuuds as well as fmm the salary of the 
workers f07 the victims of natural calamities from time to time. 
. __ The Sub-Committee was given to UJ1.demand that though liZL was profit making PSU, 
one of its smelter at Chanderiya was unable to operate to its full capacity. It was giving only 
50% production of its/capacity. The Unions also expressed apprehension about the adequacy 

37 
of safety measures taken at the Rajpura Oariba Mine as a result of w}:lich number of 
accidents took place there. Some of the problems relating to other industries in Rajasthan 
highlighted by the Unions were viz. exploitation of workers engaged in marble mining, child 
Jabour, benami mining, non-payment of minimum wages and lack of infrastructural facilities 
such as road, power, water etc. 
Management of Hindustan Zinc Umited 
The Management of the Hindustan Zinc Limited informed the Sub-Committee that it was 
a profit making Public Sector Undertaking. It operates 7 lead zinc mines with a total ore 
production capacity of 3.22 million tpa - 4 smelters with combined installed capacity on 
149000 tpa zinc and 65000 tpa lead besides a rock phosphate - tungsten mine. The physical 
and financial performance of the company for the past five years had shown significant 
upsurge in lead-zinc concentrate and metal production besides maintaining a good track 
record of generating surpluses. HZL was one of the few PSU's identified by the Government 
of India for disinvestment of its holdings. HZL shares are now listed on the Bombay, Delhi 
and Jaipur stock exchanges. The management is very responsive to the genuine demands I 
problems of its employees and workers and employee employer relations are cordial. HZL is 
also conscious of its responsibility for protection of the environment. The company is in the 
forefront in taking well planned and effective steps to check pollution and create healthy 
surroundings for its work force and populace residing in and around its units. The company 
is equally concerned about its socio-economic role. The concern manifests itself in the form 
of numerous rural health campaigns, family welfare activities, ~dult education programmes, 
water supply, installation of handpumps, providing grains and fodder etc. The management 
attributed the low production from Chanderia smelter to the ·adoption of new technology 
and assured the Sub-Committee that it was a temporary phase and once the operational 
problems and other things settled down, the plant will work to its fullest capacity. They also 
allayed the fear of the workers about inadequate safety measures at Rajpura Dariba Mine 
and assured that all possible safety measures were being taken by HZL in all its units and 
the number of industrial accidents was negligible. 
On 15th September, the Sub-Committee held discussions with the district authorities of 
Sirohi and representatives of Abu Chamber of Commerce and Industries. 
District Authorities of Sirobf. 
The District Magistrate of Sirohi informed the Sub-Committee that there were about 3500 
individual units-seven large, some medium and rest SSI in Sirohi district. Eight industrial 
areas are being developed by. the Rajasthan Government in the district. Mo~~ of the 
industries in Sirohi are mineral based and as such the minerals have an important role in the 
e~onomy pf the district. The main minerals are limestone, woallast.onite base metal like lead, 
zin<:; .copper, tungston, calicide .etc. The Sirohi district was of the category of "No Industry 
District" earlier and drawn all the. concessions and facilities of NID. However its industrial 
growth has accelerated very fast with the incoming of entrepreneurs from within the State as 
well as from Gujarat State. The District Magistrate pointed out that to further accelqrate the 
.. pace of development in the district, it was essential first to upgrade the existing 
infrastructure such as availability of industrial plots, enhancement in technical as well as 
entrepreneurial skill and improvement in the conditions of artisans and household sectors as 
well as augmentation of power, rail and road network, communication and industrial training 
facilities. Potential for export can be explored for the products lik~ cement, marble I granite 
tiles and polyester and synthetic yarns. The Sub-Committee has been given to understand by 
the district author~ties that the main problem being faced by the entrepreneurs is 
the grant of loan for working capital by the banks. The bankers are flouting 

38 
norms laid by the RBI and ask for surety. The Banks are reluctant to give loans to even 
those entr-epreneurs whose names are cleared by the DIC. Similarly, charging of entire 
cost of electrification from the entrepreneurs for their industry by Rajasthan State 
Electricity Board is adversely ~ng the growth of industry. Unemployment/ 
under employment is rampant in the State. For industrial development, they were of the 
opinion that among other things, tax holiday scheme be made applicable to Sirohi district 
as the growth centres were located in tribal areas which are very backward. 
Abu Chamber of Commerce and Industries 
lbe Sub-Committee had been given to understand that despite declaring Abu Road as 
backward area and a notified Tribal belt and a "No In~ustry District" by Central 
Government the position was that out of 300 odd units, .almost 100 were in possession of 
Rajasthan Finance Corporation, and ab9ut 100 were closed an~ out of the remaining 100 
odd units, only 10 to 15 were running well and the rest were being termed as defaulters 
by the RICCO and RFC. In a memorandum to Sub-Committee Abu Chamber had 
submitted that this situation was mainly due to non-development of desired infrastructure 
even though the desired funds might had been made available. According to them the 
major bottle-necks leading to weak infrastructure were:-
(a) Lack of banking facilities and non-availability of working capital funds to S.S.I. 
sector in particular. 
(b) Non-availability of even unskilled and skilled labour due to the tribal belt. 
(c) Non-av~Hability of qualified managerial and technical staff since none wants to 
settle down in such a backward place even at Jucurative terms due to absence of 
educational, medical, health and otber growth facilities. 
(d) Non-availability of quality power. 
r 
(e) Non-availability of local market due to absence of large public sector unitS as 
compared to areas in Gujarat where one big project will sustain more than 50 to 100 
. S.S.I. units in its surroundings. 
(f) Non Co-operative attitu~e of th.e Government to backward areas. 
(g) Lack of infrastructure for raw material support by State and Central Government. 
(h) Lack of communication facilities. 
(1) Lack of transportation net-work etc. 
(J) Non-compliance of RBIIBIFR guidelines by the State financial institutions ~4v 
commercial banks for rehabilitation and revival of potentially viable sick/weak ..,,~i .,;. 
The Chamber of Commerce and Industries also submitted that the exd.~t: rules which 
were framed in 1944 have become old, outdated and misfit in the }.1reseat context. and 
therefore, should be made simpler, rationale and realistic. The tax holiday benefit scheme 
for the units established in. North East backward areas should be · extended to the 
backward area like Abu Road which bas been declared '"No Industrial District" and is 
situated in tribal backward area. Banks/financial institutions should be directed to help 
SSI in order to save them from extinction. Adequate measures should be taken to ensure 
that g1,1idelines issued by the RBI to all commercial banks and financial institutions for 
granting of reliefs and concessions which cap be extended to potentially viable sick/weak 
units under rehabilitation scheme be complied by the financial institutions and bank&. 
The Sub-Committee met the Industry Secretary of Government of Rajasthan and 

39 
officers of other government departments connected with the industrialisation and the 
Deputy Chief Minister on 17th September, 1995 .at Jaipur. 
Meeting with State Government Otncers 
::·.' ~ . ~ 
The Sub-Committee was told by the Industry Secretary that RICCO which had been 
entrusted with the work of development of infrastructure and_ arranging capital i~v~tmeat 
for the growth of industries in Rajasthan was. doing a good job. It had acquiredl311ds and 
developed industrial areas in many districts. He also-informed the Sub-Committ<?e·,-~at with. 
a view to keep the pa~ of industrial growth in Rajasthan, Government was giving subsidy to 
"No Industrial Afea" which had earlier been given by the Central Government. The 
Government was also giving sales-tax exemptions and had started merchant banking 
activities. He suggested that with a view to keep balance between the under developed and 
developed States particularly after liberalisation, the Central Government should make 
sufficient allocation of funds for the development of infrastructural facilities in the 
industrially backward States. This would also check migration of entrepreneurs from under 
developed States·· to developed States. Natural Gas should be arranged for industries in 
Rajasthan from HBJ pipeline which passes through the State. Bankers attitude towards the 
private entrepreneurs for granting loan be changed and working capital be made available in 
time to save industries. Research and Development and training institutes be opened in 
Rajasthan. It was pointed out to the Sub-Committee that whatever industrialisation had 
taken place in Rajasthan was in or around cities or town. No industry had come up il) the 
backward remote area. Interest rate by financial institutions, therefore, needed tp be 
lowered for backward areas with a view to encouraging the setting up of industries in .. those 
areas. Subsidy by the Centre may continue but in the form of central assistance. IDBl policy 
of non-financing a substantially exposed sector in a State was not justified and had shown 
adverse effect. It should, therefore, be changed and the exposure should be in the conte~ of 
the country and not in the State. V set earth station was required to develop electronics in 
the State particularly for the development of -software. Earth Station refused by the Orissa 
may be given to Rajasthan. 
Some of the other suggestions for industrial development in Rajasthan given to the Sub­
Committee were setting up of tool rooms in the State for proper development of industries 
particularly SSI, training facilities for the development of ceremic industry, central fiscal 
policies be directed towards backward States, creation of funds by the Central Government 
for development of backward areas. It was also pointed out to Sub-Committee that the 
procedure of registration of Khadi Samities was very complicated and cumbersome and 
discouraged development of Khadi industry. This should be simplified and training 
programme for village artisans engaged in Khadi industries be arranged. All power grids in 
the country should be united together by creating National Power Grid instead of regional 
grids. This would enable utilising the excess power being generated by the State Electricity 
Boards of one State by the other power deficit State Electricity Board. Working capital be­
arranged exclusively for SSI by banks or some other separate agencies. Marketing of the 
produce of SSI be arranged and ensured. Handicrafts need to be encouraged. 
The Deputy Chief Minister of Rajasthan had suggested that the Centre must ensure 
equal growth of all the States. Competition in giving incentives by States should be checked 
by the Central Government with a view to stop the fleeing of entrepreneurs to a State giving 
more incentives. As the banks are not cooperating in the implementation of the Prime 
Minister Rozgar Yojna, the implementation of same may be given to the bank themselves or 
it should be made mandatory for them to accept the recommendation of the State industry 
department. The Khadi Development Fund on which interest rate is 15% should be reduced. 

i 
40 
Inland container depot for exporters should be expedited and impediments/delays in 
clearance of projects by the various Central ministries/departments should be eliminated. 
On 18th September, 1995 the Sub-Committee visited Khetri and heard the views of the 
Workers' Union of Hindustan Copper Limited and U&;·$anagement. 
Representatives of the Worker Union 
The representatives of the recognised Union told . the SUb-Committee that import of 
copper by private sector after liberalisation had advenely affected the Company. The prices 
were now required to be made competitive with international market and fixed according to 
the London Metal Exchange. The Coinp~ny ·had faced financial crunch in the past and in 
that period of financial crisis the worke~. and employees had agreed to suspend some of 
ithefi" faCilities such as LTC and OTA. It was pointed out that the Hindustan Copper Limited 
was the· only industry in Khetri. No ancilliary or auxilliary industry ci:>uld .come up in Khetri 
mainly due to lack of infrastructural facilities and as such there was .. unemployment in and 
around Khetri. It was suggested that the present capacity of Khetri smelter and refinery 
plant producing copper be extended from 31000 tpa to 100000 tpa. Further, instead of 
importing ore concentrate for that purpose, Ule Company shpuld develop mines in and 
around Khetri and other places in India which will not only save [Qteign· eKI!ange. but will 
also generate employment. There was acute water scarcity in th~ area and wliatever ground 
water was available had got polluted by the effluent •barged by tbe plant. Khetri was not 
linked. with the railway. There was no techttical institute in Khetri where local populace 
could be trained in mining and other technical jobs. It was also brought to the notice of the 
Sub-Committee that though there were three senior secondary schools in Khetri, none of 
them was exclusively for girls. The people of the town, therefore were hesitant to send their 
girls to school after middle level. Transport facility had not been provided to the employees 
and workers and they had to depend mainly on the State roadways for commuting to and 
from their places of work. It was also brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee that the 
matters of compensation to the dependents of the deceased employees/workers either by 
way of providing employment or compensation, linger on for years and there was no 
provision for giving employment to the lady member of such families. Some of the 
representatives of the unrecognised unions told the Sub-Committee that the management 
was anti-labour. No elections had been held for the unions for the last 10-15 years or so. 
The management did not take . into confidence the office bearers of unrecognised unions in 
any matter connected· with the workers. There was rampant corruption in the company. 
Engineers and officials were in league with the contractors. There were no promotional 
avenues at lower level and many plants in the Company were being run without tfiking 
adequate safety measures as a result, number of industrial accidents take place every year. 
The Management of HhJdustan Copper Limited 
The Sub-Committee had been given to understand by th~ management that copper 
mining industry in India had some inherent constraints due to low grade of Indian ore and 
poor geometry of the Indian ore body. As agatnst 3-4% copper content in ore in most of the 
o~er copper producing countries in the world, the average copper content of Indian ore was 
just around 1%. Though mining cost in terms of per tonne ore in India was more or less 
comparable with international standard but in terms of metal it was 2.5-3 times higher than 
the international cost because of poor grade of ore. 
In the initial years, the company incurred losses because of teething troubles, 
unremunerative administered prices not related to the cost of production from very low 
grade of copper ore available in India and lack of investments ·in mines. However, the 
company continued its efforts for increasing production and productivity through various in-

41 
house measures. HCL ·also built up sound foundation for workers' participation in -
management with forums being availed at various tiers from shop floor to the Joint 
Consultative Committee for the copper indqstry at the national level. Cordial industrial 
relations in all the production units was also a contributory factor for improvement in 
physical performance. With liberalisation of the economic policy, import of copper had been 
decanalised and the administered pricing system of copper bad been abolished from March, 
1992 thereby leaving HCL to sell its products in competition to the landed cost of imported 
copper notwithstanding the inherent constraints of Indian copper ore. 
With the implementation of KCC expansion scheme at a marginal cost of around· 
Rs. 365 croresJ HCL's profitability was estimated to increase by around Rs. 165 crores per 
annum. The expansion in Malanjkhand Mine and opening of Cbapri-Shiddeswar Mine.. would· 
increase the availability of concentrates and to that extent there would be lesser import of 
concentrates for meeting the requirements of expanded capacity of Khetri smelter. The 
expansion in the capacity of ICC smelter to about double its present capacity at' a very 
marginal cost, would further boost the profitability of the Company, besides narrowing the 
gap between demand and domestic production. The Company had a plan of entering into 
export market in the long run, once the self-sufficiency in the production of copper in the 
country was achieved, as HCL's incremental cost of production was likely to be much lower 
compared to other prospective producers in the country. HCL thus plans to be the leader of 
copper industry in India. ,.

bringing imported concentrate from Kandla Port to the plant: This will also help in the

recognised or unrecognised, frequently and takes the ~iews of all the unions into

expectation of the local populace are too many from the Company. HCL is conscious of its 
social obligations and protection of environment for people working in its units as well as the 
people living in and around Khetri. The Company is running a 209 bed hospital where five

sections. It organises free eye camps every year and medical camps in surrounding villages 
every month. Considering acute drinking water problem of the area, the Company is

Khetrinagar. Number of handpumps have been installed fot the villagers where .direct supply 
is not possible. In order to meet the requirement of water for its operations, the Company is

The visit of Sub-Committee to Bombay arid Goa was earlier scheduled from 14 to 18 
Oct.ober, 1995. But due to unsatisfactory stay arrangements at Bombay, the Convenor of 
Sub-Committee had to postpone the visit. The Sub-Committee rescheduled its visit from 13 
to 17 November, 1995 after getting assurance from the Ministry of lndu~try to the effect that 

42 
' such lapse would not occur and all possible care would be taken to make the visit 
purposeful. 
The Sub-Committee camped at Bombay on 13.- November, 1995 where it interacted 
with the representatives of the State government of Maharashtta followed by · a 
discussion with the management of Metal Scrap Trade Corporation Limited. On 14 
November, the Sub-Committee held discussion with Maharashtra Chamber of Commerce 
with regard to . the problems the entrepreneurs were facing in setting/running industries 
in the S~~te. This was followed by a discussion with the bank management regarding 
the pregress of Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana in the State and also a meeting with the 
management of Mazagaon Dock Limited regarding port facilities available for industrial 
uriits in the State. Before leaving for Goa, the Sub-Committee held discussion with the

At Goa, the Sub-Comiiuttee ·started its business With a meeting with the 
representatives of the State government of Goa on 16 November, 1995 on issues 
concerning industrialisation of the State. Thereafter, the Sub-Committee met with the 
representatives of Goa Chamber of Commerce and Industry and the Goa Mineral Ore­
Exporters Association in two separate meetings. Before dispersing on 17 November, the.
