PreseJJtatloa of State Governmeat of Mabarssbtra 
The Sub-Committee started its business with a meeting with the representative of 
State gevernment of. Maharashtra at Bombay. The Industry Secretary in the beginning 
mentioned about the excellent infrastructural facilities available for setting up of 
industries in the State. Udyog Mitra was working as a single window agency in the 
State to guide/help entrepreneurs in setting up industries. He conceded that high 
subsidy was provided for industries in backward areas but of late, a pocket of 
prosperity in terms of concentration of industries had grown up. In Bombay itself there 
was no further scope of setting more industries though entrepreneurs were desperate 
enough to have their industries in the city. The State government had now applied 
.disincentives in the city. 
The Industry Secretary very honestly admitted again that there were certain lacunae 
in the concept of single window agency for setting up industries. Certain environmental 
clearance requirements were alsO' coming on the way of dispersal of industries. Entrep­
reneurs interested in setting up 'industries in backward or far off areas fund it extremely 
difficult to get green, si~:as•~- had to, pass through inany agencies. The Secretary 
emphasised that the Central Governm-ent 'Should give clear cut instructions/guidelines on 
pollution issue. The Ministry of Envirotftnent should review its policy to delegate 
powers in setting up of industries. 
He was also critical of the present labour laws which according to him needed to 
be simplified. An entrepreneur, particularly who was willing io set up a small unit, 
found the labour laws very difficult to be tackled with •. He mentioned specifically about 
the Factories Act which at present was applicable to industries/units employing more 
than ten persons. He suggested that in order to mitigate the difficulties of entrepreneurs 
and persuading them to set up industries in backward areas it was important that 
industries employing less than fifty. persons should be kept out of the Factories Act. He 

43 
also emphasised that non-core sector should be controlled which otherwise had become a 
den of corrupt practices. According to him Coal Control Order should be dispensed with. 
As regards small scale sector, the Secretary mentioned that production of items reserved 
for this sector declined after reservation of certain items. He said that the experience 
indicates that subsidy beyond a point was not a sustainable step. It was equally important 
that the government· should oonsider enacting new legislation ·for the protection of small 
scale entrepreneur who was under pressure from different directions. According to him small 
scale sector needed to' be totally reoriented as the speed of decision making was too sJow for 
this sector which was producing 40% of the ·total turnover of the country with only 11% 
credit available. It would also be desirable to have separate banks for industrial financing. 
As regards the Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana, the Industry Secretary informed that 
banks were not interested in more cases. He wanted that banks should scrutinize double 
cases than what had been fixed as the target so that the target of disbursement was achieved. 
The Sub-Committee feels that the Government should appreciate the positive suggestions 
put forth by the State Government with regard to simplification of labour legislation and need 
for separate legislation for small scale entrepreneurs. The Sub-Conumttee also feels that the 
suggestions regarding the need for clear cut guidelines by the Environment Ministry on 
pollution and also tile need for decontrol of non-core sector are worth examining. This would 
certainly strengthen the single window system for industries. 
Metal Scrap Trade Corporation (MSTC) 
The management informed the Sub-Committee that MSTC was primarily engaged in 
two activities viz., (i) Foreign Trade, and (ii) Domestic Trade. 
In Foreign Trade, MSTC was the sole Canalising Agency for import of scrap till 
February, 1992. Import during the last two years of canalisation were 15,44,000 tonnes and 
2,80,000 tonnes. Imports.during 1992-93 went upto 6,82,000 tonries. However, in subsequent 
years, only 307,000 & 278,000 tonnes were imported. Total imports of scrap by the country 
have also come down during these years resulting in market share of MSTC being around 
30% . MSTC had, therefore, embarked upon a Corporate Planning exercise to identify areas 
with potential for diversifying so as to remove dependence on one item, namely, scrap. It 
has also simultaneously been trying to expand the basket of imports by including other items 
such as Pig Iron, "Billets, Coke, HR Coils, etc. 
The management suggested that MSTC should diversify into imports of other items, 
develop export business as well as set up joint ventures for manufacturing steel relate<I 
items. 
In case of Domestic Trade, MSTC is handling disposal of scrap worth about Rs. 300 
crores for over 100 Public Sector Undertakings Government Departments. Hewever, this 
number and volume can be substantiall~ increased if Railways and Defence also entrust the 
disposal of their scrap to MSTC. The management suggested that it is imperative that the 
Government renders support to MSTC both in Foreign Trade and Domestic Trade since 
there are definite disadvantages to the country in decanalisation of imports of important raw 
materials like scrap. 
Similarly in case of Domestic Trade, organisations like Defence, Railways and some 
other Public Sector Undertakings are utilising the services of private auctioneers who are 
offering such low rate of servi~. charges that it does not even justify the bare minimum cost 
of the operation. This ·means that there are c;>ther incentives available to the privat~­
auctioneers which is at the cost of the Public Sector Underta~ings depriving them of the 
necessary revenues. 

44 
The management was of the view that the proposed diversification project of MSTC 
should be given top priority for clearances by the Ministry, Planning Co11;1mission, CCEA 
etc. so as to enable MSTC to create its own productivity and give sufficient business volume 
to its existing infrastructure. 
On enquiring as to what has been the impact of liberalisation on the Company, the 
management clarified that liberalisation has l)rimarily involved decanalisation of import of 
scrap, -reduction of import duties and simplification of systems and procedures in this sphere. 
A-s a result of these measures, following developments have taken place:-
( a) A .number of private units including large organisations such as Reliance, TISCO, lTC, 
etc. and international traders as well as individual furnace upits have come into the field 
of import of scrap. This has resulted in foreian suppliers taking advantage of a number 
of players in the field and dilution of strict quality criteria follo~ed by MSTC during the 
canalisation period. MSTC has also had as a result to follow suit, since the prices offered 
to MSTC otherwise were necessarily higher and as such uncompetitive. 
The mushrooming has led to simultaneous import by a number of parties and avoidable 
infructuous cost by way of bunching of vessels at ports, demurrage, necessity to keep 
material in stock for extended periods due to inability to sell in advance, introduction of 
extra commercial practice. 
(b) Impact of Reduction of Duties 
Import Duty on melting scrap has been reduced to 5%. Simultaneously, duty on finished 
products has also been reduced. For example, duty on billets stands at 20%. As a result, 
billets are being imported in preference to scrap import since billets produced out of 
imported scrap after melting in furnaces is .costlier than the landed cost of imported 
billets. As a result of this as well as th~ hike in international price of scrap, a large 
number of furnaces have closed down, resulting in gross under-utilisation of investment 
and capacity built up. 
(c) Impact of Removal of Definition qf Heavy Melting Scrap Rerollable Scrap 
HEAVY MELTING SCRAP (HMS) was defined in the import policy in 1985-88. 
However, in the last few years, this definition has been withdrawn and as a result large 
scale imports of HMS are being undertaken now. Material actually coming under this 
category however is in higher sizes which can only be categorised as rerollable scrap. 
Duty on rerollable scrap ranges upto 40%, whereas duty on HMS is just 5%. This leads 
to large scale evasion of duty. 
MSTC cannot obviously be party to such practices and is not in position to import HMS,_ 
being otherwise unviable. In the present decanalisation regime, HMS is being imported 
from sources which include zones of conflict. As a result there have been a number of 
cases of presence of explosives in these consignments which have led to loss of life. This 
is primarily due to non-eQforcement of quality control, due to inability of private 
importers to enforce such a discipline on the sellers . • 
The management has highlighted the following facts regarding import of scrap after 
decanalisation:-
1. There have been several instances where scrap imported by private importers have 
been accompanied by explosives and· on a few occasions there have been serious 
casualities. 
/ 

45 
2. There has been every possibility of radio-active contamination being present in such 
material imported. 
3. Due to substantial differential in customs duty between HMS and rerollable scrap, 
there is every possibility/tendency on the part of private importers to import 
rerollable -scrap in the guise of HMS which naturally results in revenue loss to the 
country. 
4. Price manipulation through under-invoicing/over-invoicing depending .upon the 
circumstances cannot be ruled out which evidently is at the cost of revenue and loss 
of foreign exchange to the country. 
0~ th~ basis of evidence of the m$magement and the papers/documents submitted before 
it the Sub-Committee feels that though it would be difficult to extend full monopoly to the 
Com.pany in the field of importing Scrap but Govem~ent may still help out MSTC by 
' r~ti~nulising the duty structure for raw materials and S1!mis to enable it to compete with 
Pri\'ate Sector. It should also be wet within the consideration of Government of India to 
expedite clearance of the diversification proposal, of the company. Ministry of Railways and 
Defence may also be persu~~.tJed to prefer MSTC -in respect of disposal of their scrap. 
Meeting with the represen~tives of Trade & lndustry 
The Sub-Committee also got an opportunity to interact with the representatives of trade 
and industry in the context of their problems/experiences in running/setting up industries .in 
the State of Maharashtra. · 
The representatives informed the Sub-Committee that at present there was no system to 
guide the entrepreneurs, particularly small scale entrepreneurs, about the incentives 
available for them for setting up industries in backward areas. They emphasised the need to 
conduct training programmes for small scale entrepreneurs. 
As regards the infrastructure, the representatives emphasised the need to streamline the 
supply of raw material. The representatives were critical of the existing poor power supply 
condition in the State. According to them the transmission and distribution of power should 
be privatised and for that a joint venture by private parties could be launched in four States 
viz. Maharashtra, Gujarat, Goa and Karnataka. So far as the road network was concerned, 
the representatives felt that the Government had not framed any guidelines for private 
parties engaged in the construction of roads. According to thertl construction of regional 
highways should be preferred as it was quite profitable. The representatives also emphasised 
on the need to build schools, hotels, hospitals, etc., alongwith physical infrastructure so as to 
attract industries in baekward areas. As regards labour legislation the representatives 
submitted that existing legislations were pro-labour. They wanted a review of the legislation 
so that it might be made pro-production. 
The representatives were- critical of the delay in the present procedure of processing 
applications. They wanted that the procedure should be reviewed and efforts should be 
made to arrange applications for clearance from different agencies for setting up industries, 
particularly in l:fackward areas. They suggested that as far as possible all projects should be 
certified by private agencies. 
The Sub-Committee endorses the views of the representatives about the need for 
development of infrastructure and feels that the Government should take the opportunity. of 
utilising their experience in the field. The Sub-Committee recomm~nds that the Government 
should also review the procedural issues for processing applications and formulate new 
guidelines tuned to the requirement of the industry.

~tatioo of Lead BaDk MlmagemeDt at Bombay 
The Sub-Committee met the bank authorities in connection with Prime. Minister Rozgar 
Y ojana and also issues concerning credit facilities available for industries in the State of 
Maharashtra. · 
The Management of the State Bank of India, lead bank in Maharasbtra, informed the 
Sub-Committee that the Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana launched on 2nd October, 1993 
primarily provided credit facilities to educated unemployed, urban youth for undertaking 
mit;i'o-enterprises in manufacturing, service, business and allied agricultural sector. As per 
the existing guidelines the scheme provided for financing the project cost upto a maximum of 
Rs. 1 lakh with margin of 5% of the project cost. There was a provision to extend capital 
subsidy of 15% of the project cost. The banks extended a loan of 95% of the project cost, 
which was repayable in three to seven years after a moratorium of six to eighteen months. 
Under the scheme, the training was made compulsory for industrial and service ventures, 
while for certain activities in business sector it might not be required. District PMRY 
Committee imparted a four weeks training covering, (i) elements of book-keeping (ii) simple 
knowledge of marketing (iii) acquaintance with product costing and (iv) familiarisation with 
project financing by banks. 
As regards banks performance under PMRY in Maharashtra the State Bank of India 
authorities informed the Sub-Committee that in 1993-94, SBI ·had sanctioned 76.20% cases 
to the target, made 72.48% disbursement against sanctioned cases and total 55.24% 
disbursement had been made to the target. The achievement against such entries had been 
97.93%, 90.23% and 88.37% in 1994-95. Upto September, 1995, the bank had been able to 
achieve 9% target as sanctioned number of cases and the cases in respect of which 
disbursement has taken. On enquiring about the reasons for poor. performance during year 
1995, the bank authorities clarified that very few applications were sponsored to the bank 
compared to the target which might result in bunching in the latter months. 
The bank authorities highlighted the following problems they were facing in sanction/ 
disbursement under PMRY;-
Sanction: 
(i) Applicants were ineligible on account of income more than stipulated. 
I 
(ii) Applications did not belong to the service area. 
(iii) Projects were unviable. 
(iv) Applicants were already defaulters in oth~r banks. 
Till September 1995, very few applications were sponsored to the bank compared to the 
target which might result in bunching of applications in the latter months. 
Disbursement: 
(i) Delay in training programme. 
(ii) Beneficiaries not coming forward to complete the formalities, despite reminders. 
(iii) Requisita margin-money was not arranged. 
(iv) Want o-rsuitable location and power connection. Non-allotment of autorickshaws. 
(v) Insistance on cash drawings. 
The bank had, however, taken the following steps to improve its performance:­
(i) Adequate powers had been delegated to all Branch Managers to sanction loan 
under PMRY; 

47 
' 
(iit~ Branches had been strictly advised not to take any collateral security. _______ ---~"---=--+-+--' 
(iii) The circles had been advised to instruct the controlling authorities to visit branches 
where the progress was slow and take suitable steps to dispose of the pending 
applications. The issue was also discussed in the CSMs conference held on 30 
0Gtober, 1995. Circles had been requested to ensure that 
(a) all pending applications were processed without delay. 
(b) ail applications were dealt within the prescribed time-frame. 
(c) sanctioned applications were disbursed expeditiously. 
The performance of the scheme was being reviewed in the Executive Committee of the 
Central Board every month an~ corrective steps taken wherever necessary. 
The Sub-Committee ·had been informed that Dena Bank had taken much more effective 
steps as indicated below:--
1. Each and every branch manager of tke bank, even the smallest in rural areas, had 
been given special powers to sanction loans upto Rs. 1 lakh under the scheme. 
2. Branches had been asked to dispose off the applications within the time stipulated 
by the Reserve Bank of India. 
3. In lead districts, L.B.O.'s had been asked to work in close liaison with DIC to 
ensure sponsoring of quality applications in sufficient number, that too in a phased 
manner, so as to avoid large rejections and last minut~s pressure. 
4. The progress of the bank's branches, as well as all banks in lead areas was being 
monitored regularly and wherever required, immediate actions initiated to overcome 
the hurdles. PeriOdical letters to regional authorities I branches had been addressed 
impressing upon them the urgent need to promptly attend the applications and 
implement the scheme in letter and spirit. 
5. L.B.O.'s in lead districts had been asked to persuade the DIC to arrange meetings 
of the Task Force Committee at block level and also invite the local branch 
managers at the time of screening/interviewing the borrowers so that the benefit of 
their experience about the potentiality for various activities in the area and the 
applicants could be taken. 
6. Regional authorities had been asked to closely monitor the progress and concentrate 
on the branches having longer pendency and high rejection and take necessary 
corrective steps promptly. -
7. Branches had been advised to properly record the _ movement of the loan 
applications received under the schem~ and reconcile the same with DIC officials 
from time to time. -
8. Branches had been advised to identify suitable beneficiaries in their area and refer 
them to DIC, which helped in achieving the targets as also to avoid rejections. 
9. Branches had been advised not to insist on collateral security guarantors or deposit 
and not to reduce the amount of loan applied for without justification. 
The bank authorities put forth the following suggestion for improvement under the 
scheme:-
-~- :(i) Need-·for awareness training programmes in cluster of villages and township about 
the sc~eme; 

48 
(ii) The training programme should also cover simple management areas like cash-flow 
management, marketing, quality control etc., with special emphasis on financial 
management and book-keeping; 
(iii) It was also necessary to take steps to highlight the importance of repayment. This 
might be done by effective publicity through media like T.V., Press, emphasizing that 
the loans under the scheme were not doles or grants and would not be written off; 
(iv) Initial scrutiny needed to be made more effective to ensure that only the deserving 
applicants were considered and rejections were minimal. 
The secOJ!~t item for discussion with bank authorities was banking facilities for industries 
particularly the small scale industries. The SBI authorities informed the Sub-Committee that 
advances to small scale industry constitute a major portion of SBI's landing to priority sector. 
In Maharashtra the total advances to small scale industry had been steadily increasing from 
Rs. 765 lakhs, in 1992-93 toRs. 794 lakhs in 1993-94 and Rs. 910 lakhs in 1994-95. The bank 
had initiated the following steps to bring under coverage more and more of the SSI units of 
the small scale industry (SSI):-
(i) A vast network of 590 branches had been established to deal predominantly with 
SSI advances. These branches included 16 exclusive SSI branches, 99 branches in 
industrial estates, 54 branches in industrial areas, and 421 SSI intensive 
branches. In the State of Maharashtra 81 such bran<;hes Were located, out of 
which 15 were in industrial estates, 12 in industrial areas and 54 were other SSI 
intensive branches. It was planned to open 20 more specialised SSI branches 
during 1995-96. 
(ii) All branches had beell directed to accord high priority to extend credit to small 
scale industrial units. 
(iii) A growth of Rs. 930 crores in SSI advance was planned for 1995-96. 
(iv) No loan proposal from SSI unit could be declined except with the prior 
concurrence of the controlling authority. 
(v) With a view to increasing the uninterrupted flow of -credit to SSI the seven point 
action plan of the GOI was being vigorously implemented in the bank. 
(vi) The discretionary powers of the sanctioning.authorities had been increased upto 
a maximum of 400% for speedier flow of credit to SSI. 
(vii) The officials dealing with the cre<iit to SSI were being given specialised training 
to respond better to the demand of SSI borrowers. 
(viii) Recommendations of the Nayak Committee were being fully implemented. 
Working capital of SSI units upto Rs. 1 crore was now being assessed on the 
basis of turnover rather than on the basis of working capital cycle. 
(ix) A sum of Rs. 1,000 crores was being extended to Kh~di and Village Industries 
Commission by a consortium of commercial banks to enable them to lend to 
eligible small scale industrial units. The share of SBI in the ·consortium was Rs. 
475 crores. 
The bank authorities had the following suggestions in the matter:-
(i) The larger of SSI units were graduating to medium and large scale units. The upper 
ceiling put up on the value of plant & machinery for SSI was not adequate. The limit 
which was at present Rs. 60 lakhs and Rs. 75 lakhs for SSI and the ancilliary units 
respectively, required to be raised. 

49 
(ii) The present system of linking the growth of priority seCtor which· wa~ SSI to a large 
extent, to the contemporaneous growth of total bank credit was operationally not 
realistic. As credit to other sectors often expanded rapidly ovet. a JShOrt ,pe~od by 
high volume, it became practically impossible to match· s:u.ch : ~a~ion with 
corresponding expansion of lending to PS/ SSI in the saine time~frame as tliis sector 
typically absorbed credit in relatively small individual doses spread over a large 
number of units. Thus there was a need to establish an alternative bench-mark for 
measuring performance of banks in lending to priority sector. ' 
(iii) The limit for granting composite loans was Rs. SO,OO<Y- only. This was required to 
be enhanced. 
(iv) Administrative costs of lending to SSI, particularly the sma1ler units was high as 
compared to large scale industries. In addition, in case of smaller loans, banks also 
bear the premium payable to the DI & CGC. 
(v) There had been a steep increase in the rate of premium payable to DI & CGC 
(2.5% p.a.) thus adding of the cost of the SSI sector. On the other hand, the extent 
of c:over had been reduced, thus adversely affecting both the banks, and the SSI's 
borrowers. The DI & CGC scheme needed to be reviewed I rationalised. 
On the basis of the evidence of the bank authorities and the paper submitted before it the 
Sub-Committee appreciates the efforts made by tfle banks under PMRY. The Sub-Committee, 
nonetheless, feels that much more prf:!ctically oriented view is required. The conflicting views 
of banks and the state authorities indiCltte a need of review o: the guidelines under the scheme 
on the basis of problems being faced in the field. Time has come that Reserve Bank of India, 
the Ministry of the Finance, the Ministry of Industry and the State government may sit 
together to sort out these problems and help in making· PMRY a success. Similar reorientation 
could also help out in financing small scale .industries. 
Mazagaon Dock Limited (MDL) 
The management informed the Sub-Committee that the Mazagaon Dock Limited 
Bombay, was the leading ship building and offshore fabtication yard in India. The yard was 
established in the 18th century, and over the two hundred odd eventful years, earned a 
reputation for quality work and a tradition of skilled and resourceful service to the shipping 
world, in general and the Indian Navy in particular. Ship building technology between 1965 
and 1971 in the Mazagaon Dock underwent extensive expansion and modernisation. The 
conversion of two small shipways into a single large shipway also enabled the Company to 
construct vessels upto 27,000 dmt. 
The first modern warship to be built by the company was the leander class frigate 
"Nilgiri". The design was obtained from the British Admiralty and the frigate itself was built 
in collaboration with Messrs. Vickers Ltd. and Messrs Yarrow (Shipbuilders) Ltd. of U.K. 
The Nilgiri was launched in October, 1968 and commissioned in 1972. During the next nine 
years Mazagaon Dock built and delivered five more frigates to the Navy. The Nilgiri, 
Himgiri, Udagiri, Dunagiri, Taragiri and the Vindhyagiri proved to be powerful units of the 
Indian fleet and earned trem~ndous .acclaim for the Indian .industry. A new .class was 
christened as the "Godavari Class" and the first ship of the series 'Godavari' was launched in 
May, 1980 and commissioned in December, 1983. The secon~ ··Ganga' was launched in 
October, 1981 and commissioned in December, 1985, the third and last of the series 
'Gomati' was launched in March, 1984 and commissioned in April, 1988. The construction of 
the Godavari class frigates was milestone in the achievement of self-sufficiency in design 
capability. 

so 
The Sub-Committee was given to understand that in May, 1984, Mazagaon Dock was 
also entrusted with the construction of sub-marines. The construction of sub-marines 
required a totally new technology and very stringent quality standards. The first sub-marine 
'Shalki' was launched on 30 September, 1989 and after undergoing extensive trails joined the 
·sub-marine fleet of the Navy in February 1992. The second sub-marine 'Shankul' was 
launched m March 1992 and commissioned in May 1994. Besides warships for the Navy, 
Mazagaon Dock had also completed the construction of a series of offshore patrol vessels for 
patrolling, policing, search and rescue operations in the exclusive economic zone of the 
country. They had also been provided with pollution control and fire fighting capability and 
could carry a helicopter on board. The design of vessels was evolved entirely at Mazagaon 
Dock. Seven coast guard ships, Vikram, Vijaya, Veera, Varona, Vivek and Vigraha today 
form thr~ mainstay of the coast guard fleet. MDL had also completed the construction of the 
two seri~~ of offshore supply vessels to the ONGC. 
On th.:: export front also, Mazagaon Dock had been on the first place. It was the only 
shipyard in India to have built ships for foreign clients. Since 1974-75, the Company had 
built and supplied two ~go vessels to Singapore, two large water tankers for the Iranian 
Navy, silk general cargo vessels to U.K., over a hundred barges to the Gulf and ten police 
launch to Mozambique. 
The di~overy of oil in Bombay High opened for Mazagaon Dock, a new horizon for 
diversification. The oil crisis of the early 70's led to Mazagaon Dock esta~Iishing indigenous 
capacity for the manufacturing of offshore platforms for ONGC. Mazagaon Dock augmented 
the !acilities for the fabrication of platforms by developing yards at Nahava and Mangalore. 
In order to achieve allround self reliance in the strategic field of offshore oil exploration and 
exploitation, Mazagaon Dock further developed capacity to provide offshore services. The 
foreign monopoly in many areas of offshore technology was replaced by the indigenous 
capacity developed by Mazagaon Dock froin 1976 onwards. 
Mazagaon Dock had so far constructed and installed over fifty platforms, fabricated and 
delivered two Jack-up rigs, two single bouy mooring systems, three flare tripods, six bridges, 
coated about seven hundred kms. subsea pipe line and laid ·about fQur hundred kms. of 
:;uosea pipe line. Jack up rigs "Sagar Kiran" constructed by Mazagaon Dock for ONGC, 
were the fast ever jack-up rigs built in India. 
The Sub-Committee feels that Mazagaon Dock has come a long way from. being a ship 
repairing yard in 1960, to its current status as the premier fabrication yard .. Lf~:the country 
and the government should extend all possible partronage to the Dock in ·•~;_growth. 
MSDguese Ore India Umlted. (MOIL) ":., 
The Sub-Committee met the management of Manganese Ore India Limite~ at Bombay. 
As a part of introduction the management informed that the Company had teri major mines 
spread over the states of Maharashtra and Madhya Pradesh. The Company was the single 
largest producer of high grade manganese ore in the country. MOIL was also producing 
electrolytive manganese dioxide (EMD), a value added product. This was required for the 
manufacture of dry cells. 
On a specific query about the capacity utilisation of the Company, the management 
clarified that as far as MOIL was concerned, the term capacity utilisation might not be 
strictly applicable because the production of manganese ore depended on the prospects of 
steel aDd ferro alloy industry. However, after taking into consideration. the production of 
steel and ferro alloys, the Company fixed/yearly targets for the production of various grades 
~ ore and planned its strategy to. achieve the same. 

51 
The achievement in respect of target and actual production of manganese in 1995-96 
(upto October; 1995) was more than 101% while that of EMD in the same period was 95%. 
The management was quite satisfied with the-current level of production of mangenese ore 
and EMD. In .view of the likely increase in demand of EMD, the management proposed to 
enhance the capacity of the existing EMD plant from 700 tpa to 800 tpa by suitable 
modification of some of the components at an estimated cost of Rs. 90 ·lakhs. They also 
proposed to set up another EMD plant of 1200 tpa capacity by the year 1997-98, at a cost of 
Rs. 9 crores. 
' -
Simultaneously, the Company brought changes in the product mix resulting in better 
sales realisation. All these steps helped in meeting the challanges from the private sector. 
On eriquiring as to whether there was a peed for modifications in existing mining laws 
and regulations,·. the management informed that there were some-, old dumps from which 
large· quantity of saleable grades of manganese ore could be recovered. However, the work 
was of a temporary nature and it was also economically viable for departmental exploitation. 
flence, it was necessary to engage petty contractors for the extraction of ore from these 
dumps. Th~ management suggested that the engagement of contractors for dump mining 
might be allowed which would go a long way in conservation and optimum utilisation ,af this 
valuable mineral resource. The management also suggested that rule 24 of Mineral 
Concession Rules-Disposal of Application for Mining Lease, be amended by prescribing a 
time limit for disposal of mining lease applications by the State government. The 
management also wanted that proper statutory provisions be made for mining of dumps 
since it contained saleable minerals. 
The Sub-Committee appreciates the performance of the_ . Company in achieving the 
production targets in respect of both manganese ore and EMD. The Committee is equally 
happy that the Company has equipped itself to. meet. the challanges from private sector and 
fully geared to the tune of liberalised economy. The Sub-Committee would, bowever, urge 
upon the government to accommodate the suggestions of the management regarding 
amendment in mining laws or regulations so that the Company could perform better in the 
interest of the nation. 
Presentation of the Stste Government of Goa 
At Goa, the Sub-Committee started its business with a detailed discussion with officials 
of the State governm~nt of Goa on 16 November 1995. Initiating the discussion, the Industry 
Secretary to the Government of Goa informed the Sub-Committee that Goa became a state 
in 1987. Earlier, it was being administered by the Central Government and there was no 
problem of funds. Goa lost its protection umbrella after attaining the Statehood. 
Unfortunately the credit deposit ratio in the State was also very low. According to him the 
government of Goa participated through Goa, Daman & Diu Industrial Development 
Corporation (GDDIDC) for helping to acquire the land; as GDDIDC was a government 
company. It also participated through Economic Development Corporation which was an 
agency dealing with the finances to the entrepreneurs. In addition, the State government 
directly participated by bringing water and power lines, construction of roads etc. The State 
government also invited private participation in roads construction and power generation. As 
a result, eleven power stations were proposed to be set up in the next two years. 
As regards the employment generation, the state authorities informed that the State 
government had established industrial estates in the hinterland where there was no industrial 
activity earlier. In these estates about 18000 people got the employment which was expected 
to touch around 25000 in near future. Beside_s many multinationals with Indian collaboration 

52 
were functioning .in the State. During the I'ast three years, seventeen large and medium ~cale 
industries would gener.ate employment for 2626 people and. another 125 units could geli~rate 
employment for. 22;000 ·person·s: In small scale sector, the existing 651 units had provided 
employment to 3583 persons and the number was expected to touch 23,891, with 
approximately 6816 · more units coming up. 
Regarding progress of growth centres-in Goa, the State authorities informed that Goa 
had been allotted· one growth centre at Verna. The State government had spent an amount 
of Rs. 5.03 crores and the Government of India had also contributed Rs. 4.;72 crores on it. 
Large scale developments were going on in the area and the growth centre w~s functioning 
in a smooth way. The State government· had not faced any practical or financial difficulties, 
so far as the growth centre was concerned. The State government .had decided to put up a 
second growth centre at Pernem in north Goa for which the Ministry of Industry was being 
approached shortly. 
The Sub-Committee hopes that the Central Government would favourably consider the 
proposal of second growth centre in Goa for balanced industrial development of the State. 
On the issue of Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana, the State authorities indicated the 
following difficulties:- ' 
(i) In some of the cases banks asked for collateral security, verbally from the 
beneficiary, which was not in conformity with the rules. · 
(ii) In some cases banks directed the beneficiaries verbally to build up infrastructure 
even before sanctioning the amount. 
(iii) In the case of industries some banks refused to sanction loans on the pr~text that 
there was no potential market in the area where the party wanted to set up the unit without 
considering that the product manufactured in a particular area had demand in other area. 
'fhe Sub-Committee was given to understand that during 1994-95, the banks disbursed 
, loans· in 210 cases against· 261 sanctioned cases amounting to Rs. 141.78 lakhs. The Sub .. 
Committee feels that there seems to be some dislocation in · the implementation of the 
guidelines under the scheme. The Sub-Cormr&ittee recommends that instructions should be 
· issued to banks to implement the scheme on the basis of guidelines issued by the Government 
of India and also not to raise objections on petty grounds, once the case is recommended by 
the Task Forte Committee, wherein .-epresentative of the banks is also a member. 
'j f • 
The Sub-Committee gathered during the discussion that the entire State of Goa was 
inaustrially backward as per the Central Government norms. The details of incentives 
extended by State government of Goa to small scale entrepreneurs were as under: 
(a) The State investment subsidy @ 25% of capital cost limited to a maximum of 
Rs. 25.00 lakbs. 
(b) Exemption from sales tax for a period of twelve years or till reaChing of liability 
amount equal to the capital cost, whichever was later, for large and medium 
industries and for a period of fifteen years in respect of small scale industries. 
(c) Subsidy on diesel generating sets to SSI units @ 50% of the cost of generating set 
limited to Rs. 1.00 lakh. 
(d) Power subsidy@ 0.09 paise per unit to small scale and cottage industries which had 
a total electrical load not exceeding 20 hp. 
(e) Supply of free tender forms, exemption from earnest money deposit to small scale 
industries. 

53 
Besides, .the State authorities also indicated the following concessions extended for 
development of industries in the State:-
(1) Capital subsidy: 25% of the capital cost of the project or Rs. 25.00 lakhs whichever 
was less. 
(2) Reduction in stamp duty: The government was remitting 50% of the stamp duty 
payable under the Stamp Act in respect of bonds or mortagage deeds executed in 
favour of financial institutions. 
(3) Price preference: Price preference of 15% on the purchase made by the government 
departments was available to small scale units. · 
(4) Sales tax & Central Sales tax exemption facility: 
(i) Sales Tax Benefit Scheme had been liberalised and it had been decided to grant 
sales tax exemption facility to large and medium scale units for a period of twelve years and 
to small scale units . for a period of fifteen years or up to sales tax liability amount equal to 
the capital cost of the project, whichever was reached later. Capital cost for this purpose 
would imply the cost of fixed assets such as lands, buildings and machinery only. 
(ii) The sales tare, exemption was available up to the final point i.e. actual consumer 
to whom it was sold in Goa (subject to issue XIA/XIB by a registered deal~r). 
(iii) Certain hig\lly pOllutant industries were not eligible for sales tax exemption, 
unless the government specifically exempted them. ' 
(5) Income tax holiday: Five Year income tax holiday available till 1998 for units was 
being set up in Goa as it had been declared industrially backward. 
On tile basis of the evidence of the State government authorities and the papers submitted 
to it, the Sub-Committee comes to·· the conclusion that the State government has taken aU 
necessary steps to attract industries in the State •. The Sub-Committee hopes that. the 
industrialists and the Centra• Government would reciprocate an.d co~~inue to ext~nd all 
possible assistance to this industrially backward State. 
Goa Mineral Ore Exporters Association 
The representatives of Mineral Ore Exporters Association deposed before the Sub­
Committee that iron ore industry was the backbone of Goa. Ore was primarily produced in 
fines and not in lumps. Haemetite ore was an upgraded quality of ore. According to the 
representatives, there were about a hundred mines .in Goa owned by _private people. Ore 
was available below sea level. From the mine, ore was transferred to river heads by barges. 
The representatives submitted that unloading of cargo from barges was the main problem for 
their business. According to them the upgradation of infrastructure was required from 
mining production to the task of shipment. They felt that un.loading plant at Berth No. 9 at 
Port Trust was outdated and needed modernisation. 
Another problem was the problem of poor power situation. The representatives said 
that they were facing power crunch situation for mining production and shipment. According 
to them the quantum of power received from Korba (M.P.) and Maharashtra was not 
sufficient. Exporters were paying heavy demurrage due to lack of power as vessels were not 
easily available. 
The exporters were also critical of the denial of permission for the blending of iron ore. 
According to them the Mormugao Port Trust was not permitting them, the required 
blending of low grade reject of Bellari Hospet which could otherwise be very profitable in 
the national interest in terms of fetching foreign exchange. If permission was granted for 

54 
. ble~ding, the ore·•so produced would be a quality ore and the indigenous industry would also 
prefer to. use it .. ;, . 
' 
On a specific~ query about the working condition oC·workersllabours and welfare 
activities-undertakell· by the Association, the representatives, 'Clarified that workers were 
earning very well. Average pay of a worker came aro~nd;'four to five thousand rupees p.m. 
On the questibn of employment generation, the Sub-Committee was informed that mining 
industry was generating direct employment directly to 65000 p~~ons and indirectly to 22,000 
pef.soris. Villagers around mining areas were given employment. On the environment front, 
the Association· was planting ah9ut one million trees every ·.year .• / 
· . On the basis of the evidence the Sub-Committee feels that .{In other States the power 
c:r,unch. has al~ a.dversely atrected the predominent mining Industry in Goa. Since Goa Is 
declared as industrially backward State, the power problem needs immediate attention of the 
Union Government for its redressal. The Sub-Committee observes that it should also be well 
. , ~~ithili Union Government's consideration to examine the possibility of permission for blending 
ef lo~. Jiad+'' r~ject ot Bellari Hospet to improve the quality of ore. The Sub-Committee 
appreciates the weltate steps the Exporters Association has taken but it feels that as there is 
no room for complacency and the Association should spend more for the welfare of poor 
people in the State. 
Goa Small Industries AssOciation 
The Sub-Cbrrimittee also took an opporttmity to interact with the trade and industry 
representatives of Goa. It was gathered that there was no large or medium scale industry in 
Goa ancf-the industrialisation of the State was totally depending on small or tiny industrial 
units_. The representatives of Goa Small Industries Associati<m )li_ghlighted certain difficulties 
which they were facirig in running/setting up small indUstries in the State. 
According to the representatives of the Association, the major problem was the 
reluctance on the part of banks to extend necessary working capital. They informed the Sub­
Committee that credit-deposit ratio in Goa was very poor. But Goa which was an 
industrially backward State had a higher growth potential for the small industries owing to 
substantial capital investment in the industrial sector and the booming tourism trade which 
consumed. a lot of products manufactured by small industries. Even after obtaining sanction 
. of term finance from SFC's and SIDBI, the working capital funds from banks were not 
coming forth due to delay, excessive paper works, imposition of capital on 20% of turnover 
and imposing a low growth of turnover. The banks in Goa delayed the sanction because 
according to them the Nayak Committee norms permitted only 20% maximum finance of 
turnover to small scale industries, while actually the Committee recommended that the 
minimum lending should be 20% of the turnover. The representatives of the sm&!l scale 
industries did not accept the banks' contention that there existed no lending avenues in Goa, 
in view of substantial lending business done by cooperative banks and private financial 
institutions. 
Another problem with the banks was that they did not extend prompt help to sick units 
in Goa. The representatives informed that as per RBI guidelines the decision with regard to 
calling up or rehabilitating such an advance must be taken within six months of units' 
sickness. Banks however, advised small entrepreneurs on signs of sickness. As a result they 
are forced to use their surplus and borrow money to pay the excessive interest to banks. 
The representatives also informed about the functioning of the State level Inter 
Institution Committee, which was earlier set up to address the malfunctioning of finance 
sector. Banks had scant regard for this Committee as the senior most functionary of each 

55 
bank seldom attended the meeting of the Committee. Very few banks had filed a list of sick 
units to the Committee which was required two years back. · · 
The Sub-Committee would like to invite special attention of the Government of Indi,i 
on the following suggestions put forth by Goa Small Industries Association:-
( a) The granting of limits in principle should be done in full recognition of 
the entrepreneurs' optimism and within a weeks' time. A limit for bills 
and book debts should be extended without hesitation as the unit can 
utilise- these limits only on actual performance. 
(b) It should be made compulsory on the part of the bank to recognise 
sickness in a unit by co-ordinating with the term lending institution on a 
,g.uarterly basis. Whenever the unit defaults for two successive quart~rs 
by way of interest, repayment or irregularity to either of them, it should 
be recognised as the onset of sickness and a rehabilitation or a call up 
plan should be drawn up to be effective within six months from the 
date. 
This was an RBI guideline not implemented by the banks. 
(c) As regards the SLIIC Committee, dysfunction concerned circular of the 
Hon'ble Minister for Industries Mr. Karunakaran, should be 
immediately implemented. 
(d) The law enacted by Parliament providing fdt action against the ·large 
scale industries for utilising credit from small scale industries beyond 
sixty days needed to be amended because the small scale industrialist 
was afraid to complain. 
The large scale industry was required by law to: 
(•) Classify its suppliers into small and large units. 
(iJ) Certify that all its suppliers in the small scale sector were paid within 
sixty days of credit period. 
.,. 
Inability to produce the above duly classified and certified suppliers should 
lead to prosecution. 
(e) Enhancing excise exemption limit to Small Scale Industry to Rs. 75.00 
lakhs. ' · 
(f) Income tax exemption to Small Scale Industry units in Goa upto ten 
years from the existing period of five years.

The Brief Details of the Views Expressed by the Various Public Sector Industries, State 
Government Officials, Trade Unions, Workers before the Committee during its visit to Orissa,

Capital Goods Industries 
The Committee was informed that whenever any such undertaking start losing, the 
pressure is built up on the Central Government to take over that undertaking. Most of the 
industries under BIFR are the private industries. The following reasons were advarrced for 
the sickness of the capital goods industries:-
(!) ineffective and unresponsive management at the top level; 
(2) neglect of rehabilitation programmes in the past; 
(3) inadequacy of the replacement, repair and maintenance programme; and 
(4) lack of financial assistance from the Central Government at the right time; 
The major causes for sickness of the capital goods industries are as under:-
Burn & Co. Ltd.: The company is suffering a lot due to slash of orders of wagons from 
the Railway Board. The huge stock of finished items made by the company on the orders of 
Railways is lying there .and the same is not being lifted by the Railways. The offshore 
division of the Company is totally crippled due to lack of orders from ONGC. The 
refractory units have also been facing serious problems due to inadequate orders from SAIL 
beside chronic shortage of coal and ~orking· capital problems. The representatives of the 
Company requested for (i) non-plan support of Rs. 37 crores to meet working capital 
requirement and to liquidate statutory dues. The Railways and ONGC may place orders 
with the company. The SAIL may place order on the Refractory Units for atleast 25,000 
tonnes per annum. 

61 
Indian Iron & Steel Co., Burnpur: The company submitted no less than 12 proposals but 
there is no response from the Government. The production cost of hot metal at the plant is 
one of the lowest in the country but the cost jumps up drastically in the steel making stage due 
to outdated technology and old plant equipments. This cost could be reduced by almost Rs. 
2()()(Y- per tonne by introducing new technology. The SAIL has not given free hand to 
modernise liSCO. The representatives stressed that liSCO can earn profit and repeat history 
of its golden era by its modernisation within the framework of SAIL. The most of mills are 
out-dated and need replacement or modernisation. The Company can earn a good amount of 
foreign exchange after modernisation as the Company will be able to stand in the international 
competitive market. The Committee was informed that due to the fear of closure, the work 
culture has gone up. The workers are ready to help and sacrifice for the cause of revival of the 
plant. The company needs Rs. 1100'- crores for modernisation, etc. over a period of five years 
for the sake of lakhs of people indirectly associated with the giant organisation. 
Mining and Allied Machiraery Corporation Ltd., (MAMC): The Company bas an intrinsically 
sound technological infrastructure. To make the company viable, drastic reduction in 
manpower is to be organised through VRS. The Company had already reduced the strength 
from 7700 to 4432 through VRS. Upto August, 1995, the sales tum over was Rs. 2163 lakhs: 
With some fund assistance for the working capital, the year's turnover would reach Rs. 7000 
lakhs. The Company has participated in tenders of more than Rs. 200 crores which are still 
live and under evaluation of respective customers. To achieve viability, financial restructuring, 
cash infusion of Rs. 165 crores as direct assistance from the Government in the form of equity 
is required. 
Alloy Steels Plant, Durgapur: The plant has the best of sophisticated technological 
facilities for production of 400 grades of special steels, alloy steels and stainless steels. ASP is 
operating in a fiercely competitive market where the private sector producers have a slight 
edge. The plant needs modernisation, modification and replacement of obsolete machines. 
The manpower of the plant as an austerity measure has been reduced from 8000 plus to the 
level of· 5000 plus. In the preceding years; the company was searching for customers but now 
the customers have been chasing the company. The Commitee was informed that the market 
position is better now. The Committee was requested for immediate sanction of necessary 
funds for modernisation, modification and replacement of obsolete machines. 
Hindustan Photo Films Mfg. Co. Ltd.: The company was making profit till1991-92. The 
workers infonned that due to mismanagement at the top, the company has gone sick. The 
Company is having fully integrated black. $nd white technology. The obsolete technology is to 
be changed. The current assets of the Company are less than the liability. The multinationals 
have entered into the market. This has affected the Company's 'prospects. The losses of the 
Company as on 31.3.1995 are to the tWle of &s. 218 crores. The Company is annually losing 
8-10 crores. The Company has already reduced the manpower from 4000 to 3100. The 
Company resorted to diversification without the approval of the Central Government. This 
has resulted sickness in the Company. The Company could not operate to the required level 
because of lack of finances. For opening Qf LC, the Company has to provide 100% margin 
money. The Committee was requested to help for the release of Rs. 35 crores for the 
Company to come into operation. Another Rs. 60 cror~ are required for 100% operation of 
the Company. 
Solem Steel Plant: The Salem Steel Plant is the youngest member of SAIL. lt is the first 
SAIL plant to bave ISO 9002 certification from Mls TUV-CERT Germany, in 1993. The sale 
of the plant has gone up from 547 tonnes in 1981-82 to 59352 in 1994-95. The Company has 
earned a profit of Rs. 21.14 crores during 1994-95. 

62 
Jessop & Co. Ltd., Calcutta: The Committee was informed that the Company is a 
subsidiary of Bharat Bhari Udyog Nigam limited. The major item i.e., cranes and structures 
are being supplied to big industries. The Company has got the distinction of doing the crane 
repair work. The Committee was also informed that there is a salary bill of Rs. 30 crores yet 
to be paid and the Company is two months behind in making payment to the staff. The 
present · state of affairs of the Company is due to:-
(1) deart~ of order in mining and paper machinery area; 
(2) shortage of working capital; 
(3) the accumulated losses have gone very high; and 
( 4) insufficient orders from Railways for MG coach bogies. 
The Committee was informed that the Company is ·upgrading the technology 
continuously. They have already undertaken t}le revival plan. The Company is likely to get 
orders to the tune of Rs. 106 crores this year. The Company submitted their requirement to

(3) Payment of gratuity Rs. 8.56 crores 
Rs. 33.56 crores 
Besides that the Company requires Government guarantee for cash credit with SBI and 
revised pay scales for the executive. 
Recommendations of the Sub-Committee 
The Committee is of the view that most of these units are perennially sick units with 
obsolete plant and machinery. These units could not be revived due to paucity of funds. 
There has been mismanagement at ·the top level of some· of these companies. The 
modernisation and replaeement of obsolete parts is not done regularly. Before the takeover 
by the Government, these companies were making profit. The Committee is of the view that 
the work culture now has changed and there i~ a maximum cooperation between the workers 
and the management and it has started yielding results. Towards this change in work culture, 
support and co-operation has been forthcoming from all sectors-be it unions, employees, 
State and local administration. The workers have realised that their existence is linked with 
the survival of these companies. For example, the Jessop & Co. Ltd. has not paid salary for 
the last two months to the workers. The workers emphatically assured the Committee that 
they are ready to sacrifice more for the revival of the Company. The Sub-Committee is of 
the view that the Government should make use of this change in work culture and their 
cooperation with the managerlient at' this hour of need and sanction the necessary financial 
assistance for the revival of these companies. The Government should not lose this 
opportunity. The Committee was also made to understand by the management of Jessop & 
Co. Ltd. That for the present crisis the company has been held responsible. The Holding 
Company Bharat Bhari Udyog Nigam Limited has not been asked to explain its role for the 
present state of affairs of the Company. The Committee desired that a meeting of the 
Holding Company may be called for the purpose. 

63 
Capital Goods Industries 
The Sub-Committee feels that the enthusiasm among the workers for a new work-culture 
for the survival of the sick units, particularly liSCO in Bumpur, MAMC at Durgapur, Jessop & 
Co. at Calcutta, Bum Standard, West Bengal, Hindustan Photo Films, Ooty, Alloy Steel Plant, 
Durgapur needs to be promoted further and for this the Government should make all sincere 
afforts to the redressing of the problems which presently beset the workers. The Government 
should arrange additional financial resources to provide budgetary support to these units so that 
they become commercially viable and profit earning. The Committee is of the opinion that the. 
workers at present are ready to fully cooperate and this naturally indicates towards the 
possibility of reviving these industries provided Government is determined to help, The 
Committee has also noted that MAMC at Durgapur which was incurring huge losses about a 
year back has now drastically reduced its losses and surviving only on the orders it is getting 
without the support of any financial institutions. The Committee notes the fact that this 
company in the public sector was created for manufacturing of underground coal mining 
equipments but at a later stage Govemment policy was changed ftom underground mining to 
open cast mining. This naturally led to the reduction of orders received by the company. 
It is observed by the Committee that Government orders are not given to the public sector 
companies. Moreover these companies have also to compete with private sector to get orderS 
and that sometimes it so happens that for want of Government orders as in the case of Bum 
Standard, which is manufacturing wagons, these Companies have suffered. MAMC was set up 
mainly to cater to the equipment needs of Coal India Limited and other undertakings. But these 
undertakings did not place the sufficient orders with MAMC. In some cases even after 
execution of the work, due payments are not made to the companies and sometimes delivery of 
the material is not accepted. The Committee is of the opinion that some mechanism should be 
devised at a high level to protect the public sector undertakings which are capable and the 
quality goods can be produced with certain preferential treatment for giving of orders. 
The Committee has also noticed that the post of Chief Executive of many of the public 
sector undertakings, including Director level positions have been lying vacant for months and 
these undertakings are being looked after by the Joint Secretaries of the Ministry as additional 
charge. The perception of the experts and skilled professiOnals and that of the bureaucrats is 
always different, as a result the growth of the companies and their competitiveness is ·being 
compromised. If the public sector has to be made commercially viable and strive t()wal'ds 
excellence, it is necessary that immediate steps should be taken to de link the bureaucracy from 
the professional jobs. 
Industrial Backwardness and Working of Small Scsle Industries . 
Orissa: The representatives of the Small Scale Industries Association informed that the 
small scale industries have no separate forum to .seek justice promptly, except going to a court of 
Law where justice is invariably delayed abnormally and consequently denied. The Committee

The District Magistrate, Burdhman informed that all industries were sick in the area. 
The Mafia Raj was there and the economy had collapsed. The small scale industries had 
completely finished. The SSI u11its were suffering because of delayed payment by the 
medium and large scale establishments. 
The District Magistrate, Bankura informed the Committee that Bankura is an 
undulating terrain of laterite soil. Having rainfall of 1260 mm. in a year, yet this region have 
got charaCteristic of sub-humid zone due to run off rain water. Hence, most of the populace 
have to depend on mono-crop. Eventually job creation for a large portion of the working 
force could not be ensured throughout the year. The number of SSI units functioning in the 
District is 3081 employing 17,740 persons. The reasons for the backwardness of the District 
are as under:-
(1) Shortage of water is a major cause; 
(2) lack of infrastructure facilities i.e.' metal road, powet and communication, etc.; 
(3) the banks are not cooperating in gtanting loan for the setting up new units; 
( 4) the District is not having any growth centre; 
(5) the number of unemployed people in the District is on the high side; 
(6) the mining industry has a scope for improvement but there is no Central help in this 
regard; 
(7) ~he ancilliary industries do not get payment in time and that is the reason for which 
they are ·suffering. 
The Committee was also informed that the idle manpower could be absorbed by setting 
up cottage and small scale industries. The vocational institutions might be opened to meet 
the demand of high professional and skilled manpower for industries to be set up in the 
region. The District has a good treasure of handicrafts. The large industries might be asked 
to create more ancilliary industries for the SSI units. The District is famous for its handloom 
sarees like "Baluchari". The Government might make all efforts for the development of 
handloom industries in the District. The Committee was informed that the District was 

66 
pre-dominantly agricultural-based having reserves for industrial raw materials. As such there

The Committee had a meeting with the officials of the UT. The Chief Secretary 
informed the Committee that the UT had been declared as industrially backward and placed 
under category 'A'. The Committee was informed that in spite of a number of constraints on 
resources, there had been a steady development of Small and Village Industries in UT. 
From about 10 units in 1978 it had now 650 units and about 4000 persons were employed 
therein. The Committee was informed that more industrial sheds were to be constructed. 
Three more industrial estates had been .planned. All the required facilities would be there. A 
Standing Technical Committee was proposed to be set up for the identification of industries 
for location and promotion. The Committee would prepare a list of desirable and 
undesirable industries and it would be reviewed periodically. The SSI units had generated 
larger employment. A special care was being taken for the tiny units. The Committee was 
informed that with the growth of industries, sickness in industries was also increasing. 
Necessary corrective steps had been taken in this regard. The Central Scheme for Margain 
Money for the revival of small units would be implemented. In order to promote exports, 
the following steps had . been ·taken:-· 
(1) adequate priority to exporting units for registration and other aids; 
(2) giving recognition to meritorious units by way of granting awards etc.; 
(3) providing warehousing facilities, etc."; 
( 4) encouraging earning of foreign exchange through export of technology and export 
services within the policy of the Government; 
(5) identification and encouraging units for 100% export performance; 
(6) concessional finance upto Rs. 4.50 lakhs to tiny and SSI;for acquiring land and 
machinery, etc. and a working loan of Rs. 0.50 lac at J09k interest for the tiny 
units; 
(7) more power would be provided to Garacharma Industrial· Estate and a sub-Station 
was being set up; and 
(8) for speedy development of SSI sectors, ANIIDCO would stand for guarantee for 
collateral security.

Recommendations of the Committee 
Orissa: The Committee is of the view that as the State has been declared as backward State 
the SSI units with an investment below 20 lakhs be treated as a special category for the 
purposes of taxation. The Committee has also come to understand that as there is no forum 
for redressal of justice for SSI sector, a Tribunal may be set up for the purpose. The 
following are the recommendations of the Committee for the State:-
(1) infrastructure facilities in the field of transport and communication may be 
improved; 
(2) being a rich state so far as the availability of mineral and other natural wealth is 
concerned, the resources have not been exploited to the desired level. The 
Government may take concrete steps in this regard. Adequate power supply may be 
made available for the purpose; 
(3) the limit of small scale sector be raised from Rs. 60 lakhs to Rs. 2 crores; 
( 4) the growth centres may be established by declaring the State as 'no industry 
district'; 
(5) BIFR may be introduced to revive the SSI units; 
(6) SIDBI may sanction loans to SSI units below Rs. 30 lakhs also to the help the 
sector; 
(7) the lSI, an export promotion council and DGS&D may be established in the State; 
(8) the banks may provide sufficient support to the SSI units; 
(9) the place near Hirakud Dam may be developed for tourism on the pattern of 
Vrindavan Garden; 
(10) the air-strip at Sambalpur may be improved; 
(11) the special stress may be laid in the field of agro-based and handloom sector;· 
(12) the State Government may take steps to reduce the theft of .electricity which is 
38% now; · 
(13) the Central Government may introduce special norms for the dt;,velopment of the 
under-developed States like Orissa; 
(14) the central excise rate may be enhanced from Rs. 30 lakhs to Rs. 60 lakhs; 
(15) the important rail links may be provided for speedy transportation; and 
(16) the work culture in the State may be improved.

may use the Malaysian seeds for plantation, to increase the output thereof. 
lndusJJ_ial Backwardness-
/// (1) The Committee is of the opinion that to combat the industrial backwardness, the 
infrastructural development in a package made should be strengthened assured 
power supply, road ~d rail linkages telecom facilities, providing timebound single 
window system clearance should be assured for any entrepreneur so that he may 
avail himself of the Government concessions in the backward areas. 
(2) No entrepreneur will like to invest his hardearned m~ney in a backwa:rd ·area 
unless there is enough motivation for him to do so. This should be looked into by 
the appropriate authorities who should develop package of incentives. 
(3) The Committee noted that there were widespread complaints from the ·trading 
community and their associations. That the financial institutions were not that 
responsive to invest in the backward areas. The Government should make it 
compulsory that a certain part of the money deposited with financial institutions is 
compulsory invested in the backward regions; For this a percentage can also · be 
fixed. The Commi~ee also noted that in some of the States, CD ratio allows 
prospect of large inyestment_ in that region. There ~ere also complaints about 
environment clearance which was delaying the projects and escalating the price. 
The Committee is of the view that in built mechanism should be created so that 
environment clearance is expedited and takes minim'um time. 
(4) The Committee feels that the transport subsidy for the Island territories should 
continue and should apply to all the units without any restriction. 
Raw Material- ·For industrialisation in the backward areas, raw materials which are 
controlled by the State/Central Government should be assured. Government should explore 
the possibilities of encouraging investment in tne..food processing units and should also make 
efforts to create marketing facilities for the farmeis;·This naturally would have cumulative 
effect on the generation of employment, utilisation of fruits and vegetables and would add 
strength to the rural economy. 
Working of PMRY 
In Orissa, the representatives of the workers' union informed the Committee that the 
progress in regard to the working of PMR Y was not satisfactory as the banks were not 
advancing money to the beneficiaries. The Collector of Sambalpur informed the Committee 
that the responsibility for the recovery of advances from PMRY beneficiaries had been fixed 
on the junior level officers of the banks and as such they were reluctant in advancing money 
to the beneficiaries. The banks generally did not understand the scpeme and its importance 
in the upliftment of the lower strata of society. The bank officials were not keen to finance 
the Scheme. In the year 1994-95, 400 cas~s were sanctioned and disbursement was made for 
96 cases only. The representatives requested the Committee as under:-
(1) the orientation courses might be started for the entrepreneurs; 
(2) The banks might not insist for collateral security for granting loan under PMRY; 
(3) the banks might be given the target and if they did not complete it, the entries 
might be made in the confidential reports (C.Rs) of the concerned officers; and 
(4) the banks officials were under the fear that if the loan was not recovered, the 
entries would be made in their C.Rs. 

'~:' '' -
75 
In Calcutta, the:Committee was informed that the Banks were more interested in giving 
fiHancial help to the bigger industries than the SSI units. In Tamil Nadu the implementation 
under PMRY was above average. In Pondicherry, the performance of PMRY was improving 
year by year. In regard to Andaman & Nicobar Islands, the Committee was informed that a 
Task Force had been constituted to process the applications. The Task Force Committee met 
fortnightly for selection of the candidates under the Scheme. The performance was being 
monitored at the District level and State level committees headed by the D.C. and Chief 
Secretary, respectively. 
The Committee was informed of the following constraints being faced by the Banks in 
the implementation of PMRY:-
(1) Dearth of, enthusiastic entrepreneurs; 
(2) the required number of applications were not received; 
(3) the persons selected under the Scheme were later on found ineligible; 
(4) there was an increase in the income level; · · 
(5) lack of infrastructural facilities; and 
(6) non-availability of the Scheme in view of the limited existing scope. 
The officials of the State Bank of India informed the Committee that the bank officials 
should not have any kind of fear and they would not be hauled up in case of non-recovery of 
loan. The Committee was also informed that the State Bank of India would be able to 
sanction 100 per cent cases recommended to it during the current year. 
Recommendations of the Committee 
(1) the restriction on family income may be raised from Rs. 24,000/- to Rs. 30,000/­
per annum; 
(2) the restriction of 30 per cent on business activities may be removed; 
(3) the amount of training fund may be raised from Rs. 1000/- to Rs. 1200/- per 
beneficiary; 
(4)' the Task Force Committees may be constituted to process the applications and it 
may meet fortnightly. 30 days time may be given to the banks in sanctioning of the 
case after its submission by the Task Force Committee. The training may be 
completed in four weeks; 
(5) maximum three months time be taken by the banks in the disbursement of the 
Loan and in reviewing cases one month more may be taken; 
{6) the cooperative banks may also be associated with the Scheme; 
(7) the applicants may be educated about the Scheme and the formalities to be 
completed. The applicants may be asked to furnish information at one time and 
not in piece meal; 
(8) the fear may be removed from the minds of the bank officials that they will not be 
hauled up in case of non-recovery of the loan advanced by them under the 
Scheme; 
(9) the orientation courses may be started for the entrepreneurs; 
(10) the bank may not insist for collateral security for the grant of loan under PMRY. 
The State administration may stand for collateral security. In A&N Islands, 
ANIIDCO had agreed to stand for guarantee; 
(11) the banks may be given the target under the Scheme and if they do not complete 
it., the entries may be made in the C.Rs. of the concerned officers; 
(12) the bank officials who understand the Scheme and are trained enough in the field 
may be posted for the purpose; 

76 
(13) the lead banks in the respective StatesiUT may co-ordinate the work relating to 
the sanctioning of loans under the Scheme and watch their performance and issue 
instructions from time to time in regard thereto; and 
(14) in the circular issued by the State Bank of India, Calcutta on 30.11.1993, it has 
been indicated that if a member of the family is a defaulter to a bank/financial 
institution, other member of the same family will not be eligible for assistance 
under the Scheme. The Committee feel that in case the father is a defaulter and 
the son is doing his business separately and not dependent upon his· father, the 
said restriction may not come in his way. 
The Committee also enquired about the PMRY, a Central Government project to 
provide self-employment to the educated youths by establishing various types of small sector 
industrial units, etc. and discussed with the officials of the banks and the State Government 
authoriti.es and others. The Committee arrived at the conclusion that generally the attitude 
of the banks is not positive in such investments. The Committee is of the view that the 
Government should monitor the PMRY advances regularly so t'bat the very purpose of 
socio-economic development contemplated by the C.entral Government for removing the 
poverty from the rural masses is not defeated. The Committee also appreciates that after the 
Committee's interaction with authorities and representatives of various organisations 
Andaman & Nicobar Islands and Tamil Nadu, things have shown some improvement in this 
direction but at the same time the Ministry should remain vigilant as regards the 
implementation of this Scheme.

Study Note on the visit of Sub-Committee Ill of the . Department-Related Parliamentary 
Standing Committee on Industry to U.P. from 22nd to ~8th September, 1995 
The Sub-Committee III of the D.R.P.S.C. Committee on Industry was constituted to 
study the problems of Industrialisation in backward areas and Capital Goods Industry. The 
Sub-Committee consisted of seven members, namely:-
1. Shri Naval Kishore Rai 
2. Shri Raj Nath Singh 
3. Shri V. Rajeshwar Rao 
4. Shri Manjay Lal 
5. Shri Rajendra Agnihotri 
6. Shri Rama Krishna Konathala 
7. Shri Purna Chandra Malik 
Shri Naval Kishore Rai was the Convenor of the Sub-Committee. 
2. The Sub-Committee visited U.P. from 22nd to 28th September, 1995 for the purpose 
and held discussions with the representatives of Chambers of Commerce and Industries, 
Officers of the State Industry an~ other Departments,· District authorities of Varanasi, 
Mirzapur, Alla~abad, Jhansi, Agra, Firozabad and l.ucknow and other workers 
organisations. The salient features of the outcome of the study are as under:-
Meeting with Representatives of State Industry Department, Vam#J-it 
On 23rd September, 1995 the Sub-Committee held its first ~eetin~.!'::ith representatives 
of State Industry Department where it was informed that although' Varanasi is well 
connected to various major cities of the country but not much head way has been made in 
the field of industrial development. The Sub-Committee was apprised that the main reason 
for the backwardness is insufficient infrastructure, shortage of power, non-availability of 
working loan and withdrawal of subsidy. It was also informed that to increase the industrial 
activities sales tax exemption for 8 years has been provided to small entrepreneurs· and ·all' · · 
the land under Industrial Estate has been allotted. It was further informed that in SSI there 
are three sick units, revival of which have been undertaken. On the status of handier~ in 
the region, the Sub-Committee was informed that Carpet, Moti, Zari, Banarsi Silk Saree, 
wooden toys and embroidery are few handicrafts which are exported to various countries. To 
keep this handicraft alive, various steps such as grant of subsidy, welfare activitres and 
prevention of exploitation of weavers· are being taken care of. 
In PMRY it was apprised that the ..target is largest in U.P. The Sub-Committee asked 
for a report in the matter since 1993 which the representatives agreed to supply. ·

was informed about the scores of problems being faced by entrepreneures. 
Firstly, there is lack of fi'nance/working capital. The entrepreneures represented that 
banks are not cooperating and are asking for equal collateral securities. Secondly, there is 
problem of marketing since the Government is the main buyer 'of products which is unable 
to buy these products. Thirdly, the entrepreneures pointed out towards the erratic power 
supply and bad road conditions which are counter productive to industrial Growth. Fourthly,

never accepted. They also demanded representation in various committees and establishment
