M6etiq wUit the Repnllnlflli,es of TrtUI.t a11d llldustry, Mil'zapur 
~t Mirzapur on 24th September, 1995 the Sub-Committee's first meeting was held with 
the Trade and Industry representatives.where it came to understand that Mirzapur, which is 
world famous for its · carpet weaving industry, is also known for Brassware and 
Aluminiumware industry. In this meeting, diverse problems of Mirzapur wet:e brought into 
the awareness of the Sub-Committee, viz, problems relating to raw m~terial, effect of 
western propaganda on carpet exports, recognisation of Bronze metal utensils industry, 
infrastructural problems; viz, electricity, telecommunication, roads, water transport etc. 
The Sub-Committee was apprised · that Mirzapur's Brassware and Aluminiumware 
industriea were facing grave problems in acquirUlg raw material, whi~h is ·mainly imported 
from abroad. Hence, more active coordination from central agencies is needed in this 
respect. The Sub-Committee was told .that for importing the raw material in O.G.L., the 
duty payable is SS%. However, in other neighbouring countries, .such a· duty is either not 
there, or is just lSo/o. Hence, this duty needs to be rationalised, in order to benefit the 
growth of industries. 
It was also suggested to the Sub·Committee that the Government should ensure 

81 
imparting of proper training, and better technology for the betterment of the area's bartan 
(utensils) industry. It would, besides improving quality and promoting cost effectiveness, 
promote exports. Hence, an industrial training centre needs to be opened in the region. The 
Sub-Committee was also told that the local small scale industry was also plagued by high 
rate (18% p.a.) of interest on loans given by banks. The trade representatives recommended 
that the interest rate should be rationalised @ 10% p.a. 
The Sub-Committee was suggested, that in order to remove mass unemployment, 
Government should encourage setting up of large and medium scale industries in the region; 
viz, food processing, metal alloy, garments and apparel, and other ancilliaries. Mining can 
also be encouraged in the region. 
The thrust of the discussion then shifted on to Mirzapur's famous carpet weaving 
industry. The industry, which till recently had been a major foreign exchange earner for 
India, is now sagging under the false western propaganda on child labour, blown up beyond 
all reasonable proportions. 
In this context, the Sub-Committee came to understand that the work-force in this 
industry consists of both, migratory population from Bihar, Madhya Pradesh, and as far as 
Nepal (around five lakhs), ~nd the natives of Mirzapur. The majority of workers consist of 
children. The Sub-Committee could comprehend that what the foreign countries were 
decrying as exploitation of bonded child labour was in fact, their misunderstanding of the 
Indian context of 'putting out system', where the traders advance money to craftsmen to 
produce for'themselves. While bonded labour is by compulsion, the labour involved here is 
traditional and especialised. 
The Sub-Committee suggested that the traders of Mirzapur should form their association 
and should work with administration to reduce the hours of work of child labour and 
implementation of· Government Scheme. 
It was also represented to the Sub-Committee the Mirzapur lacks even in basic 
infrastructure, viz, roads, transport, electricity, potable water, telecommunications and 
health facilities. To promote Mirzapur's linkage with the country in order to promote its 
marketing and tourism, it was suggested that important trains passing through this district 
should have a stoppage here. A fast shuttle .service should be started, linking Allahabad­
Mirzapur-Varanasi. It was further suggested that a washing plant for carpet industry also 
needs to be set up there.

Various issues such as infrastructure development, raw material, PMRY, Cooperative sector, 
K.V.I.C., TRYCEM Yojana, DRDA training, etc. came up for discussion.

Municipal Boards. Its total area of 4952 sq. kms. has been divided into 12 development 
blOcks. Carpet weaving, Bronzeware, Chinaware and Plaster of Paris Bust making are its 
main SSI's. The district has only one large scale and two medium scale industries, viz, 
manufacturing cement and blanket yam, flour respectively. 
The Officials told the Sub-Committee that the child labour problem, which. was 
adversely affecting the carpet industry was most severe in Mirzapur's remote areas, which 
were not connected by transport. 
The Sub-Committee was told that problem of child labour coming from other States had 
been considerebly checked sinee 1992. The Sub-Committee was told that child labour was

Meeting with the Represenltllives of Trade and Industry, Jlumsi 
At Jhansi, on 26th september, 1995, the Sub-Committee's first meeting was held with 
the trade and industry representatives. The Sub-Committee came to understand that 
Bundelkhand region is highly backward in industrialisation. The industries presently 
functioning with the exception of BHEL are on the bJ"ink of sickness. Industrial development 
is at present more on Government records than in reality. 
The Sub-~mittee was apprised that Government had proposed development of 
growth centres in Jhansi as early as 1989, on about 1500 acres of land entailing an 
investment of Rs. 30 crores. However, Goven1ment policy changed and this scheme was 
limited to 500 acres and for last four years, no effective steps to implement the growth 
centre scheme have been taken. As a marked contrast, regions .of M.P. located near Jhansi 
have developed many industrial areas and are now attracting investment and industries at 
the cost of the latter. However, UPSIDC, the Sub-Committee was tol~, presently insists that 
developed industrial land is abundantly available and it is the industries which are failing to 
take advantag~ of it. The trade representatives, thus, pleaded that unless the basic 
infrastructure is developed, aH other subsidies like power, capital, sales tax etc. cannot be 
taken advantage of. Thus there is a necessity to start a growth centre at the earliest and for 
giving the accompanying subsidies for the next five years. 
The Sub-Committee also came to understand that the conditions of the industries still 
functioning is moving from bad to worse. More than 90% of the industrial units have either 
been closed down or are on the brink of sickness. Many factors work in tandem to account 
for this, viz, lack of raw materials, marketing infrastructure, trained labourers, high 
electricity cost, anomalous tax structure as compared to the bordering Madhya Pradesh. 

8S 
Jhansi's Geographical ·situation together with the inaction of nodal Government agency 
UPFC in planning and implementing rehabilitation packages have also contributed in no less 
way to Jhansi's industrial sickness. The Sub-Committee was told that State Financial 
Institutions demand 100% collateral security for sanctioning loans and exercise severe 
stringency at its recovery placing this backward region at par with industrially developed 
areas. Thus, help from banks and financial institutions is also lacking. 
The Sub-Committee was also requested to recommend for setting up of Bundelkhand 
Industrial Area Development Authority on the lines of NOIDA, so that specific policies for 
industrial development can be .formula~ed. The Sub-Committee .was also apprised that non­
availability of electricity· due to overloading of transformers is resulting in depletion of 
industries. It was pointed out that a general improvement in elC!=tricity combined with tariff 
concessions would also encourage power based capital goods industries such as cement plant, 
induction furnace, rolling mill etc. 
The Sub-Committee learnt that in Mauranipur district, famous for production of 
terrycot garments, about 10,000 handlpowerlooms are in operation employing about 20,000 
people of weaker section. These poor weavers are being immensely exploited by middlemen 
who, while giving them a meagre profit of 2S paise per metre, themselves get one rupee per 
metre profit in the market. Hence, there is a need to save them fronrthe clutches of the 
frandulent brokers by forming a cooperative for marketing their products and ensuring 
adequate remuneration to them. 
The Sub-Committee also learnt that there are vast untapped resources in the region 
whose tapping can boost the regions' economic potentialities. Thus in this regard, the granite 
base stone crushing, fresh water fishing and tourism industries may be encouraged. 
Finally, the Sub-Committee learnt of the negligible contribution of the profit earning 
BliEL towards the development of ancilliary units and the economy of the region. The Sub­
Committee was informed that out of the 6 ancilliary units of BHEl. 4 are closed due to 
wrong policy of BHEL (only 20% supply order is given at very low rate). Furthet BHEL is 
not purchasing even S% of its total purchase from SSis in Jhansi. Same is the position of 
railway workshop which is not even giving 1% supply order ·to SSis. 
The entrepreneures also complained about low CD ratio of about 36%. They also 
demanded that in view of the large potential for stone crushing industry a Stone Crushing 
Zone be established.

Meeting with Management of Bharat Leather Corporation, Agra 
On 27th September in its meeting with Management of BLC the Sub-Committee was 
informed that initially the main object of the corporation was to perform development 
activities for leather industry but later on in 1981 it was turned into a commercial enterprise 
and aCtivities were mainly restricted to trading in leather footwear and leather goods. In

87 
The Sub-Committee was informed that with the increase in demand for Direct Injection 
Process (DIP) for sole making, a DIP plant was commissioned, in 1990 under Package

Even after this, the Sub-Committee was apprised that .the· <2orp~ration incurred

\ 
The Sub-Committee observed that besides all theses the performance of the corporation

expected orders to the company. The management further informed that a detailed revival 
plan is under the active consideration of the Government and right now Government oi 
India is providing non-plan assistance depending upon cash loss to run the corporation.

and industry representatives. The Sub-Committee came to understand that Firozabad, which 
specialises in glass industry, is facing diverse problems. The region has four large and 
medium scale industries with a gross capital investment of 1~ crore rupees, giving 
employment to about 3415 people. The Small and tiny sector having 2789 registered SSI 
units, involving capital investment worth 4150.85 lakh rupees and employing about 56,202 
people, however, dominates the industrial spectrum of the region. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that pollution to which the smoke emitting chimneys 
of Firozabad are contributing as much as the Mathura refinery, is giving sleepless nights to 
the districts . entrepreneures. Due to the area's location in the Taj Trapezium zone, where 
polluting industries are now banned, these units are facing threat of closure or re-location. 
The Sub-Committee was apprised that Vardarajan Committee Report had clarified that the 
Taj was being mainly affected by the Sodium Carbonate content of the smoke. Therefore, if 
sufficient electricity and gas is· provided in the region, they could switch Qver to better 
technology and thus tide over the pollution problem altogether. The Sub-Committee was 
told that the glass industry of Ferozabad was also facing ·the problem of sales tax which is 
also being imposed on the raw material,. thus raising the cost of production .. The 
enterpreneures further pleaded that the new Provident Fund Law, which necessitated 
deduction of P.F. even if a labour had worked for a single day was also creating problems 
for the industry. The labour too, the Sub-Committee was told, did not want his P.F. to be 
deducted, since he did not want a permanent enlistment which would only block his money. 
The Sub-Committee was told that coal, the basic source of energy for majority of these 
industrial units was in the hands of the Government. Apart from coal being of sub-standard 
quality there was an acute shortage of coal which is available in· open black market. The 
Sub-Committee directed both the trade and industry representatives to give copies of their · 
correspondence made with. Government and the district administration, in this regard. 
The Sub-Committee came to understand that the condition of glass development centre 
too, is not good. Inspite of having spent crores of rupees on it, no development in glass 
industry has been made from it. Thus, its aim of making a model project has not been 
fulfilled. Though some foreign experts came and offered technology for upgradation and 
modernisation of Firozabad's glass industry, the Sub-Committee was told that it entailed an 
investment of 18--50 lakh rupees~ which was beyond the potential of the regions' 
entrepreneures. 
The Sub-Committee was told that the Railway Coal Siding, .located in the centre of the 
city was also a source of problem, since the movement of trucks damage the roads, besides 
causing freqhent traffic jams; 
The Sub-Committee was apprised that the glass industry was facing problems in 

89 
procuring raw material. The companies which sell soda-ash increase their rates at will four 
to five times every year. Hence, government should exercise some control over these 
companies. Firozabad which sold its products both within India and abroad, the Sub­
Committee was told, would benefit a lot, if its Railway station was expanded and stopage 
of important trains was allowed there. 
The Sub-Committee was apprised by the D.I.C. officials that besides glass, Firozabad 
also had other SSI units viz. food processing, iron, chemicals, paper, rubber and plastic, 
machinery and machine parts, wood, hoisery garments, leather and tannery, cotton and 
textiles, Tobacco and other miscellaneous products. 
Industrial areas have been developed by D.I.C. at Firozabad, Shikohabad, Ussayani 
and Tundla. Thus, plots I sheds have been allotted at Firozabad and Shikohabad. Industries 
have already begun production on the allotted plots I sheds. The Sub-Committee also 
found that out of the 3442 people trained under the entrepreneurial development 
programme between 1989 till 1994-95, only 157 have been able to open their own 
industries. Targets hav.e also not sufficiently. been met in PMRY loan disbursal. In 1994-95, 
out of the target of 302 only 296 applications were sanctioned and only 197 loans were 
distributed. In 1995~96, out of the target of 393, only 44 applications were sanctioned and 
only one loan given by the bank. 
The Sub-Committee was told that an Industrial Training Centre was imparting training 
in General Mechanic and Electrician courses for the duration of one year. This institution 
was also imparting training under TRYCE~. 
Meeting with the Representatives of Workers Union, Scooter India Ltd., Luc/fiJOW

also represented that the company was referred to BIFR in 1992 and now the position is 
improving. The workers also complained that no executive has so far been retrenched. The 
Sub-Committee came to know that executive worker ratio was very high and there is no 
appointment of technk:al experts in the company.

On the development of Growth Centres it was informed that under the Growth Centre

Centres at Jhansi, Shahajanpur, Moradabad, Bulandshahar, Jaunpur and Gorakhpur have 
been approved and the remaining two at Etawah and. Pauri-Garhwal are under appraisal. It

On the basis of the eviden<,;,e tendered in Varanasi, the Sub-Committee observed that 
Varanasi has a vast potential of exporting its handicrafts such as Silk Sarees, Zari 
embroidary and Carpets. 
Accordingly, the Sub-Committee recommends that incentives may be given to promote 
export of these handicrafts items besides nltodernisationlupgradation of the technology in 
this field. On the functioning of.BH_EL's Heavy Equipment Repair Plant, Varanasi, the Sub­
Committee observed that this plant of BHEL is not -working according to the reputation of 
BHEL and presently facing competition due to similar companies in the field. The Sub-

91 
Committee therefore, felt that BHEL must aggravate its activities to stay in the race. The 
Sub-Committee further observed that BHEL, Varanasi has three phases and even after 10 
years of its establish.ment only one phase is working. The Sub-Committee, accordingly, 
recommends that the viability of the second phase may be studied and it may be started at 
the earliest. 
On child labour problem in Mirzapur, the Sub-Committee understood that one of the 
main factor accounting for child labour is the abject poverty and economic backwardness of 
this region. Therefore, the Sub-Committee recommends that steps may be taken to impart 
education to as many children as possible. It also recommends that schemes for training and 
employment generation formulated by the CentraVState Government in this regard may be 
implemented in right earnest and working hours of children may be reduced. 
The Sub-Committee felt that carpet industry is the main industry of Mirzapur and 
therefore there is an urgent need for fixing minimum wages in this field. The Sub-Committee 
further recommeds that steps, to send the Children in carpet industry, to schools may be 
taken and they may be prevented from exploitation. Further, for improving industrial 
scenario of Mirzapur, the Sub-Committee recommends that import duty on raw material for 
Brass and Aluminium Ware industries may be reduced and Government should ensure 
proper training and better technology for these industries. 
In Allahabad, the Sub-Committee feels that there is very heavy rush on old Yamuna 
bridge and there is an urgent need to build a new Yamuna bridge (up stream of existing 
bridge) connecting Naini. It also feels that Allahabad may be brought on the air map of 
India and flight to atleast, New Delhi may be started. The Sub-Committee further observes 
that in view of availability of best Silica Sand in Allahabad, there is a vast potential for 
developing glass industry. The Sub-Committee also express its· concern over the high 
percentage ( 46%) of sick Small Scale Units in Allahabad and recommends that elaborate 
plan may be chalked out to revive them. On observing that most of the plots/sheds in 
industrial area of. Pratapgarh are vacant, the Sub-Committee recommends that it may be 
declared as zero industry district. 
On Bharat Pumps and Compressors Limited, Naini the Sub-Committee observed that 
the executive employees ratio is very high in the company which is counter productive and 
therefore should be brought down by VRS. The Sub-Committee further obser-Ved that delay 
in supply effect the reputation of a company. Accordingly every effort may be made to fulfil 
the order in time. 
For Jhansi the Sub-Committee observed that a Growth Centre under Growth Centres 
Scheme was sanctioned in 1989 but due to change in· the policy, the State Government 
decided to shift it. In this regard the Sub-Committee recommends that there is an urgent 
need for developing a Growth Centre at Jhansi. Accordingly the Stat~ Government may be 
asked to review its decision on shifting Growth Centre from Jhansi. 
The Sub-Committee feels that the role of BHEL, Jhansi, one of the premier 
organisation of the country, in developing small scale and ancilliary units, has not been very 
encouraging. Nor the Sub-Committee finds that there is any help from the Railway 
Workshop, Jhansi. Accordingly, the Sub-Committee feels that there may be an agreeme!.lt 
between the State Government and BHEL for assured supply orders to local entrepreneurs. 
A similar agreement may also be worked out with Railways. The Sub-Committee feels that · 
local entrepreneur also have to equip themselves to be capable of fulfilling the supply orders 
of BHEL and Railway workshop. 
For the growth of Bharat Leather Corporation, the Sub-Committee feels that there is an 

92 
urgent need to establish emporia and other retail outlets in various cities. The Sub­
Committee furthet feels that a policy may be formulated wherein all the Central and State 
Government :Qepartments and PSU's rnay be directed to purchase a fixed percentage of 
safety and uniform shoes from Bharat Leather Corporation. 
The Sub-ComJilittee observes that air pollution by smoke emitting from chimneys of 
founderies in Firozabad are givittg sleepless nights to the entrepreneurs of Firozabad since 
under the Taj Trepeziul'n Zone most of the area of Firozabad has been declared as air 
pollution, protection area. The Sub-Committee, therefore, recommends that for preventing 
air pollution, sufficient electricity artd gas may be provided to this area in order to switch 
them to better technology. 
The· Sub-Committee also feels that glass industry of Firozabad is facing problem due to 
short supply and inferior quality of coal. The Sub~Comniittee. therefore recommends that 
steps may be initiated to make good quality coal available in Firozabad. The Sub-Committee 
feels that the Glass Industry Oevelopment Centre is also not functioning satisfactorily in the 
field of development of glass industry. The Centre has· to aggravate its activities to make its 
presence felt in the region. The Sub-Committee further recommends that the Centre/State 
Government may plan to exercise some control over the companies which are supplying 
soda-ash to glass indu~try so that the rise in prices can be checked. 
Apart from these regional problems, the Sub-Committee also noticed some common 
problems accounting for backwardness of the areas it had visited. 
The Sub-Committee feels that finance (either term loan or working capital) is the major 
problem of all the areas it had visited. The Sub-Committee observed that banks are not 
cooperating and ate reluctant to give loan besides· demanding 100% collateral securities. 
Therefore, the Sub-Committee recommends that there is a need to have proper guidelines 
for banks with regard to distribution of loan. 
The Sub-Committee also feels that many boardering areas in Uttar Pradesh are being 
affected due to different sales tax in other States. · TherefOre,· ·the · Sub-Committee 
recommends that the Central Government may consider inftoducing a uri'fform sales tax 
system throughout the country. The Sub-Committee also recommends that anotHer 
important reason for backwardness in the State is the under developed infrastructure. 
Accordingly, there is a need to set up an Infrastructure Development Fund with the help of 
Central Government for developing infrastructure in the State. 
The Sub-Committee observes that the power supply position is pathetic in most of the 
areas of the State and needs to be improved. The Sub-Committee is of the view that single 
window scheme may be initiated in its right earnest and there should be simplified bye-laws 
and administrative procedure for giving a boost to the industrial progress in the State. The 
Sub-Committee feels that there is a need to improve the CID ratio of banks in the State 
since banks are not ready to take risk and are investing the money out of the State. 
_ Study note on the visit of the Sub-Committee-Ill of the Department-related Parliamentary 
Standing Committee on Indu,St(y to Bihar from 4th to lOth November, 1995 
The Sub-Committee-III of the D.R.P.S.C. Committee on Industry was constituted to 
study the problems of Industrialisation in backward areas and Capital Goods Industry. The 
Sub-Committee consisted of seven members, namely:-
1. Shri Naval Kishore Rai 
2. Shri Raj Nath Singh 
3. Shri V. Rajeshwar Rao 
4. Shri Manjay Lal 

93 
5. Shri Rajendra Agnihotri 
6. Shri Rama Krishna Konathala 
7. Shri Puma Chandra Malik 
Shri Naval Kishore Rai was the Convenor of the Sub-Committe~. 
2. The Sub-Committee visited Bihar from 4th to 11th November, 1995 for the purpose 
and held disc;:ussions with the representatives of Chambers of Commerce and Industries, 
Officers of the State Industry and other Departments, District authorities of Patna, Ranchi, 
Bokaro, Mu:zaffarpur and Sitamarhi and other workers organisations. The salient features of 
the outcome of the study are as under:-
Brleling wltb the omcJaJs of the State GovernmeDt 
On 4th November, 1995 the Sub-Committee held its first meeitng with the Officials of 
the State Government where it apprised them about the purpose and objectives of the visit. 
The Sub-Committee also directed that representatives of various concerned department may 
be present in the concluding meeting of the Sub-Committee with the State Government on 
lOth November, 1995 so that matter concerning their department can be clarified then and 
there. The Sub-Committee directed that meeting with the representatives of the concerned 
Industrial Area Development Authorities where the Sub-Committee is visiting may be 
arranged at the respective places of visit of the Sub-Committee and the representatives of 
remaining Industrial Area Development Authority of places where the Sub-Committee is not 
visiting may appear at the meeting at Patna on lOth November, 1995. · 
On learning that matter regarding approval of the Growth Centre for Bihar is pending 
with IFCI the Sub-Commi~tee directed that representatives of IFCI may also be called from 
New Delhi to appear before the Sub-Committee at its meeting with State Government 
officials on lOth November, 1995 so that matter can be sorted out in the presence of State 
Government officials. 
Meeting with Worhers Unions HEC, Ranclli 
On 5th November, 1995 the Sub-Committee reached Ranchi, where it met with the 
worker's unions of HEC. The Sub-Committee cam~ to understand that once a prestigious 
profit making organisation and pride to nation, HEC is now on the verge of death. 
As a natural corollary to the company's sad state, the Sub-Committee came to 
understand that diverse labour problems have cropped up. The employees of HEC are 
facing a very tough time as all the facilities extended to them have been curtailed/suspended 
due t.o shortage of funds. · 
The workers apprised that their wage revision is pending for last four years and they are 
not getting L.T.A., leave encashment and due D.A. point anymore. For the last two years 
even salaries to workers have not been paid in time and the casual workers have not been 
regularised. The managem~nt was not able to deposit CPF and even the transport subsidy 
has been withdrawn. The Sub-Committee was told that due to a fear psyschosis in workers 
that HEC will not run, the work force is fast depleting and many experienced workers have 
opted for VRS resulting in qualitative degradation of work. Even some foreign trained 
technicians have been given .administrative jobs rather than technical work showing lack of 
professionalism in regard to utilisation of work force. 
The Sub-Committee was also apprised that unorganised labourers engaged in the plant 
are given minimum wages fixed for the construction work rather than that of engineering 
industry which is higher. 
The Sub-Committee also came to understand that due to deteriorated state of HEC the 

94 
basic civic amenities to workers are also affected. In plant hospital at Ranchi where the 
facilities used to be very good, qualified doctors have left under V.R.S. and doctors present 
are also indulging in private practice resulting, only those patients -who attend their private 
clinics get good medicines (though free of cost) from the hospital. Majority of medicines are 
not available at the plant hospital and though HEC reimburses for the local purchase, the 
procedure is time consuming and causes a lot of inconvenience to workers. There are also 
apprehensions that the hospital may be given to some private organisation. At the township, 
the petty maintenance works are not being attended to. 
The representatives of Trade Unions told that no land has been· allotted for their office 
and there are cases of disciplinary action on workers on anonymous complaints. 
The workers, naturally apprehensive about their fate, pleaded that­
( a) sufficient work order may be provided to HEC, 
(b) financial aid may be given to the compauy immediately, 
(c) HEC may be taken out from B.I.F.R. 
Meeting with Trade and Industry Representatives, Ranchi 
The second meeting of Sub-Committee at Ranchi was with the Trade and Industry 
Representatives where it came to understand that the industrialists are facing diverse 
problem, in setting up/runmng successfully their industrial units. Firstly, there is a prevalent 
insecurity among the entrepreneurs, due to law and order problem in the region which is not 
conducive to industrialist for installing industrial units. Secondly there's lack of adequate 
basic infrastructure in the State. The poor condition of roads results in high costs of 
transportation of raw materials and finished products. The supply of power to industriaL 
units is very erratic and is moving from bad to worse and above all there are draconian rules 
of minimum guarantee, and minimum electricity charges which again contribute to higher 
cost of production and poor productivity. Telecom services are also not upto the mark in the 
region. The industrial areas are not well connected by local and STDIISD lines and there is 
frequent breakdown of the system. 
The entrepreneurs also told the Sub-Committee about rampant corruption in the local 
bureaucracy, including RIADA. There the attitude of officials is not to guide but to grab the 
entrepreneurs the moment they commit a mistake and rules are interpreted in such a way so 
as to corner the entrepreneurs. 
Contrary to assertions made by the State Government there is still widespread 
prevalence of Inspector Raj, and the entrepreneurs are invariably harassed and threatened 
by the inspecting officials from various department such as Labour, Sales Tax and Central 
Exdse, Pollution Control, Transport and there are many administrative procedural wrangles 
which are not existing in other States. Thus in place of producing anything worthwhile, the 
entrepreneurs are mentally engaged in these activities only. The attitude of Bihar State 
Financial Corporation, too, is more of a Money-lender than that of an agency for industrial 
development. Its rehabilitation programmes are neither timely nor constructive. The 
industrialists feel that they should be given representation in its Board of Directors. 
The entrepreneurs did not approve the recent policy of deducting P.F. of worker who 
has worked in an establishment a single day and stated that this scheme is not favourite even 
among the workers. 
Another big problem faced by the industrialists is the shortage of coal, though the area 
is situated on the pit-head of coal reserves. This problem is actue during rainy season and 
whatever the entrepreneurs manage to get is at high rates. 

95 
The present state of HEC is also affecting the industries in and around Ranchi, since 
most of them are its ancilliaries or are indirectly dependant on HEC. 
Apart from this the entrepreneurs stated that there are no compelling reasons for 
entrepreneurs to start industry in the region and in order to allure the entrepreneurs the 
Government should give certain incentives such as-
(i) tax holiday for Jharkhand area and more powers to Jharkhand Autonomous Council; 
(ii) uniform tax system throughout the country; 
(iii) modernisation of Ranchi airport for night landing; 
(iv) maintenance of National highway by Central Government; 
(v) offices of all major Central Government in Ranchi; 
(vi) settlement of E.S.I. cases in Ranchi not in Patna; 
(vii) increase in railways facilities and availability of wagons; 
(viii) simplified administrative procedure and restriction on the visit of Government 
Officials in the industries. 
On the basis of the evidence tendered, the Sub-Committee observed that one of the 
main reasons for the sickness in industrial units in and around Ranchi is the sickness in 
HEC. Therefore revival of HEC is must for the health of units in and around Ranchi. 
Besides this, the sub-Committee observed that erratic power supply is contributing to further 
sickness in the industrial units and therefore needs to be improved. The entrepreneurs are 
also suffering due to different tax structure in different States. Accordingly the Sub­
Committee recommends that there should be a uniform tax structure throughout the 
country. The Sub-Committee further recommends that development of infrastructure such as 
good road conditions, modernisation of airport, availability of wagons and increase in 
Railway facilities are the need of the hour for creating industrial atmosphere. 
On the functioning of RIADA the Sub-Committee observed that it should hetp the 
entrepreneurs instead of engaging them in procedure wrangles. For this the Sub-Committee 
recommends that administrative procedure may be simplified and the visit of Government 
Officials may be reduced to once in a year. 
The Sub-Committee also observed that functioning of SISI is not satisfactory and 
therefore recommends that it must aggravate its activities to help out the entrepreneurs. 
IHeeting with District Collector and Representatives of State Industry Department, Ranchi 
The Sub-Committee held its next meeting with District Collector and representatives of 
states industry department where it was informed that in Ranchi there are mainly 
engineering and mineral industries with other units which have also started in the area. The 
performance of Ranchi in achievement of PMRY is good and last year, (1994-95) out of the 
target of 900 loan 427 proposals were actually disbursed. The Sub-Committee observed that 
working of SIS!, inspite of running certain EDP, is not very satisfactory. There was a testing 
lab at Ranchi which has been shifted to Dhanbad due to which entrepreneurs of Ranchi are 
facing difficulties. 
The recoveries of B.S.F.C. are not good and out of the 414 units financed by it, 217 are 
sick mostly due to working capital. B.S.F.C. is also considering the rehabilitation of these 
sick units and is also starting to give working capital from this year. 
On the working of RIADA the Sub-Committee was informed that all the land under it 

96 
has not been allotted and there is no sickness in the industrial area under it. The Sub­
Committee examined the issue of sickness and found that it is contradictory to the statement 
given by B.S.F.C. which has also financed certain units presently sick under RIADA. The 
Sub-Committee asked for a report in the matter. 
The Sub-Committee also came to know that among other things unpleasant state of 
HEC is one of the root cause of sickness among the industries in the regic;m besides power. 
About the local industries and handicraft the Sub-Committee came to know that 
production of vegetable, honey, lac and mushroom has increased. in the area. The 
production of handicraft has also increased and the administration is helping in providing 
· market to these products. Keeping in view the good harvest of vegetable and fruits the 
adminsitration requested that a booklet on food processing industries, giving details about 
the cost of various food processing units and procedure for establishing it, may be made 
available to develop the said industries in the area. 
On financing by Banks in PMRY and other proposal the Sub-Committee was informed 
that Banks are reluctant to give· loan due to 
(i) bad recoveries 
(ii) non functioning of big industries 
(iii) too much loaning. 
The administration was of the opinion that in PMRY it has moved proposal· to forward 
projects of ITI graduates who are already .trained. 
Meeting with Management ol HEC, Ranchi 
On 6th November, 1995, the Sub-Committee met with the Management of HEC, 
Ranchi where it came to understand the HEC needs financial help from Central 
Government, for its revival. The Management stated that it was also trying to improve its 
internal financial condition with active cooperation of its workers. The Sub-Committee was 
told that the Corporation was engaged in the manufacture and supply of capital equipments, 
machine tools and spares needed for the core sector industries, namely, Steel, Coal, 
Cement, Power, Defence, Aluminium, Ship buildina, Railway, etc. The Corporation has 
been a major contributor in the indigenisation and expansion of steel plants and has also t. 
played a significant role in the modernisation ·Of the coal sector in the Country. It has so far, 
the Sub-Committee was told, supplied over one lakh ton of Mining Equipments to coal 
mining industries the Corporation is among the few exclusive companies in the world to 
manufacture draglines. It has a turnkey project division which has succesfully completed 
projects in different core sectors of the economy, viz, Steel, Coal, Power, Cement besides 
carrying out modernisation of a cement plant in Bangladesh. Currently it is engaged in the 
execution of Raw Material Handling Plant at Rourkela Steel Plant. 
The Management also gave the details of the equipment supplied by it to Railways, 
Aluminium Industry, Ship Building, Sugar Industry, Cement and Defence Industries. 
The Management then represented to the Sub-Committee about the problems being 
faced by it and the assistance it needed from Government. Firstly, it pleaded that to revive 
operations of HEC, Group of Ministries had taken a decision to given certain financial 
assistance to company according to whicb for 1995-96 interest free loan to compensate cash 
losses of Rs. 80.61 crores were sanctioned. 
This decision of Group of Ministries was communicated to BIFR to which the company 
has been referred. The Sub-Committee· was further informed that du.ring the current year 
only 18 crores has been released by the Government. The management further submitted 
that despite the Government's intentions, efforts made by the company, and suggestion from 

97 
BIFR, the attitude of the S.B.I., sole bankers to HEC, poses a series concern in the revival 
of the company. To bring their point home, the management informed that S.B.I. has 
adjusted Rs.2.7 crores as 15% deduction (adjustment against the frozen account) and 
Rs. 8.84 crores against interest leviable as on September, 1995, thus taking away Rs. 11.6 
crores out of the funds of Rs. 18 crores provided by the Government for Salary/Wages. 
Despite the assurance that the company's performance is improving and therefore the 15% 
of collection would adequately service the interest burden is not accepted by the Bank. 
To compound the problems, bank is now planning to further restrict HEC's ability to 
utilise fund fully. This gives a feeling as if bank is behaving in a manner to negate the 
purpose of the Government's financial assistance to the company and therefore its revival. 
The management pleaded that instead of restricting HEC's ability to utilise its funds 
properly the S.B.I. should help the company within the guarantees provided by Government 
and RBI guidelines. Secondly, the company pleaded that Coal India Ltd., which was 
extensively using equipments supplied by HEC, is now turning towards foreign countries. 
The Government should direct CIL, to continue buying its equipments from HEC which are 
no less efficient than any other product. Thirdly, the company informed that it was running 
into problems with B.S.E.B. to which the Chairman, B.S.E.B. has agreed to consider 
sympathetically .. Fourthly, the management pleaded that the revised performance targets for 
1995-96 can be achieved if the balance amount of Rs. 62.61 crores is released by 
Government immediately.

Meeting with Workers Union, Bokaro Steel Plant, Bokaro 
On 7th November, 1995 the Sub-Committee met with the Workers' Union of Bokaro 
Steel Plant where it came to understand that Bokaro Steel Plant is infested with 
problems of working class like violations of labour laws, and highhandedness.: of the 
Management towards the workers. 
The Sub-Committee came to understand that the provisions made in the Industrial 
Disputes Act for the speedy disposal of labour cases within three months are not being 
adhered to. There are abundant cases of· termination of service of the workers without 
enquiry. An agrieved workman has to undergo a gruelling process of making 
representation to the Management, awaiting for conciliation proceeding, awaiting for 
reference by the State Government and only. then he can go to the Labour Court or 
Tribunal. Thus, labour cases drag for as long as ten years, by which time the victim 
labourer looses both his hope and his power of resistance. Hence, the worker~pleaded 
before the Sub-Committee that they should be directly allowed to go to the ·Labour 
Court or Tribunal. 
The workers also told the Sub-Committee that the Management of Bokaro Steel 
Plant was flouting section 10 of Contract Labour (Abolition and Regulation) Act, 1971 
by employing contract labour in jobs of permanent/perennial nature. 
These workers are paid minimum wages of the State and stand no chance of 
promotion, hike in salary, provident fund or any othe,r benefits. The contractors keep on 
changing, but the labour contracted often continuously works for 20-30 years. Moreover •. 
the wages paid by Bokaro Steel Plant to the contract workers, (Rs. 3V-. per day). is also 
not at par with other steel plants (Rourkela Steel Plant pays its contract workitS· @Rs. 
701- per day, which is more than the prescribed wage by the Government or'Orissa). 
Hence, the workers unions' pleaded before the Sub-Committee to get justice done to 
these contract workers by trying to get their services regularised, and/or at least get them 
parity of wages with other steel plants. The Sub-Conuni.ttee was also apprised about the 
malpractice of cheating contract workers at the time of disbursement of their wages, 
where they are made to sign on more amount than what they are receiving. 
The Sub-Committee came to understand that in case of accidents during werk, 
compensation is paid to the worker/his nominee, but the fact finding committee of such 
accident does not fix responsibility on the person(s) causing such accidents! M,any 
accidents the unions pointed out, occured due to out-dated and worn-ouf machinery 
which has already surpassed its life but has not been changed. The unions demanded that 
one dependant of the worker should be given employment in case of h'is death/disability 
during accident, or retirement, safe and pollution free working place should be provided 
to workers; measures should be expedited to stop such accidents; good quality indi1strial 
uniforms like shoes, clothes and other safety appliance~ should be provided to workers. 
The Sub-Committee was also told that there is no arrangement for routine medical 
check-up of the workers, to treat industrial diseases which subsequently develop into 
chronic nature and take the life of workers. Hence, the unions asked for provision of 
better medical facilities, procurement of' life-saving drugs, necessary medical equipments, 
beds and appointment of medical specialist, so that occupational diseases to workers in 
such hazardous places like Blast furnance, coke ovens, etc. can be identified in their 
primary stage~ and their life can be saved. 

•• 
100 
Besides highlighting their problems and the highhandedness of the Management, the 
workers' unions of Bokaro Steel Plant also apprised the Sub-Committee about; 
·' !.it> 
(i) irregularities in awarding contract works of c"apital repair of different units of the 
Plant ,by the concerned officials of the unit to their henchmen without prior 
approval of the Factory Inspector of the· Government of Bihar. 
(ii) modernisation works worth thousands of crores have been awarded to foreign 
companies bu'tthey in turn, have awarded the same works to local contractors and 
themselves siphoned-off large commission. 
(iii) there are also some irregularities as workers' unions pointed out, in auction of 
scrap steel by Bokaro Steel Plant. 
Meeting with Trade and Industries Representatives, Bokaro 
. ' -
· The second meeting of the Sub-Committee on 7th November, 1995 was with the trade 
and industry representatives of Bokaro where the Sub-Committee was given to understand 
that the development of BIADA industrial units has been stagrtant since long. The 
representatives of. Chamber of Commeree expressed their opinion, that better 
encouragement by BSL is warranted, so that these an~illiary units can flourish. The 
·entrepreneurs deposed before the Sub-Committee that according to the guidelines of Bureau 
of Public Enterprises (BPE) it is the duty of the BSL to promote development of industrial 
units and give working orders to the established units. But unfortunately these guidelines are 
never adhered to. Even the policies formulated and decision taken by the Plant Level 
Committee (PLC) constituted by BPE and Development Commissioner, Small Scale 
Industries for the development of ancilliary and sick and dependent industrial units, were 
neglected. 
Mostly, adhoc and unsystematic arrangement is resorted to while placing orders and no 
concrete method is adopted to in this respect. Resultantly the local industrial units are 
working in constant insecurity since two· 'decades and have to run for day to day order. 
Hence, the BIADA Chamber of Industries conveyed to the Sub-Committee, their demand 
for assured orders from BSL for at least five years, to ensure the development of local 
ancilliary enterprises in the· region. The Sub-Committee also learned that erratic power 
supply in Bokaro Industrial Areas and failure of financial institutions to provide timely loans. 
and work.ng capital have also contributed to the present state of sickness in BIADA 
·.·industrial units. 
' ~' : ; ' 
The BlADA has also not been farsignated as they have not been consistent in adhering 
to fixed policies and systems for all-round development of industrial units. 
The representatives of Chamber of Commerce also complained about harassment by · 
BSL. The products manufactured though approved by the BSL quality control team, on 
inspection, are returned subsequently and orders are cancelled by BSL on ground of poor 
quality. They also face difficulties in securing working capital from banks, which insist on 
collateral security while sanctioning loans in clear violation of R.B.I. guidelines. 
Besides the above, difficulties of general nature such as bad road condition, shortage of 
water and law and ori:ler problem ·were also mentioned by the entrepreneurs quring the 
meeting. · 
Meeting with Managenumt of .llokaro Steel Pla11t 
At 4.30 P:~. qn. 7th N~vember, 1995 the Sub·Committee met with the Management of

absence of the Managing Director of Bokaro Steel Plant who proceeded to his preoccupied

units, staffing pattern, reservation policy, recruitment policy, construction works, wages, 
contract labour, modernisation of plant, relations with BIADA, leasing of BSL land and

from workers over the issue of payment of bonus, which has been delayed. The workers 
were basically demanding parity in bonus, for people eligible under the Bon~s Act 
(numerically only 10%) and. people not eligible under the act. The Management deposed 
before the Sub-Committee that an agreement has recently been reached with the workers' 
unions over the issue.

lands have been acquired by BSL. The Management's plea that they had solve<;t all cases of

Organisations in their memorandum to the Sub-Committee. When this was pointed out, the

Government of Bihar, in return of which BSL had paid the whole compensation to it and 
further stated that apart from this whatever will be claimed should be borne by the Bihar 
Government according to their agreement with the State Government. The Sub-Committee 
asked the Management to supply a brief in the matter. 
On the specific question of maintaining parity with its Rourkela Plant in payment of 
minimum wages to daily wages workers BSP Management clarified that minimum wages 
being paid to workers, were different in all the States, and BSP was paying the minimum 
wages fixed by Bihar Government. 
The Sub-Committee also asked BSL the reasons for its declining orders to HEC, 
Ranchi, once its 'mother organisation' and the Management assured that they are trying to 
give as much as possil?l~ ~,o ,~EC. 
Quoting from the brief given by BSP, that it is spending Rs. 4000 per workers per year 
on medicine, and was maintaining a 900 bed hospital the Sub-Committee enquired about the 
% ratio of money spent on establishment: and medicine separately. The Sub-Committee 
came to understand that out of the total hospital budget of Rs 24 cJ,'ores per ye~r, only Rs. 6 
crores are bejng spent on medicine. Thus, BSP is spending only 25% of money'qn medicine 
as against 75% on establishment costs. The Sub-Committee directed the Man.agement to 
supply the. break-up of amount spent on medicine and establishment for the last tbif!e years. 
Medicines are purchased from market by limited tender ,policy and are not being purchased 
from IDPL BSP Management accounts for it by stating that the latter is not in a po~ition to 
supply medicines on time. When BSP Managemen~ .was asked about the reasons for not 
opting for open tender policy for buying medicines, the Sub-Committee was told that with 
50-60 companies competing for a single medicine e.g. Ampicillin, it would not be possible to 
buy the best quality medicine at the lowest cost. The Sub-Committee conveyed to BSP the 
representation made by workers' Unions, that since the instances of industrial diseases are 
rising it should have made compulsary medical check-up for workers every year. l3SP. 
Management, replied that compulsory medical check-Qp is a regular feature, and the' 
attendence of workers had risen from 15% to 45% as previously workers not attending such 
check-up were being compulsarily forced to do so,

The Sub-Committee asked Management about its staffing pattern and enquired whether

STs, reservation in promotions, reservation for O.B.C.s, recruitment policy and details of 
recruitment made in last three years and the numbers of casual workers in last three years.

know that for purchasing goods worth above Rupees one crore, open tender policy is being 
followed, while in other cases, purchase are made by limited tender based on quality control for 
which B.S.L. had registered parties. The Sub-Committee directed B.S.L. to give a report on it, 
for the last three years. 
The Sub-Committee came to understand that for purchase of cloth, B.S.L. had tried to 
prefer NTC and KVIC but they did not respond. 
The Sub-Committee further enquired about the procedure of civil construction in BSP and 
came to understand that maximum construction work in BSP has been given to H.S.C.L., and 
little work is given through open/limited tenders to small contractors. The Sub-Committee

about the civil construction undertaken in BSL.

last ten years.

with police remained unanswered by the Management to which the Sub-Committee directed the 
Management to send a report of last 10 years about the cases of theft which were registered with 
police and which were not registered. 
At last the Sub-Committee expressed its decision to visit B.S.P again to have deliberati ... 1:; 
with Managing Director on certain specific issues. 
On the basis of the evidence tendered the Sub-Committee recommends that the contract 
workers may not be engaged to the work of permanent nature which can be performed by 
regular workers. The Sub-Committee also recommends that to stop the malpractice of cheating 
contract workers at the time of disbursement of wages, the principal employer may be asked to 
open pay counters specifying venue, date and time so that it can be monitored. The Sub­
Committee is of the view that accidents in any establishments can not be ruled out but utmost 
care should be taken that it should not happen due to outdated and worn out machinery. The 
Sub-Committee further recommends that routine medical check up every year may be made 
compulsory for evey worker to protect the workers from chronic occupational diseases. 
The Sub-Committee observed that ancilliary units in Bokaro are not flourishing the way 

103 
they should be in the present sound position of B.S.P. The Sub-Committee, accordingly, 
recommends that -
(i) reservation of jobs/orders may be annually fixed for the BIADA units; 
(ii) a high level technical and bureaucratic Committee may be formed to study the 
basic hindrances in the way of development of BIADA units and to monitor their 
functioning; 
(iii) a Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) between B.S.L., BIADA units and 
Government of Bihar may be signed on an annual basis for procurement of 
orders; 
(iv) existing units could be encouraged for enhancing their capacity and diversifying 
into manufacture of other related items required by B.S.P. and which presently · 
are being supplied from outside BIADA; 
(v) a technical cell for development, improvement and monitoring of the 
manufacturing process and quality control may be formulated; 
(vi) training may be imparted to entrepreneurs and employees of BIADA units for 
enhancement of productivity, quality · improvement and Human Resqurce 
Development by B.S.P. Training Department. 
In addition to above the Sub-Committee also feels that implementation of the decision 
taken· by Plant Level Committee (PLC) is also necessary for the development of BIADA 
units. The Sub-Committee further feels that steps may be expidited to stop incidents of theft 
in BSL. 
Meeting with Workers Union, IDPL, Muzaffarpur 
On 8th November, 1995 the Sub-Committee met with the workers union of IDPL at 
Muzaffarpur where it was apprised that the Muzaffarpur unit of IDPL is an alcohal based 
organic chemicals manufacturing plant producing Acetaldehyde, Acetic Acid, Methl-Ethyl 
Pyridine and Niacinamide starting from Ethyl Alcohol as the principal raw material which is 
abundantly available indigenously in North Bihar from the bye-product of sugar mills, i.e. 
molasses. The plant has locational advantages of four alcohol producing distilleries and 28 
sugar mills within the radius of 150 kms. Althou~ the plant started its business with the aim 
of providing "Medicines for Millions", today however, it is seeking medicines for its own 
health, as it has been declared sick and referred to B.I.F.R. for reviv.al. The Muzaffarpur 
unit is thus on the verge of closure . 
.In course of its deliberations with the workers' union, the Sub-Committee came to know 
about some of the reasons for the losses suffered by the Company. 
Fir$1ly, M/s. A.B. Bofors (Sweden), who had been awarded the contract for supplying 
the machines had presumably installed outdated technology and machinery, violated the 
contract, left before the production trial, and thus left the fate of the plant hanging in 
balance. The production was thereafter started by Indian engineers which was much below 
the capacity that the project, if fully completed, WO\lld have generated. Hence, this project 
was sabotaged ab initio. The workers demanded that suit for damages may be initiated 
against A.B. Bofors which left affairs of the Company in mess. 
Secondly, different captive plants viz. refrigeration, water treatment, pump house, boiler 
plants had been set up with huge investments keeping in mind the large -capacity and large 
product range. However, the under-utilisation of their capacity, due to the project's non­
completion has again accounted for wastages and losses. Heavy high capacity machines were 
set up in production which_ operate only ten days in a monthi boiler for captive power 
c \ 

104 
plant was set up for 20 MT, but since presently the plant needs 7 MT steam only, the 
fuelling cost of the boiler is higher; only one reactor out of two and only four autoclaves out 
of seven are presently being utilised. 
Due to these problems the IDPL's Muzaffarpur unit has drifted towards sickness. The 
employees are working under immense pressure and worried about their future. In this plant 
there are 183 workmen out of the total staff of 215. The Muzaffarpur unit has been declared 
as non-viable on stand-alone basis according to the lOBI report and as per the revival 
package approved by B.I.F.R. on 10.2.94, it has been converted into a separate 100% 
subsidiary of IDPL, in collaboration with State .Govermnent of Bihar. The workers' unions 
regard this as a major conspiracy by IDPL management to declare this unit as surplus and 
one which cannot work. The reduction of manpower from 327 employees to 215 in one year 
by giving VRS (45 employees) and effecting transfers (57 employees) have only enhanced 
the fears of workers. 
The. workers union told the Sub-Committee that status of subsidiary or joint venture 
with the State Government or leasing it out to private p~rties will not deliver any goods and 
will·be disastrous. They had on 24.12.93 submitted a memorandum to Prime Minister, signed 
by 21 M.P's and proposed a revival package of 15 crore rupees, in which the Muzaffarpur 
unit would be run by a cooperative management of workers and engineers. But nothing 
came of it. In the given situation, the workers feel that the best way of revival of the unit is 
its merger with HOCL, as suggested in the revised viability report submitted by lOBI on 
24th May, 1993, and approved by B.I.F,R. on lOth February, 1994.
