informed that the plight of weavers in the area is miserable due to shortage of raw material 
and lack of finance and there is no work for weavers. 
Meeting with Workers Union, Sitamarhi 
On 9th November, 1995, the first meeting of the Sub-Committee was with the 
representatives of workers union. The Riga Sugar Mill Workers' Union represented that 
bonus limited of Rs. 3500/- is not sufficient since most of the workers are getting a higher 
salary and also that Fourth Pay Commission for workers of sugar industry be constituted at 
the earliest. On the specific question of using the same workers by rotation in Riga Sugar 
Mill in its distillery and fertiliser factory the union's representative answered in negative. In 
the absence of any other major industry in Sitamarhi district of North Bihar, almost 30,000 
workers are employed in Bidi Industry. But neither the State nor the Central Government is 
taking care of them. They are not even paid the minimum wages fixed by the Government 
of Bihar and nave no working capital of their own. Hence, they are victims of great 
exploitation. · 
The Bidi workers' union complained that in the hospital there is always shortage of 
medicine. Central Government take control of the hospital and ambulance is always under 
repairs. Bidi workers further represented to the Sub-Committee that the rising price of 
Tendu Patta and Tobacco and the basic raw-materials for the industry> be controlled. Steps 
alsc()ieed to be taken to establish their cooperative and to make W;Qrking capital easily 
accessible to J3idi workers, through banks. ;,~~'~. 
The district local transport union apprised the Sub-CoJ,Ilmittee that transportation 
system in Sitamarhi is predominantly road based and there has been no significant 
~ · development of railways. Hence, Banks and financial institutions should provide adequate 
finance for this industry, as it will generate employment and faster trade. Secondly, the 
union apprised the Sub-Committee about the dilapidated condition of roads which have not 
been repaired since long. Since Sonbarsa and Meethapur of Sitamarhi district touch the 
Nepal border, the highway should be declared as International Highway and maintained 
accordingly. They also demanded a railway ov~rbridge at Mehsaul railway crossing and the 
Bus-depot furnished with facilities of water, electricity toilet and rest house be constructed 
and located adjac~nt to the railway station and the city main market. All these steps, they 
pleaded would further boost trade and commerce and tourist flow in and through Sitamarhi. 
Sitamarhi district weavers' union represented to the Sub-Committee about the massive. 
exploitation facea by weavers. Out of 10,000 weaverS in th.e district only about a thousand 
are able to secure work, and that too, only in accordance with seasonal demand. They have 
not been given the service card and other facilities by the labour department. Their illiteracy 
rate is as high as 99% and in the absence of any medical facility educational economic and 
social welfare, .fhey have nothing to lose except their lives. The union also gave suggestions 
that the weavers could be given proper training under TRYCEM and training centres could 
.be opened for them. The weavers may also be provided working capital through banks to 
start ·their own work. 
Workers of the Bihar Laghu Udyog Nigam pointed out that banks are proving to be the 
main fiimterance in the industrialisation of the area and demanded that action against branch 
manager of bank may be initiated if he flouts the RBI guidelines. 
While demanding a broad guage railway line in Sitamarhi the workers were in a very 

107 
agitated mood. They further apprised that the region has a large potential for establishing 
sugar and Khandsari Mills and therefore it should be a thrust industry for the area. It was 
also mentioned that Sitamarhi harvests a good vegetable and fruit crop and therefore 
incentives for establishing food processing industries may be given to invite entrepreneurs. 
Afeeting with Representatives of Trade & Industry Sitamarhi 
The representatives of Trade and Industry Association told the Sub-Committee that 
Sitamarhi is almost an industryless place because except Riga Sugar Mill, and a few SSI's 
and Khadi and Village Industries' Units, there is no other industry. There are many rice and 
sugar mills, which are rusting, though raw material is abundant. They pleaded that if 
Government took some initiative, this situation can be reversed and Sitamarhi may become 
a rice and sugar SLlrplus district, and give employment to many people. 
The Sub-Committee was told by trade and industry representative that though D.l.C. is 
working in the district, it has done nothing to develop industries there. Industrialists who do 
(once in a blue moon) come to invest, face many obstacles in their way and are scared away 
due to official apathy and red-tapism. Thus, no industrial enviornmentworth its name exists, 
and its creation, the Sub-Committee feels, is the need of the hour. 
Giving their suggestions about the potential industries that could be set-up in the area, 
the entrepreneurs apprised the Sub-Committee that straw-board, H.D.P.E. Bags, solvent 
extraction plant, leather, bone crushing units could be set up. Establishment of women 
Polytechinc was also demanded, in order to productively harness the dorment potential of 
women. 
Entrepreneurs pointed out about the difficulties faced in installing Khandsari units due 
to harassment by financial institution and monopolistic attitude of Riga Sugar Mill. The Sub­
Committee also came to know that due to this attitude of Riga Sugar Mill few farmers 
growing sugarcane in their fields are also suffering. The Management of Riga is neither 
procuring sugarcane from their fields nor it is freeing them from its reserve area. 
Afeeting with District Megistrste and State Industry Department, Sitamarhi 
The Sub-Committee met with officials of the State Industry Department where it was 
given to understand that the land alfotted to industrial estates has not been fully disbursed 
and a seminar is being organised with all concerned lead banks and cooperatives to disburse 
the remaining 14 acres of land within one month. The Sub-Committee observed that the role 
of D.l.C. in creating an atmosphere for development of industries is not very encouraging. 
The Official also informed the Sub-.Committee that the cost of production of items, such as 
fish, food, poultry, etc. is higher in Bihar due to lack of subsidy (other States are. giving 
subsidy). This alongwith lack of infrastructure and finance are also responsible for industrial 
backwardness of the area. The working of Cooperative Department was not satisfactory in 
so far as they had no industry based programme, nor programme for unorganised labourer 
has ever been organised. Besides this there are cases of fake societies registered with the 
department. In P.M.R.Y. the Sub-Committee came to know that last year the performance 
was 40% of the target whereas this year it is yet to accelerate. 
On the basis of its meeting with the workers union representatives of the Trade and 
Industry Department the Sub-Committee observed that Sitamarhi district of north Bihar has 
a large potential for development of Sugar and Khandsari Mills but at present there is only 
one big sugar industry i.e. Riga Sugar Mill. Therefore, the Sub-Committee recommends that 
some concrete steps need to be taken to promote more such mills in the are'a. Keeping in 
view the good harvest of vegetable and fruits, the Sub-Committee also recommends that 
incentives may be given for starting food processing units in the area. 

108 
In the field of developing infrastructure the Sub-Committee felt that there is long 
pending genuine demand for broad guage railway line from Dharbhanga Narkatiya Ganj 
and new line from Muzaffarpur to Sitamarhi which if acceeded to may give a boost to 
trade activities in the area. The road condition may also be improved for the purpose 
and the road to Sitamarhi may be declared a National Highway. 
The Sub-Committee further observed that the plight of the bidi workers and weavers 
in the area needs to be considered sympathetically. 
The Sub-Committee showed its concern on the attitude of Riga Sugar Mill towards 
the conditions of Sugarcane growing farmers of Sonbarsa, Parihar, Susand, Pupsi Bajpats, 
Naupur and Runi Saidpur which are under Riga Sugar Mill's reserve area and from 
whom it is not purchasing sugarcane. The Sub-Committe, therefore, recommends that 
either these farmers may be freed from the reserved area of Riga Sugar Mill or the Riga 
Sugar Mill should buy sugarcane from these farmers. The Sub-Committee felt that Riga 
Sugar Mill must take other routine welfare measures like Kisan Bhavan etc. for the 
benefits of farmer who are supplying them sugarcane. 
The Sub-Committee further recommended that subsidy in certain industries may be 
given by the State Government to invite entrepreneurs in the area. The Sub-Committee 
also suggested that in P.M.R. Y. providing loan to a group of person instead of individual 
may be considered to make it competitive and preference in loan may be given for 
establishing vanety of enterprises rather than same type of enterprise. 
Meeting with the Officials of Government of Biha1· 
The final meeting of the Sub-Committee was held on the lOth November,. 1995 with 
the State Government officials, where it was apprised that the Chief Minister has recently 
taken a lot of initiatives for the creation of industrial environment and promotion of 
industrial development in Bihar. Thus new Industrial Policy was announced by the State 
Government which came into force on September 1, 1995, with the aim and objectives of 
creating on environment for optimal utilisation of State's agro climatic, mineral and 
human resources; reviving potentially viable and closed industries; boosting exports; 
providing quality infrastructure and simplifying procedure to expedite and impart 
transparency in decision making. 
The Sub-Committee came to understand that Bihar is currently facing vast magnitude 
of problems. For the improvement of infrastructure, Government is taking steps for 
improving telecom, power, transport, roads, etc. Role of private sector has been utilised 
in the concept of Build-Operate-Transfer (BOT) for development of infrastructure in the 
State. Except in few parts of the State, the power position is not good. The Sub­
Committee was informed that Government is actively going to seek help from private 
sector for power generation and distribution. Further the Government would encourage 
setting up of captive power plant to meet the existing as well as future demand for 
power of industrial unit and would review the tariff rate besides granting certain 
exemption/incentives to new units. 
The Sub-Committee was. told that under the new policy for attracting investment for 
sustaining industrial development, the State Government is going to allow sales tax 
incentive to new units. The earlier ceiling of Rs. 15 crores on industries for aid has also 
been removed and new units get sales tax deferment benefit on purchase of raw materials 
and finished goods for 8-10 years, irrespective of their investment. This is a big 
advantage and in many industries like telecommunication, computer software/hardware 
and electronics, its limit is 300% of the fixed investment. The freight equalis,ltion policy, 

109 
due to which Bihar's industries were not gaining from proximity to raw material like coal, 
iron ore,. etc., has been abolished. Policies regarding coal have also been modified. 
The Sub-Committee was told that the Chief Minister had a campaign during which 
Industrialists were apprised about investment climate in Bihar. In a single day, 80 proposals, 
for eOO crores worth of investment have been received 31 MoU's worth Rs. 7700 crores were 
received from foreign countries. 5-6 companies, the Sub-Committee was apprised, were 
already in Patna to work out modalities regarding project implementation. 
In industrial policy administration too simplification has taken place and single window 
system both at State and district level has been introduced with the Industrial Development 
Commissioner and Collector, respectively as Chairman. The Chief Minister holds an open­
House every month in which problems of industrialists are solved. An Industrial Investment 
Promotion Board under the Chairmanship of Chief Minister is soon going to be constituted. 
An Empowered Committee under the Chairmanship of Chief Secretary comprising 
concerned Departmental Secretaries for coordination and expediting decision making 
relating to investment proposals has been formed. 
The Sub-Committee was told that for simplification of the procedure for land allotment 
for industrial purposes, power has been decentralised upto the level of Collectors in the 
district, who would be authorised to allot upto 5 acres of Government land and land above it 
would be leased to the entrepreneurs with the approval of the Empowered Committee. 
Hence, the land allotment will no-longer get delayed due to red-tapism. Similarly, procedure 
in electricity department have also been simplified- Entrepreneurs no longer require 
permission to use Diesel Generating sets upto 500 K.V.A. capacity. There is exemption 
from electricity duty for 5 years on captive power generation up to 25 M. W. for own 
consumption to industrial units. 
Deliberating upon the possible causes of industrial sickness, the State Government 
officials opined before the Sub-Committee that the negative approach of the banks was a 
major cause for industrial sickness. There is either inadequate finance or no finance. During 
the last 4-5 months, the Chief Minister advised the General Manager of banks to increase 
their Cash Deposit ratio. Bank of India has assured to increase its investments in the State. 
Such commitments are being deliberated with other banks also. The officials requested the 
Sub-Committee to pursue the matter with Finance Ministry in this regard thereto. 
The Sub-Committee was apprised that in P.M.R.Y. about Rs. 200 crores of annual 
investment can be made, to generate employment through establishment of micro enterprises 
in the rural as well as urban areas. About 22000 applications are received every year. Last 
year 11000 cases were sanctioned but only in 7000 cases loans were actually disbursed. Thus, 
there is about 50% gap in target, sanction ratio, and about 40% in sanction: disbursement 
ratio. One reason for this state of affairs is that branch managers are not cooperating. Thus, 
banks are not doing their job up.to the mark and this is growing into a major problem. 
The Sub-Committee was then apprised about Growth Centres. U:nder the Growth 
Centre Scheme, 1988, the State was allotted six Growth Centres out of which two centres, 
namely, Hazaribagh and Begusarai have been finally approved and a sum of Rs. 50 lakhs 
each has been released to these two centres on 3rd May, 1995. Representatives of IFCI 
informed that Chhapra is also going to be approved soon. The official requested the Sub­
Committee to make efforts for expediting sanctions for Growth Centres at Bhagalpur 
(pending since May, 1994), Muzaffarpur (pending since April, 1993) and Darbhanga 
(pending since, April, 1994). The Sub-Committee had called IFCI officials in this meeting, 
and referred this issue immediately to them. IFCI officials apprised the Sub-Committee that 

110 
reports regaramg Chhapra, Muzaffarpur and Bhagalpur were ready. They assured to the 
Sub-Committee that by 25th November, 1995 fonnal sanction to these Growth Centres will 
be sent to Ministry of Industry. The Sub-Committee was apprised by IFCI · officials that 
Darbhanga's sanction is getting delayed due to the fact that the site chosen for Growth 
Centre there, is located in flood prone area. The Sub-Committee directed IFCI to 
coordinate its site inspection in Darbhanga with State Government officials, and to sort out 
the issue at the earliest. The IFCI asked for one months' time to finalise Darbhanga Growth 
Centre. The State Government officials then apprised that the sum of Rs. 50 lakhs per 
Growth Centre released by Ministry of Industry was far too insufficient and demanded a 
sum of Rs. 5 crores towards the Growth Centre which has been sanctioned. The official 
substantiated their demand by stating that unless 80% of the total cost of the land is not 
deposited, the land can not be acquired and the cost of required land is too higher. 
The Sub-Committee then observed that disbursement in P.M.R.Y. by Banks in Bihar is 
quite low. Relating their problems, when the .Bank officials accounted for delay in training 
as one of the reasons, the Sub-Committee was not satisfied. The Convenor intervened and 
queried, that lead bankers are present in the task~force at district level with District 
Megistrate as head, to accord sanction to projects in P.M.R.Y. Then, there should be no 
reason for bankers to !ater on shelve a project as 'unviable' which had been approved by the 
task-force. The Sub-Committee also observed that R.B.I. guidelines on P.M.R.Y., are not 
being adhered to in the State due to callousness of banks. On a specific question whether 
the officials were receiving complaints about non-compliance of R.B.I. nonns by banks with 
respect to P.M.R.Y., the officials answered in the affirmative. 
The lead banks officials were informed that D.C. of Ranchi had complained that in 
violation of R.B.I. norms banks were demanding collateral guarantee in disbursing loans 
under P.M.R.Y. The lead banks assured that an enquiry would be conducted, and clarified 
that banks in Bihar had been given clear instructions not to demand any security or 
guarantee for P.M.R.Y. loan disbursement. 
The Sub-Committee then enquired about the position of I.I.D. (Integrated 
Infrastructure Development) in the State and came to understand that four_ projects at 
Fatwah (Patna), Belchampa (Daltonganj), Kumarbagh (West Champaran) and Alamnagar 
(Madhepura) have been formulated. Each centre envisages an investment of Rs. 5 crores on 
development of infrastructure. The Sub·Committee directed the state officials to give a 
report on the achievements made in this regard. 
The Sub-Committee came to Wlderstand that Khadi and Village Industries Sector 
employs 3.45 lakh people in the State. Government of India pays for the establishment cost 
of the Khadi Board but the funds given by Government are not enough and the State 
Government also has to supplement. 
The Sub-Committee conveyed to the officials the general complaint of SSI's that (i) 
Head Quarter of big industries set up in Bihar should be located in the·state, and (ii) sale of 
scrap outside the State should be regulated to give preference to its consumers within Bihar. 
The Commissioner of Industries replied that industries cannot be pressurised to locate their 
Head Quarter in the State and denied that any losses in terms of taxation, excise etc. were 
incurred by the State due to this fact. For Scrap sale, the Commissioner asserted that though 
no such law for reserving scrap for units in the State existed he had, nevertheless, written to 
the Managing Director of Bokaro Steel Plant to give preference to Bihar's units before 
sending it outside. 
The Sub-Committee took up the case of non-payment of subsidies to K.V.I. for the last 
two years at the district level, due to which it was becoming sick. The Commissioner of 

111 . 
Industries replied that two crore rupees had been received from the State Government 
alongwith claims from 40 districts. Verification process was underway. The money has been 
placed in a special account. 
The State officials pleaded that for North ·and Central Bihar, which are rich in sugar 
production, they had got 28 application for setting up sugar mills, since 1991. But the 
Ministry of Industry had sanctioned only one application which was a pathetic situation. 
They further pleaded that Government of India was overlooking Bihar for declaration of 
certain areas as backward areas and giving them tax exemptions. They stated that Bihar had 
65 areas with industrial backwardness. The Sub-Committee was fm1her apprised that 
Industry in Bihar was facing a severe shortage of working capital which was further 
contributing to industrial backwardness. The Sub-Committee apprised the officials that there 
were several small Khandsari units which were being exploited e.g. Riga Sugar Mills in 
Sitamarhi had kept five prakhands reserved for itself. The mill owner had stated on record 
before the Sub-Committee that he would neither put up Kantas there, nor dereserve it. The 
officials promised to take action in the matter. 
The Sub--Committee was further apprised that by March the State would also take 
decision to privatise the sugar mills owned by it. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that a world bank project with Rs. 1.55 Million is 
soon going to begin to increase efficiency of Bihar's current power projects. They will give 
report on investment programme, load forecast and least-cost option. 
The Sub-Committee also enquired about non-conventional energy projects and was told 
that they were not commercially viable for the State. Nevertheless, bio-gas plants are there, 
and the State is seeking to develop wind power. 
Thereafter, the Sub-Committee asked from General Manager telephones the reason for 
Bihar being at the bottom of telecom facilities with only 3 'lakh connections. General 
Manager pointed out !ack of coordination at State level. He assured that if the Secretary of 
Industry gives a copy of the planned projects, Telephone Department would take action to 
develop the hfrastructure accordingly. He asserted that telephones were available in Ran chi 
and P:.otn;.J on demand and big cities were having electronic exchanages with sophisticated 
':~quipment. The Sub-Committee agreed that while it would be true of big cities, but the 
picture was hardly rosy in villages, where it was sometimes difficult even to communicate 
between one block and another, within the same district. Waiting lists in villages and district, 
it was pointed out were large. The Sub-Committee did not agree to the submission of 
General Manager telephones that cabling had been done in 50% of the State. The Sub­
Committee asked him to submit a detailed report. 
The Sub-Committee felt that irregularities were committed in the allotment of industrial 
land. The state officials denied this. The Sub-Committee suggested the state officials to hold 
camps for allotment of industrial land. The state official answered in the affirmative and 
informed the Sub--Committee about their renewed effort regarding infrastructural 
upgradation, viz., roads, water supply schemes. A Committee under the Chief-Secretary of 
the State was trying to work out rehabilitation of 18 sick corporations and some mergers 
were consequently, likely to take place. 
The state officials represented to the Sub-Committee that th:? most important ·financial 
organisation of the State, B.S.F.C., is itself facing sickness du~ to bad recoveries. 75% of 
units financed by B.S.F.C. are in bad shape. The care for rehabilitation of B.S.F.C. has 
been pending with I.D.B.I. for last two months. There are also, scores of medium and SSI 
units in the State, which are sick. A proposal is under way, to make a Committee under the 

112 
Directorate of Industries to work out a revival package for the SSI units which are outside 
the" purview ·of BIFR. 
On the basis 9f the evidence tendered, the Sub-Committee observed that the condition 
of industrial units in Bihar is in bad shape due to scores of reasons, main being the shortage 
of funds and erratic power supply position in the State. 
The Sub-Committee observed that the other reason for backwardness in the region is 
the deteriorated conditions of big industries on which many small and medium industries 
were dependant. Accordingly, the Sub-Committee recommends that banks must increase 
their finance in the State to give a new lease to the industries. The Sub-Committee also feels 
that improvement in the power supply position is equally important alongwith the finance. 
The Sub-Committee lauded the provisions of New Industrial Policy, 1995 of the Government 
of Bihar wherein elaborate plans for creating industrial environment has been laid down. 
But on the other hand the Sub-Committee is apprehensive that these aim and objectives of 
new industrial policy must not glow on papers only but put into practice to change the 
industrial scenario of the State of Bihar. For this the Sub-Committee desired that the 
Central Government should also extend its cooperation by way of incentives to make it a 
success. The Sub-Committee further observed that the various industrial areas development 
authorities which were created for acquisition of land and development of infrastructure in 
their respective areas have not worked upto the expectation. Accordingly the Sub­
Committee recommends that instead of having offices at different places, these authorities 
may be monitored from one place which win also reduce the establi~hment cost. These 
Authorities must shed their bureaucratic attitude and help in develoqing an environment 
conducive to industrial growth in the State. f 
The Sub-Committee also observed that in view of the good harvest of vegetables and 
fruits in few districts, steps need to be taken to exploit this potential by way of establishing 
food processing units in these areas. 
The Sub-Committee feels that there is urgent need to consider ena·t~ing a uniform tax 
structure throughout the country since in many parts of the country entrepreneurs are 
suffering due to different tax structure ·in different States. 
The Sub-Committee further recommends that the head of a public sector undertaking 
must be for a full term and there should not be any time gap arrangement since it is not 
good for the health of the public sector undertaking. 
On observing the conditions of sick public sector undertakings under the control of the 
State Government, the Sub-Committee recommends that some amount of NRF may also be 
provided for workers of these public sector undertakings. 
Lastly the Sub-Committee recommends that to improVe disbursement of loan under 
PMRY, performance of bank particularly branch manager's performance may be entered in 
their annual performance report since this would encourage them to work harder. Secondly, 
the Branch Manager of banks fear that if loan under PMRY is given without security, 
recovery will not be made and they will face problem. Hence, the Sub-Committee feels that 
this accountability procedure about fixing responsibility needs to be modified and if they 
follow RBI guidelines and then a loan is not recovered, personal responsibility should not be 
fixed. 
Study Report of visit of Sub-Committee III of Department-Related Parliamentary Standing 
Committee on Industry to Gurgaon, Patna, Dhanbad and Bokaro during 9th to 12th January, 
1996 -
The Sub-Committee-III in its first visit to Bihar in November, 1995 had covered Patna, 

113 
Ranchi, Muzaffarpur and Sitamarhi. However that visit to Bihar turned out to be incomplete 
due to certain reasons, one being that the visit to Dhanbad had to be postponed due to pr~~· 
occupation of the local Administration in rescuing trapped miners. Even in Bokaro, the Sub­
Committee could not hold deliberations properly in the absence of the Managing Director of 
Bokaro Steel Plant. Certain vital issues raised by the Committee remained unanswered and 
the Sub-Committee had decided to visit Bokaro again to clarify these issues. 
In this lag of visit the Sub-Committee started its business with discussion with workers 
and management of Maruti Udyog Limited, Gurgaon on 9th January, 1996. On lOth 
Jaimary, the Sub-Committee left for Patna where it met with the officials of Industry 
Department of Central Government and the State Government · in connection with 
development of six Growth Centres approved for Bihar and als9 the progress of Integrated 
Infrastructural Development Scheme. The Sub-Committee left the same day for Dhanbad 
and on 11th January it held discussion with ·District Collector, Dhanbad followed by two 
separate discussions with management and workers of Bharat Coking Coal Limited. 
On 12th January, 1996 while at Bokaro, the Sub-Committee held detailed discussions 
with managements of Hindustan Steel Works Construction Limited, Bharat Refractories 
Limited and Bokaro Steel Plant respectively. 
The details of study visit are given in succeeding pages. 
Visit of Maruti Udyog Ltd. at Gurgaon 
The Sub-Committee III started its business on 9th January, 1996 with some internal 
discussion at Delhi and left for Gurgaon for visit to Maruti Udyog Ltd. at Gurgaon. The 
Convenor of this Sub-Committee Shri Naval Kishore Rai could not attend the visit due to 
his pre-occupation at Patna and in his absence Shri Manjay Lal, MP presided over the 
meeting of Sub-Committee at Gurgaon. 
The Sub-Committee visited various units of the Plant and noted that use of latest 
automobile technology in the plant has helped in manufacturing of cars by Maruti Udyog 
Ltd. In October, 1995 the plant capacity of 200,000 unit per annum was enhanced to 
2,50,000 units per annum. 
The Sub-Committee gathered that Maruti Workers Employees Union is the only 
workers union of this company. At present 3800 workers are the ·members of this union. The 
representatives of the union informed the Sub-Committee that workers put in 7% hour out 
of eight (8) working time in a day, which is a unique thing. They informed the Sub­
Committee that after the liberalisation, large number of facilities for workers have been ' 
curtailed. Increased dearness amount has not been released by the management till now and 
those who have crossed the bonus ceilings could not get the amount despite Government's 
instructions on this behalf. The Sub-Committee was informed by the workers that the union 
had opposed at the time of increase of equity share of Suzuki and the management of 
Maruti had also assured that every thing would normalise gradually but till date nothing has 
come out from management side. The Sub-Committee was given to understand that out of 
50% of equity of Maruti, only 0.26% is to be given to Maruti employees mutual fund and 
Government of India is not thinking any amendment on this equity pact. At present there 
are no union's activity due to the suspension of nine employees. The represeg.tatives of 
workers union requested the Sub-Committee to look into this matter sympathetically and 
recommend their case. 
The Sub-Committee, thereafter, met with the management of Maruti Udyog Ltd. The 
Sub-Committee was informed by the management that the company was established as a 
Government company in February, 1981. At that time target of equity was proposed to 
/ 

114 
60:40. But that time none of any foreign company was ready to sign on this 40% equity. So 
considering the recession in automobile industry in the country at that time a joint venture 
agreement was signed with Suzuki motor company of Japan in October, 1982, by which Suzuki 
acquired 26% share of the equity, with an option to increase it to 40% after five years. Suzuki 
exercised this option in 1987 by increasing its equity to 40%. Suzuki Motor Corporation 
further increased equity to 50% in the year 1992 converting Maruti Udyog Ltd. into a non· 
Government company. At present Maruti's total equity is Rs. 1322.92 million. Maruti Udyog 
Ltd. is at present adopting the managerial practice of Japan. This year Maruti's profit is Rs. 
600 crores; last year it was Rs. 265 crores. This year total domestic production target is 2.75 
lakhs units. The Sub·Committee was informed that the company is not able to fulfil the 
d'emands of cars. At present demand is 2.75 lakh car per annum. Maruti 800 and its OMNI 
cars are in great demand. Besides car financing process has also helped its marketing. The 
Sub-Committee was informed that to meet the demands of the customers and to provide them 
with ex,:dlent service, the company has developed an extensive marketing and after sales 
servi.::e ne:·.vork. At present the company has 133 dealers, 31 spare parts stockists and over 700 
Maruti authorised service stations spread all over the country. Maruti has now started exports 
of its cars. Till August, 1995, the company has exported over 90,000 vehicles to more then 70 
countries, and 80% of its exports has been to Europe. The Sub·Committee was informed that 
more than 250 ancillary units have been developed by Maruti. This year the company will buy 
parts and other items worth Rs. 3200 crores from the vendors. When enquired about the 
relation with the workers, the management informed that the relation are harmonious and the 
company have established a suggestion scheme and quality circles for the purpose. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that when the Maruti Company was launched, 
excise duty was around Rs. 6000 per car which has gone up around Rs. 49000 per car and this 
is the main reason of price hike of the Maruti car. The Sub·Committee was informed that so 
far as the design and research work is concerned, all design and research are being done by 
the Suzuki Motor Corporation which wants its royalty and this is the main reason of increase 
of equity shares of Suzuki. 
f...feeti1J6 with State Government Officials of Bihar regarding Growth Centres and Integrated 
lnfrastructural Development Centres 
On lOth January, 1996 while at Patna the Sub·Committee first met with the Officials of 
Industry Department of the Central Government and the State Government on the 
development of Growth Centres and Integrated Infrastructural Development Scheme in 
Bihar. The Sub-Committee was informed that out of the six Growth Centres allotted for 
Bihar, two Growth Centres viz., Begusarai and Hazaribagh have been sanctioned and the 
Central Government has released a sum of Rupees 50 Lakhs to each of these centres. It was 
further informed that appraisal reports in respect of Growth Centres at Bhagalpur, Chhapra 
and Muzaffarpur have been received from IFCI and the Ministry of Industry has sought 
clarification on certain points from the State Government. On being asked to specify the 
points on which the Ministry of Industry have sought clarifications, the State Government 
officials informed that IFCI wants an agreement from State Government on the availability of 
requisite electricity. The lFCI further seeks State Government comments as to why 
agricultural land is being acquired for these Centres and lastly the IFCI also wants to be 
convinced about presence of perennial water resource for the Centres. 
The Sub-Committee impressed upon the State Government officials to expedite their 
replies to objections raised by IFCI. The Sub-Committee strongly recommends that much 
awaited appraisal report from IFCI, regarding Growth Centre at Darbhanga may be expedited 
on urgent basis. 

115 
The next item in the agenda was Integrated Infrastructure Development Scheme. The 
Sub-Committee has been given to understand that the Scheme was initiated in June, 1992 
for promoting and strengthening small, tiny and village Industries in ruraVbackward areas 
with active participation of State Government/financial institutions. Under the Scheme 50 
Centres are proposed to be set up at the cost of Rupees 5 crores which will be paid by the 
Government of India and SIDBI in ratio of 2:3. The State Officials infonned that the State 
Government has selected Fatuha (Patna), Belchampa (Garhwa), Alamnagar (Madhepura) 
and Gopalganj for Integrated Infrastructure Dcvel"pment Scheme. BITCo had been 
authorised to prepare project report for the LI.D. Scheme in June, 1994. IADA Patna, 
Ranchi, Darbhanga and Muzaffarpur was selected nodal agency for implementation of the 
Integrated Infrastructure Development Scheme. BITCo has prepared the project report of 
Integrated infrastructure Development Scheme at Fatuha (Patna) and Belchampa 
(Garhwa). 
The Sub-Committee noted that Government of India has been communicating with State 
Government on the issue of setting up liD centres but no response coming forth. The Sub· 
Committee further noted that the State Government has not even sent its report on the liD 
centre for Sitamarhi to Government of India. The Sub-Committee urges upon State 
Government to explore the possibilities of setting up of more liD Centres in the State in the 
interest of overall industrial development of the State. 
Bharat Coldng Coal Limited (BCCL) 
\ 
The Sub-Committee met the workers .and management of BCCL at Dhanbad with 
regard to the status of the company in overall industrial scenario of the State. 
The Sub-Committee gathered that the coal region of the company is spread from 
Bardhman to Ranchi in an area of 300 kms. The production is 30 mt. per annum. Around 
4.5 mt. of coal is sent to steel plants through washeries. At present 6 to 7 mt. coking coal 
is imported as the internal availability of the same is very less. The company is incurring 
loss to the tune of Rs. 350 crores per year .and presently referred to BIFR. There is 30% 
coking coal and rest non-coking coal. About 40 mt. good quality coal is under fire. The 
management informed that world bank has extended aid for controlling the fire. The 
project report is being prepared. According to the management, if the population of Jharia 
is evacuated, the mines could live for 50 lllOre years. 
Highlighting the reasons for losses, the management pointed out that SAIL is not 
extending the required cooperation. SAIL is paying Rs. 3300/t on coal imported from 
Australia while it pays only Rs. 1500/t to BCCL. According to the management if SAIL 
agrees to pay Rs. 2000/t BCCL could not go to BIFR. Thus SAIL is earning profits at 
BCCL's losses. 
Other problems include a number of coal theft cases. Though the company is bearing 
Rs. 2 crores per month wages bill of CISF still the small theft cases here and there have 
been very difficult to be controlled. The management further mentioned about the 
depleted condition of 92 washeries. These washeries have lost efficiency and atleast 55 
crores are required for their modernisation·. The management also informed that the work 
culture and the discipline in the company is very bad. 
While interacting with the workers union the Sub-Committee gathered that company's 
production can be increased by inviting workers participation in management. On the 
question of mechanisation and planning the workers wanted both mechanised and semi 
mechanised patterns on the lines of China. The workers further wanted that open cast 
mining be suspended and better working condition for workers be created. As regards· 

116 
work culture the unions felt that the same can be improved by workers participation 111 
management. 
The Sub-Committee feels that it is still time to improve the work culture. For this the 
management and the workers should sit together and set up a joint committee to monitor 
the same~ 
Meeting with District Collector, Dhanbad on Industrial backwardness 
At Dhanbad, the Sub-Committee first met the District authorities about the overall 
industrial status of the district. The District Collector informed that in Dhanbad there are 6 
large scale, 26 medium scale industries and about 4076 (435 SSI units and 3641 Tring units) 
registered units out of 435, 106 SSI units are sick. No foreign investment has so far come in 
the district. However NRis have been suggested to set up Food Processing Industry. 
Under Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana last year out of a target of 1250 cases only in 302 
cases loan could be disbursed. This year the target is 1719 out of which disbursment has 
been done in only 147 cases. That means only 11.6% achievement. The District Collector 
informed that the reasons for poor performance is that bank's efficiency is calculated with 
.deposits it has and not by disbursments. The district authorities have brought to the notice 
of different agencies the poor performance of banks. 
When asked whether any utilisation survey has been undertaken in respect of the 
disbursed cases, the District Collector replied that all units are working. The District 
Collector however made it clear that the banks are notaccountable to State Government 
and if district authorities are to be asked about Stattbovernment's performance on PMR Y 
some con.trol on banks could be desirable at district level. 
The Sub-Committee feels that the District Collector may be empowered to analyse the 
performance of banks under the scheme. The Sub-Committee equally feels it important that 
bank authorities should also realise the seriousness of the scheme and cooperate with district 
authorities to the maximum possible in making the scheme a success. 
The Sub-Committee was very disappointed togather that the CD ratio in the district is 
18%. It wonders as to how the area can industrially grow with such a low CD ratio. The 
Sub-Committee suggested to the bank representative that a meeting of district bankers be 
held on urgent bases and a note on reasons and suggestions for improving CD ratio be 
prepared and sent for consideration of State Government, Central Government and the Sub­
Committee. 
In a communication subsequently sent to the Sub-Committee the Bank of India, 
Dhanbad indicated following suggestions for improvement in CD ratio:-, 
1. Identification of potential units in the District; 
2. Injection of working capital and term loan as per requirement; 
3. Financing in aggressive manner to Self-Help Group for prospective projects; 
4. Removal of Bank notion for non-recovery of debts; 
5. Adequate powers to be vested with the individual banks 111 line with the Non­
Banking Financial Companies; 
6. Setting up proper industrial climate in the District; 
7. Government sympathy for adequate power supply. This is one of the major 
impediments for industrial growth and improvement in Credit Deposit Ratio; 
8. Adequate linkage of coal and timely delivery should be ensured by CIUBCCL; 

117 
9. Infra-structural development is to be strengthened; 
10. Setting up commercial trade complexes at strategic places like, Jharia, Chirkunda, 
Katrasgharh, Kirkend and Bank More; and 
11. Composite plan for industrial growth, service oriented industries along side G.T. Road. 
-Viz : transport repair shops/ garage motel facilities, breakdown van etc. 
The Sub-Committee hopes that the Central as well as the State Government would take 
note of the aforesaid suggestions and devise some concrete method in consultation with the 
Bank Authorities to improve the CD ratio. 
Hindustan Steel Wol'lfS Construction Limited (HSCL) 
The · management informed the Sub-Committee that Hindustan Steel Works 
Construction Limited ~as incorporated in the year 1964 with the primary objective of 
creation of public sector organisation capable of undertaking complete construction of 
modern integrated steel plants from the stage of site investigation to the stage of 
commissioning of the plant. Pooling up the available expertise and know-how in the 
company today has within its ambit of activities a wide range of specialised work in steel 
sector, power plants, dam construction, bridge, coal handling plants, underground 
communication and transport system, industrial and township e,omplexes etc. involving high 
degree of planning, coordination and sophisticated construction techniques. 
Elaborating the highlights of the company the management informed that the company 
earned profit till 1977-78. However slowing down of construction tempo in steel plants 
coupled with unplanned requirement of workforce resulted in accumulated losses. Turn 
around started from 1994-95 under a no profit no loss situation. The turnover of the 
company has risen from Rs. 239 crores in 1990-91 to Rs. 390 crores in 1994-95 with a 
projection of Rs. 411 crores in 1995-96. Even under the existing financial strain, HSCL 
generated Rs. 380 crores which takes care of 97% of direct annual expenditure. 
The management informed that last year Bokaro Steel Plant gave an order of Rs. 117 
crores which is not sufficient enough. Highlighting the turnover position of the company the 
management indicated that the company has taken up I completed certain important 
assignments such as Reheating Furnace No.4, Modernisation Package of Continous Casting 
Plant and Rebuilding of Coke Over Battery No. 4. The management pleaded before the 
Committee that a little work support from SAIL awarding even 25% of its annual outlay of 
additional, modification and repair works I operational jobs approximately valued at Rs. 200 
crores only will enable the company to gainfully engage its workforce. On the front of Non­
Steel Sector, HSCL has completed prestigious work of International Stadium at Cochin. 
The Sub-Committee has been given to understand that HSCL directly employs over 
14000 employees and indirectly supports half a lakh contract workforce besides other 
ancillary support services. Out of this technical strength of the company is 2100. There has 
been no recruitment in the company during last five years. Infact there is an excess man­
power of six thousand. 
On the basis of the evidence of the Management and the papers I document submitted 
before it, the Sub-Committee has come to the conclusion that the company is faced with the 
problems such as (i) Lack of adequate orders, (ii) Lack of strong technology base, (iii) Low 
Equity base and huge burden of Government loan & interest and (iv) Virtual withdrawal of 
purchase & price preference. The Sub-Committee hopes that Government of India would 
sympathetically look into these problems and come to the help of the company as. far as 
possible keeping in view the contribution of the company in the steel sector of the country. 

118 
The Committee also urge upon Government to ensure orders for the company so. that it may 
be able to strengthen its technological base. It should also be well within the consideration of 
Government of India to restore the purchase and price preference of the company. SAIL may 
perhaps be in a better position to work out some solution for revival of the company. 
Bharat Refractories Limited 
The Management informed the Sub-Committee that the Bharat Refractories Limited 
was incorporated on 22nd July, 1974 and at present it has three Units viz (i) Bhandaridah 
Refractories Plant at Bhandaridah, (ii) Ranchi Road Refra~tories Plant at Ramgarth and (iii) 
Bhilai Refractories Plant at Bhilai. The Company is incurring losses and referred to BIFR 
since 1992. BIFR had appointed IDBI as the operating agency for working out the revival 
plan. The recommendation of the BIFR on the revival plan submitted by IDBI is awaited. 
Highlighting the problems faced by the Company the Management informed that the 
Company is not getting the orders. The following major constraints were indicated:-
!. Increase in the cost of inputs such as raw materials, fuel, power, salary and wages, 
etc. Although -some of the finished products fetches higher price, the price rise is not 
in proportion to the price rise of cost of inputs, 
2. Frequent power failures particularly units situated in Bihar, 
3. Shortage of Raw Materials, 
4. Lack of Working ~apital, 
5. Accumulation of Inventory leading to liquidity problems, 
6. Lack of adequate orders, particularly for premium products such as Mag-Carbon 
Bricks, Refractories for Sliding Gate Systems, Blast Furnace Bricks and Masses etc. 
When asked, the Management indicated that help from Government I SAIL I Financial 
Institution of the following sectors would be very useful in revival of the Company:-
1. Financial: 
(A) Approval of Revival Package consisting, inter-alia:-
(a) Waiver of interest on loans to Government amounting to Rs. 60.36 crores, 
(b) Conversion of 50% of Plan Loan amounting to Rs. 39.76 crores into equity, 
(c) Conversion of Non-Plan loans amounting to Rs. 12.05 crores to '0' rate interest 
of Debentures. 
(B) Sanction of Additional working capital facilities. 
2. New Projects: 
Government to fund Refractories for Continuous Casting Project costing Rs. 2v.00 crores 
approx. This would help the Company to meet growing demand for hi-tech refractories. 
3. Marketing: 
(a) To secure more orders for Mag-Carbon Bricks, Blast Furnace Bricks and Masses. 
(b) SAIL to help in lifting the inventory of finished goods against orders. 
The Sub-Committee takes· note of immense contribution of the Company in growth of 
Steel Sector over the years. It is somewhat unusual that inordinate delay in proceedings of 
BIFR in respect of Companies like BRL leads to demotivation amongst the workers. The Sub­
Committee would urge upon Government of India to expedite the revival of the Company on 
the basis of suggestions put forth by the IDBI. 

119 
Meeting with Bokaro Steel Plant 
The Sub-Committee before visiting Bokaro Steel Plant had received answers from the 
management on a questionnaire prepared by it on the basis of a discusson earlier held with 
the management in November, 1995. Apart from that the management informed the Sub­
Committee about certain problems the plant is facing. It was given to understand that BCCL 
would not be able to supply the required quality of coal for the Coke Oven and Blast 
Furnace. Further elaborating the position of coal, the management informed that the Blast 
Furnace requires more quantity of coal. Similarly lack of coal is also adversely effecting the 
growth in hot metal sector. When enquired as to why the plant is preferring import of coal 
at a much higher price to that coal available from BCCL, the management informed that 
they will import coal only to the extent of its requirement not met by CIL. It was further 
clarified by the management that the heat value of coal is determined by the ash, fixed 
carbon, Volatile Matters and Inherent Moisture. Sulphur has no affect on heat value. 
Though Indian Coal has low sulphur, but due to presence of high ash, the heat value is low 
compared to low ash imported coking coal. 
When the Sub-Committee enquired about the constraints in extending civil works to 
HSCL, the Bokaro Steel Plant Management informed that of late HSCL has been 
transferring the work to some other agencies on contract basis which could be stopped. On 
the question of support to Hindustan Engineering Corporation the management informed 
that Bokaro Steel Plant has extended full support to HEC whenever required by giving 
Advance payment against purchase orders. Sometimes raw materials as well as bought out 
items have been provided for completion of urgent jobs although it is not stipulated in the 
scope of BSL. Apart from this regular orders are being placed by Projects Division, Project 
Modernisation as well as Contract Cell for IPU schemes. Delivery extension has been 
accorded without any financial recovery. 
On the question of future requirement of steel in the country the management informed 
that the perception of BSP about future requirement of steel in the flat product category is 
to be around 16 MT for the domestic market alone out of which HR products will comprise 
a market demand of 8.5 MT and CR products near about 7.5 MT. Considering the futuristic 
d'emand, BSP is considering to expand its capacity to the level of 5.25 MT of liquid steel 
corresponding to saleable steel production of 4.74 MT. 
The Sub-Committee thereafter enquired about the reasons of not reporting 45 theft 
cases of scrap/finished product in the plant since 1987. The management clarified that in 
most of these cases recovery of scrap has been made from outside the perimeter wall of the 
plant. In every such case an enquiry is initially made to find out and fix responsibility. But in 
these cases most of the property is found in an abandoned condition or it is being collected 
by miscreants outside the plant for petty gain. CSF regularly checks the area and wherever 
such collection is found it is brought inside the plant. 
The Sub-Committee thereafter deliberated qn the purchase policy .of the plan~ 
particularly purchase of clothes. The Sub-Committee desired that the management may 
approach National Textiles Corporation for such purchases. The management informed that 
NTC cannot meet the requirement but assured that possibilities would be explored this time. 
On the issue of deputing employees abroad for training, some discriminatory attitude of the 
management was pointed out by the Committee. The management assured that any case if 
brought to its notice would:/ be examined.

The Convenor introduced the members and officers of the Sub-Committee and 
explained in brief the background and the purpose of the visit of the Sub-Committee. 
The District Magistrate, Nalbari welcomed the Committee and introduced the officials 
of his side. Shri P. Dutta, while giving a brief account of the industrial scenario of the 
district informed that Nalbari was originally a part of Kamrup and was carved out of it in 
1984 as a seperate district having a major portion of land producing paddy and jute. He also 
informed that there was a spinning mill which, due to some problem of electricity supply 
etc., remains almost closed. He also informed that there was large scope for establishing 
cane and bamboo industry in the district and SSI Units of tea waste. The district industry 
centre was set up. The area has almost no marketing facilities. Fertilizer consumption is very 
low. Remi cultivation, which is of very high quality, is six times tougher than cotton is 
proposed to be taken up under UNDP assistance. Cultivation of medicinal plants also need 
to be encouraged in the district. The Sub-Committee was also informed that some local 
people of the district were paying irrigation tax to Bhutan. However, there was no record as 
to who was collecting the said tax and there was no way to check it. It was also informed 
that due to ·lack of transport facilities, good roads, means of communications and erratic 
supply of power, the expansion of the present industrial un,its and the establishment of new 
SSI Units was suffering. 
The convenor suggested the officials that they undertake a techno-economic survey of 
the area regarding marketing so that concrete measures for industrial development of the

district may be taken up. One of the members suggested that since coconuts were grown in 
the district in plenty these could be sold outside of the district as well. He also suggested 
that the farmers should be encouraged to increase the yield of coconuts.

a brief account of the circumstances which prevailed in the city before its development and 
how the city reached its present state of development. 
Shri P. Dutta informed the Committee that Bongaigaon was carved out as a seperate 
district in 1989. Agriculture is the main stay of the people. There is large scope of 
employment in the district. Though BPRL is a huge industrial unit but not many ancillary 
units have been developed there. There is a large scope for developing spinning and yarn 
units. 
Among the main hurdles in the way of industrialisation was the poor condition of 
infrastructure particularly roads and power supply. The Committee itself experienced the 
first hand knowledge of almost unmotorable roads while travelling from Guwahati to 
Bongaigaon which is a national highway. Another proble~ was that of banking facilities. 
There are very few branches of banks in the district resulting in lack of banking facilities 
which affects industrial development adversely. On the power front it was informed that 
there was a Thermal Power, Unit of 60 MW capacity but due to the shortage of wagons 
resulting in short supply of coal the project is not working to its full capacity. The record of 
the central organisations functioning in the district is also not upto the mark. ' 
Regarding benefits under the PMRY, the Committee was informed that the banks

working in the district were not showing any expansion of banking facilities due to fear of

one company to BIFR as has been done in the case of Hindustan Fertilizers and Hindustan 
Paper Mill. Profit making units are made to suffer at the cost of loss making units.

and Meghalaya and the river Bramputra on the other. The district has for the present no rail

with other parts of the State. There is -Hb major industry in the district worth mentioning. It 
has been declared no industry district. The Committee was also informed that the industrial 
backwardness of the district was due to inadequate financial assistance, lack of power supply, 
banking fucilities, good means of transport, means of tele-communications etc. The 
Committee was also informed that the district had a large scope for industries relating sheetal 
patti, herbal based medicines, food processing banana chips, granite etc. The boulder supplied 
to Bangladesh had also scope for industrial development in the district. 
The Committee was further informed that in the district there were many "one man bank 
branches" which were unable to take any decision. Therefore all cases were referred to higher 
authorities for taking decisions which resulted in undue delays. 
Regarding PMRY, the Committee was informed that banks were reluctant and resort to 
delays in sanctioning and making payment of loans sanctioned by concerned authoritie6 by 
referring these cases to regional offices.

WEDNESDAY, THE 13TH SEPTEMBER, 1995 
I 
The Sub-Committee proceeded to Jagiroad and visited the Hindustan Paper Corporation 
Limited at 11.15 A.M. The Sub-Committee saw the working of various units of the plant 
there. The Committee had informal discussion with some of the officials namely Shri S.M. 
Rahman, Executive Director, Shri P.S. Nirwan, ACT, G.M. (Works) and Shri Binod Bihari, 
Deputy General Manager (P & A). The Committee was informed that Hindustan Paper 
Corporation Limited was a Public Sector Undertaking of the Government of India and was 
engaged in the manufacture of superior quality writing and printing paper. The Committee 
was also informed that though bamboo was available locally but the other raw materials like 
lime, alum, coal, china clay, soap stone, powder, salt cake rasine and other dyes and 
chemicals were not available locally. These item were not manufactured in the State and the 
Mill had to depend on other States producing these chemicals. This increases the production 
cost of the paper. The Committee was also informed that the operations of the Unit were 
not viable without financial restructuring and the Unit was not in a position to bear the 
interest burden of government loans due to poor performance in the past few years. It was 
demanded that an interest of Rs. 290.16 crores and penal interest of Rs. 186.80 crores of 
Government of India should be written-off.

REPRESENTATIVE OF MINISTRY OF INDUSTRY, CENTRAL GOVERNMENT 
Shri V.K. Mutreja, Director 
At the outset the Convenor explained the purpose of Committee's visit to the area and 
asked about the difficulties they had in the development and what help they expected from 
the Government of India, Ministry of Industry. 
It was informed to the Committee that although tea and oil industries were about 150 
and 100 years old respectively in the State but _they were still backward. The main reason for 
industrial backwardness was that the concerned Ministries of the Central Government had 
not been providing any backup assistance to supplement the State Government's efforts. The 
Central Government should reformulate its capital subsidy scheme. Export Promotion 
Scheme was told to be adverse to the State's interests. It was demanded that transport 
subsidy scheme should continue for some more time and disbUrsement under the scheme 
should be direct to the industrial units instead·iof through the State Government. Out of the 
four spinning mills in the State, two were closed due to problem of working capital. After 
the securities scam banks were over cautious in lending. Debt-Equity ratio was also adverse 
to the interests of the State. 
It was demanded that norms of financing should be liberalised. Special concession be 
given to the State. Tax benefits for new industries for ten years and deffered policy of 
payment of excise duty was also demanded. 
It was also mentioned to the Committee that a proposal of Gas Cracker Project was 

131 
submitted to the Central Government in 1984 and a letter of intent was issued in 1991 but 
no further progress had been made so far. 
Agro-based industries like Jute, food and vegetables processing, cement, granite 
industry have great potentional in the State. 
Trade and industry representatives were of the view that the Gov~rnment policies were 
not implemented in the true spirit. First of all existing industries should be harnessed, then 
only new one will come up. Limit for SSI Units should be increased. Preference to the 
doubling of railway broad gauge line c(>nnecting Delhi with the N.F. should be completed 
and more trains for the North-Eastern Sector were demanded. It was brought to the notice 
of the Committee that financial institutions were reluctant to finance the various projects in 
the State. While the Industrial Policy of 1991-96 talked about single window clearance, but 
in the State, entrepreneurs were made to go from one department to the other for clearance. 
North Eastern Council was not playing its role upto the expectations of the people. All 
tariffs for .power etc., should be rationalised. 
SSI Units were facing three major obstacles: (i) lack of infrastructure; (ii) lack of proper 
policy; and (iii) lack of package of incentives to provide level playing field. The ·condition of 
national highway was poor. 
The State Government of Assam was also lagging behind in comparison to other States in 
industrial policy formulation. The State h~d not developed any. industrial site worth its 
name. Though Assam was an oil producing State but the retail price of petrol in the State is 
higher than its price in Delhi. The level of industrial backwardness of the State can be 
measured from the. fact that even wax produced in Assam was sent from Assam to 
Maharashtra and the processed candles were brought to the State from there for want of any 
such processing industry in Assam. Ancillary units' development was . very poor. 
The representatives of financial institutions namely NABARD, SIDBI and others 
~nformed the . Committee that their institutions were affording the maximum possible 
financial assistance to the S.S.I units and the entrepreneurs of the N.E. region but still the 
pace of industrial development has not gained full momentum. The representatives of trade 
and industry pleaded very strongly for:-
(1) continuation of transport subsidy because the State can noi be at par with other 
developed States. 
(il) re-introduction of freight equalisation scheme in the N.E . .rqi a.w 
(in) restoration of capital investment subsidy by Central Government in addition to the 
State subsidy, as a special case. 
(iv) excise duty exemption on items produced for mass consumption. 
( v) giving preference to local industries in purchases by Central Government. 
( v1) opening and development of waterways. 
( vi1) setting up of entrepreneurial development institute on a permanent basis. 
( vii1) increasing the air services to the towns of N .E. region. 
(ix) various types of concession in dealing with banking and financial institutions. 
The meeting then adjourned at 2.35 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

REPRESENTATIVE OF THE MINISTRY OF INDUSTRY, CENTRAL GOVERNMENT 
Shri V.K. Mutreja, Director 
· The Committee was informed that there was shortage of infrastructural build-up in the 
State due to which there was industrial backwardness. Industrial growth centres meant for 
industrial development were also suffering due to the lack of ancillary industries. The 
Committee was apprised that the infrastructural build-up was promotional and ·· not 
commercial. The State Government was willing to contribute its ,share in the industrial 
development but the -State had not been receiving any feed . back from the Central 
Government. 
Finance was the most important requirement for industrial growth. There existed the 
North-Eastern Development Bank. However, financial provisions for the priority sector of 
industry were required but all efforts made so far were not meaningful. More financial 
concessions was required to be given to industries in the North-Eastern region. Giving an 
example, it was stated that a comprehensive proposal for the industrial development of the 
region had been pending with the Central Government for the last about more than eight 
months. The grant component which was important for the sustenance of industries had 
132 

133 
been stopped by NABARD. The Credit Deposit ratio was also very low in the case of 
Meghalaya. There was large scope for development of wasteland which could be utilised for 
setting up industries based on mushroom, floriculture, banana, sericulture etc. 
There was also need for setting up another university to conduct research on medicinal 
herbal plantations available in the State. 
In the State of Meghalaya there was large potential for· development in adventure 
sports, golf course, hotel and amusemeut parks, development of tourism, air, water and 
surface transport. Good quality coal was available here. There ·was potential for setting-up 
lime-stone, granite and agro-based industries .• But in the absence of financial package these 
were not being made use of on a large scale and consequently the State was lagging behind 
in industrial development. 
The Power scenario of the State was, however, stated to be of a satisfactory level as the 
State was generating a total hydel power of 185 M. W. against its requirement of about 75 
M. W. The excess of the power generated was sold to the neighbouring States. Still the State 
had potential for a 25 M. W. hydel power project. If some big industrial units were set up in 
the State, it could cope with the power requirement of the units and the State would 
consume the entire power generated. 
It was pleaded before the Committee that for the rapid industrialisation of the State an 
airport, some good hotels, adequate publicity for the region and navigati~nal aids were very 
necessary. 
The representative of the Ministry of Industry of the Central Government enlightened 
the Committee that the Central Government were certainly proposing to make some changes 
in the industrial policy specially for the North-Eastern region. 
The meeting then adjourned at 9.20 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

·· Shri V.K. Mutreja, Director, Ministry of Industry. 
The Committee was informed that in Jowai there was good production (.\ oranges, 
tejpatta, ginger and some other herbal medicinal plants. The oranges grown in Jowai were of 
very good quality and taste. A part of the produce of this item was being exported to 
Bangladesh. But for want of adequate infrastructure and finance full advantage of the 
potential of these products was not being taken. The Committee was also informed that the 
district as a whole was a hilly region with difficult terrain and, hence, the scope of 
development of other sources like rail or waterways was extremely limited. Roads form the 
vital means of communication in the entir.e district. Thus the . district lacks in adequate 
transportation facilities. The only industry in the district worth the name was a cement 
factory called Jaintia Cement Limited. This cement project had finally cost Rs. 6.5 crores 
instead of Rs. 4.5 crores due to late receipt of· financial assi.stance and due to want of 
adequate transport facilities the ancillary inputs brought from outside cost very high. 
Therefore, the finished product was also expensive. There was also no organised market in 
134 

135 
the district. The Committee was further informed that there was complete lack of industrial 
culture in Jowai as a whole .. The industries were not receiving full financial support from the 
Government of India. The Committee was further informed that generally people of the 
district were engaged in trade of coal, horticultural produce etc. They were also earning 
foreign exchange by exporting coal, orange, ginger, tejpatta etc. to Bangladesh through 
custom check post Dawki. People of the ·district were enterprising but ·for want of 
infrastructural facilities the industrial development was lacking. There was dearth of banking 
facilities in the district. 
Transport subsidy scheme of the Central Government was still unknown in the district. " 
Though the district contributes lakhs of rupees in the form of excise to the exchequer, but 
even that amount was not ploughed back to the district. The rate of excise duty should be 
decreased. 
One very important problem with which this remote district was grappling was that the 
agmark offices were situated in far-off places like Guwahati and Calcutta. This made very 
difficult for the small entrepreneurs to avail themselves of the facilities. 
About Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana, the Committee was very happy to note that all 
the cases earmarked for the district and recommended by the concerned authorities had 
been sanctioned loans with full disbursement. The banks accepted the fact that the recovery 
rate under the scheme was satisfactory. Some beneficiaries under the Y ojana al~o informed 
the Committee that there was potential for expanding the turmeric powder industry as there 
was a good crop of best quality turmeric in the district. The Committee was also apprised 
that saw industry had ~ good ·future ·in the district. 
The Committee was satisfied to know that there was very good co-ordination between 
the bankers and the Small Scale Industrial Units and the district administration. 
The district adminisp-ation strongly pleaded before the Committee for introduci­
capital subsidy to off-'Set the high cost of projects in order to promote industries in the 
district and for extending further the transport subsidy on raw materials as well as on 
fmished products to the region for promoting industrial development of the area. 
The meeting then adjourned at 1.00 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

The Committee was informed that Darjeeling was famous fo,r 3 Ts i.e. tea, timber and 
tourism. Out of these three, timber had already gone. Tea industry was in a critical 
condition and tourism had not been developed to its full extent. Development of Darjeeling 
was only the same which was done by the British before independence. The tea industry had 
remained and would remain the main avenue and prospect for concentrated investment. 
136 

137 
Although between 9 to 12 million kgs. of tea was produced per year, it account for hardly 
1.5 per cent of the total production and its share in exports was nearly 30% of the total 
produce of tea. To enable the tea industry in the hill areas to retain its primacy and cater to 
the niche market as well as to stay environment friendly, a major criterion for export of tea, 
it was necessary for the industry to continue to make investments. Investments were 
required on the plant and machinery to achieve higher process efficiency, energy efficiency 
und also in the plantation sector to improve labour productivity as the labour cost 
component was high and finally to steam the tide of deterioration in the conditions. This 
need must be recognised by all concerned. 
Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council had identified few sectors such as · diversified 
commercial crops, educational services, tourism products and services, animal resource 
development produce etc. which were capable of quickly meeting development objectives as 
well as generate surpluses. It was necessary to consider these while framing any policies for 
industrial development in these areas by the Go'f'~rnment. This included measures for 
integrated fodder development plan, integrated energy management with improvements in 
the availability of L.P.G./natural gas and guidelines for entrepreneurs for providing 
educational services and training and orientation of the operating personnel. 
It was also contended before the Committee that the special gauge railway line which 
was laid in the 19th century and no improvement had been made in it since then. 
The toy train running on that line was in the red. The Committee had the opportunity 
of seeing the poor condition of roads leading to Darjeeling from various points. Though 
power produ~tion in the district was sufficient but there are problem of distribution. There 
was no air link with the district due to climatic conditions and hilly track. 
Darjeeling Gorkha Hill Council was established in 1988 for preparation of an integrated 
development plan for the district. But not much had been achieved in this regard. It was 
contended that jurisdiction of DGHC did not extend to the whole of the district. This results 
in overlapping of the jurisdiction of the district administration on the one hand and DGHC 
on the other. 
lt was also suggested before the Committee thpt the district required an industrial 
development plan different from other requirements.
1
The need of the hour was to encourage 
growth inducing investment. This would result in the upgradation of industry. 
The district has large potentional for handicrafts and cottage industry. Agro-herbal 
industry, electronic industry and other related industries which require clean environment. 
Another area which had remained unexplored for which district had a unique position 
was education. Similarly situated hill stations had a number of reputed school in the country 
but no s<.~hool of repute had come up in the district so far. 
It was alleged that the district had been given a step motherly treatment both by the 
Central and the State Governments, though it was vital from the defence point of view. The 
district had acute shortage of drinking water and L.P.G. For promotion of tourism no 
proper publicity had been given so far. People of the district were ignorant about the various 
concessions and facilities provided by the Government for starting new ventures. 
Enterpreneurs present in the meeting were ignorant about the transport subsidy scheme. 
Office of the D.l.C. had only. one Mam~gcr though there were seven vacancies. 
Though Darjeeling had a land problem us 70% of the land is locked up in inalienable 
resources yet some areas should be earmarked for industrial units. 
Implementation of the PMR Y was also very tardy and the blame was put on the banks. 

138 
The only hope for the development of Darjeeling was development of tea industry. For 
promotion of Darjeeling tea which is known for its uniqueness all over the world, there was 
need to give· wid~ publicity in interna·donal market. As cost of production of tea had gone 
up over the years, some integrated state of the art research institute was must to gain firm 
footing in the international markeL The development of New Jalpaiguri as a dry port, was 
also demanded. 
A strong demand was made before the Committee for a special treatment in the form of 
special financial incentive/package for industrial development of the hill district. 
The meeting then adjourned at 1.30 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

Parliament of India was thanked through the Committee for including Nepali language 
in the Eighth Schedule to the Constitution of India. 
The Committee was informed that the tea labour was thankful to the English people for 
establishing tea gardens and tea industries in the district which were profit making industries. 
However, after independence, the position had changed and about 15 tea gardens had closed 
down. The industry came into the hands of private parties who were making huge profits 
from the industry by exploiting the tea plantation workers. The Committee was informed 
that the labourers working in the tea gardens were paid a meagre wage of Rs. 24.50 per day. 
A wage of Rs. 50/- per day was demanded. The Management of the tea gardens did not 
provide any basic necessities like residential houses etc. People were not paid benefits of 
service after their retirement. 
The Committee was informed that in the tea gardens owned by private persons bonus 
was paid to the workers whereas no bonus was paid to workers of tea gardens owned by the 
Government. This was creating dissatisfaction and. heart burning among workers of tea 
gardens. Temporary workers had not been confirmed and thus the fate of the workers in tea 
gardens had become worst. 
lt was pleased before the Committee by the plantation workers that the Labour Ministry 
139 

140 
of the Central Government may be impressed upon to do something positive to increase the 
wages of tea garden labour and to ensure timely payment of wages to solve their problems. 
If need be the labour Jaws might be suitably amended/changed so that the workers of tea 
gardens might also feel satisfied. 
There was large scope for establishing/expanding industries in the field of seri-culture, 
herbal medicinal plants, mushroom,· soap making from chi a seeds, umbrella making, food 
processing, paper making tannery and broom industry. 
In the field of tourism there were no infrastructural facilities and this sector was 
required to be improved. The representatives demanded that a package of incentives and 
financial assistance should be provided in the region like any other State, otherwise the 
future of the tea gardens and their workers would be bleak. 
The Cinchona plantation was started in 1867 and so far with the increase in the 
plantation of Cinchona the labour force had also increased manifold. This industry had great 
potential for its development in the district and needs to be encouraged. 
A strong appeal was made to the Committee to do something positive for the 
betterment of the poor workers of tea gardens. 
The meeting then adjourned at 5.45 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.
