The C.M.D. of Andrew and Yule Company Limited informed the Committee that they had 
four tea gurdens located in the interior. The Company was setting up some new projects in the 
field of mushroom and flori-culture with the advent of new technology. They were also starting 
export oriented projects with foreign collaboration doing business for a long time with &tbe 
Company. The Company was one of the oldest in India established 150 years ago. Earlier they 
had 57 companies with a total of 87,000 employees. With the nationalisation of coal industry by 
the Government of India the business of this group· was considerably curbed. All companies 
except 16 were disposed of by 1974. In the year 1979 the main company became a Government 
company with 51 °/.> shareholding except 14 tea garder:ts, five in West Bengal and 9 in Assam. All 
tea gardens were doing well and the tea was suitable for consumption. After the break-down of 
USSR, the Company had some difficult time but now it had improved its position. The 
assistance given by the IDBI was not of much help due to the slow pace. The Committee was 
also informed about their problems like its situation in the much interior from the main road, 
bad condition of roads, up-rooting of some tea bushes, long gestation period of tea plants, the 
formation of the hill council, scarcity of water, fluctuation in voltage, no further expansion of 
tea gardens etc. Darjecling tea had great future as such superior quality tea was not found any 
where in the world. It was anticipated that if the present conditions were permitted to prevail 
there was likelihood of these tea gardens being damaged/finished in the next 20 years. It was 
pleaded before the Sub-Committee that it was high time for the Government to review the 
'-., 
situation and make a pragmatic policy so that tea industry could be expanded and continued for 
years to come. The Central Government's investment was very much required in industries like 
tea, tourism, mini-hydel projects and remodeling of the system of subsidy. 
141 

142 
It was contended before the Sub-Committee that tea bushes were not considered as 
plants. Andrew Yule Limited would like to expand its tea gardening if some forest land was 
given to them as it would have the same ecological effect as the forests leave. 
Grants given by the. Tea Board for uproorting and re-plantation of tea bushes was 
utterly meagre. The Government should make more pregmatic and realistic policy in this 
regard. 
Agro-based fudustry, particularly floriculture required regular and fast transportation 
but some flights of the Indian Airlines fr6m Bagdogra had been cancelled/withdrawn 
without giving the routes to any private airlines. Consequently these products could not be 
transported quickly. Time factor was very vital for the survival and growth of such 
industries. 
The representatives of the Company strongly pleaded before the Sub-Committee for 
increasing some flights from Bagdogra airport and also to increase the frequency of some 
flights which are already in operation. They also pleaded for the speedy infrastructural 
development which in tum will improve the industrial fate of the region as a whole and the 
district in particular.

REPRESENTATIVES OF GOVERNMENT OF SIKKIM

Shri Bhutia, Secretary, Industries expressed his profound sorrow for not being able to 
welcome the Sub-Committee at Gangtok as the road from Siliguri to Gangtok was not in 
good condition and the alternative road would have taken long time to reach Gangtok. For 
that very reason Shri Bhutia was requested to appear before the Sub-Committee at 
Darjeeling. 
He explained to tlie Committee that Sikkim had joined the Union of India not long ago. 
The State was facing some teething problems in the way of industrialisation of the State. 
The Committee was informed that in view of the peculiar geographical location and 
typographical features, the Himalayan State of Sikkim suffered from constraints of lack of 
infrastructural facilities, particularly in respect of transport communications and socio­
economic resources, limiting the scope for vigorous industrialisation in a big way, thereby 
resulting in the backwardness of the State. However, despite limitations, the welfare 
conscious Government of Sikkim had been able to make substantial progress in the 
industrial sector towards economic upliftment of the people in the State. 
The new policy of economic liberalisation, deregulation, decontrol and delicensing 
alongwith its emphasis on market economy might be correct remedy to stimulate the 
country's economy in general. But these very measures might prove to be death blow to the 
economic development and industrialisation of backward State like Sikkim which suffered 
from locational disadvantages, under-developed infrastructure, lack of natural resources, 
technical skills etc. To avoid such a situation and for ensuring development of such regions, 
it was imperative that the Central Government must take very active part in finding out 
effective ways and means to bring about economic development and foster industrialisation 
so that employment opportunities could be generated. 
143 

-
144 
The Committee was further informed that there was not a single Central Public Sector 
Undertaking in Sikkim. The State Government had been persuading the Central 
Government to set up some of its Public Undertakings in the State which somehow had not 
materialised. The pollution free atmosphere, excellent labour relations and disciplined work 
force prevalent in the State offered an excellent scope for establishment of electronic and hi­
tech industries. The Central Government should consider setting up some of its Public 
Sector Undertakings · in Sikkim and reserve licences for setting up certain hi-tech and 
electronic industries in the State to provide employment opportunities and to foster the 
economic development of the State simultaneously. For this purpose a separate industrial 
policy fCir hill areas like Sikkim and other North-Eastern States was required to be 
formulate<! by Government of India providing special package of incentive etc. 
The Central Government subsidy scheme had been vital in promoting industries in 
backward and hilly States like Sikkim. The discontinuation of the scheme bad very adverse 
effect on tile entire industrialisation process. In order to bring about industrial development 
in hilly and 'no industry districts' the Central Government invest,uent subsidy scheme should 
be restored immediately·. 
The reimbursement claims preferred by the State Government on account of Central 
transport subsidy were inordinately delayed and at times, the reimbursement was not 
effected even after a lapse of about two years. Such an inordinate delay in reimbursement 
had caused great hardships to the industrial units and the viability of the Units was adversely 
affected. Government of India should make reimbursements of Central transport subsidy 
within . 45 days from the date of preferring claims; 
The interest rate on the borrowings from financial institutions was high due to which the 
entrepreneurs were shying away and the prospects of setting up new industries were negated. 
In order to ensure that such a negative trend did not continu.e indefmitely, if was of 
immediate necessity to effect downward revision of the rates on the borrowings from 
financial institutions and in any case, the lending rates should not be more than 12% in 
backward and hilly areas. 
The Central Government should also entirely fund the industrial growth centres 
sanctioned for hilly and backward States like Sikkim where the investment in no case would 
exceed Rs. 10 crores as compared to growth centres in other States where the investment 
was between 40-50 crores. 
The presence of central organisations like ISSI, All India Handicrafts Board etc., was 
not satisfactory as their functioning was not very smooth as they always lacked proper man­
power etc. 
Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana scheme had also been imp~emented in Sikkim but due 
to lack of support from the commercial banks the targeted results had not been achieved. 
During the last financial year the target under the scheme was only 250. Against this target, 
ihe banks had sanctioned loans to 75 beneficiaries only thereby achieving 30 per cent of the 
target. 
The State had sufficient power to meet its demand but because of lack of proper bank 
facilities SSI units were not able to grow at the desired rate. 
The meeting then adjourned at 7.05 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

development, agriculture and irrigation had been most backward in the State of Tripura.

whatsoever, could not be made use of. Small Scale Industrial units were lying closed in the 
State. There are about six lakhs tribal people. There are Bangladeshi refugees also for whom 
there is no job or work. There are ·some traditional weavers in the State. No foreign 
investment has been made in the State.· Conces&ions, financial assistance and investment is 
required to be made on priority basis to develop the industrial activities in the State. MeanF 
of communications are also needed to be ilpproved.

After introduction from both the sides the Convenor made two customary 
announcements regarding confidentiality of the proceedings and that the proceedings of the 
meeting be sent to its Secretariat within ten days. 
145 

146 
The representatives of F ACSI informed the Sub-Committee that North-Eastern region 
was a victim of the step motherly and colonial attitude by the Central Government. The 
continuous economic backwardness had affected social and political conditions of these 
States leading to a situation threatening the integrity and security of the country. Massive 
improvement of their economic condition can only help to change the situation. 
The economic found~tion of the State of Tripura is weak. Financial institutions are rigid 
,~ in giving assistance. There is no branch of RBI. A branch of RBI is very necessary in the 
State. The infrastructure facilities are poor. Railway line has· its token presence at one 
corner of the State. Moreover narrow gauge .rail track from Lumding to Kumarghat is too 
congested and burden with carrying essential commodities. Tnmshipment facilities for goods 
from broad gauge to narrow gauge line at Lumding are inadequate. There is no storage 
arrangement. Railway wagons are not available. The movement of raw-material by rail for 
industry is almost nil. National Highway 44 which is considered to be the life line for the 
State is not as per the specification laid d~wn for National Highway. Travelling by air which 
is the easiest means of communication for the people of this land locked State with the, rest 
of the country has become costilier with the hike in air fares over the last few years. The 
Committee was also informed that Hilsa fish is found in abundance in the State which can be 
sent out of the State. There should be an adequate infrastructure arrangement for 
international trade specially with adjoining 5angladesh and a corridor to go to Calcutta via 
Bangladesh (350 kms.) which, otherwise is 1600 kms. 
On a query by the Convenor the Committee was informed that the power sector had 
bright prospects in the near future but the system of transmission and distribution needed 
improvement. Mineral resources were totally absent in the State. Availability of essential 
raw-material and fuel are difficult. Markets was limited. Capital was scarce. Over and above 
in the absence of industrial culture few entrepreneurs dared to come forward for setting up 
industries in the State. 
The entire economy of the State was based on agricuiture. Cultivable land was limited. 
System of agriculture had not been modernised as a result of which a large number of 
agriculture labourers were either unemployed or underemployed. Industri8lisation provide 
alternative scope for employment to the large section of unemployed labour force. There 
was oo foreign post office in the State. Fax messages are not transmitted due to absence of 
facilities therefor. The State Government did not have adequate means to provide basic 
infrastructure required to build up industry in the State. The Central Government, 
therefore, had to come forward with a 'Crash Programme', determined attitude and finn 
•·· industrial policy frame keeping in view the inherent difficult situation of the North-Eastern 
region. The representatives also informed the Sub-Committee that they were paying all taxes 
like other States but were not getting the facilities like them. The suggestion by one of the 
~ Members of the Sub:-Committee that the· use of available gas and floriculture could .be made 
use of in the State was welcomed. 
The representatives strongly pleaded that :-
(i) Four industrial estates should be built up in all the four districts of the State with 
100% Central aid. 
(ii) Rail line should be extended upto A~artala and the narrow gauge line from Lumding 
to Kumarghat should be conver.ted into broad gauge. 
(iii) The National Highway 44 should be improved to the standard of a National 
Highway. 
(iv) Central assistance should be extended for generation of power and for improvement 
of transmission and distribution system. 

147 
(v) The Central investment subsidy which was discontinued in the name of 'Growth 
Centre Scheme' should be re-introduced for the North-Eastern States. 
{vi) Central transport subsidy should be extended for another ten years. 
(vii) The advertisements on All India Radio and Doordarshan for the products of the 
State should be subsidised. 
(viii) Gas should be supplied at subsidised rates considering the extreme backwardness of 
the State. 
(ix) Commercial financial institutions should be brought under a policy making it 
obligatory for them to invest a part of their capital in industrial sector and lower 
interest rates charged. 
(:c) Tripura should be exempted from payment of all taxes for a period of at least ten 
years. 
(xi) A full fledged branch office of Ministry of Agricult}lre for agro-based and food. 
processing industry should be set up in the State. 
(xii) Central Government should financially assist the Government of Tripura for reviving 
the Tripura Jute Mill which was the first venture of the State Government in the field 
of medium industry. 
(xiii) Central institutions like NSIC, NEIIDB, RTC etc. should open their offices at 
Agartala. 
(xiv) Private air-lines should be permitted to operate flights to and fro Agartala airport. 
(xv) The.SISI Directorate should function properly and a testing laboratory should also be 
set up in Agartala. 
The Members of the Sub-Committee asked certain questions which were replied to by 
the representatives. 
The meeting then adjourned at 4.50 P.M. ·with a mutual vote of thanks.

After introduction the Convenor, at the very outset, made two customary 
announcements regarding the confidentiality of the proceedings of the meeting and that a 
copy of the proceedings be sent to the Secretariat within ten days. The Convenor also 
explained in brief the scope and purpose of the visit of the. Sub-Committee and requested 
the Dy. General manager of the United Bank of India to give in brief, the picture about the 
working of the Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana in the State of Tripura. 
The Deputy General Manager informed the Sub-Com_mittee that Tripura was small 
148 

149 
State in the North-Eastern part of the country. There was no big industry due to financial 
and infrastructure constraints. At present there were two lakh unemployed youth registered 
in the employment exchanges. The problem of the unemployment could be solved only by 
generating productive employment in the private sector and reducing dependence on 
Government for jobs. Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana essentially was an important and 
ambitious scheme to promote and create sustainable employment opportunities for the 
educated unemployed youth. During the year 1993-94, Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana was 
implemented in the State of Tripura in urban areas only. From 1994-95 onwards, the scheme 
was implemented to cover both the rural and urban areas of the State in accordance with the 
guidelines of the Central Government. This is basically a credit linked programme and is 
being implemented by commercial banks through branches in Tripura. Under the scheme 
Government provides a subsidy of Rs. 7500'- per beneficiary as against five per cent by the 
beneficiary. 
In the State of Tripura as on 30.6.95, 218 branches of commercial bank, RRBs, State 
Coop. Bank, Tripura Coop. Land Development Bank and Tripura Urban Coop. Bank were 
functioning. The UBI was the lead bank for the State. The Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana 
was being implemented by 391 branches of commercial banks spread all ove,r the State. 
Coming to the practical working under the Yojana the bank man~gemc;n! infprmed the 
Sub-Committee that the scheme was first implemented in 1993-94. ·Certain clarifications 
therefore, were required regarding income criteria etc. ofbeneficiaries. A target of fOO was 
set for 1993-94. Disbursements were however, could be made to 103 beneficiaries only due 
to want of clarifications on the aforesaid doubt. Most of the proposals I projects submitted 
by prospective beneficiaries were of stereotype and most of the cases could not pass the test 
of viability and were rejected by the banks' branches. The average size of disbursement 
during the year 1993-94 was around 0.67 lakhs. For the year 1994-95, the target was fixed at 
1000 cases. Out of that figure only 796 cases were sanctioned by branches of commercial 
banks and money could be disbursed only in 388 cases. 
The Committee was also informed that 451 cases were rejected/referred back/returned 
to the Task Force for the reasons that (i) the proposals were beyond the target fixed 
therefore, and returned to the DICs, for re-sponsoring, (ii) change/modification of the 
scheme required by the entrepreneurs, (iii) entrepreneurs were not interested in availing 
themselves of the ·loan under the scheme. 
For the year 1995-96 the target has been fixed at 1300 cases. But so far, out of that 
figure only 494 cases had been sponsored mainly during September, 1995. There were 
restriction in sponsoring new cases owing to elections to Tripura Autonomous District 
Council. It is hoped that involvement of the elected representatives of the Panchayati Raj 
institutions would expedite sponsoring of applications. 
The Committee was further informed that the lead bank has been working with the 
State Government very well. The tribal girls were being trained as entrepreneurs under the 
scheme. The Convenor enquired as to what sort to industries were viable in· the State. The 
Management replied that there was good potential for gas based, agro-based and timber 
based industries. It was also informed that the State had natural resources in the form of 
rubber, the production of which was fairly good as compared to Kerala. Rubber-based 
industry had also a good f~ture in the State for which intermediate processing industries 
were essential. On a further query by the Convenor as to what were the constraints coming 
in the way of industrialisation in the State, it was informed that transport/communication 
was a major hurdle. Calcutta by road from Agartala was 1600 Kms whereas through 
Bangladesh it was only 350 Kms. Once Government permitted trade with Bangladesh then, 

150 
of course, some big industries would come up in the field and industrial development of the 
State was bound to progress. The Committee was also informed that the C.D. ratio was six 
per cent for rural branches and that this ratio was falling everywhere. It would increase only 
when big industries were set up. 
It w.as strongly argued before the Sub-Committee that for successful working of the 
scheme it was essential that:-
(i) Quarterly phasiflg. of sponsoring of proposals should be adopoted to avoid bunching 
of proposals· at the fag end of the financial year. 
(ii) The concerned Government departments should expeditiously release the licences, 
SSI registration, electriCity connections etc. in favour of the beneficiaries. 
(iii) More training institutions should be identified to quicken the process .of completion 
of training programme required for expeditious disbursement. 
(iv) Adequate discretionary powers were granted to the Managers of certain commercial 
banks who did not have the power to grant advances. Therefore, they had to refer 
the proposals to their controlling officers for sanction, resulting in delays~ 
(v) The scope of involving of Tlipura.Gramin Bank in the implementation of PMRY 
especially in areas where there was .no commercial bank branch or the commercial 
bank branches were situated ·far away. 
(vi) The maximum age limit of 35 years should be relaxed to 40 years and the annual 
income limit be enhanced to Rs. 50,0001-: 
The Convenor observed that for further successful working of the scheme modalities of 
operations may be decided by discussions between the banks and tpe Government and any 
problems coming in the way of implementing PMRY be sorted out expeditiously. 
The meeting then adjourned at 5.35 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

The Convenor made two customary announcements regarding confidentiality of the 
proceedings and that the proceedings should be sent to the Secretariat within ten days. He 
also explained in brief the scope/purpose of the Sub-Committee. 
The Chief Secretary while giving a brief scenario of the State informed the Sub­
Committee that about 60% of Tripura's terrain was undulating. About 53% of the land was 
under forest cover. The State is served by a large number of streams and rivers which are 
not suitable for inland water transport. It has abundant reserves of sub-soil water and the 
several parts of the State artesian wells are frequent. Tripura's population is 27.6 lakhs. The 
rural population is about 85% and 74% of the people live below poverty line. 85% of the 
border of Tripura is international. About 50,000 tribal refugees from Bangladesh are 
sheltered in South Tripura district. The State has three Industrial Training Institutes. 
Roughly 13% of the cropped area is irrigated. Limited availability of irrigation has restricted 
cropping intensity of the major crops of rice, wheat, maize, oilseeds, pulses etc. Fruits like 
jackfruit, coconut, lichi, pineapple, mango etc. grow in the State. ·~ 
The Committee was also informed that the State was industrially backward mainly 
because of geographical isolation, lack of infrastructure, non-availability of raw material and 
associated problems. The State as a whole had been declared 'No Industry District' under 
Category 'A'. The State had been assigned special status for the purpose of rapid 
industrialisation. The industrial units set up in the State were in small scale and rural 
industries sector, except for Tripura Jute Mills Ltd., which was State PSU. The total Small 
Scale Industries registered upto 30.9.95 with the District Industries Centres were as:-
SIDO Units : Provisional 5737 and permanent 1524. 
Non-SIDO Units : Provisional 2564 and permanent 386. 
Out of 1910 units many had turned sick or closed down. The functional units were 950 
i.e. 49%. The investment involved in these units was about Rs. 1838.02 lakhs and their 
combined annual turnover was Rs. 1590.27 Jakhs. These employed about 11 ,000 workers. 
The sickness in the small industries sector was due to raw material non-availability 
problems and higher costs leading to non-competitiveness of local credit and delayed or 
inadequate finance from commercial institutions and lack of assured power supply. 
Some of the salient features of the Small Scale Industrial scenario in the State of 
Tripura were stated to be: 
(i) nearly all the units were established by the first generation entrepreneurs. 
(ii) their product range was also by and large conventional and technologically 
simple. 
(iii) the raw material for most of these was transported from qutside the State 
resulting in higher cost of production. 
(iv) the income level and purchasing power of major portion of the local population 
was low. Therdore, the local market could not sustain the industrial units. The 
dependence on State Government for marketing support and for departments as 
purchasers was therefore, considerable. 
(v) the units also faced the problems of bringing in qualified manpower and advanced 
technologies because of remoteness and transportation bottlenecks and associated 
costs. 
The Tripura Jute Mills Ltd. which was the first medium scale industrial unit in the State 

---------- ------ -----, 
153 
was set up in 1974 as a PSU with an authorised share capital of Rs. 10 crores. Its installed 
capacity was 40 M.T. of product per day. But this was now a sick unit. The North-Eastern 
Regional Agricultural Marketing Corporation had set up a pineapple processing plant in 
1988 at Nalkata in North Tripura district with an investment of Rs. 3.5 crores. The 
pineapple processing capacity of the plant was 5760 M.T. per annum and the targeted 
working days were 120. However, the maximum capacity utilisation had been of the order of 
10% to 15% only. 
The Committee was further informed that the State had not gained from liberalisation. 
The rail link was necessary for economic and industrial development. National Highway 44 
connecting Tripura to Guwahati and Shillong was not in good condition. The transit passage 
through Bangladesh and trade with it could only bring economic and industrial development 
of the State. Natural resource in the form of rubber could be developed. The production of 
pineapple, mangoes, jackfruit etc. had a great potential for industrial development. Bamboo 
and wood industries too had a good potential. Growth Centre Scheme was required to be 
revised. The State did not have even a single five star hotel. 
On a query by the Convenor about meeting the power requirement the Committee was 
informed that at present 50% of the requirement of power was being met but at the peak 
hours the requirement was not met in full. Replying to a query by the members, it was 
informed that though one offer for power generation project in the private sector was 
received from M/s Gujarat Gas Company Ltd., but the purchase agreement could not be 
entered into. On an enquiry about the functioning of SSI units in the state, the Committee 
was informed that 36,000 SSI units were working in the State but their main problem was 
the non-availability of raw material, non-investment and the non-availability of subsidy 
scheme. 
There was a strong need for easy availability of r~w materials, development of means of 
communications, good connections of rail, air, flow of capital by banks, patronage of 
industries etc. Foreign investment for the development of North-Eastern region was a must. 
The meeting then adjourned at 7.10 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

After introduction the Convenor at the outset made two customary announcements­

Explaining the working of the financial institutions with regard to the Prime Minister's 
Rozgar Yojana · the Chief General Manager SBI informed the Sub-Committee that the 
scheme was introduced in Mizoram during 1993-:-94. In the firs(year the progress was slow. 
Under the scheme the entrepreneurs were first trained and that is why its implementation 
was somewhat delayed. Now, the work had been started in the right earnest. In the year 
1993-94 as against the target of 40 cases only 30 cases were cleared, loan was sanctioned and 
disbursed to them. The remaining 10 cases were rejected not being found viable. No doubt 
in the year 1993-94 the progress was slow but during the year 1994-95 the scheme progressed 
well. In 1994-95 the target was 250 and 305 candidates were sponsored. Out of this 223 cases 
were cleared, money was sanctioned and disbursed to them. The remaining 82 cases were 
under consideration. 
During the year 1995-96 the work under the scheme had gained· further momentum. 
Training of selected candidates for Lunglei and Chhifmuipui. districts organized by the 
industries department of the State Government was completed in the last week of September 
and first week of October, 1995. Disbursement to the beneficiaries in the two districts could 
not be completed for non-completion of training. However, it was expected that by the end 
· of the month free disbursement in all eligible cases would be made. For 1995:-96 the target 
set was 250 cases. So far 145 cases had been sponsored and the selection process was still in 
progress. 
The training of candidates was delayed following very heavy land-slides in Lunglei 
district which completely paralysed normal life in the affected areas for months together. 
Now that the training had bee~. compl~ted and road communication restored, the process of 
disbursement was likely· to" be completed by the end of October, 1995. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed that towards the closing months of the financial 
year there was heavy pressure of work in the. banks and hence the authorities concerned of 
the industries department were requesled to work regularly throughout the year to avoid 
pressure towards the closing months. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that in some cases it was found that the selected 
candidates were not serious in establishing their unit. A few got employed elsewhere and 
could not start the project and, therefore, only serious candidates need be selected. On the 
recovery front the Sub-Committee was informed that the some was poor. Though recovery 
of loan was equally important yet more important was the propef utilisation of the loaned 
amount. This would take some time. 
The Minister of Industries of the State Government informed the Committee that there 
was no agency to impart training to the trainers. The State should, atleast, have one such 
agency of its own. He also informed the Sub;.Committee that in many rural areas there was 
not even a single branch of the bank. However, the Chief General Manager, SBI suggested 
that in areas not being served by any branch of any bank the burden can be put on the 
branches of cooperative banks functioning there. The idea of mobile van bank was also 
suggested. ~ut none of the two were found possible. It was contended before the Sub­
Committee that keeping in view the backwardness and peculiar circumstances prevailing in 
the North-Eastern regio1,1, the· prescri~4 JllaximUin age of 35 ·years should be relaxed to 
40 years and the rate of interest on the loan should be Somewhat lower in comparison to 

156 
other regions I States. The Convenor and Members of the Sub-Committee emphasized the 
need to cover the entire area by the existing network_of banks! branches and if necessary, 
more branches could be .opened. 
It was strongly pleaded before the Sub-Committee to recommend that:-
(i) Attending the work of sponsoring candidates processing/recommending their cases 
for disbursement of loans and sanctioning and disbursing loans should be spread 
throughout the year instead of piling up the cases for clearance I disbursement at 
the fag end of the year; and 
(iz) Keeping in view the extent of industrial backwardness in .the North-Eastern region 
in general and Mizoram in particular, the rate of interest on loans be less and the 
maximum age limit be relaxed from 35 years to 40 years for candidates to be 
eligible to get the benefit of the Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana. 
Thereafter the Sub-Committee heard the views of the representatives of trade and 
industry. The representatives informed the Committee ·that Mizoram was the most 
industrially backward State in the counl!)' and the entire State had been notified as No 
Industry District For all purposes of de~elopmental plan like areas specific infrastructure 
development and specific project proposals in Mizoram a proper techno-economic survey 
had to be conducted by a central team ftrst and a geographical map had to be prepared for 
meaningful healthy growth of indilstries in the State. Further, till date the infrastructure in 
Mizoram was nil. Keeping in view the geographical conditions of the . State with its scattered 
population the infrastructure cannot be developed in all the areas and therefore, an area 
specific development should be identified. The new scheme for growth centre appears to be 
the best means for ~ding up industrial infrastructure in Mizoram. They pleaded that for 
the purpose the plot already acquired should be approved by the Central Government for 
implementation and that another suitable l9C8tion for the growth centre may be identified 
having hinterlands where thousand of families may be given sufficient land for settlement to 
feed such centre with their products. 
It was also pleaded before the Sub-Committee that while making proposal for the 
centrally sponsored scheme of growth centre in hilly terrain of Mizoram with a thinly 
scattered population, certain relaxations suitable for the local conditions should be provided 
for better implementation of the project or certain relaxations to provide! scope for making 
proposals without loan component from financial institutions be given. 
The Committee was informed that the availability of capital was not satisfactory and 
hence the investment climate was not good. Capital was not available for the construction I 
repair of roads and bridges. Power was in short supply. Means of communications were 
lacking. An air field was to be established I expanded. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed that the scheme of Central Investment Subsidy 
was discontinued in 1988 on introduction of the enhanced scheme of industrial growth 
centre. It was really strange that even after lapse of seven long years of introduction of 
scheme for growth centres the State Government of Mizoram had not got even one .such 
centre approved by the Central Government so far. Even if such a centre was approved in 
the near future, its implementation would take yet some more years. The result was that the 
entrepreneurs within the· 'No Industry District' areas of Mizoram had been facing. difficulties 
and the growth of industrial development had further been affected adversely. The problem 
of regional imbalance in the country had worsened due to lagging behind of States like 
Mizoram. It was pleaded before the Sub-Committee that the old scheme of central 
investment subsidy for backward areas should be revived for Mizoram as a special case till 

157 
be Industrial Growth Centre scheme was not implemented for the benefit of the industrial 
:ntrepreneurs in the State. The Committee was also informed that just before the dis­
:ontinuation of the scheme for Central Investment Subsidy during 1988-89, a claim 
mounting to Rs. 70 lakbs was sent by the Government of Mizoram for reimbursement but 
iO far nothing had been reimbursed to the State. It . was also pleaded before the Sub­
:Ommittee that the amount of Central Transport Subsidy be paid direct to the State 
>romptly. 
The Sub-Committee was requested that if need be the scheme might be modified to the 
~xtent possible in relation to its implementation to achieve fast industrial development of the 
~orth-Eastem region in general and the State of Mizoram in particular. 
The meeting then adjourned at 7.45 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

The representatives of the State Government of Mizoram informed the Committee that 
the Mizoram was one of the youngest and industrially most backward States in the country. 
Twenty long years of insurgency till1986; lack of infrastructure, specially in respect of power 
and technical knowhow, poor communication links and dearth of capital investment had all · 
contributed to this backwardness. There was no capital goods industry, no large scale 
industrial unit at all. There was only one industry in medium scale unit which was engaged 
in food processing. This was owned by a public sector undertaking. There were 2700 
registered and about 1500 unregistered or provisioDally registered SSI unit which could more 
appropriately be termed as tiny units. 
The representatives further informed the Committee that Mimram was quite. rich in · 
natural forests. One of these resources was babmoo forests. An estimated 405 lath M. T. of 
bamboo were available. This wealth was a renewable source. There were good potenti81 for 
bamboo based industries like bamboo chipping plant, chopstick, toothpick, agarbatti stick, 
apart from larger plants for paper pulp and paper mills. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed that due to limited potential for cereal 
158 

159 
oduction, Government of Mizoram had been putting more emphasis on horticultural 
ctor. Consequently, production of fruits had been increasing rapidly over the past few 
:ars and food processing offered good potential. The Mizoram Food and Allied Industries 
orporation, a Public Sector Unit, had been mainly engaged in food processing. There were 
few tiny units in the private sector also. In fact food processing had good potential even 
,r export. Passion fruit was grown only in Mizoram iJl the country. Processed juice from 
tssion fruit and also canned bamooo shoot could find a market outside the State and even 
)road. · 
Under the new land use policy of the Government, thous~nds of families had taken to 
ung (aburiates species) plantation in large scale. Mizoram was the only State that could 
~ow Tung till now. Very high cost paint and aviation oil were produced from Tung seeds. It 
as necessary to establish a processing unit urgently within the next two years or so in order 
• offer a market to the cultivators. In this field also the State needed expertise and 
Jidance from outside the State and the assistance of Central Government was required in 
tis regard. 
The handloom and handicraft sector had provided industrial employment to largest 
umber of people. There were approximately 6,200 handlooms, 2430 frame looms and 172 ..... 
andloom cooperative societies with 3925 members. About 12,000 people were directly 
mplo)Gtd in the handloom sector and more in subsidiary activities. The Mizoram Handloom 
nd Handicraft Development Corporation, a P.S.U., and another corporation in the 
:>Operative sector had been mainly providing marketing facilities. The State required 
rgently a reputed export agency to conduct export potential survey and guide the State in 
roduction of required quantity with desired quality. 
Large deposits of shell lime stones have been identified in the State. These mineral 
esources can been used for manufa~uring different sizes of slabs and tiles. Equally 
romising are the natural mineral water springs. But due to lack of resources and expertise, 
~e State has not been able to exploit these resources. 
In the absence of regular and adequate power supply specially in the villages, candle is 
n item of daily use for the people. Quite a number of entrepreneurs in this. tiny sector are 
ngaged in production of candles. However, all these units are under-utilised for want of 
equired quantum of parafin wax. Even the minimum local demand for candles cannot be 
net by them. At present, the State is allotted only 45 M.T. ~f .parafin wax per quarter 
,gainst the minimum requirement of 80 M.T. The Ministry of Petroleum and Natural Gas 
hould be requested to look into the matter and arrange adequate supply of parafin wax. 
lhe industrial growth centre for Mizoram was approved by the Government of India in 
1rinciple. A project report, with a total project cost of Rs. 25.55 ci'ores, was submitted to 
he Central Government during 1992 ·for approval. But the same was still pending. 
~xpeditious approval of the project report and release of funds was necessary for developing 
nfrastructure at the site already selected. The funding pattern of growth centres was also 
·equired to be streamlined and reviewed by the Central Government so that the State 
:Jovernment might. not feel it difficult to raise the required funds. 
The Convenor and the members asked a few clarifications/questions which were replied 
>y the State Government officials. 
The meeting then adjourned at 8.50 P.M. with a mutual vote of thanks.

At the outset the Convenor of the Sub-Committee made two announcements relating to 
confidentiality of the proceedings and that a copy of the proceedings be sent to the 
Secretariat within ten days. He also explained in brief the scope and purpose of the visit of 
the Sub-Committee. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that the Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana was 
implemented in the State .of Nagaland in October, 1993, and all other formalities were 
completed by March, 1994, so it will be too early to comment on its implementation like 
sponsoring of cases, disbursement of loan instalment and recovery thereof. The 
representatives of the banks informed that there was a lot of political pressure and pressure 
of muscle power to sanction loans to all those cases which were sponso{ed by the task force. 
One alternative to avoid this situation was suggested that the task force should sponsor as 
many as have been allotted, though it may be different from the practice adopted in other 
parts of the country. The cases sponsored under the PMR Y are stereotype like bakery, 
grocery etc. A few youths were coming to undertake manufacturing activity. 
In this connection the attention of the Sub-Committee was drawn to the various 
problems faced by the banks in the implementation of the Yojana like delay in sponsoring 
applications by the task force, few applications sponsored from the far flung areas outside 
the area of operations, concentration of few activities without linkage, project reports not 
according to present market activities, hurried/perfunctory scrutiny by the screening 
Committee, project cost uniformity, non-availability of quality assets/insurance facilities 
except at Kohima and Dimapur, borrowers' insistence on cash disbursement etc. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that during the year 1993-94, 161 applications 
were sponsored, 103 were sanctioned and out of that an amount of Rs. 80.84 lakhs was 
disbursed to 101 cases. During 1994-95, 229 cases were sponsored, 216 cases were sanctioned 
and an amount of Rs. 147.06 lakhs was disbursed. The target fixed for the year 1995-96 was 
at 300 cases but - far no case had been sppnsored by the task force. To avoid rush of work 
regarding disbursement of loans at the fag end of the financial year, the representative of the 
Finance Ministry suggested that the whole work should be distributed evenly throughout the 
year. It was alsp suggested that the situation should be monitored from the ·very beginning of 
' 
161 

162 
the year. When the bankers informed the Sub-Committee that they are really willing to 
finance any commercially viable project, the State Government officials stated that 
commercially viable aspect was relative term as a project commercially viable in other parts 
of the country might not be viable in the State due to topographical reasons, lack of 
infrastructure, marketing facilities etc. On a query from the Convenor, the Sub-Committee 
was told that in the State of Nagoland, the CD ratio was' 34.06% as against the national ratio 
of 60%. The Sub-Committee ·observed that the CD ratio of the State was very low. 
II 
The Sut>-Committee thereafter started discussion with the State Government officials of 
Nagaland at 8.00 P.M. ,., 
At the fustance of the Convenor, the Secretary (Ind.) of Nagaland gave a brief account 
of the industrial scenario pr.evailing in the State. He informed the Sub-Committee that the 
policy of libera1isation had yet to have its effect in Nagaland. In the longer interest of the 
balanced industrial growth in the country, it was necessary that the steps should be· taken for 
the fast industrial development of Nagaland. The Sub-Committee was also informed that in 
line with the liberalised economic policy of the country, the State Government had recently 
announced its rather ambitious industrial policy. The successful implementation of the policy 
will only be possible with generous financial assistance from the Central Government and 
substantial investments from entrepreneurs and investors from outside the State as the 
investments from all India financial institutions, banks and investors from within the country 
were negligible. The Sub-Committee was further informed that there was no major industry 
except a··sugar ndll in Dimapur and a paper mill at Tuh, both of which had been lying sick 
for the last sewnl ~rs. The revival plans of these two were pending with the Central 
Government. Three proposals were pending in respect of ~ugar mill, first for cane 
development fund with Ministry of Food, the second for rationalisation of labour with the 
Labour Ministry and the third with the Ministry of Industry, Department of Industrial 
Development, for upgradation of capacity from 1000 TCD to 2500 TCD. These units had 
good potential if revived soon. The rate of return on investments in Nagaland cannot be 
compared with other advanced regions in the country. It was therefore, necessary to lower 
the leading rates by subsidising the rate of interest by at least 3%. In the interest of 
balanced e<;Q~omic growth of the country it was necessary to give some protection! to the 
North ~s .. ~·- Some of the industries in the ·reserved list of Government of India 
could be ~itted' to be set up. only il! ,the North East in· order to ensure that actual 
industrialisatiOn took place. A large population of the State was engaged in cottage and 
traditiorud industries like bamboo etc. The products 'Yere very popular and had potential for 
growth in the State. There were large number of saw mills, few wood based industries like 
plywood and some flour mills. Though the State had potential for agro-based industries, yet 
poor infrastructmal facilities leading to higber cost ·of production were making them 
unviable. The means of communications and transportation too were lacking in the State and 
conversion of railway track at Doyang for fast movement was necessary. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that the industrialisation of the State through 
small scale industries was the only answer for the economic development. The State 
Government in accordance with the new industrial policy invites investment from outside the 
country for the development of the State. Government_ of India has also been requested to · 
set up some of the viable central public sector undertakings in Nagaland to bring up the 
industrial atmosphere at par with other developed States in the country. Due to the. 
constraints of internal resources and . inadequate allocation of funds· from the Central 
Government, adequate infrastructural facilities COtlld not be created in the State for its 

163 
industrial development. Developing adequate infrastructure for attracting investment was 
one of the main objects of industrial policy of the State. Efforts were also being made to 
identify some agro-based and mineral based industries in the State through State Industrial 
Promotion Council. Some NRls had also been approached for foreign collaboration through 
the Government of India. But so far not much success had been achieved in tbis regard. The 
plan allocations to the State were meagre. As a result the State Government was unable to 
maintain the assets for development of industries. In the State plans priority was given to 
power, roads, agriculture and allied sectors. Industries had not been given priority so far. 
There was total lack of private participation in industrial activities. The hilly topography and 
absence of markets were hindering industrialisation. The high transportation . cost of raw 
material, plant and machinery and finished products had seriously affected the normal 
growth of industries. The expected generation of employment for the educated local youth 
was marginal. The all India financial institutions like IDBIISIDBI/NABARD had almost 
completely stQpped refinancing the State financial institutions. The IDBI office in Dimapur 
had not been functioning for the last few years. On the ground that RBI was giving financial 
support to the State of Nagaland. NABARD was also in the same situation. The 
representatives strongly pleaded before the Sub-Committee: 
1. to give a special status to Nagaland for financial package, 
2. to grant subsidy in making payment of the interest component, 
3. to permit the NRis to invest money in the State for industrial development, 
4. to approach the World Bank for financial assistance for repairing/developing and 
construction of good network of roads, 
5. to review the funding pattern of growth centre scheme, 
6. to promote/establish power projects in the State for fast industrialisation. Though 
the State had micro and mini power plants yet power plants of bigger size were 
required. One Hon'ble member suggested that some•good plans may be sent to the 
Central Government for approval and for extending financial assistance. 
7. for the early clearance and payment of Rs. 193 lakhs of transport subsidy 
reimbursement scheme which was pending with the Department of Industrial 
Development for the last two years whereas during this period many units had 
already gone sick or closed as they were relying heavily'on the subsidy scheme, and 
8. the revival of the capital outright grant would be beneficial for industrial 
development. 
The Sub-Committee was not satisfied with the pace of economic and industrial 
development of the State. 
Thereafte~ the meeting adjourned at 9.00 P.M. with a mutual vote of. thanks.

The Sub-Committee was welcomed. Thereafter the Convenor explained the purpose and 
object of the Sub-Committee. He also made two announcements - one. relating to the 
confidentiality and the other that the copy of the proceedings be sent to the Secretariat 
within ten days. 
164 

165 
About the working of the Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana, the Sub-Committee was 
informed that during the year 1993-94 in Sibsagar district 45 cases were sponsored against 
the target of 39 and 21 cases were sanctioned and a sum of Rs. 14.96 lakhs was disbursed 
amongst all the 21 beneficiaries. In Golaghat district the target for 1993-94 was 26. A sum of 
Rs. 1~.05 lakhs was disbursed. Achievement was 26 cases. In Jorhat district the target for 
1993-94 was 36 and achievement was 100% ~ For the year 1994-95 the target for Sibsagar 
district was 265. 241 cases were sanctioned. An amount of Rs. 126.35 lakh was disbursed 
amongst 214 beneficiaries. In Golaghat the target for 1994-95 was 209. An amount of Rs. 
13.31 lakh was disbursed. The achievement was 209 cases. In Jorhat district the target, for 
1994-95 was 257 cases. An amount of Rs. 40.21 lakh was disbursed. The achievement was 
257 cases. For the year 1995-96, the target for Golaghat had been kept at 350 and so far the 
achievement was 129. Similarly, for Jorhat the achievement was 194 as against the target of 
280 cases. The progress under the PMRY had not been good. The main reasons for this 
were the absence of good and sufficient publicity of the Yojana, lack of infrastructure 
facilities, shortage of trainers, lack of banking facilities etc. However the D.I.C. had given 
stress for better implementation of PMR Y in respect of Agro-allied sector for rural areas 
e.g. dairy farming, fishery, poultry farm, piggery, duccary, banana plantation, tea, nursery, 
pine-apple garden, green house etc . 
. The climatic conditions of Golaghat and Jorhat districts were suitable for agriculture, 
live-stock and industry. The economic compulsions had forced more and more educated 
unemployed youth to take up employment through trade, business and industry. From the 
industrial development point of view the entrepreneurs of Jorhat and Golaghat districts 
required more encouragement and facilities to enter in the manufacturing business. 
However, the available infrastructure was inadequate. There was only one industrial area 
each in Golaghat and Jorhat. The transportation and roads were not suitable for vehicular 
traffic and also did not cover all the resourceful areas. The existence of nationalised bank 
branches were limited. The singleman bank branches were not in a position to render 
services to the entrepreneurs because they had a large area of 10 to 20 K.Ms to serve. 
Availability of finance was yet another problem for industrial ventures. Financial institutions 
gave less priority for providing financial assistance to the deserving entrepreneurs. TlJe 
entrepreneurs were also not in a position to fulfil banking formalities as per the R.B.I. 
norms as they belonged to very poor sections of the society. The availability of power was 
yet another problem. 
However, there was good scope for plastic industry, chemical industry, drugs and 
pharmaceuticals, spice manufacturing, sericulture, hqrticulture etc. On f1 query from an 
honourable Member of the Sub-Committee about potential of floriculture, the Sub­
Committee was informed that though there was little scope for floriculture yet the agro­
based industries and agro allied sectors wete found more suitable for both the districts. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that during the last three years the industrial 
scenario of the State undergone a lot of changes and now as a result thereof it is gaining 
ground welL The State Government had given exemption for five years for sales tax. and 
stamp duty. Even then there had been wide gap due to lack of transport and communication 
facilities. Still there was lack of capital formation and capital goods industry. The 
entrepreneurs had to bear high cost in establishing their units. Subsidy was required on term 
basis. Capital investment was required to be accelerated. The tiny sector should also be 
developed. One honourable member of the Sub-Committee asked whether they ever thought 
of establishing a sugar unit. The Sub-Committee was informed that there was already the

Meeting with Lead Bank MBIJagement in Connection with P.M.R. Y. (3.4S p.m. to 4.20 p.m.) 
The Members of the Sub-Committee were welcomed and the officers of the banks were 
introduced. Thereafter the Convenor introduced the Members and officers of the Sub­
Committee. The Convenor made two announcements- one relating to the confidentiality of 
the proceedings till the report was presented to the Parliament; and the other that a copy of 
the proceedings should be sent to the Secretariat within ten days. 
The Zonal Manager of United Bank of India, whicll was the lead bank, explained the. 
position about the performance of the banking institutions with regard to working and 
implementation of the Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana. He informed the Sub-Committee 
that during the year 1993-94, the yojana was implemented in the urban areas of the country 
while during the 1994-95 it was extended to the rural areas. The Yojana had a target of 
setting up seven lakh micro enterprises which was expected to generate employment for 1.4 
million persons by the end of the 8th five year plan. 
In Manipur the scheme covered only urban areas of five districts namely, lmphal, 
Thoubal, Bishenupur, Churachandpur and Chandel during the year 1993-94. But from the 
year 1994-95 it had been covering both the urban and rural areas of all the eight districts of 
the State. For the year 1993-94, 212 beneficiaries were sanctioned against the allotted target 
of 220 and money was disbursed ~o all. For the year 1994-95, 2040 beneficiaries were 
sanctioned against the allotted target of 2200 and money was disbursed to 1642 beneficiaries. 
For the year 1995-96 a target of 2000 had been allotted. Only 119 cases had so far been 
•t recommended and none had been paid so far. The State Government had requested the 
· . Central Government. to allot additional target for the year 1995-96 on the ground of large 
number of applications and good achievement of the scheme. 
On a query by the Convenor, the Sub-Committee was informed that under the Scheme 
the money was to be giwen direct to the firm or company supplying the machinery or spares 
or the infrastructural item~ but sometimes, under various pressures money was paid direct to 
the beneficiaries also. 
On another query by the Convenor, the Sub-Committee was informe<l that it was not 
proper and feasible for the banks to handover money for distribution and recovery. Shri 
Patil thanked the banks for fulfilling the targets and enquired what kind of industries were 
included for aid. The Sub-Committee was inf<>,rmed that f9r the p~p!Q~ of self employment, 
assistance was given to beneficiaries engaged in industries like IJ)iluery, poultry farming, 
dairying, power tillers etc. under the scheme. 
On another query by the Convenor the Sub-Committee was informed that generally a 
time of 20 to 25 days was taken between sanctioning and making disbursement to the 
beneficiary. The Sub-Committee w~ sorry to note that the recovery was very poor. For poor 
recovery of loans, the Zonal Manager informed the Sub-Committee that the beneficiaries 
were running good business and were creating assets. Dealing in piggery and grocery items 
had good chance in the hilly areas. Handloom was also a flourishing industry. The Zonal 
Manager also informed the Sub-Committee that infrastructural facilities were. wanting in the 
State. Good means of communication and transport were .-not there. The nearest railway 
• 

169 
station was Dimapur. Shri Patil suggested that they should demand a corridor through 
Bangladesh. The Sub-Committee was informed that the C~D. ratio was very high. Deposit 
was low because due to insurgency, people did not keep their money in banks etc. 
A strong plea was made for the special economic package for the State.

Meeting lfith representatives of trade and industry (4.25 p.m. to 5.00 p.m.) 
The repFeSentati.ves of trade and industry informed the Sub-Committee that due to lack 
of infrastructural facilities and insurgency, the industrial development of the State was 
suffering badly. The Sub-Committee was also informed that there was no good roads, no rail 
connection. Though there was an air connection yet that too was not regular. There was no 
regular power supply. Whatever industrial units were there they did not prosper due to the 
fear of 15 underground insurgent organisations which collected money forcibly as taxes. The 
State police was another problem which extracted money from the industrialists and their 
truck loads of goods were not allowed to proceed without greasing their palms. The 
industrialists could not export their goods due to non-viable import· of raw-material. There 
was a very good potential for export of ginger and resins. The heavy import duty of 45% 
dampens the enthusiasm of the entrepreneurs. Corruption was rampant in the State. 
The Indo-Myomar trade agreement, signed seven months ago was still limping. The 
barter system was still continuing at the Indo-Myomar border. In the State there were no 
incentives from the State Government for establishing industrial units. Whatever subsidy was 
given by the Central Government that too was not paid to them. 
The representatives strongly pleaded for :-
{i) Development of road and means of communications; 
·~~· (ii) Uninterrupted regular supply of power; 
(iii) Early implementation of Indo-Myomar trade agreement; 
(iv) Provision for subsidy to industrial units; 
(v) Reduction in import duty; and 
(vi) Special financial package to the State for its industrial development.

The Chief Secretary welcomed Sub-Committee and informed that the Government . of 
India had declared the whole of Manipur as industrially backward . area and all the eight 
districts of the State were categorised as 'No Industry Districts' ..• 
There was no centrally sponsored industrial estate in Manipur. The State Government . 
had set only one industrial estate at Takyelpat, Imphal covering an area of 10 acres with 40 
·constructed sheds. The State had no demarcated export zone at present. However, with the 
opening of the Indo-Myomar border trade, two trade centres, one at Moreh in Chandal 
district and another at Imphal in lmphal. district were proposed to be set up.

the main incentive had bee]l discontinued. The central transport subsidy which was 
withdrawn had again been continued, the orders for which had been received by the State

manpower development, power subsidy, State investment subsidy, State transport subsidy on

impediment in industrial development and the existing units also were badly affected. There 
was load shedding every third or fourth day in the State at present. . 
On the transport front the Sub-Committee was informed that the National Highway 39 
(Indo-Burma Road) was the main life-line of the State linking lmphal with Dimapur.

with Silchar in Assam. All the districts of the State were connected to each other by a 
number of State highways and also via lmphal. Railway network was practically negligible in 
the State. A metre gauge line had been extended recently upto Jiribam which was 225 K.M. 
from the State capital. 
Air transport also play a vital role in transportation of the State next of road transport. 
The State was connected with Silchar, Dimapur, Guwahati, Calcutta and Delhi by air 
transport service, but t~ !'as very costly due to the ever increasing fares by the airlines. 
Inadequate transport facilities, no proper rail link,\insurgency, uncertain conditions for

people did not feel safe. The iilsurgents had beea well equipped with arms and ammunition. 
Moreh was an important townfor trade butat Moreh there were no customs facilities. 
It was intended that goods should be brought from upland but the main problem was that 
there was no security on way. 
The Sub-Committee was further informed that there were no forests in Manipur. 
However, some teak was available which was smugled from Mynmar·. The State did not get 
any value addition by this. There was however, good scope for development of sericulture, 
handloom was traditional and fine quality of clotla was manufactured. There were 2,70,000 
looms and 3,00,000 handlooms. In the silk sector there were about 3oo:-400 looms.

Meeting with State Govemment Officials 
At the very outset the Convenor of the Sub-Committee made two custon 
anneuncements -one relating to the confidentiality of the proceedings of the meeting anc 
other that a copy of the proc_eedings be sent to the Secretariat within ten days. The Convc 
also explained in brief the scope of the Committee and its purpose of visit to the S1 
172 

173 
The Secretary Industries while giving a brief background of the Industrial scenario in 
the State informed the Committee that Arunachal Pradesh was the largest State in terms of 
the geographical area, among the North Eastern States with a population of 8.65 lakhs. It 
had so far lagged behind the other States of India in the pace of Industrial Development 
because of the severe lack of infrastructural facilities, communication bottlenecks and 
inherent constraints like difficult topography, geographical isolation, remoteness, etc. He 
urged that there was an urgent need to stimulate and encourage the growth of'industries 
which would go a long way in reducing the dependence on forest resources and create 
employment opportunities for ensuring all round rapid development. 
Giving the present industrial profile of the State he informed the Sub-Committee that 
there was no large and major industry in the State, though there were 17 medium Industries, 
3146 village and small scale industries, 155 craft/weaving centres, 28 sericulture centres, 5 
show rooms, 13 emporia, one growth centre and 13 Industrial Estates in the State. Though 
the State was endowed with abundant natural resources- like limestone, dolomite, graphite, 
coal, oil and gas, rice, maize, potato, tea, coffee, rubber, horticulture products like apples, 
oranges, pineapples, peas and forest resources but these were yet to be harnessed. This 
could not be possible so far due to problems like difficult terrain, Jack of transportation and 
infrastructural facilities. 
He also informed the Sub-Committee that the banks, due to commercial consideration, 
were very rigid in granting the required credit facilities to the entrepreneurs. No working 
capita1 was given by the banks in the State. The network of nationalised banks except State 
Bank of India was yet to extend the credit facilities in different distri"cts: The entrepreneurs 
were not in a position to invest big amounts. As the entrepreneurs of Arunachal Pradesh 
were the first generation entrepreneurs there was a need to have separate norms which may 
be softer than those made applicable in other developed States. 
He also informed the Sub-Committee that industries based on locally available resources 
and demand had better possibilities in the State of Arunachal Pradesh. There were (a) 
agricultural and horticultural based industries like mustard . oil mill, ginger drying and 
grinding etc. (b) Forest based industries like wooden furniture, wooden articles and toys, 
electrical accessories, tea chest battens, tea chest plywood, cane and bamboo products, etc. 
(c) demand based industries like printing press, barbed wire, blacksmithy, brick making, tyre 
retreading and volcanising, automobile servicing units, jobbing workshop, drycleaning unit, 
electronic industry, woollen knitted garments, readymade garments, etc. 
More than 70 per cent of the area of Arunachal Pradesh is under forests and almost the 
entire mineral wealth is found to be within the forest area. In the absence of suitable 
industries forests are exposed to over exploitation. Therefore, a pragmatic approach of forest 
protection vis-a-vis development of mineral resources of the State is of utmost importance. 
Arunachal Pradesh being predominantly forest area, needs some relaxation in the existing 
forest and Environment Protection Laws. The State Government views mining of economic 
mineral deposits on a small scale, as an activity complementary to forest protection. The 
Government of Arunachal Pradesh feels that the environmental clearance to the new -
industrial units sho!Jld be delegated to the State Government at least upto 20 sq. km. area 
and more fu.nds should be allotted under the mining sector as mineral exploration is a high 
risk and high cost venture. The present allocation of funds under the mineral sector is too 
meagre to undertake any mineral exploration work by the State Government on its own 
affecting adversely the development of vast mineral exploration and exploitation work. 
The Secretary. Industry of the State also informed the Sub-Committee about the various 

\ ; 
I 
174 
impediments in the way of developing the Industrial Sector. He suggested for the fast 
development of roads, transport network, means of communication and a special financial 
package for the State. 
When an Hon'ble Member asked about the outcome of the foreign visit of the Chief 
Minister (C.M.) of the State, the Chief Secretary informed the Sub-Committee that as a 
result of the visit of the C.M. now some of agreements/MOUs were likely to be arrived at/ 
signed with some foreign units for the industrial development of the State. He also informed 
the Sub-Committee that power in the State was only meant for domestic purposes. Though 
there w:.;s a high potential for generation of power but due to insufficient funds it was not 
being tJti!ised. The Chief Secretary also informed that an MOU had been signed with a 
compal:J' for exploration of gas and a gas-based power project in the eastern part of the

pen.-Jiog with the Department of Power~ He also informed that the scheme of single window

The Chief Secretary informed the Sub-Committee that the State Government offered all

industries in the State. 
Meeli11g witll representatives of Trade & ltJdustry 
The President of the Council of Arunachal Pradesh Industries Association gave a 
detailed account of the various difficulties faced by the entrepreneurs and other Industrialists 
in the State. He stated that ther.e was an acute shortage of infrastructural facilities like road 
communication, railway track, shortage of power, means of communication, etc. He 
informed the Sub-Committee that the transport subsidy was available upto Siliguri which 
should be extended upto Patna. He also informed the Sub-Committee that the 25 percent 
Central Capital Investment subsidy which had been stopped should be restored. The banks 
were reluctant to finance the units for want of collatoral security. As there was no patta 
system, land can not be owned by anyone as far as documentary evidence was concerned 
and hence the difficulty in getting loan from the banks. He pleaded for the removal of 
restricttd area permit system atleast for Indians. He also pleaded for a concessional rate of 
interest. He wished that in Arunachal Pradesh the growth Centre Scheme should be 
successful though it was not so in other States. He informed the Sub-Committee that there 
was a very good coordination between the entrepreneurs and the government officials 
concerned. 
The Committee was informed that in the field of coal and oil the State was suffering 
badly. because it was not getting royalty as the concerned units are not extracting them while 
the Coal India Limited received Rs. 15 lakhs on the plea that they developed approach 
roads, etc. The entrepreneurs were happy and satisfied and assured the Sub-Committee that 
the target under PMRY would be over achieved. 
The Convenor of the Sub-Committee enquired about the complaints of Arunachal 
Pradesh's pavilion in the Trade fair in Delhi. The Sub-Committee was informed that as far 
as the State Pavilion was conceined it sold its own products whereas some other states sold 
goods not of their own States and, therefore, there were some complaints. 
The President of the Council of Arunachal Pradesh Industries Association pleaded 
for:-
(i) making transport subsidy operative for atleast 10 years to both the old and new 
industrial units; 

175 
(ii) creation of a revolving fund for speedy payment of transport subsidy claims 
amounting to crores of rupees in respect of the industrial units of the State 
pending for want of sufficient funds; 
(iii) renewal of 25 percent Central Capital Investment subsidy for the State~ 
(iv) the increase of credit flow in the State by banking institutions; 
(v) increasing the C.D. ratio in the State; 
(vi) increasing the National Equity Fund upto Rs. 30 lakhs term loan through CISBI; 
and for taking the financing of the projects directly by SIDBI; 
(vii) scrapping the 3 percent interest tax for the N.B. region; 
(viii) suggesting the R .B .I. to monitor credit flow effectively and regularly for the· 
healthy industrial development in the region; 
(ix) the review of the Growth Ce!'!~i"e Scheme which, in its present form was not 
feasible for the State and introduce the required changes to make it fruitful in the 
State; 
(x) increasing the annual income limit of Rs. 24000/- to Rs. 36000/- and removal of 15 
sq. k.m. area operational limit under the PMRY as in some areas there was no 
nationalised bank branch within an area of 100 sq. k.m.; 
(xi) making the available industrial products in the State mandatory to be procured by 
the State Government with 15 percent price preference and the bill be paid within 
30 days of presentation; 
(xii) lowering the power tariff and for scientific tapping of the available hydro-electricity 
potential; 
(xiii) the rehabilitation/revival of the sick industrial units; 
(xiv) scrapping the area restriction permit system; and 
(xv) inviting the investors both Indian and foreign, to invest in Industrial Units of the 
State. 
The Sub-Committee was satisfied with the discussion and recommends tiJat . the banking 
institutions should determine the ownership right as per the State's Land laws for grjlnting 
loans only to the residents of the State. It also recommends that the e~tra interest burden of 3 
percent should be stopped in the case of Arunachal Pradesh.

Meeting with Lead Bank Management 
The Chief General Manager, SBI gave a brief overview of the working of the branches 
of the lead bank in Arunachal Pradesh. He informed the Sub-Committee that under the

The Chief General Manager informed the Sub-Committee that there were a number of 
constraints being faced in the implementation of PMR Y scheme. For example delayed 
sponsoring, stray applications sponsored from far flung areas, crowding of similar actions, 
hurried/perfunctory scrutiny by the screening committees leading to subsequent rejections,

collateral security was demanded from the prospective beneficiaries. However, in many cases 
the loan amount could not be disbursed for want of proper title deed, etc. in respect of 
landed properties.

should not reject it otherwise. The amount should, in no case, be allowed to lapse. Tlte'-Svb­
Committee also observes that the restriction on the area limit or service area should be 
removed and bank branches should be opened in all the areas having no branch. A reasonable 
time limit should be fixed for deciding tbe case for payment and the actual payment •of the 
loan money. 
The Chief General Manager wanted that the Government should scrutinise the cases 
and sponsor them right from the beginning of the year so that banks might process then 
smoothly and payments be made. This would also avoid last moment pressure of work in the 
banks. The Sub-Committee was informed that on the whole the work was being done 
smoothly in cooperation with the State Government officials and entrepreneurs.
