Study Report of the 'tst Phase visit of Sub-Committee-V of Department-rehited 
Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry to Hyderabad, Visakhapatnam .and 
Vizianagram during lOth to 15th September, 1995 
The visit of Sub·Committee- V to State of Andhra Pradesh emanated from the decision 
of Department-related Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry to constitute six Sub­
Committees of the Committee to study the aspects concerning industrialisation of backward 
areas and status of capital goods manufacturing units in the country. It was also decided by 
die Committee that these Sub-Committees would undertake field trips in different regions of 
the country. 
Sub-Committee- V comprised of the following members:-
1. Shri Bolla Bulli Ramaih - Convenor 
(Telugu Desam) 
RAJYA SABHA 
2. Shri Ish Dutt Yadav (SP) 
3. Maulana Habibur Rahman Nomani (Nominated) 
LOK SABHA 
4. Shri · K.H. Muniyappa, Cong.(I) 
5. Shri· Gurudas Kamat, Cong.(I) 
6. Shri Udaysingrao Gaikwad, Cong.(I) 
7. Shri M. Baga Reddy, Cong. (I) 
·I' 
8. Shri Harish Narayan Prabhu Zantye, Cong.(I) 
9. Shri Prabhu Dayal Katheria, BJP 
10. Shri Satyanarayan Jatiya, BJP 
The purpose· of the visit was to appraise the steps taken by the State Government of 
Andhra Pradesh towards removing the industrial backwardness of the State, vis-a-vis their 
coordination with central agencies in the matter. The Sub-Committee felt that it was equally 
important to seek the views of trade and industry representatives based in the State on the 
problems/constraints being encountered by them in running/establishing industries 
particularly in the light of new economic policy and its impact on the pace of 
industrialisatiOfl in the State of Andhra Pradesh. Another purpose was to interact with the 
workers as ·well as managements of the central industrial units in the State with a view to 
take stock of their status in the light of overall industrial scenario of the State. 
The Sub-Committee camped at Hyderabad on 11th and 12th September, ~995. The Sub­
Committee had a series of meetings with representatives of the State Governmegt of Andhra 
Pradesh, representatives of Federation of Andhra Pradesh, Chamber of Commerce and 
Industry and the workers and management of BHEL plant. The Sub-Committee deliberated 
its next day's business in three separate meetings with managements of National Mineral 
Development Corporation, Sponge Iron India Ltd. and Praga Tools Ltd. 
The Sub-Committee after completing its business at Hyderabad camped at 
Visakhapatnam on 13th and 14th September, 1995. At Visakhapatnam the Committee held 
discussions with Joint Collector of Visakhapatnam District, the representatives of trade and 
industry and also visited the plant of Bharat Heavy Plates & Vessels and met the ;workers 
and management of the unit to ascertai,!l the company profile. 
On 14th September, 1995, the·Sub-Committee took an opportunity to meet the workers 
and management of Visakhapatnam Steel Plant to analyse the progress of work in that 
179 

180 
upcoming steel plant and the problems related thereto. The Sub-Committee also met 
Visakhapatnam Port Trust authorities and the management of Hindustan Shipyard in 
connection with their contribution towards industrial development of the State and also on 
the basis of their separate representations submitted to it. Later the Sub-Committee visited 
the smelter plant of Hindustan Zinc Limited and discussed the activities of the unit with 
workers and manageme1.1t of Hindustan Zinc Limited. 
The Sub-Committee after completing its business at Visakhapatnam proceeded to 
Vizianagram on the 15th September to see the progress of Growth Centre being set up at 
Bobbili and discussed with the District Collector, Vizianagram the possible steps to arrange 
the requisite infrastructural facilities for the Growth Centre. The Sub-Committee also met 
the trade and industry representatives regarding prospects of industries in and around 
Vizianagram. 
Based on the evidence and the papers/memoranda submitted before it, the Sub­
Committee has come to the conclusion that representatives of the State Government, trade 
and industry representatives and the workers/management of industrial units at Hyderabad, 
Visakhapatnam and Vizianagram have similar views on certain issues like JX!Wer shortage, 
need for setting up of more ports and provisions of requisite infrastructural facilities iii the 
State. Still the Sub-Committee feels, that there are certain other related issues which have 
direct bearing on the overall industrial scenario of the State in general and particular impact 
on the performance of industrial units functioning in the State. The Sub-Committee would, 
therefore, before indicating· its recommendation like to give a brief account of all the issues 
connected with industrialisation of backward areas that came up for discussion, issue-wise, 
with different agencies in the State. 
Power shortage: The Principal Secretary (Industries and Commerce), State Government 
of Andhra Pradesh had informed the Sub-Committee that though the State was tlie third 
largest power producer State in the country but power generation had to be increased nearly 
by 100%, from 6000 m.w. of installed capacity. The Comnuttee was given to understand that 
State Government had allowed setting below 30 m. w. power projects which needs no 
clearance by the Central Government . . 
The Federation of Steel Small Industries had also highlighted the issue of lack of power 
supply. According to the Federation the power supply was needed to be iinproved in the 
State in particular. The ,small scale entrepreneurs in particular were facing lot of problems in 
running their units in peak hours. The power cut was applied for three to four hours daily 
which was affecting the infrastructure of SSI. 
The management of Bharat Heavy Plates and Vessels at Visakhapatmim was equally 
concerned about power shortage and emphasised the need for captive power plant for better 
performance. 
Power shortage. was one of the main constraint in development of industries at district 
level. The district 'authorities expressed its deep concern ovef the power shortage at 
Vizianagram, where a Growth Centre was proposed to be set up under the Central Scheme. 
A captive power plant of 100 m. w. capacity had been proposed for proper development of 
the district. According to the trade and industry representatives at Vizianagram atleast two 
power plants were required at Bobbili and Vizianagram respectively to cater the industrial 
needs of the district. 
In view of the submissions made before it the Sub-Committee opines that power was 
most crucial factor for the industrial developmen(of any area/State. While it was gathered 
that the State Government of Andhra Pradesh was making all possible efforts within its 

181 
resources for power availability, it was the Central Government which should come to the 
help of the State Government in augmenting power generation. The Sub-Committee, 
therefore, recommends that clearance of power projects proposed by the State Government 
should be expedited on priority basis by the Central Government. The private parties may be 
allowed to enter in power sector as the State Government had no bitch on that score. The 
State Government should also ensure that regular supply of power to industrial units 
particularly to small scale industries was maintained. 
Port facilities: Andhra Pradesh has an added advantage of having a considerably long 
coastal belt and industrial development of the State would thus largely depend on the port 
facilities. Nearly all witnesses with whom the Sub-Committee interacted during its visit had 
unanimous view on the need to develop more ports in the State. 
According to the State Government representatives at Hyderabad, a major port was 
required between Visakhapatnam and Madras to cater the need of the industrial units of the 
region. State Government was also in favour of developing Gangavaram port and the 
proposal had already been sent to the Central Government for clearance. 
The workers as well as the management of Bharat Heavy Plates and Vessels and 
Visakhapatnam Steel Plant broadly highlighted the problems of port facilities in the State. 
According to them the facilities at Visakhapatnam Port Trust seemed to have reached a 
point of saturation and unless the new port was developed at Gangavaram, their problems 
were bound to increase. BHPV in particular had severe constraints in getting some place 
near Visakhapatnam Port Trust for fabrication purposes. The workers of Visakhapatnam 
Steel Plant also highlighted the issue of high demurrage charges paid by the plant due to 
delay at port. 
Visakhapatnam Port Trust on the other hand had its limitations. However, on the issue 
of Gangavaram Port, the Port authorities informed the Sub-Committee that both the States 
as well as the Central Government had to take a decision quickly. 
The Sub-Committee observes that development of more ports could definitely increase the 
pace of industrialisation of State. The Sub-Committee recommends that the Surface Transport 
Ministry should take urgent initiatives in developing Gangavaram port and declare it a·major 
port. 
Incentives in investment: Development of infrastructure needs a coordinated approach 
and a positive policy for attracting investors for development of. infrastructure. It was well 
accepted that investor would invest his capital only if incentives were available. In the 
present era of liberalisation, where Central Government had formulated certain incentives 
for investors, the StateGovernments were going ahead by offering extra incentives wherever 
possible to attract maximum possible investment in their respective States. These industrial 
incentives had normally three determining factors i.e. (i) incentives (ii) procedural issues (iii) 
infrastructure. 
According to the State Government authorities at Hyderabad, agro and food processing­
industry had good prospects in the State. The State had equally good base for drugs and 
pharmaceuticals. There was single window clearance for investors. The investors were 
provided all information with regard to their proposals at one place. There was good 
coordination between the State and Central Governments_in regard to incentives to be given 
to investors in infrastructural field but sharing of data required more coordination. The 
procedure for filing proposals was SO· simple that the investor had simply to fill up a single 
form (Industrial Entrepreneur Memoranda) for his proposals and need not go for different 
forms for different proposals. The paper work had been reduced to the minimum possible 

182 
extent. Five years tax Holiday scheme from the date of completion had also been extended 
to investors for infrastructural projects. 
The representative bodies of trade and industry in the State had however some 
reservations abput procedural issues in the State. The representatives at Vizianagram were a 
bit dissatisfied that all approval papers were processed at Hyderabad instead of at district 
level where the officers were available. The Sub-Committee appreciated the assurance given 
by the District Collector to make himself available to discuss the problems of industrialist. 
He further assured the possible delegation of powers and setting the time frame within which 
procedural issues would be resolved. 
The Sub-Committee had already highlighted this issue in a meeting with the Chief 
Minister of Andhra Pradesh at Hyderabad and he had promised to look into the matter on 
priority .. The Sub-Committee hopes that the procedure will be streamlined. 
The Sub-Committee would nonetheless urge the Central Government to formulate certain 
guidelines on procedural matters in such a manner so that entrepreneurs get their problems 
solved .at District Industries Centre level itself. This would prove to be the biggest incentive 
and the entrepreneurs would also not be forced to run from pillar to post for getting their 
proposals approved. The Sub-Committee would further suggest for a centralised data system 
for information on investment for the convenience of investc::-s. 
Other factors for infrastructure: The Sub-Committee had been given to understand that 
other means of transportation such as railways, air traffic; equally contribute in development 
of infrastructure. For instance, the representatives of Federation of Andhra Pradesh 
Chamber of Commerce and Industry highlighted the problems of cargo facilities for products 
meant for export. According to them, Air India should shoulder the responsibility of 
carrying export goods as ships were not easily available and also the demurrage charg~s take 
a major chunk of their capital. Hyderabad airport should be declared an international airport 
for export purposes. 
The Sub-Committee feels that Government may sympathetically consider this positive 
suggestion. 
There were certain issues linked with the railways which came to the notice of the Sub­
Committee. For instance, the workers of the Visakhapatnam Steel Plant deposed before the 
Sub-Committee that only 65 wagons per day were available for transportation against total 
requirement of 135 wagons. The Sub-Committee feels that the shortages of wagons should not 
come in the way of production capacity of the plant and urges the Government to solve the 
problem of wagons on urgent basiS. The Sub-Committee would also like the Railway Ministry 
to rationalise the special tariff levied for steel products in Visakhapatnam for overall 
development of steel sector. 
The district authorities at Vizianagram pointed out another problem connected with the 
railways. According to them the movement of coal to the St.ate was very tardy. In their 
view, the railway link facilities could be improved by constructing a railway bridge between 
Gajhapatinagaram and Dattira Jeru. The Sub-Committee hopes that Government would take 
care of this suggestion. 
Water Supply: Water was one of the significant components of infrastructural facilities. 
State Governments authorities deposed before the Sub-Committee for diversion of the 
Godavari water for industrial purposes at Visakhapatnam. Estimates were being prepared 
for drawing of three MGD of water from Vengalraya Sagar reservoir to cater the needs of 
major industries expected in growth centre at Bobbili. 
Raw Materials: The State Government authorities informed the Sub-Committee that the 

1. 
183 
State had plenty of raw material resources. The main raw materials were coal, iron ore, oil 
and natural gas, bauxite, manganese ore, copper ore~ dolomite, limestones, lead and zinc 
ore. There was plently of scope for diamond mining in the state for which permission of 
proper aerial survey was required. The. present provision regarding aerial survey upto 25 sq 
km. only had restricted mining of raw material. The Sub-Committee feels that such 
restriction could adversely affect the prospects of mining in the. country in general and the 
State of Andhra Pradesh in particular. The Sub-Committee would urge upon the Government 
to lift such restriction and permit the mining authorities to conduct the survey. The Sub­
Committee would further suggest to make best use of satellite survey facilities in locating the 
prospective areas of mining in the State. 
Before moving on to the presentations of various industrial units of the State the Sub· 
Committee would like to give a brief account in the following paras, of the views it gathered 
from the State Government authorities and representatives of trade and industry on other 
relevant issues having a bearing on the industrialisati<?n of the ~tate. 
Presentation by State Government Officials: The State Government officials appreciated 
the need to encourage exports in the wake of globalisation but equally emphasised that the 
corresponding benefits be extended to the State Government from the export earnings. 
On the issue of taxation, the State Government emphasised the need of inter-state 
coordination. They wanted similar coordination in regard to movement of goods. 
The State Government officials informed that there was no modern textile industry in 
the State and the matter be examined in depth. Regarding sugar industry it was informed 
that licence to 15 private parties had been given and it was hoped this industry will flourish 
with participation from private entrepreneur. 
Presentation of Trade and Industry representatives: On the question of taxation, the 
trade representatives wanted uniform policy througout the country. They further deposed 
that while several labour laws had been enacted for workers no protection had been given to 
industry. 
The Sub-Committee appreciates the views of the State Government that corresponding 
benefit from exports should be given to State Government. This will simultaneously encourage 
the entrepreneur of the State to maintain quality control of their products. 
The Sub-Committee feels that high sales tax on some products in Andhra Pradesh had 
adversely affected the pace of industrialisation in the State. The Sub-Committee met the Chief 
Minister of the State in the matter and he assured to look into the matter sympathetically. 
The Sub-Committee would nonetheless recommend that uniform taxation should be effected 
throughout the country. The Sub-Committee feels that problems of industrial units like BHEL 
could be solved by rationalising sales tax structure. 
The Sub-Committee observes that small scale entrepreneurs are facing problems in 
getting credit from banks. The Sub-Committee recommends that possibilities should be 
explored for opening new banks which would exclusively cater to the needs of small scale 
industry. There is also a definite need of fixing time frame to process applications. 
The Sub-Committee agrees with trade people that Vizianagaram should be declared a 
backward area and all benefits given. to it in that capacity. 
Bharat Heavy Electrical Limited 
The Committee visited the Bharat Heavy Electrical Ltd's heavy power equipment plant 
at Hyderabad. Members of the Sub-Committee were taken round and shown various 
workshops/plants by its officials. 

184 
The Sub-Committee held discussions with the workers who drew the attention of the 
Sub-Committee towards the problems/constraints being felt by the BHEL in the wake of 
new policy of economic liberalisation. They emphasised the need for allowing BHEL to 
participate in open bidding and not to put it to disadvantage vis-a-vis other bidders. They 
also pointed out that BHEL had capacity to deliver goods but it was not getting orders due 
to financial crisis. They emphasized that the Government should stand as a guarantor jor 
BHEL. The representatives of the workers also brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee 
that technicians/executives of BHEL were quitting BHEL and joining multinational joint 
ventures like Siemans, ABB, General Electric etc. ' 
During discussion with the management, the Committee was informed that liberalisation 
had not affected BHEL adversely. Orders from power sector, however had come down due 
to other reasons which were likely to improve in future. Certain country-specific, industry· 
specific and company-specific constraints being felt in the wake of liberalisation were also 
pointed out. 
The management pointed out . that BHEL had to face problem when they had to 
compete with General Electric, ABB, Siemans etc. The problem as pointed out by the 
management was in the area of taxation. They informed the Sub-Committee that BHEL had 
to bear with the policy of double taxation. On being asked by the Sub-Committee to 
elaborate on the point, it was clarified that when BHEL imported any particular material it 
had to pay import duty. Thereafter, on the fislished products sales tax also had to be paid. 
This problem they informed the Committee, was not being faced by companies like General 
Electric, ABB and Siemans. These companies when they import any machinery for their 
plant, they did not have to pay any duty. They therefore, urged the Sub-Committee that 
counterveiling duty (duty on import of machinery) required to be abolished. They also 
informed that the World Bank bidding process should be adopted and BHEL should be 
permitted. to participate in open competitive bidding. They also emphasised the need to 
introduce 'Credit Insurance Scheme' and that the Government 'should also stand as a 
guarantor. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that the problems of this premier undertaking should 
be solved on priority basis particularly with regard to adjustment in duty structure and the 
question of allowing company to participate in bidding including financial bidding. 
Rationalisation of sales tax may also help the company a lot. 
National Mineral Development Corporation (NMDC) 
After hearing the performance achievements of the NMDC, the Sub-Committee 
enquired from the management whether the performance achievement was mainly due to 
export of raw materials and whether instead of exporting iron ore, NMDC could perform 
well by exporting steel? The Sub-Committee .further enquired about the activity of NMDC in 
the field of diamond mining and also the possibility of limestone mining in Andhra Pradesh 
to give a boost to cement industry. The Sub-Committee also enquired about the effect of 
liberalisation and globalisation on the Corporation? 
The Sub-Committee was informed by the management of NMDC that wherever possible 
finished product instead of raw material should be exported. However, they pointed out that 
the export of raw material to Japan was being done on the basis of MOU after getting 
clearance from the Government of India. They also informed that for few more years they 
had to export raw material. They further informed that their total production was 9.5 million 
tonnes and they had a long term contract with Vizag Steel Plant and Essar Steel in terms of 
supply of raw material. Since they had to export raw material they were keeping in stock 
with them iron ore for the purpose. Therefore, they w.ere unable to meet the requirement 

---------- ----- ------ -- ------
185 
(1.2 million tonnes) of the Vizag Steel Plant. At present they were supplying 0.78 million 
tonnes of Vizag Steel Plant. 
The Sub-Committee appreciates the performance of the Corporation in the field of iron 
ore mining and recommends that more emphasis should be given to meet the requirements of 
indigenous steel plants as regards the supply of iron-ore is concerned. 
As regards the diamond mining the Corporation informed that the mining done by them 
at present is of international standard and the success rate was also on par with the foreign 
companies engaged in the process. They informed the Sub-Committee that in order to 
increase the sphere of activity in diamond mining, 'Auto Magnetic Survey' needed to be 
conducted. The cost of the survey would be around Rs. 515 crores and the areas involved' 
will be about 25,000 sq. kms. At present Government permits to conduct survey in 25 sq. 
kms. and there was proposal to increase it to 40 sq. kms., which was again not sufficient for 
the purpose of conducting 'Auto . Magnetic 'Survey'. In this connection they sought the 
assistance of the Sub-Committee. The Sub-Committee hopes that the Government would take 
positive steps in the matter. 
As regards mining of limestone was concerned the Sub-Committee was informed that 
the Corporation was supplying limestones to cement industry in Rajasthan and Madhya 
Pradesh. They also informed that Vizag Steel Plant had captive mines but they were finding 
them· uneconomical. 
After some deliberation the Sub-Committee gathered from the NMDC that they were 
pleading with the State Government of Karnataka and the Government of India to hand 
over the captive mines in Karnataka namely the 'Kumaraswamy Mines' to them. They were 
pleading for it because the Donamalai Mines in Kamataka where at present they were doing 
mining was likely to be exhausted in the next 7-8 years and the task force engaged there 
would have no work in hand. 
The Sub-Committee feels that apparently the pleading of NMDC for getting captive mines 
in Kamataka sounds logical and the Government should consider their case on merit. The 
Sub-Committee further observes that development of Bailadilla mines should help supply to 
VSP. 
Sponge Iron India Umited (SilL). 
Tlie ·management of the sm.. gave detailed account about the working of the Company. 
They informed the Sub-Committee that the prime objective of SilL was to establish the 
techno-economic feasibility of manufacturing sponge-iron suitable as substitute for steel 
making though electric arc furnace route and thereby develop an alternative to the 
conventional process of manufacturing of steel i.e. via the blast furnace route which required 
high grade metallurgical coal (coking coal), reserves of which in the country were fast 
depleting. In order to achieve this objective the Company completed the erection of the first 
kiln in the year 1980 and commissioned the same on 31st December, 1980 and was able to 
produce quality sponge iron suitable for steel making by electric arc furnaces. Encouraged 
by the successful result of the first Demonstration Sponge Iron Plant, Government of India 
approvd doubling its capacity by setting up a second. kiln. The second kiln incorporating the 
impiQ._vements and design modifications which was build with in-house expertis~ and 
indigenous equipment, went into commercial production in October, 1985. Since then both 
the kilns had been producing quality .sponge iron suitable for manufacture of steel by electric 
arc furnaces. 
The management further informed that after achieving its prime objective the Company 
entered into the field of offering engineering oonsultancy services. The Company entered 

186 
into five such consultancy contracts and successfully implemented five projects. The 
Company also discharged designs and prepared all the requisite engineering drawings for 
kiln of 60,000 tpa and above capacity. The Sub-Committee was informed that on the 
international front the Company had carried out several assignments for Vietnam, Hungary, 
Pakistan; Zambia etc. which included testing of raw materials for establishing their suitability 
for production of sponge iron, providing specialised technical services such as engineering 
expert assistance for creation, supervision and commissioning and training of foreign clients. 
Currently the Company was executing a prestigious overseas contract for a Peruvian Steel 
Company. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed by the management of SilL that the Company, 
keeping in line with its general philosophy of taking up innovative developmental 
programmes, had selected the following two new projects for implementation: 
(a) A ~aptive power plant based on waste heat recovery system. They had set up for 
the first time in the country a captive power plant (CPP) of 4mw capacity based on 
waste heat recovery system. 
(b) Submerged Arc Furnace (SAF) for manufacture of high grade pig iron. 
The Sub-Committee enquired from the management about the problems being faced by 
the Company. The management highlighted the following problems:-
(i) The Company was unable to achieve full capacity utilisation due to poor quality of 
coal. Over the years the quality of coal from M/s Singareni Collieries Company Ltd. 
(SCCL) had deteriorated (with ash content some times exceeding 40%)~ · 
(ii) Till 1992-93, the Company was marketing almost 50% of its production .as captive 
consumption to A.P. Steel Ltd. However, due to certain operational problems of 
A.P. S.L. resulting in its closure, SilL had no other alternative but to sell its product 
to other customers located at far away places incurring additional costs in 
transportation. 
(iii) On account of severe power cuts in the State of A.P. in the last 3-4 years, many of 
the secondary steel making units, which were consumers of SIIL·Sponge Iron, had 
either to cut down their operation partially or close down totally. As a result they 
had to markc:tt the product to consumers located at far off places in the States of 
Tamil Nadu and Karnataka, once again involving high element of freight. 
(iv) Reduction in import duty in melting scrap, a substitute for sponge iron from 35% to 
5% has also resulted in lower demand for indigenous sponge iron. 
(v) It is the only unit in public sector competing with major manufacturers in the marht 
in private sector with much larger installed capacity. 
(vi) Substantial increase in the cost of coal which was one of the major inputs in the 
manufacture of sponge iron had affected the company adversely. 
(vii) The major source of revenue for the Company had been earnings from Consultancy 
services which· the Company was rendering in the field of coal based sponge iron 
, technology. However, du~ to saturation in the industry, the consultancy income over 
· the last two-three years had reduced considerably. 
(viii) Increase in the interest on Government loans and depreciation during 1993-94 mainly 
on account of commissioning of captive power plant in March, 1993, had also 
affected the Company on the financial front. 
The Sub-Committee was informed by the management that they had taken steps to 
/ 

187 
contain the operational loss. This included raising the production from 48,500 tonnes in 
1993-94 to 50,375 tonnes in 1994-95. To overcome the problem of usage of high ash coal, the 
Company had effected certain modifications/improvements to the process parameters, which 
had enabled the kilns to be operated even with ash upto 32%. Improvement in the captive 
power generation had also contributed for better working results. There was improvement in 
income from consultancy consequent upon securing an overseas contract from a Peruvian 
steel company. 
They also informed the Sub-Committee that with signs of upward trends in the steel 
market the Company might make profit in the year 1995-96. 
The Sub-Committee recommends that the Company should be given financial assistance 
to increase its installed capacity. The supply of good quality coal with less ash content from 
M/s Singareni Collieries Company Ltd. may be ensured. Subsidy should be given to the 
Company to purchase coal. Fo~ R & D activity the Central Government should also extend 
financial assistance to the Company. There was also need to review and rationalise the import 
duty on melting scrap so that indigenous production of sponge iron got proper boost. 
Praga Tools Limited (PTL) 
The Sub-Committee was informed that the Praga Tools Limited (PTL), Secunderabad, 
originally incorporated as a public limited company in May, 1943, for manufacture of high 
speed cutting tools and precision measuring instruments, became a Central Public Sector 
Undertaking from 31st March, 1959. The Company was initially under the administrative 
control of the Ministry of Commerce and. then the Ministry of Industry and was transferred 
to the Ministry of Defence, w.e.f. December, 1963, to facilitate more effective utilisation of 
the available capacity for production of defence items. The Company was brought back to 
the Ministry of 'Industry w. e.f April, 1986. PTL became a subsidiary· of HMT Limited, 
w.e.f February, 1988, when 51% of the paid-up share capital of the Company was 
transferred in the name of HMT Limited. 
Presently, the Company was manufacturing various standard machine tools viz. cutter 
and tool grinder, CNC cutter & tool grinder, surface grinder, milling machine, CNC milling 
machine, thread rolling machine, Jig boring machine and CNC Jig boring machine in various 
models besides machine tool accessories and Defence items. · 
During the year 1993-94, the Company achieved a production of Rs. 35.62 crores 
against a production of Rs. 34.89 crores in 1992-93. The anticipated production for 1994-95 
was Rs. 25.16 crores. 
The management informed the Sub-Committee about the problems faced by the 
Company. They informed that in order to meet their financial liability waiver of loans was 
required. After takeover by HMT, they were not getting the requisite orders for their 
products. The bulk orders which they used to get from the erstwhile Soviet Union had 
reduced. The management wanted that HMT should extend helping hand for the survival of 
the Company. 
The Sub-Committee was satisfied with the performance of the Praga Tools Limited and 
felt that the. Company was capable enough to compete with others in the wake of 
liberalisation. The proposal submitted by the Company to enter into technical collaboration 
with Mitsubishi, whith was awaiting clearance from the Central Government should, 
therefore, be expedited. If possible, the Company should be transferred back to the Ministry 
of Defence to facilitate more eff~ctive utiliSation of the. available capacity for production of 
Defence items. 

188 
Bharat Heavy Plates and Vessels Limited (BHVP) 
The Committee was informed that the Bharat Heavy Plates and Vessels Limited 
(BHVP) was set up in the year 1966 primarily to manufacture and supply custom built 
capital equipment required for process industries in the core sector such as fertilizers, oil 
refineries, petrochemicals, etc. 
The Company had three product divisions, which had been formed on the basis of the 
concept of product divisionalisation. These were process plant division, cryogenics division 
and the boilers division. The process plant division which was set up primarily to cater to the 
capital equipment needs of fertilizer, ref:~1eries and petrochemicals, had extended its 
operations to serve a number of other industries. This division had also taken up fabrication 
of many sophisticated equipment some of which were manufactured for the first time in the 
country. The cryogenics division, which was taken up as a diversification scheme, had 
emerged as one of the major production divisions of the Company. This division could 
secure orders for a majority of small and medium size plants during last eight years. All the 
above divisions were in a position to execute the jobs on a turnkey basis and the Company 
was gradually shifting its emphasis from mere manufacturing and supply of equipment to 
system saJ.es. 
In a meeting with the representatives of the workers union, problems relating to bonus, 
pension etc. were raised. They expressed general satisfaction with the existing management. 
The Committee then held discussiorr with MD and other senior executives of the 
BHVP. Impact of liberalisation, they said, had made them more competitive both in India 
and abroad. The management pointed out certain disadvantages such as the Government 
insisting to import things on FOB basis through Indian flagships only which were generally 
not available on time and not on C&F basis and foreign companies getting loans on low rate 
as compared to the companies in India, high L.C. charges, foreign bidders forming 
consortium which was not done in India. Need for improving power situation, 
communication, and transportation facility etc. was also brought to the notice of the Sub­
Committee. Demands were made f':lr encouraging captive power plants, exemption from the 
payment of terminal excise duty (which was not reimbursed), reduction in Bank guarantee 
charges and streamlininglharmc.;;ising work contract tax being levied by various States. 
The Sub-Committee feels that BHVP being a profit making organisation Should-·be 
delinked from Bharat Yantra Nigam Limited (BYNL), the holding Company. The Sub­
Committee recommends that forming of holding companies like BYNL should be avoided as it 
is likely to harm the interest of profit making units in the public sector. The Sub-Committee 
further recommends that requisite budgetary resources for investment be extended to BHVP. 
The Sub-Committee feels that the Company should be relieved from the burden of Works 
Contract Tax levied. by various State Governments. 
Visakhapatnam Steel Plant (VSP) 
The Committee had a meeting with the representative of Steel Plant Employees Union, 
CITU District Committee and AITUC. Apart from general problems of power and water 
scarcity, the representatives of the workers union emphasised the need for providing more 
educational and medical facilities to the employees of the steel plant and creation of more 
employment opportunities particularly for the displaced persons. The workers did not have 
anything to complain against the management apart from their demand to implement a 
pension scheme and arranging bonus for them. Some of the main demands/suggestions put 
forth by them were:- (i) conversion of loans that VSP had borrowed from outside agencies 
at huge rates of interest, into equity, (ii) expansion of steel plant particularly, steel melting 

189 
shop, (iii) construction of Gangavaram Port and (iv) development of ancillary units to VSP. · · 
etc. As regards the pension scheme, the Sub-Committee expiained to workers that it had to be 
taken up with the Government of India, as it was a policy matter. Since the Plant was· not 
showing net profit, the question of paying bonus to the employees mi.g~t not be possible. For 
rest of the demands of the workers, the Sub-Committee advised them that it might be taken 
up in the appropriate forum in a proper manner. 
The Sub-Committee, thereafter, met the Chairman-cum-Managing Director of VSP along 
with other senior executives. The CMD, while giving brief account of history and performance 
of the VSP and also apprised the Committee about the problems, being faced by the plant on 
different counts. He informed the Sub-Committee that despite several disadvantages and 
financial problems, the VSP was one of the major producer of steel and exporter of steel in 
India. It has been able to develop its own market in India and abroad. The main problems 
enumerated by CMD were heavy burden of capital charges and large interest liability rates, 
transportation problems due to shortage of railway wagons and lack of adequate port facilities, 
non-availability of good quality of coal in India. He wanted that VSP should be given an 
additional facility at the Visakhapatnam Port Trust and should be permitted to have its own 
captive jetty in the upcoming Gangavaram Port so that it could save huge amount of money 
which is being paid currently as demurrage charges. 
The management also informed the Sub-Committee that it had submitted a proposal to 
the Government of India for financial assistance to increase capacity utilisation of the Blast 
furnace from 3 million tonnes to 4 million tonnes. 
The Sub-Committee observes that the VSP needs to be suitably nourished for it being an 
upcoming steel plant, as in spite of several constraints the plant was making cash profit. The 
Sub-Committee appreciates the problem faced by the plant on account of non-availability of 
requisite good quality iron-ore and recommends that it should be solved by the agencies 
supplying it. The Sub-Committee feels that more wagons should be made available to the VSP 
to transport raw material from the VPT to the plant. The Government should give freight 
subsidy to the steel plant. VSP should also be permitted to set up its own aptive jetty in the 
upcoming Gangavaram Port and the clearance for the purpose should be given as early as 
possible. 
Visakhapatnam Port Trust (VPT) 
The Sub-Committee also met the officials of Visakhapatnam Port Trust .and discussed 
some of the problems which were being faced by various industries with regard to port 
facilities in and around Visakhapatnam particularly the Visakhapatnam Steel Plant and 
BHVP. As regards the BHVP's demand for providing some space near the Port for 
assembling their products, the port authorities informed the Sub-Co.mmittee that they had 
already provided them the required space and that they-were already making use of it. As 
regards VSP, the Sub-Committee was informed that the Plant was nearly 10 kms. away from 
the Port and the cargo had to be sent from there to the Plant by railway wagons which were 
generally not available in adequate number. The delay, therefore, was not because of Port 
Trust but because · of other problems. Proposals of using floating cranes by VSP, the 
Committee was informed, _was under consideration. The authority hoped that with the coming 
up of the Gangavaram Port the situation would improve to l! great extent. They urged to the 
Sub-Committee that the proposal for development of Gangavaram Port should be cleared as 
soon as possible and that it should be declared as a major port. Regarding prov~ding separate 
jetty for the VSP also, the port authorities did not have any serious reservation. 

190 
The Sub-Committee feels that the Port Trust which is managing the job of cargo handling 
in spite of several operational constraints may be entrusted with the job of developing the 
Gangavaram Port. 
Hindustan Shipyard Limited (H5t.) 
The Sub-Committee also met the management of the Hindustan Shipyard Limited and 
discussed with them the problems being felt in running/expanding the shipyard. One of the 
major problems being felt by the HSL was regarding sales tax levied by the Government of 
Andhra Pradesh. The Andhra Pradesh Government had levied sales tax on HSL for all the 
production activities which included mainly ship building, ship repair and off-shore platform 
construction. The shipyard authorities argued that it was not in a position to pay sales tax on 
ship building activities. The Government of India had recommended to the State 
Gm·~ ~nment of Andhra Pradesh to waive the sales tax but no remedy had far been given to 
the HSL in this regard. The capital restructuring proposal of the Shipyard for one time 
ct:;:;i~ ;,~nee was pending with the Miilistry of Finance for its approval. However, a formal 
decision had not yet been taken. The Sub-Committee was further informed that off-shore 
platform construction unit had been rendered idle because ONGC was not placing any order 
to the Shipyard and instead they were going to multi-national companies, although the 
shipyard had completed all their orders to their specification and on time. 
The Shipyard authorities further informed that in the last three years the HSL which 
was being closed by the Government, had taken the complete turn around and had started 
making some profit, though it was still suffering net-loss. They had orders worth 
approximately Rs. 800 crores at hand and were hopeful to fulfil all their obligations. It was 
hoped that in the coming seven to eight years it should start making net-profit. The shipyard 
representatives also demanded converting loans, aids etc. into equity. 
The Sub-Committee observes that the problems of Hindustan Shipyard such as waiving 
sales tax by the State Government could be examined sympathetically. The Sub-Committee 
would urge the Central Government to treat Hindustan Shipyard as export oriented unit in 
ship building activities in order to enable it to get sales tax relief. Capital restructuring 
prof)Osal of the Shipyard should also be taken care of without further delay. This would hel1> 
the shipyard to meet the challenges from multinational companies in the ship building 
activities . .It might also be desirable to consider conversion of loans given to Shipyard into 
equity. 
Hi11dustall Zi11c Limited (HZL) 
During its visit to HZL the Sub-Committee found that huge amount of waste material 
was being dumped near the factory without any plan for either recycling it or taking it away 
for dumping somewhere else. 
The workers of the HZL deposed before the Sub-Committee that they were satisfied 
over the functioning and facilities being provided by the management of the HZL. They 
however, complained of some minor health problems that they generally faced which was 
not very serious and the management was doing regular medical check-up of the workers. 
They were against the Government's policy of investment and demanded expansion and 
modernisation of their plant. They also brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee that due 
to amendment in the Bonus Act nearly 95% of its employees had become ineligible for 
bonus. 
The Sub-Committee, during discussion with the management enquired about the ;1u1in 

191 
hindrances being faced by them in running the plant particularly, in view of the policy of 
economic liberalisation. 
The HZL authorities informed that they w~·re meeting nearly 65% of the· zinc demand 
and 35% lead demand in the country. After liberalisation. their captive mines had become 
uneconomical and zinc industry which was producing less margin product cannot sustain it 
for a long time. It, therefore, was contemplating to process some precious metals in-order to 
strengthen its financial position. A demand of putting up 120MW power plant for Vizag was 
also raised. The Sub-Committee was also apprised of the measures taken to prevent 
environmental pollution, welfare and community services like housing, subsidized transport. 
education, medical aid, electricity etc., on concessional rates. The Sub-Committee expresses 
its happiness over the calm and peaceful environment in the HZL and various measures being 
taken to expand and modernise various plants of HZL and in maintaining ecological balance 
and doing social and community services. 
Study Report of lind Phase Visit of Sub-Committee-V of Department Related Parliamentary 
Standing Committee on Industry to Bangalore, Cochin and Trivandrum 
during 9th to 15th October, 1995 
Sub-Committee- V in its second phase of tour programmes visited the States of 
Karnataka and Kerala to study various aspects of industrial backwardness and performance 
of industrial unit in those two States. The Sub-Committee selected Bangalore in Karnataka 
and Cochin and Trivandrum in Kerala for its study. 
1.1 In addition to original strength of ten Members, two more Members namely Shri 
Purna Chandra Malik and Shrimati Suseela Gopalan, M.Ps. belonging to Sub-Committee-HI 
and Sub-Committee- VI, respectively, assaciated themselves with this Sub-Committee. While 
Shri Malik accompanied the Sub-Committee throughout its visit, Shrimati Gopalan joined 
Sub-Committee at Cochin and Trivandrum. 
1.2 The Sub-Committee camped a't Bangalore on 9th October where it interacted with 
the managements of Bharat Electronics Ltd., Kudremukh Iron & Ore Company Ltd., 
Visvesraya Iron & Steel Ltd. (VlSL), Hindustan Machine Tools Ltd., Bharat Earth Movers 
Ltd. and Bharat Gold Mines Ltd. and wherever possible the workers as well. The Sub­
Committee also interacted with the State Government authorities and the trade and industry 
representatives at Bangalore. The Sub-Committee also took opportunities to meet the 
Industry Minister and the Chief Minister of Karnataka. 
1.3 After completing its business at Bangalore on 12th October, 1995, the Sub­
Committee left next day for Cochin. At Cochin the Sub-Committee held discussions with 
district authorities of Ernakulam followed by meetings with Coir Board and workers as well 
as the management of Hindustan Newsprint Limited. 
1.4 On 14th October, 1995, the Sub-Committee left for Trivandrum where it held 
discussion with the State Government officials of Kerala relating to industrialisation in the 
State. The Sub-Committee thereafter met the management of lead bank and participating 
banks in conneetion with Prime Minister's Rozgar Yojana. The Sub-Committee also took an 
opportunity to meet Chief Minister of Kerala on 15th October, 1995. 
1.5 The Sub-Committee would like to make some preliminary observations about its 
experiences/difficulties faced in this phase of visit to Karnataka and Kerala. It was for the 

192 
first time that such a study was undertaken to gather certain first hand information from the 
Government. The ministries of Industry, Steel and Mines were requested some time back in 
July, 1995, to supply certain vital information, region-wise well in.advance of visits of Sub­
Committees. Unfortunately, the Government indicated its inability to furnish the requisite 
information and instead sought extension of time of five months. That being the situation 
Sub-Committee-V forwarded some questionnaire well in advance to the States of Karnataka 
and Kerala and the industrial units located there with a view to guide them on the purpose 
of the visit. The intention was to address supplementaries on the basis of their answers to 
such questionnaires. This was equally advantageous for the Sub-Committee as well as the 
witnesses to arrange discussions of the meetings. The Sub-Committee appreciated the 
attitude of those witnesses who came forward with their replies. However, in certain cases 
such as Kudremukh Iron and Ore Company Ltd. and VISL under the Ministry of Steel the 
replies were not ready, resulting in some confusion for the Sub-Committee and the witnesses 
about the concept of the study during discussion. The answers were got ready after a 
reminder given by the Sub-Committee during the meeting. The.Sub-Committee would thus 
like to proceed on the basis of the belated replies. 
1.6 The Sub-Committee would also like to mention about some dislocation of 
arrangements including meeting arrangements with the lead bank at Trivandrum. The Sub­
Committee Secretariat could not get even seating accommodation in the meeting haD. The 
Sub-Committee regrets that due to such dislocations it missed certain vital issues which· 
otherwise could have been covered in the presence of the Secretariat. The Sub-Committee 
feels that perhaps better coordination through Ministry could have saved the situation. 
1. 7 The details of discussions held during the tour are given 'in the following paras as 
per the programme of Sub-Committee. 
Bhant Electronics Limited (BEL) 
2.1 The Sub-Committee started its business with a visit to the plant of Bharat 
Electronics Ltd. located at Bangalore, The Sub-Committee took a round of various divisions 
of the plant such as High Frequency Equipment, Digital Micro Wave System, Digital 
Communication Equipment and Broadcasting & Television Division. The Sub-Committee 
during its visit gathered that Bharat Electronics Ltd., set up in 1954, being country's premier 
electronics manufacturing organisation, was engaged in design, development and 
manufacture of sophisticated ·state of the art electronics equipments, sound and vision 
broadcasting equipment, optical and opto-electronic equipment and electronic components, 
for the use of the defence services, para-military organb3tions and other governmental users 
like All India Radio, Doordarshan, Department of Telecommunications, police and 
meteorological department. 
2.2 While interacting with workers of BEL, the Sub-Committee noted that the capacity 
utilisation of the company was coming down. This was primarily due to hJ.;:k of orders. While 
the orders from defence had considerably come down, the orders from Department of 
Telecommunications were going somewhere else. According to the workers the broadcasting 
policy was also adversely affecting the order book position and as a result sound and v1sion 
broadcasting equipment orders were not forthcoming. The workers 'informed that though the 
Company had an order book position of nearly 2000 crores from defence, it had not been 
able to utilise the installed capacity fully. As on date the Company had executable orders for 
only Rs. 500 crores in the Bangalore complex even though the target for 1995-96 was 
Rs. 733 crores. As a result most workers were sitting idle for many hours. Only towards the 
end of the financial year does the work load picked. 

193 
2.3 As regards diversification, the workers deposed that the Company had a very strong 
R&D base with adequate infrastructure. Nearly 8% of total investme!J.t was in R&D sector 
but no outcome was forthcoming. The Company had not been able to go in for commercially 
viable diversification and modernisation. The workers submitted that the Company needed 
investment in a big way if it was to make a break through. The Semi Conductor -Gomplex 
will require huge investments upto Rs. 1000 crores. The workers further wanted the 
Government to give full autonomy to the Company to take timely decisions to meet modem 
market conditions through modernisation. Ibey further suggested that the Company should 
be allowed to enter into manufacturing of colour TV picture tubes. 
2.4 The workers also highlighted the problem of brain drain. According to them BEL 
had become a training camp from where people leave for good jobs somewhere else. 
2.5 The management of BEL had expressed similar views about capacity utilisation and 
took order position of the Company. According to the Managing Director, BEL, defence 
electronics was a small segment of its production. The Company was facing competition 
from private sector both in the civilian and defence sectors. Such competition was expected 
to be very severe in the coming years due to the dwindling defence market (in the world) 
and due to entry of multinationals. 
2.6 On a specific query about circumstances those led to disinvestment of BEL and the 
use of amount collected from such disinvestment, the management clarified before the Sub­
Committee that the Company was not aware of the circumstances but no amount collected 
by the Government had been utilised in diversification of the Company. 
2. 7 The Sub-Committee was given to understand that the Company had evolved 
following plans to meet the challenges it was facing following liberalisation:-
(i) Major thrust for marketing an~ customer satisfaction; 
(ii) Diversification of its product mix in consonance with its core competence with clear 
focus on specific products/technology; 
(iii) Joint ventures with foreign companies . in terms of technology and attaining 
specialisation; and 
(iv) Greater attention to high tech and high value added products. 
2.8 The management emphasised massive support from Government of India in 
component industry in view of following limitations:-
(i) For micro electronics, there had not been any change in the customs duty structure 
on the capital equipment; 
(ii) Products of component industry become obsolete much faster due to lack of 
investment; 
(iii) Component industry heavily depended upon imported raw material and 
components. With high interest rates on working capital/investment on capital 
~oods, the manufactu~ng costs tend to be very high; and . 
(iv) Duty on finished goods bad been reduced to 25% from 80% and on raw material 
and components to 15% from 55%. This had resulted into a reduction of 20% in 
Company's value addition on components manufacture rendering the manufacturing 
operations non-profitable. 
2.9 The Sub-Committee feels that B~L has got requisite talent and resources to be 
exploited to meet the international cluillenges. Being the only premier public undertaking in 

194 
the field of electronics the Government should ensure orders for the Company in the national 
interest. This would definitely motivate the workforce to attain the capacity utilisation. The 
Sub-Committee recommends that experts, the management, officer from Ministries of 
Defence, Communication and Information & Broadcasting and workers should sit together 
and fmd out some radical solution to bring back this premier Company on the track on 
priority basis. 
2.10 The Sub-Committee ~uggests that the Company should be encouraged to execute its 
specific action plans to enable it to meet the challenges it was facing from private and 
multinational ' sectors. 
2.11 The Sub-Committee urges the Ministry of Finance to sympathetically consider 
reduction of customs duty on raw material and components from S% to zero. The Sub­
Committee recommends that requisite fmancial support with low interest rates be extended to 
BEL for investment in capital equipment and for technological upgradation. 
2.12 The Sub-Committee appreciates the status of BEL as a capital goods industry. It, 
therefore, underlines the impenltive need to waive customs duty on capital goods required 
particularly in micro electronic industry. 
Kudremukh Iron Ore Company Ltd. (KIOCL) 
3.1 The Sub-Committee held meeting with the management of KIOCL on the 9th 
October, 1995. At the outset the Chairman-cum-Managing Director of the Company gave a 
brief account of the Company. The Sub-Committee was informed that KIOCL, a wholly 
owned Government of India Enterprise, was established in 1976 to develop mine and plant 
facilities to produce 7.5 million tonnes of concentrate a year. The mine and plant facilities 
were commissioned in 1980 in time within the estimated cost. Iran which was the main buyer 
of iron-ore, because of changed political situation in Iran failed to lift the concentrate. The 
Company was left without a market for its product in the depressed international market. 
An intensive study of the global steel industry was taken up to find other markets for the 
product. It soon became clear that the global industry standards demanded more iron 
content in the concentrate as well as lower silica and alumina. The original process had to be 
modified to meet this demand from a customer-designed, dedicated single-product facility, 
Kudremukh had to convert itself to •a multi-product Company. Alternative strategies to 
utilise the facilities and make the Company viable was considered. The strategies included 
setting up of a downstream plant for value-added product in the form of iron-ore pellets. 
With an agreement with the Romanian Government, a three million tonne annual capacity 
pellet plant was commissioned in Mangalore in 1987. An unprecedented marketing drive 
established new customers in the Western· Europe and Japan. 
3.2 The Sub-Committee was further informed that KIOCL at present was India's major 
exporter of iron-ore concentrate and iron-oxide pellets. Its products were exported nearly to 
a dozen countries around the world, from Australia to Hungary, besides two domestic units 
viz., Essar and Grasim Industries. The Sub-Committee was also informed by the 
management that Kudremukh represented a series of firsts in the Indian mining industry:-
(1) It was the first magnetite iron-ore mine in India. 
(2) It was the first in the country to deploy the largest mining equipment. 
(3) It was the first commercial exploitation of low grade ore in the country. 
(4) It was the first fully integrated operation from mine to port. 

195 
(5) It was the first iron ore long-distance slummy pumping operation in the country. 
(6) It was India's largest iron ore mining and pelletisation project and it was the 
largest 100% export-oriented unit. 
(7) It was India's first iron ore mine to introduce floatation process for improving 
concentra'te quality. 
3.3 The Sub-Committee was apprised by the management that the Company was 
currently operating close to its capacity utilisation and had drawn up its long-term 
perspectives. To overcome problems of erratic power supply and operations downtime, the 
Company was setting up its own captive power generation plant of about 50MW capacity at 
Mangalore. The capacity of the mine and concentrator was being increased to eight million 
tonne a year besides improving the quality of products, ensuring better rate of recovery. 
3.4 Thereafter, the Convenor of the Sub-Committee enquired from the management 
about the replies of the questionnaire sent by the Secretariat to them to facilitate the 
discussions. The management initially expressed its ignorance about the questionnaire but 
subsequently admitted the lapse committed in not supplying the replies to the questionnaire. 
The Sub-Committee expressed its displeasure for non-supply of replies to the questionnaire 
and observed that in the absence of the same no fruitful discussion would take place. Only 
discussions of general nature were possible. The Sub-Committee was assured by the 
management that the replies would be supplied to the Sub-Committee during its stay at 
Bangalore. Subsequently, the management supplied to the Sub-Committee replies to the 
questionnaire. In its reply the management had stated that KIOCL being a 100% export­
oriented unit, had since its inception been a seller in the international market and had 
always been exposed to global completion. As such the present liberalisation policy had not 
affe,eted the Company's operations as it had already been performing to international levels 
both on specifications and deliveries. As far as the mining laws and regulations were 
concerned, it was stated that mining lease was initially granted only for a period of 20 years 
after which it was subject to renewal again. Since the life of the mine in many cases would 
extend beyond 20 years, they had requested that mining lease be granted for the entire life 
of the mine without any time limit. It had also been stated that for two stage clearance 
before mining operations commenced, namely, grant of prospecting licence and subsequent 
grant of mining licence, it was necessary to obtain clearances .from a number of agencies 
such as Pollution Control Board, Forest Department etc., both at State and Central levels. 
In order to facilitate early clearance of applications for prospecti~g and mining licences, they 
had requested that a Single Window Agency should be set up which would be the nodal 
agency for granting of all necessary clearances. This agency should have clear directive to 
process all applications within a specified time limit. In order to safeguard environment 
protection and preservation of ecology there could be a condition attached to the mining 
licence that a certain minimum amount of profit earned should be invested on environment 
protection measures. Furthermore, it could also be made a condition for grant of mining 
licence that specified percentage of profits earned by the Company should be earmarked for 
social welfare activity in the neighbourhood of the mine. 
3.5 In their reply they had further stated that the Company had entered to the following 
joint ventures:-
( a) Alongwith ME CON and MSTC, 2 other PSUs under Ministry of Steel, KIOCL 
had promoted a joint venture company, named as 'Kudremukh Iron and Steel 
Company' to implement the Pig Iron and Duclite Iron Spin Pipes ~roject. To be 
located at Mangalore, the capital cost of the project would be about Rs. 240 crores 
and would also have direct employment generating potential to the extent of 350. 

196 
(b) MOU signed on 519195 for promotion of a joint venture alongwith SAIL and 
RINL. The company when formed would carry shipping activities by buying and 
mining of fleet of vessels. , 
(c) Alongwith NMDC and 1MECON, two other PSUs u.nder Ministry of Steel, the 
company has a proposal to set up a joint venture company to render consultancy 
services in the field of iron-ore and other minerals mii~ing activities. Consideration 
of the proposal by the Government of India was at an advanced stage and 
clearance was expected in due course. 
(d) There was a proposal to take up prospecting and subsequently mining for gold, if 
fund feasible, in Ghana in joint venture with BEML and another Ghanian 
entrepreneur. Prospecting licence for the purpose had been obtained from the 
Government of Ghana, covering 65 sq. Kms and actual prospecting work was 
expected to commence slowly. 
3.6 As regards the tax structure, it was stated that KIOCL being a 100% Export 
Oriented Unit, qualifies for exemption from income-tax on all projects earned from exports. 
Moreover, imports as well as indigenous purchases of machinery and materials required for 
production purposes were also exempted from payment of customs duty and e~cise duty 
respectively. 
3. 7 The Sub-Committee appreciates the endeavours made, by the Company in crisis 
management during its formative period as well after commissioning when its major buyer 
(Iran) backed out. The Sub-Committee also appreciates the achievement and performance 
made by the Company since its inception. The Company from the management point of view 
was performing well and was striving to keep up with the upward trend. 
State Government of Karnataka 
4.1 The Sub-Committee before meeting the State Government representatives visited 
the birth place of renowned scientist late Shri Visvesvaraya at Mudanhalli to offer tributes to 
him for his contribution in the field of science and technology. 
4.2 The Sub-Committee observes that the role of Late Shri Visvesvaraya in the 
development of industdes in the country in general and in his home state in particular 
becomes quite relevant in the present . context. The Sub-Committee recommends that a 
training centre should be set up at Mudanhalli in the memory of the scientist. A museum 
should also be developed in recognition of his contribution. Any proposal from the State 
Government in this regard should therefore be favourably considered by the Central 
Government. 
4.3 The Sub-Committee thereafter proceeded to Nandi Hills to meet State Government 
representatives. The State authorities informed the Sub-Committee that the State currently 
had 759 large and medium industries with an investment of Rs. 6692.70 crores and 
employing 2.97 lakh workers. The State also had 1,63,524 small scale industries with an 
investment of over Rs. 1936.17 crores employing 10.76 lakh workers. Thtt State had 60 
industrial areas with 17430 acres of developed industrial plots and 104 industrial estates with 
more than 4100 sheds. According to the State Government, units with an investment of Rs. 
60 lakhs and below in plant and machinery were defined as small scale industries. The limit 
of investment is Rs. 75 lakhs in respect of those SSis undertaking to export at least 30% of 
their annual production. 
4.4 Industrial units wherein investment in plant and machinery was Rs. 5.00 lakhs or 
below were defined as tiny sector units and those with investment of Rs. 60 lakhs or more 
on plant machinery were treated as medium and large industries. 

197 
4.5 As regards the subsidy and concessions extended to the industries the State 
authorities informed that State investment subsidy at 25% to 30% was offered to tiny/SSI 
units. Additional investment subsidy at 5% to special category entrepreneurs viz SCIST, 
minorities, women, physically handicapped and ex-servicemen was ·also available. Sales tax 
concessions in the form of exemption/deferred for period ranging from four to eight years 
was offered for tiny/smalVmedium and large scale industries, with option exercisable by the 
entrepreneurs. Incentives available for 100% Export Oriented Units as thrust sector were 
also available for other EOUs exporting a minimum of 50% of their turnover. Finance 
facilities were extended . to Mega projects proposed to be set' up in the State. 
4.6 On procedural matters, the Sub-Committee was informed that 'Single Window 
Agency' (SW A) was efficiently discharging its duty and the mechani~ had been 
strengthened with Karnataka Udyog Mitra (KUM) was acting as the secretariat for SW A. 
Further, the district level SW As headed by Deputy Commissioners were empowered to 
sanction incentives and concessions as also infrastructural facilities for tiny and small scale 
industries. 
4. 7 With a view to strengthen the infrastructure, the State Government in association 
with Tata Group and Singapore investors had taken up a project for setting up of a High 
Tech. Information Technology Park near Bangalore to meet the infrastructural requirements 
of high tech. industries especially in the area of electronics, computers and information 
technology. The company had taken over 65 acres of land acquired for the project from 
KIADB. Civil works were in progress. These investors had also been selected for setting up 
of a new airport at Bangalore which had been recently approved by the Government of 
India. 
4.8 On a specifiC query about power situation, the State authorities informed that to 
provide for peak hour requirements large and medium scale industries had been subject to 
restrictions during certain times of the day. The State Government help the industries to 
install captive power plants for which sales tax paid on the full purchase for running the 
plant was refunded alongwith w~iver of electricity tax. While private sector was being 
pursuaded to invest in pawer generation, action was also being taken to strengthen the 
transmission and distribution net work. · 
4.9 As regards small scale industries, thG Sub-Committee was informed that 2849 SSI 
units had been registered during 1995-96 (upto 31.7.95) with investment of Rs. 89.18 crores 
employing nearly 19,246 workers. The registration was not compulsory. However, to get 
various incentives and concession given by the State and the Central Governments for which 
the registration was a pre-requisite, all the industries obtained registration certificates. 
4.10 Based on the evidence and the papers/documents submitted before the Sub­
Committee, it appreciates the efforts made by the State Government in promoting 
industrialisation. The Sub-Committee was quite convinced with the plans/schemes with the 
State Government for removing industrial backwardness of the region by way of incentives 
available for entrepreneurs while seting up projects in the State. The positive approach of the 
State Government towards development of industries particularly the small scale industries 
was reflected · in the suggestions it submitted before the Sub-Committee particularly the 
suggestions for opening banks exclusively for catering needs for s~all scale entrepreneurs 
and the need for separate secretariat for small scale industries are worth considering. 
4.11 The Sub· Committee was in total agreement with the State Government that there 
wF.'S ;;, need to have a package of concessions to rehabilitate sick small scale industries. The 
Sub-Committee recommends that the . Government of India should urgently undertake a 

198 
survey of all sick industries in the country and evolve a package to provide assistance 
particularly for reviving viable sick industries. 
4.12 The Sub-Committee was given to understand that small scale industries were 
suffering due to delay in the release of payment by large and medium scale industries for 
purchases made from them in spite of a legislation passed by Parliament which provided for 
prompt payment. The Sub-Committee was constrained to express its anguish over the 
ignorance of legislation and impress upon the Government to monitor the implementation of 
legislation, so passed, in letter and spirit in the interest of small scale industries. 
4.13 The Sub-Committee feels that interest payable by the small scale and village 
industries on tne finances obtained from banks and financial institutions was on the higher 
side. The khadi and village industries do not get the benefit of the finance provided to K VIB 
of the States by consortium of banks, though there was subsidy available from . the 
Government of India and State Governments under the scheme. 
4.14 The Sub-Committee recommends that the interest rate on loans to small scale sector 
should be rationalised. It would also recommend that the limit for 'exemption from payment of 
excise duty by small scale sector was required to be enhanced. The Sub-Committee hopes that 
the Union Ministry of Industry and Ministry of Finance would sit together on the suggestions 
indicated above for revival of small scale sector. 
4.15 The Sub-Committee agrees with the views of the State Government that 
ancillarisation policy. for public sector undertakings has been diluted. The Sub-Committee 
observes that it may be necessary to come out with a comprehensive policy so that large and 
medium scale industries obtain components, sub-assemblies etc. from small scale industries 
and also develop the ~rea around this facility. 
4.16 During discussion with State authorities, the Sub-Committee found some glaring 
irregularities under PMRY scheme like insistence on collateral security by bank managers, 
high service charges from--beneficiaries, excess power of banks in determining viability of 
project and .declaring a beneficiary as defaulter on previous loans. State Government 
authorities put forth the following suggestions to remedy the situation:-
(i) There should be an effective reporting system in the banks so that the officers of I 
& C department could follow up the cases; 
(ii) The various service charges debited to the account of the beneficiaries such as, 
processing fee, ledger fee, ledger folio fee, credit guarantee corporation fee, be 
reduced or waived off; 

199

Meeting with the Management of Visvesvaraya Iron & Steel Ltd. GP 60 (VISL) at 
Bangalore on lOth October, 1995 
5.1 The Sub-Committee V of the Standing Committee on Industry met the management 
of the Visvesvaraya Iron and Steel Ltd. (YlSL) on lOth October, 1995. The management at 
the outset, gave a brief history of the Company. They informed the Committee that the 
plant of YISL was situated on the banks of river Bhadra in Bhadravati, a small town 260 
kms. north-west of Bangalore city in the State of Karnataka. Starting as a wood distillation 
plant in 191~. the then known Mysore Iron Works, commenced its pig iron production in a 
charcoal blast furnace in 1923 to produce 60 tonnes of pig iron per day. A pipe casting plant 
was installed in 1927 to make profitable use of the pig iron produced. Mild steel production 
was started in 1936 and in the same year the name of the works was changed to Mysore Iron 
and Steel Works. Production of ferro-alloys began in 1942 with the addition of two small 
furnaces and the production capacity was augmented subsequently in 1962. Mild steel 
production capacity was also expanded in 1962 with th,e addition of two LD convertors, 
electric arc furnace and a blooming and heavy section mill. The plant was expanded further 
and diversified into the field of alloy and special steels production in 1965 with the addition 
of electric arc furnace, combined bar and rod mill and central heat treatment slip. 
Subsequently. a modern forge plant was established in 1977 to produce high alloy steels like 
high speed steeL die block steel and valve steel etc. With that, the production capacity of 
alloy and special steels went up to 77,000 tonnes per year. As a step to improve the quality 
and yield of steel, a vacuum organ decarbonising unit was added in 1983 ~nd a continuous 
casting unit for blooms and billets was set up in 1985. A new oxygen plant was 
commissioned in 1987. In 1989 VISL was taken over by SAIL. In 1994, 1200 KVA "B" furo 
silicon furnace was modernised and pollution control system was commissioned. In 1995, 
first indigenous 530 MZ blast furnace was started and 8T electric arc furnace was converterl
