Hindustan Machine Tools Limited (HMT) 
7.1 The Sub-Committee visited the machine toois and watch division of HMT at 
Bangalore on 11 October, 1995. 
7.2 During the meeting the workers union of the HMT informed the Sub-Committee 
that the company was suffering losses due to indifferent attitude of the Government. The 
workers of all twenty-two units of the Company were concerned about their livelihood. The 
plants and machinery needed to be modernised and the Company was looking ahead for 
budgetary support from the Government. According to them the recent policies of the 
Government exposed the company for stiff competition from private sector and unless and 
until funds were released, even the diversification process could not be taken up. The 
workers also wanted waiving of interest in respect of some units particularly Ranibagh unit. 
They further informed that Voluntary Retirement Scheme had been introduced which 
reduced the ma,npower from 28,000 to 23,000 during last three or four years. They suggested 
that unskilled manpower of the company needed to be retrained. The workers union also 
brought to the notice of the Sub-Committee about huge stocks of watches worth Rs. 30 
crores compiled under goods-held-in-trust scheme. According to them there was clear cut 
case of sabotage and malpractices under the scheme. 
7.3 The management of HMT informed the Sub-Committee that HMT is facing a 
serious situation. The target had declined from 945.82 lakh in 1992-93 to 927.48 lakh in 
1993-94 and 814.20 in 1994-95. The percentage of achievement had also fluctuated from 78% 
in 1992-93 to 62% in 1993-94 and 86% in 1994-95. 
Since the liberalisation of the Indian economy in July, 1991, there had been a paradigm 
shift in the business environment of public sector, in general and HMT, in particular. There 
had been a tremendous adverse pressure on the performance of each of the businesses of the 
company. The company faced challenges not only from domestic private sector competitors 

... 
. ' 
204 
but also from the international giants who in turn increased their presence in the Indian 
market primarily due to lower import tariff. 
7.4 In addition to the above, most of the products of the Company belonging to the 
capital goods sector, also faced recession till recently, upto 1993-94. 
7.5 The company incurred loss of Rs. 12.53 crores, 120.24 crores and 79.20 crores 
during the years 1992-93, 1993-94 and 1994-95 respectively. On being enquired about the 
reasons for losses year-wise, the management gave a detailed account of the limitations such 
as slack demand and greater competition coupled with higher interest burden, unabsorbed 
excise duty and input cost increases in 1992-93. According to the management the reason for 
losses I.,Lurred in 1993-94 was substantial drop in the production and sales. The company 
was c.:,, 1 . .tined to drastically reduce the production due to poor order inflow and the reduced 
mar!:.:e>. .:~~·li:y of the products. The watch division group had to face intense competition. 
Su ckr . ~.::rmol over credit sales (GHIT) and discontinuation of the system of bulk credit 
sales against the security of watches resulted in the lower turnover during the year. As 
regards 1994-95, the Company was not able to complete quartz segment due to cost 
disadvantage. According to the management the production cost of watch modules by HMT, 
with its far flung production facilities, dispersed assembly units and overheads was over 
Rs. 200, as against the cost of imported modules of Rs. 90 inclusive of duties. In tractor 
business group, the profitability of 1992-93 was passed on to consumers while the group had 
to bear the brunt of withdrawal of excise duty exemption to ports of tractors of engine 
capacity below lROO cc. in central budget of 1993-94 resulting in a loss of Rs. 11 crores. The 
profitability oi this group was further affected by central excise notification No. 11195 
consequent to the Finance Bill, 1995-96. 
7.6 The management highlighted the following short-term and medium-term strategies 
to reverse the trend of losses:-
(i) Promotion of high value added products and high-volume economy segments watches 
- Lalit Series. 
(ii) Sub-contracting to meet shortage of capacities. 
(iii) Liquidation of SIT Rs. 106.39 crores as on 31 August, 1995. 
(iv) Reduction of sundry debtors Rs. 105.42 crores as on 31 August, 1995. 
(v) Increased advances for machine tools. 
(vi) Quick collection and transfer of funds. 
(vii) Doubling sale of stand alone tractor engines from 459 to 925 Nos. 
(viii) Increase sales of spare parts. 
(ix) Productivity improvement/value engineering/cost reduction efforts. 
(x) Reducing warranty claims. 
(xi) IS0-9000-quality improvement. 
(xii) Improving working capital management. 
(xiii) Selective recruitments to compensate skill erosion in capacity critical areas. 
(xiv) Marginal investment for de-bottleneckinglcrucial balancing facilit-ies. 
(xv) Increase of tractor production (by utilisation of critical facilities during the third shift) 
to attain 19,000 Nos. during 1995-96. 
(xvi) Increase in selling efforts for tractors to attain sales turnover from the 1994-95 level 
of 16,841 to 19,000. 

205 
Medium/Long Term Strategies: In the global business environment small operators in the 
market would find it very difficult to compete/exist. The larger business with antiquated 
manufacturing technology can hope to survive only by modernisation. HMT's basket of 
business combines small market share and large business with antiquated manufacturing 
technology. The strategic options before the company are: 
(i) The funding of the investment programme from Government funds or from capital 
market. 
(ii) To seek strategic alliances through joint ventures with leading wood manufacturers 
to bring in technology, access to global markets, finance systems, Government has 
accorded in principle, approval to convert individual business groups/units into 
joint ventures. 
7. 7 As regard the ·goods-held-in-trust scheme the management clarified that in order to 
boost sales, in the earlier years, facility of clean credit was given to the dealers, to lift stocks 
during that period. However, with the increase in volumes and consequently the risk factor 
in giving clean credit, a part. ~f the watches sold to the de~lers was held back by the 
Company in the form of ·"goods-held-in-trust". These goods were held by HMT on the basis 
of authority letters issued by the dealers. 
7.8 These transactions were considered a.s valid sales, as per section 20 of the Sale of· 
Goods Act, and were accounted -accordingly in the annual accounts. However, in view of the 
long delay by a number of dealers in clearing the outstanding towards the payment of goods­
held-in-trust, it was decided to discontinue this practice with effect from the year 1993-94 
and a structured credit policy was evolved to regulate sales of goocls. 
The reasons for suspending the goods-held-in-trust scheme (GHIT) was therefore 
primarily to: 
(i) Limit the amounts of c;edit sales. 
( ii) Reduce the imbalances in sales_ during the last quarter of the year, compared to 
other quarters. 
7.9 ·The management also informed about the following marketing strategies adopted by 
the company in watch business group:-
(i) Emphasis on high-volume economy quartz analogue watch-Lalit Series; 
(ii) Increased market presence through appointments of re-distribution of stockist to 
serve about 6000 dealers all over the country; 
(iii) Strengthening of state-of-the-art retail outlets through franchise (Time Art) 
showrooms throughout India; 
(iv) Setting up o{ centralised watch innovation/concept development group at the 
corporate head office to translate latest in aesthatics/designs to marketable new 
products; 
(v) Emphasis on institutional sales through defence c~nteen (CSD). 
7.1() The management stressed the need of the Government investment so as to save the 
Company as at present, the Company was left with no money for the same. On being 
enquired the . management informed about the joint venture it eqtered in food processing 
sector. The plant was proposed to be set up in Aurangabad with annual tum over of 
Rs. 2.50 crores.

Sub-Committee came to the conclusion that this premier public sector undertakings had suffered 
a lot due to lack of foresightednesS in devising proper strategies. Watch group of the Company 
was poised for competition with private ~ector for a long. It was, therefore, in the opinion of the 
Sub-Committee pertinent· that implementation of sophisticated technology could have been 
resorted to. Though it was difficult to recover the losses incurred under GmT scheme but urgent 
steps were required to ensure that stocks, if any, might be suitably disposed of as there seemed to 
be a conflicting picture with regard to such stocRs. The Government should also release funds to 
the Company so that it might be able to execute its marketing strategies. The Sub-Committee 
recommends that excise duty on tractor divisjon may also be rationalised so that profitability of 
the division could be restored. The Sub-Committee also feels that diversification plan of HMT 
needs sound financial protection particularly in the field of software and dyecasting unit. The 
management is also required to gear up to meet the challanges by tightening its belt wherever 
desirable. Customer satisfaction should be the uppermost for the experts deYising strategies 
because in the present stage of stiff competition it is the customer who determines the market for a 
product. The Sub-Committee. hopes that it is still not too late for the management to think and 
work on those lines and that is the only way PSU's like HMT can face competition and survive.

Bharat Earth Movers Limited (BEML) 
8.1 The Sub-Committee met the workers and the management of Bharat Earth Movers 
Ltd. (BEML) in two separate meetings, during its visit to Kolar Gold Fields (KGF) on 
12 October, 1995. 
8.2 The workers of BEML informed the Sub-Committee that total workforce of BEML was 
16000 out of which 8500 were employed in KGF. About seventy percent workforce belonged to 
backward classes. The workers were highly critical of new economic policy. According to them 
the company was facing the problem of orders and as a result workers remained idle. They 
wanted that more projects should be set up to enable- workers to contribute to the nation's 
economy and utilise their long experience in manufacturing earth moving equipment. They 
informed that the Mysore Complex needed more orders to sustain continuous production 
activities. The workers mentioned about power and water supply problems. BEML was facing 
acute power shortage due to unscheduled power tripping and low voltage. According to them 
Hola Halli Water Supply Project which estimated to cost Rs. 384lakhs, would solve the problem 
of water shortage to a great extent. The workers also highlighted the lack of orders from 
railways. The Company had the capacity to produce/manufacture 1000 coaches per year but in 
the current year the railways had asked for 200 coaches only. As a result no substantial work was 
available with Coach Division which affected total production. The workers indicated that 
industrial relations in the Company were very harmonious. On the. administrative side they 
would however wanted the management to provide appointments to the children of deceased 
employees on compassionate grounds or those who had retired and also to those who had 
completed twelve years of uninterrupted service in the Company. 
8.3 The BEML management informed the Sub-Committee that BEML was incorporated in 
1964, under the administrative control of the Ministry of Defence. The Company was engaged 
in the manufacture of heavy engineering equipment, catering to the core sectors such as mining, 
steel plants, cement, irrigation projects, railway and defence. 
8.4 In the construction & mining equipment area, BEML manufactured bulldozers 
dumpers, excavators, wheel loaders as also mining shovels and walking draglines. In the railway 
sector, BEML manufactured railcoaches,. DC EMUs, overhead equipment inspection cars, 
track laying equipment, spoil disposal units, railbus, etc. As regards defence sector, BEML 
manufactured Tata trucks which were used for army tank transportation, missile launching, gun 
towing, etc. BEML also manufactured vital aggregates for the defence sector like transmissions 
and final drives for the infantry combat vehicles and hydropneumatic suspension and stabilisers 
for tanks. 
8.5 BEML had an installed capacity to roll out 700 railcoaches annually. Of late, the unit 
had been facing a severe crisis. The withdrawal of roders from the Railway Board for 
railcoaches directly affected the bottomline of BEML and led to under-utilisation of large 
capacities and trained manpower. In the absence of high volume workload, BEML was finding 
it extremely difficult to maintain cost efficiency in the plant resulting in low morale. 
Incidentally, during 19?4-95 no orders were placed on BEML for railcoaches. 
8.6 For quite sometime now, BEML had been facing problems of both power interruption 
as well as voltage fluctuations. Frequent shutdowns badly affected the continuity of production 
at the plants. In spite of BEML's appeal to the State Government on several occasions to ensure 
constant supply of power without voltage fluctuations, the problem remained unresolved. 
Another major problem affecting the unit related of water scarcity which resulted due to 
the successive failure of monsoons and the fall in water levels of the main catchment area. 
8.7 The management further informed that BEML & Kudremukh Iron Ore Company 
Limited {KIOCL) alongwith a Ghana firm undertook prospecting of gold mining in Ghana. 

208 
The prospecting licence had been received and a team from KIOCL consisting of geologists 
and mining manager would shortly leave India for the project. 
8.8 On a specific query about the impact of Iiberalisation on the (BEML) Company, the 
management clarified as follows:-
(i) It increased the global competition which resulted in the loss of business and 
under-utilisation of installed capacity. 
(ii) Wherever business opportunities were available the customers did not agree on 
remunerative prices (due to competition) though the cost of .inputs continued to 
rise. 
(iii) Instability in the rate of exchange, low capacity utilisation, resistance from 
customers for price increases etc., resulted in low profit margins. 
(iv) In the case of global tenders where concessional project duty was allowed on 
imported components, customs duty at 25% was charged. The finished equipment 
was also subject to 10% excise duty, 4% sales tax and 1% other local taxes. In the 
absence of duty drawback for the indigenous manufacturers, there was a cascading 
effect of duties on the finished indigenous equipment, whereas for a fully imported 
equipment customs duty of only 25% was applicable thus making the prices of 
indigenous products non-competitive. 
(v) The foreign suppliers were quoting very low prices to capture the Indian market. 
Due to free availability of foreign exchange, customers preferred imported 
equipment (even second hand equipment). 
8.9 The management informed the Sub-Committee that there was a need to restructure 
the organisation, since in course of time the strength of the indirect workforce had increased 
disproportionately to the direct workforce. Also, because of steep wage/salary increases, the 
salary bill of the Company had jumped from a level of 11% to 14.6%. That had resulted in 
increase in the cost of operations and substantial reduction in profitability. 
8.10 The Sub-Committee feels that the curtailment in the orders for railways coaches has 
proved to be greatest stumbling block in the growth of the Company. The Sub-Committee 
recommends that the Government should ensure orders for the Company to enable it to 
achieve capacity utilisation and help in generating employment in the region. The Sub­
Committee would also urge upon the State Government of Karnataka to ensure proper supply 
of power to the Company. The Government of India should also help the Company in setting 
up a captive power plant. The duty structure should also be rationalised to enable the 
company in meeting the market challenges it has been facing in the global tenders/market. 
Meeting with District Magistrate, Kolar 
9.1 The Sub-Committee also took an opportunity to interact with the District 
Magistrate, Kolar, on issues concerning industrialisation of the district. The District 
Magistrate informed that Kolar had a population of twenty-two lakhs. Historically the 
people were em{lloyed in milk and silk sector. Bharat Gold Mines and Bharat Earth Movers 
Limited were two major industries. Gold extraction in the area had become uneconomical 
with per gram tonne availability of gold declining. The manpower of BGML had been 
reduced from 13000 to 7000. The surplus workers had no other employment opportunities. 
BEML on the other hand had done well in providing employment to more that 6000 
persons. 
9.2 Total eight industrial estates had been set-up in tbe district. Most of the industries 
were in small scale sector. At present, there were 6040 small scale units out of which 10% 

209 
were sick due to technological obsolescence. There were good prospects of seed production 
(commercial horticulture) and food processing industries in the district. Power supply 
situation remained quite critical though Karnataka Electricity Board was requested to 
upgrade the power grid. The District Magistrate also underlined the need of proper road and 
airport facility in the area. He also wanted that the Government should help in undertaking 
survey for extracting granite from the area. 
Bharat Gold Mines Limited (BGML) 
10.1 The Sub-Committee met the workers unions and management of BGML with a 
view to look into the matters related to the Company and the steps taken by the 
Government following the presentation of 7th Report of Standing Committee on Industry to 
Parliament in April, 1994. The workers informed the Sub-Committee that despite strong and 
favourable recommendations of the Standing Committee on Industry vide its 7th Report, the 
Government had not taken any concrete step to revive the Company. The Company which 
was referred to BIFR felt helpless so far as future of around 7000 workers was concerned. 
According to them the banks were not giving working capital to industries in the area. 75% 
industries were suffering due to high rate of interest the workers of BGML were worried 
about their livelihood. Even the salary was not being paid to them regularly. The workers 
also pointed out indifferent attitude of the management so far as coordination was 
concerned. Whatever may be their internal differences the workers stood with the 
management to work for the revival of the Company. The workers suggested that the banks 
should extend the requisite capital.· They also suggested for reduction in the rate of interest 
in the beginning. 
10.2 The management informed the Sub-Committee that following reference of the 
Company to BIFR, all decisions concerning development of the Company would largely 
depend on the outcome of BIFR proceedings which were in progress. A revival package was 
submitted the last year but the operating agency ICICI asked for a fresh revival package. In 
the meantime the Company was managing with plan provision of Rs. 6 crores and non plan 
of Rs. 3.5 crores on quarterly basis. The Company was under a serious burden of loan on 
which 18% interest plus penal interest had been paid. The Company was finding it difficult 
to pay even the salary of employees and continued to incur loans all alone. The Finance 
Ministry had turned down the request of Rs. 4 crores for ex-gratia payment. According to 
the workers, there seemed to be no possibility of a final decision on revival package in the 
near future. On a query as to whether it could be possible to revoke the reference of 
Company to BIFR, the management replied that there was no such provision under SICA. 
10.3 As a part of action taken on 7th Report of the Committee, the management 
strengthened the security by providing extra security gadgets. The plantation work in the 
surplus land had picked up. As regards the cycline of tailings, global tenders were issued. 
Five offers had been received and evaluated and partner for a prospective joint venture had 
been identified. An MOU with Ws. Normandy Anglo Asian Pvt. Ltd., a Company jointly 
owned by Normandy Possidon Limited and Anglo American Limited had been signed on 
6 October, 1995, subject to approval of the Government of India. 
10.4 The Sub-Committee feels that much time has been taken for the revival of BGML 
and the workforce has genuine fear psychosis. It would urge upon the Government to expedite 
the revival package fo: BGML so that this undertaking which was once a premier one for 
gold extraction, could come back to its track and a scheme for its further development could 
be executed. •

Meeting with Coir Board 
11.1 The coir industry had become a subject to debate in the meetings of the 
Standing Committee on Industry. Sub-Committee V, therefore, decided to study the 
problems and prospects of coir industry while visiting Kerala. The Sub-Committee was 
scheduled to visit coir units at Alleppy but on the spot study could not b~ made due to 
the law and order problem. It was finally decided to meet the Coir Board at Cochin and 
the visit to coir units was postponed to a later . date. 
ll.l The Chairman of Coir Board informed the Committee that coir industry was the 
fnost impOrtant traditional industry of Kerala. It was the means of sustenance of nearly 
four lakh workers in the coastal areas of the State. That was an export oriented industry. 
The environmental consciousness in foreign countries and the consequent affinity for 
natural fibres had caused a spurt in the demand for coir and coir products in foreign 
countries resulting in steady increase in the export of coir and coir products. The most 
important aspect of coir industry was that it was a labour intensive rural industry in 
which nearly 85% of the workers were women. 
11.3 According to the Coir Board, the coir industry consisted of two major 
sectors-spinning sector· and products manufacturing sector. The spinning sector was 
spread throughout the coastal belt of Kerala with concentration in the southern districts. 
The products manufacturing sector was concentrated almost exclusively in Alappuzha 
district. The spinning sector accounted for over 3.5 lakh workers. The share of products 
manufacturing sector in the work force was only about 30,000. 
There were two kinds of fibres used in coir industry as per details given below:-
White fibre: The traditional method of producing coir was by retting green coconut 
husk in brackish water for six to ten months, beating the· retted husk with wooden 
mallets and then spinning the fibre into yarq using manually rotated ratts. This fibre was 
called white fibre. Kerala had virtual monopoly in white fibre production. 
Brown fibre: Fibre was also extracted from coconut husk by the use of machinery. 
Such type of fibre was known as brown fibre. Other coir producing States were using 
that process. The brown fibre was generally not processed into coir products. 
11.4 The Chairman, Coir Board specifically highlighted the Integrated Coir 
Development Project (ICDP). He informed that the Government of Kerala with the 
assistance of the Government of India and the N.C.D.C., New Delhi was implementing 
an Integrated Coir Development Project to modernise the coir industry. The Project was 
implemented in the co-operative sector. 
It was proposed to establish the following units under the project:-
(a) Defibering mills of combing -
machine variety 
(b) Motorised coir ~pinning units 
each with 100 1 motorised 
ratts.

The project cost of a defibering mill was Rs. 20 lakh and that of a spinning units Rs. 18 
lakh. The project was financed as per the details given below:-
Subsidy from the Government of India .

The project would give direct employment to about 25,000 persons-mostly women. 
So far, HCDC had sanctioned financial assistance to ninety two spinning units and 
thirty nine defibering mills. Two spinning units and one defibering mill had already been 
commissioned. The rest were in different stages of implementation. 
The NCDC had sanctioned financial assistance to thirty spinning units and seven 
more defibering units in September, 1995. The work on these units would be taken up 
shortly. 
11.5 The Coir Board further informed the Sub-Committee that the scheme of co­
operatives in coir industry was being implemented as a centrally assisted scheme with 
expenditure shared 50 : 50 by the State Governments and the Central Government. The 
financial assistance extended to the co-operatives under the scheme were (a) share 
capital assistance (b) managerial subsidy (c) equipment assistance, etc. The Integrated 
Coir Development Project was also included under co-operativisation scheme. 
11.6 On enquiring about marketing of coir products, the management informed. that 
under the market development assistance scheme it was proposed to popularise coir 
products and promote the sale of coir products manufactured by cooperatives and State/ 
Central Government agencies. The assistance would be granted @ 10% of the gross 
annual turn over rates. Approval of the Government of India was awaited. 
11.7 There was a great potential of foreign markets for coir products. The Coir 
Board had formulated an export promotion assistance scheme to assist the exporters of 
coir to participate in exhibitions/fairs abr.oad. The approval· of the Government of India 
was awaited. The Coir Board was also looking for an amount of Rs. 2 crores for various 
research and development activities every year. Under the Integrated Coir Development 
Programme of the government of Kerala an amount of over Rs. 4 crores was due for 
reimbursement. The matter was pending in the Ministry for completion of technical 
formalities for more than one year. Coir Board also emphasised the need to exempt coir 
machineries from excise duty. According to the Coir Board, a welfare assistance scheme 
for coir workers was pending with the Government of India for approval. 
11.8 After hearing the evidence of the Coir Board and going through the papers! 
documents submitted to it, the Sub-Committee feels that the prosperity of coir industry is 
based on the sweat and labour of poor HI paid coir work~rs. Various schemes initiated by 
Coir Board for overall development of the coir sector are pending with the Government 
of India. The Sub-Committee urges upon the Government to appreciate the significance 
of these 3chemes and expedite clearance on priority basis in the interest of the coir 
industry. 
Hindustan Newspri11t Limited (HNL) 
12.1 The Sub-Committee would have preferred to meet the workers and 
management of Hindustan Newsprint Limited (HNL) at its location at Kottayam district 
of Kerala but due to paucity of time and also because of some law and order. problem in 
the State during the period of Sub-Committee's visit, the meetings had to be arranged at 
Cochin. 

212 
12.2 The workers of Hindustan Newsprint Limited informed the Sub-Committee that 
the Company was faced with the problem of supply of raw material which if properly 
streamlined, could help the Company in doubling its capacity utilisation. They also informed 
that HNL was a subsidiary of Hindustan Paper Corporation (HPC). Out of three subsidiaries 
of HPC only HNL was at present running with profits. However, the profit margin was 
reduced because of the burden of other two sick subsidiaries which was being compensated 
by HNL. The workers wanted that HNL should be delinked from HPC. The workers did not 
seem to be satisfied with the state of modernisation which was done on experimental basis. 
The workers also felt that modernisation had caused reduction in staff strength of the 
Company. In their opinion the Company needed requisite manpower. On a specific query 
about industrial relations in the Company, the workers informed that their relation with the 
management were very cordial. On the issue of compensate appointment raised by the 
workers, the Sub-Committee feels that Labour Commissioner of the region should take 
action in the matter. 
12.3 The management of Hindustan Newsprint Limited informed the Sub-Committee 
that the newsprint mill of HNL, with an installed capacity of 80,000 MT per annum and 
capital investment of Rs. 157.82 crores, was commissioned in 1982. The rated capacity 
during 1994-95 was 1,00,000 MT and the HNL product 1,05,369 MT which meant that the 
Company had achieved 105% capacity utilisation. HNL had also been improving upon the 
profitability year after year with a view to increase the value of investment for Hindustan 
Paper Corporation Ltd. The net worth of HNL which was Rs. 6904 lakhs in 1983-84 had 
gone upto Rs. 14,943 lakhs as on 31 March, 1995. Thqt was against the paid up share capital 
of 8254 lakhs. HNL also had the entire loan borrowed by HPC from Government of India 
for Kerala Newsprint Mill. 
12.4 The management highlighted certain problems in the area of raw material and 
marketing of newsprint. According to the management the availability of raw material from 
the State forest department was decreasing at an alarming rate. The situation turned worse 
after 1992-93. In the current year the availability of raw material was only 50% of the total 
requirement. From 1993-94 onwards HNL had to use imported pulp to make up the 
shortfall. During the current year the Company had contracted a large quantity of wood 
from Karnataka and was also contemplatingto procure Bamboo from Assam. HNL had also 
requested the Government of Kerala for the allotment of 10,000 hectares of existing field 
plantation to take up captive plantation. In fact the State Government had allotted 1000 
hectares of land for the purpose and the plantation had been taken up the current year. In 
order to operate the mill at the full capacity in the intervening period, HNL took up with 
the Government of India for permission to set up a 200 TPD de-linking plant at the cost of 
Rs. 80 crores. 
12.5 On a specific question about the impact of liberalisation and p1arketing strategy of 
the Company, the management informed that the Government of India brought the 
newsprint under Open General Licence w.e.f. 1 May, 1995. As a consequence, the 
newspaper establishments were importing newsprint in huge quantities from China, Russia, 
Finland, New Zealand and Canada. Due to the inferior quality of that newsprint, the C & F 
price was lower than the price of indigenous newsprint. As there was a scarcity of newsprint 
during 1994-95, the newspaper establishments, as a precautionary measure, bunched 
together, the import during the first half of 1995-96 to overcome any possible shortage in 
future. In the process, the stock of newsprint in various indigenous mills increased and one 
of the public ~ector mills reduced the price drastically to market its newsprint where as the 

213 
other mills resorted to softer options like extensjon of credit, etc. to market their product 
According to the management, unless custom duty was imposed on the imports, it would be 
very difficult for the indigenous newsprint mills to compete with imported newsprint. 
12.6 As regards diversification activities, the management informed that the Company 
had taken up the projects of writing and printing paper. The management also mentioned 
about power cut frc;>m 6.30 p.m. to 9.30 p.m. Though the Company has got its own 
generation still 60% of power requirement was met by the State Government. 
12.7 The Sub-Committee appreciates the efforts of committed workforce of HNL in 
achieving more than 100% capacity utilisation. Delinking of HNL from HPC could be a policy 
decision but it has certainly got to be examined by the government since some of subsidiaries 
like HNL which are performing well and earning profits generally tend to be exploited by 
holding company on account of loans incurred by other loss making subsidiaries. The Sub· 
Committee recommends that as a short term measure more autonomy may be conferred on 
Hindustan Newsprint Limited, the only public sector undertaking even suffering after 
achieving 100% capacity utilisation. 
12.8 The Sub-Committee feels disturbed over the shortage of raw material in HNL. The 
Sub-Committee is happy that the State Government has responded to HNL's request for the 
allotment of land for captive plantation and also for the sufficient quantity of forest raw 
material. The State Government has so far allotted 1000 hectares of land against the demand 
of 10,000 hectares. The Sub-Committee hopes that the State Government woulci take care of 
the remaining demand. The Sub-Committee urges upon the Government of India to expedite 
clearance for instalation of de-linking plant. 
12.9 The Sub-Committee feels that the Company requires lot .of support on marketing 
side. While it may not be advisable to revert back to pre-liberalisation stage and remove 
newsprint industry from OGL, indigenous public sector mills like HNL need equal protection 
from present import crittria. The Government, therefore, should consider to make suitable 
modifications, in Newsprint Import Policy. Imposition of customs duty on newsprint could 
perhaps help the indigenous newsprint mills to survive and cater to the requirements of the 
country. 
Meeting with State Government of Kerala 
13.1 The Sub-Committee held a detailed meeting with representatives of the State 
Government of Kerala on 14 October 1995 at Trivandrum. The State representatives 
informed the Sub-Committee that much remained to be done to increase industrial 
investment to a satisfactory level to ensure a growth rate atleast on at par with the national 
average within a reasonably short period of time. As per the figures given in the Annual 
Survey of Industries 1990-91, the number of factories was 3484 which accounted for 3.16% 
of all India total and involved only 1.95% of the total invested capital in the factory sector in 
the country. The total contributions of the national financial institutions to the industrial 
sector of the State continued to be low due to various reasons. 
13.2 The State representatives pleaded that the ·State should be declared as an 
industrially backward State. According to them in the States which had been declared as 
industrially backward the new industrial undertakings were exempted from payment of 
income tax for the first five years. The r~presentatives suggested that all districts of .Kerala 
should be declared as industrially backward on the lines of Goa, Pondicherry and Himachal 
Pradesh. 
13.3 The representatives informed the Sub-Committee that Kerala offers distinct 
advantages so far as social infrastructure and other ingredients of infrastructure like land, 

214 
water, power, communications, and manpower were concerned. Indicating the perception of 
the State Government, the representatives informed that the government assistance to 
industry was channelised through the industrial promotion agencies which were intended to 
promote and finance industrial development of the State. The assistance to such agencies 
was in the form of equity participation. Institutions like KSIDC (Kerala State Industrial 
· Development Corporation ~td. which promoted finance and developed large/medium sector 
industries) and KFC (Kenila Financial Corporation which financed SSI units, generally) 
made public borrowing by issue of bonds under State Government guarantee. Direct 
assistance was provided by the government to the industry in the form of fiscal and financial 
incentives and concessions. ,;/ 
13.4 According to the State representatives, the total number of SSI units in the 
backward districts before the coming of new Industrial Policy was 36035. The total number 
of units on 31 March 1995 was 59848 i.e., there had been an increase of 23813 units over a 
period of four years. In order· to accelerate the growth of small scale industries in the 
backward districts, in tune with the liberalised industrial policy of the Government of India, 
the State Government announced a number of incentives like higher investment subsidy, 
sales tax exemption for seven years, etc. The total expenditure in the backward districts on 
various schemes for the year 1994-95 amounted to Rs. 11.64 crores. The employment 
generated in small scale sector was 23118, 27564 and 35353 in 1992-93, 1993-94 and 1994-95 
respectively. The growth of SSI sector would have been much more if there was sufficient 
fund flow from financial institutions and enough power supply. The State Government as 
part of its new industrial policy extended following incentives to ensure accelerated 
development of industries:-
(1) 15% investment subsidy to all industrial units including large and medium, with 
a maximum ceiling· of Rs. 15 lakhs. For thrust industries like rubber, 
electronics, food processing and clay based industries etc., the maximum limit 
was 20 lakhs. The assistance was available for the units undertaking expansion, 
modernisation and diversification. 
(2) (a( Sales tax exemption for seven years to all new SSI units. 
(b) Sales tax exemption for three years for existing units undertaking 
expansion, modernisation and diversification. 
(3) Exemptio~ from electricity duty for five years for new units. 
( 4) Power tariff concession for five years for all manufacturing units. 
{5) 15% price preference to SSI products manufactured in the State for purchases 
made by the State government department and undertakings. 
(6) EMD exemption on tenders submitted to the government department and 
undertakings. 
13.5 Apart from the above, in order to speed up various clearances required for setting 
industries a new system namely Green Channel Counter was formed in all districts. To 
monitor the progress and to sort out various other issues, a district level Industrial 
Development Committee under the Chairmanship of the District Collector was also formed, 
ensuring participation from various conne<;ted departments like local body, town planning, 
forest, health, Kerala State Electricity Board etc. 
13.6 As regards the growth centres in Kerala, the State representatives informed that 
the total contribution o!Jhe centre for the two growth centres sanctioned would be Rs. 20 
crores. The project cost as approved by the Government of India during February, 1995 for 

215 
the two growth centres was Rs. 79.12 crores. -The Government of India so far released 
Rs .. 6.52 crores, the contribution of the State Government was Rs. 3.13 crores and Rs. 4 
crores were released by KSIDC {being the implementing agency with effect from 1 June 
1995). State representatives put forth the following suggestions for improving the function of 
the scheme:-
1. Since industrial growth centres were proposed to be established in backward 
districts and their gestation period was about five to ten years, the chances of 
mobilising funds from national financing institutions, banks and the public were 
remote. Hence it was suggested to set up the growth centres with joint 
participation from private entrepreneurs by giving attractive incentives. e.g. 
exemption from income tax on such investments in equity of the growth centres. 
2. The growth centres were to be declared as an "industrial undertaking" under the 
Industrial Development Regulation Act, 1956. 
3. Growth centres were to be declared as a "strike free Zone" for a minimum 
period of ten years. 
4. Extend income tax holidays for a minimum period of ten years for the 
companies setting up the growth centre and also establishing the industrial 
undertakings within the growth centre, as the scheme were being implemented in 
backward areas. 
5. The requirement of permission from various departments for establishing 
industrial undertakings within the growth centres should be dispensed with. 
6. The reporting of progress, after it was reviewed quarterly by the State level 
committee might be dispensed with. Instead the report furnished by the 
implementing agency might be accepted by the Ministry of Industry, 
Government of India. 
13.7 On the issue of Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana (PMRY), the State authorities 
submitted before the Sub-Committee that during 1994-95, against the target of 15,000 loans, 
the banks sanctioned only 11,123 loans, which were 74% of the sanctioned loans. Out of 
these only 48% loans were disbursed by the banks. The cooperation of private sector banks 
in implementing the scheme was not at all encouraging. Infact their achievement was only 
34% in 1994-95, against the State average of 74%. Even with the best efforts of RBI and 
SLBC (State Level Bankings Committee) they were not able to achieve the targets. The 
State authorities suggested that only nationalised banks should be entrusted with the task of 
implementation of PMRY. 
On enquiring as to whether there was need to redefine the role of banks under the 
scheme, the State authorities suggested that their role could be extended to the activities like 
identification of applications under PMRY with the restriction to route such applications 
through PMRY interview committee/district industries c.entres. 
13.8 On the question of power situation as infrastructure facility, the State 
representatives informed that power shortage was one of the important negative factors 
which adversely affected the uniform growth of industries in the State especially the seven 
northern districts of the State. In order to over come that the State Government proposed to 
set up diesel generating station at Kasaragod and Calk:ut along with Brahmapuram in 
Ernakulum district. However, it would take some time to get these projects completed and 
in any case the prevailing situation was not much conducive to the growth of industries so 
far as power supply was concerJ!ed. 

216 
13.9 The State authorities also deliberated on the impact of liberalisation on the 
industrial growth of the State. According to them after the announcement of the new 
Industrial Policy about 23000 IEM's (Industrial Entrepreneurs Memorandum) were filed all 
over India for setting up large and medium units and out of which the share of Kerala was 
about 200 IEM only, which did not even make one per cent of the total IEMs. It showed that 
. there was not much reflection of the liberalisation policy of the Government of India, on the 
: industrial scene of Kerala. 
Presentation of lead Bank Management at Trivandrum 
14.1 The observations of the Sub-Committee regarding arrangements for meeting at 
Triva,ndrum are mentioned in para No. 1.6. The Sub-Committee would not like to repeat the 
same and ·instead proceed ahead with deliberations with bank authorities on the basis of the 
brief· of the proceedings received through Ministry of Finance. 
14.2 The Convenor of the Sub-Committee, initiating the discussions sought clarifications 
from the bank authorities about the complaints the Sub-Committee had received about 
insistence of collateral security by banks for sanction of loans, non-sanction of applications, . 
non disbursal of amount under PMRY. He further mentioned about the representations 
from small scale entrepreneurs requesting for raising the loan limit about Rs. two lakhs with 
reduced rate of interest. Clarifications about poor performance of private banks under 
PMRY were also sought. 
14.3 The bank authorities clarified that banks in Kerala attached a lot of importance to 
PMRY and committed to achieve targets assigned to them. With regard to the reasons for 
rejections under the scheme, the Sub-Committee was informed that the non-viability of the 
project outside the service area, applicants not turning up, non-availability of premises, 
saturation of similar activity in the area etc., were found to be the main reasons. The Bank 
of India, however, deputed officers to go out and identify proposals and sponsor them to the 
branches. 
14.4 As regards the collatoral security, the representative of the Ministry of Finance 
informed that strict instructions were given to the Chairman and Managing Directors 0F 
banks to initiate disciplinary action against erring officials and the controlling ~)fk.0rs of 
banks should ensure that the instructions on that were passed on to the b:' uiC~1 <.ltl'icials. The. 
representative also suggested that to ensure achieving targets i:1 t[m<: .· ·.!istrict industries 
centres (DIC) should sponsor the applications in the beginning o: the y...:ar as the banks 
would be busy with the work during the closing month. Banks wete advised to sanction 
atleast 80% of their targets by December itself and re•naining 20% before March and also 
not to send back the applications pending in the previous year to DICs. 
14.5 The representative of the Reserve Bank of India, Trivandrum admitted poor 
performance of private banks under th.:: sch,;;me which felt dissatisfied due to non-inclusion 
of their borrowers under the Debt Rtl~ef Scheme of the Government of India. The matter 
was brought to the notice of Central office of RBI. The representative of the RBI said that a 
sizeable number of applications were rejected by banks on account of various factors which 

217 
resulted mainly due to inadequate scrutiny at DIC and task force levels. He disputed the 
issue of under financing highlighted by the State Government and informed the Sub­
Committee that a recent study conducted by RBI in three districts indicated that none of 60 
borrowers complained of inadequate finance. 
14.6 The Sub-Committee appreciates the spirit with ~hich PMRY was launched i.e. 
primarily to finance the projects for educated unemployed youth and help them in getting 
self employment. However, on the basis of discussion the Sub-Committee has come to a 
broad conclusion that there seems to be an apparent lack of coordination amongst different 
agencies such as banks, DIC and the monitoring bodies at the central level under the 
scheme. 
14.7 The Sub--Committee expresses its utter displeasure on the manner in which the 
applicants are subjected to harassment under the scheme by different agencies in their 
respective area of functioning. These agencies viz. the Central Government, State 
Government and bank authorities do not seem to be coordinating amongst themselves and 
instead are confined to their own functioning. The Sub-Committee is anguished to learn that 
DICs and task forces do not bother to expedite applications. The ball then falls in the court 
of banking sector where collateral security is insisted, failing which applications are not 
screened for disbursement. The Union Government has left everything to be settled by 
DICs, task forces and banking authorities. The Sub-Committee feels that the scheme cannot 
be implemented properly by simply issuing instructions for disciplinary action against officials 
demanding collateral security and by setting targets for banks for disbursement of loans. The 
Sub-Committee recommends that the guidelines framed for PMRY needs to be overhauled. 
More sincere coordination is required for the success of the scheme. It is understood that 
RBI has deputed one representative at Trivandrum and similar representatives must be 
deputed at all State capitals. The Sub-Committee appreciates that the scheme cannot be 
monitored from New Delhi directly but it is equally important to empower RBI officers 
deputed in the Stait':> to strictly monitor the scheme there. Performance of private banks could 
also be monitored by these officers. For this centralised data may be maintained. 
14.8 The Sub-Committee recommends that the officers of the State Governments, 
Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Industry and bank officers should sit together and review the 
procedural issues including guidelines for PMRY to ensure that (i) no beneficiary be 
compelled to accept a lower amount with the motive of limiting the loan, (ii) viability of 
projects is examined in proper way, and (iii) personnel having better experience be included 
in the task forces. 
14.9 The Sub-Committee has been given to understand that at present there is no 
system of collecting the figures pertaining to financing the women under the scheme. The 
Sub-Committee strongly recommends that such data should be maintained so as to 
encourage women entrepreneurs having skill and potential to set up industries. 
14.10 The Sub-Committee further recommends that to achieve the targets, banks should 
depute senior officials of their branches for closer monitoring of the implementation of the 
programme. It would also be appropriate if banks which show a better performance and cross 
the targets are suitably awarded. The officers of such banks could be given incentives. 
14.11 Another item in the agenda during meeting with bank authorities was 
the credit facilities for small scale entrepreneurs. The Sub-Committee brought to the notice 

218 
of the banking authorities, the demand of small scale entrepreneurs for extending loans of 
about Rs. two lakhs with reduced rate of interest. Reacting to this, the General Manager, 
RBI clarified that at present banks are free to fix the rate of interest on the loans of about 
Rs. 2 lakhs. The Sub-Committee hopes that this genuine demand of small scale 
entrepreneurs will be examined by the Reserve Bank of India sympathetically. 
. 14.12 The Sub-Committee has been given to understand that the.re is a general 
reluctance on the part of banks in lending and the credit deposit ratio of the State has also 
been abysmally low. There are certain firms in the State where even though the production 
has been much higher the needed finance was not forthcoming. from banks. The Sub­
Committee noted that the matter has already been brought to the notice of the Finance 
Minister. The Sub-Committee hopes that Government of In~ will suitably address to this 
issue concerning the State of Kerala. 
Study Report of the Illrd Phase Visit of Sub-Committee-V of the Department-Related 
Parliamentary Standing Committee on Industry to.Kerala and Karnataka during 19th to 23rd 
November, 1995 
In the IIIrd phase of its tour, the Sub-Committee-V of the Committee on Industry 
visited the remaining places and the industrial units in Kerala and Karnataka from 19th to 
23rd November, 1995. The Sub-Committee assembled at Cochin on 19th November and 
commenced its business by interacting with workers as well the representatives of Modern 
Food Industries Ltd., on 20th November, 1995. On the same day the Sub-Committee visited 
the coir units located at Alleppey and met the workers unions of the industry. 
The Sub-Committee then left for Calicut on 21st November where it interacted with the 
district authorities regarding the status of growth centre at Calicut. On 22nd November, the 
Sub-Committee left for Bangalore and met the workers and management of Indian 
Telephone Industry (ITI) at Bangalore. The next day, the Sub-Committee met the 
representatives of different nationalised banks regarding the Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana. 
This was followed by discussions with the workers and management of Cement Corporation 
of India, the representative of Vijayanagar Steel Ltd. and the management of Metal Scrap 
Trading Corporation Ltd. 
The Sub-Committee was earlier scheduled to visit the growth centre at Hassan. 
Unfortunately, due to unfavourable law and order situation, the Sub-Committee had no 
alternative but to hear the District Collector, Hassan ·at Bungalore itself on 23rd November. 
I 
The details of the discussions of the Sub-Committee with various agencies/organisations 
during the visit are indicated in the following paras. 
Meeti!'g with Workers/Ma11agement of Modem Food I11dustries at Cochi11 , 
The Sub-Committee started its business on 20th November, 1995 at Cochin with the 
workers/management of Modern Food Industries Ltd. The Sub-Committee was informed by 
the management that the Industries started business in 1966. At present the total strength of 
the Company was 2700. In 1991-92, the turnover of the Company was 60 crores which 
increased to 112 crores in 1994-95. A net profit of Rs. 5 crores was achieved in the last year. 
The Sub-Committee was informed that the Cochin unit of the Industry was started in 1968 
and· since then it was a profit making unit because of the steady increase in production and 
demand. The unit also started manufacture of cakes which was in great demand. The Sub­
Committee was informed thut the Cochin unit wus not able to produce and meet the high 

219 
demand because of capacity constraints. Since bread manufacturing waa reserved for the 
Small Scale Industries sector, the Food Industries '.Vere not ali owed to open a new plant. 
They told the Sub-Committee that their main competitors were private bread manufacturing 
industries. The Committee, therefore, urges upon the Government of India to help in 
increasing the plant capacity of the bread producing unit and if possible exempt it from Small 
Scale Industries Sector so that Modern Food Industries Ltd. could open a plant on its own. 
Meeting with Workers Union of Coir Industry 
The Sub-Committee met the workers of the coir units at Alleppey. The workers 
informed that the coir industry was spread throughout the coastal belt in the State and 
provided more than 4.5 lakhs employment to the people in the State. As regards production 
30 per cent of the production were exported and 70 per cent were marketed in India. The 
Committee was informed that nearly 85 per cent of the workers were women. The 
Committee was also informed that at present 450 cooperative societies were working in coir 
sector. 
While going around the units, the Committee found that coir industry was totally 
dependent on age old wooden spinning machines. The workers had to work mechanically 
throughout the day to complete the task. The Committee was informed that though coir co­
operative societies played an important role in the industry by protecting the interest of the 
workers by giving reasonable wages and providing better service conditions still four to five 
lakh workers needed improved working conditions. 
The workers put forth the following suggestions for the improvement of working 
conditions and overall development of coir industry:-
(1) The Government of India should extend rebate for 120 days in respect of khadi 
industry; a similar rebate should be considered for the coir. industry, i.e. 20 per cent 
rebate for coir and coir products be extended throughout the year. 
(2) Cash credit to the primary societies be extended to 90 per cent on the coverage of 
stock. 
(3) All assistance should be given to the co-operative sector f()r getting export orders. 
(4) The Central and the State government be persuaded to exempt coir yarn and coir 
products from sales tax. 
(5) Primary societies be strengthened by providing adequate funds. 
(6) The Kerala State Co-operative Coir Marketing Federation Ltd. (COIRFED), was 
the apex federation of primary co-operative societies and it should be provided 
necessary funds for its proposed projects so that workers in coir indusfry can be 
benefited. 
(7) Coir Workers Welfare Fund Boatd should be provided enough funds for 
implementing . the welfare scheme. 
(8) The Central Government should increase the present limit of Rs. 75/- p.m. as a 
pension and it should be modified. 
(9) A representative of the workers should be the member of the Coir Board i.e. its 
constitution should be modified. 
The Sub-Committee feels that all the suggestions mentianed above need to be 
sympathetically examined on priority basis. The Sub-Committee would like to draw attention 
to some positive suggestions, on more or less same lines, as suggested by the Coir Board 
during its meeting with the Sub-Committee at Cochin. The Sub-Committee hopes that Coir 

220 
Board representatives who accompanied it throughout its visit and meeting with the worken 
of coir industry could provide much more feedback to the Sub-Committee about the fielc 
problems which the Sub-Committee would place before the Government of India fo1 
redressal. The Sub-Committee urged upon the Government to ensure protection of coiJ 
industry in general and workers in particular. 
Meeting with District Collector Ca/icut 
The Sub-Committee after completing its business at Cochin stopped off at Calicw 
primarily to take stock of growth centre located there. The Sub-Committee was informec 
that at present two growth centres were proposed by the Government viz., Kannur anc 
Alappuzha growth centres and they had been split into five small centres. The Kerala Stat( 
Industria! Development Corporation Ltd. had been appointed for implementing tht.­
indu~.r~·hd growth centres. The Sub-Committee came to know that an amount of Rs. 600( 
bkhs had been earmarked for the scheme and amount so far released by the Centra 
Gover·:ment was Rs. 752.00 lakh. The Sub-Committee was given to understand that the cosl 
of lar.d in Kerala was very high. The State Government officials wanted the Centra 
Government to review the amount in view of high cost of land as the sanctioned amount wa~ 
not sufficient. The officials felt that it would be better if the Central Government release( 
Rs. 20 crores for the smooth running of the scheme. 
The State Government officials also suggested that the growth centres were to bt 
declared as industrial undertakings under the Industrial Development Regulation Act so m 
to get the benefit of tax exemptions. They also emphasised that since the growth centre~ 
were to be established in the backward areas and their gestation period was about five to ter 
years, it was proposed to establish them with private participation by giving attractiv<. 
incentives. 
The Sub-Committee strongly recommends that in view of the high price of the land in 
Kerala, it should be well within the consideration of Government of India to enhance its shan 
of fund for the proposed growth centres in Kerala. 
indian Telephone Industries (IT/) 
The Sub-Committee met the workers and the management of the Indian. Telephone 
Industries Ltd. (ITI) on 22 November, 1995. At the plant site the Sub-Committee noted that 
lTI was well equipped with. the modern instruments. The 48 years old ITI Ltd. had a sound 
infrastructure and excellent work force of experienced persons. The Sub-Committee was 
informed that the ITI worked under the Department of Telecommunications (DOT). The 
designing was done by DOT and production of telecom equipment was done by ITI. At 
present 27,000 employees were working in ITI, and it was producing all types of 
telecommunication equipment like equipment for defence, space, research and satellite 
installation. 
The Sub-Committee was given to understand that at present ITI was in critical position 
as its capacity utilization· was not satisfactory due to lack of orders by DOT. During the year 
1995-96, percentage of orders by DOT was around 8% of the total demand. The 
management informed the Sub-Committee that order placed by DOT were generally 35% by 
quota and 20 to 30% on tender basis. The problem of ITI was further compounded by the 
steep fall in prices. The Sub-Committee was informed that tender quotation submitted by 
private parties was quite cheaper than the tender quotation submitted by ITI, as a result 
private parties got the orders from DOT. These private parties started anti-dumping for 
getting orders and their quotation price was just equal to the cost price of the equip1:1ent 
built by ITI. 

221 
The Committee was. also ihformed· by the officials of ITI that the ratio of allocation of 
reserved quantity was not commensurate with the capacities available at ITI and HTL 
(Hindustan Telecommunication Ltd.). The ratio of allotment was 30:33, while the workers' 
ratio in these public service undertakings was 10: 1. HTL was allowed to create additional 
capacity for switching equipment, C-DOT, etc., even as m got large capacity for such 
equipment. On the issue of diversification, the management informed that a special 
technology scanning group was formed under the corporate planning department to explore 
the areas of wireless and local loop, CDMA, digital loop carrier technologies, etc. They 
further informed that the company was also planning for backward integration of business 
like power plant at Raibareli. The related area of diversification were radio paging service, 
mobile trunk radio service, software business and agriculture equipment, etc. 
On the basis of the discussions the Sub-Committee came to the conclusion that this 
· premier undertaking was a victim of DOT, a department which was its only patron allowed it 
to become sick because of its some wrong strategies and unhealthy policies. Following 
recommendations were made by the Sub-Committee, and perhaps a positive attitude in this 
regard might be helpful in the revival of the Company:-
(i) The ITI should be given price preference while quoting the tender. 
(ii) There should be some reservation in order supply by DOT. 
(iii) At present ITI capacity utilization is 80%, so it should be provided more orders as 
per the MOU. 
(iv) Under the new policy, old agreement (with ITI and DOT) should be changed. 
Meeting with Representlltives of Different Nationalised Banks 
The Sub-Committee then met the representatives of different banks and discussed 
regarding the loan disbursement in connection with Prime Minister Rozgar Yojana. The Sub­
Committee was informed that banks had been participating actively in the implementation of 
the scheme since its inception. They informed the Sub-Committee that various task forces 
had been formed in the districts· for interviewing prospective beneficiaries and applications of 
these beneficiaries were forwarded to the branches of banks under the scheme. Loans were 
then sanctioned by banks under industry, service and business sector after scrutinising the 
applications and finding them in order. The list of selected candidates was sent to DIC and 
these candidates were trained for twenty days; after the completion of their training loans 
were disbursed. The Committee was informed that the performance of loan disbursement in 
Kamataka was much better as compared to other States. 
The Sub-Committee was given to understand that there were some problems which 
came in the way of effective implementation of the scheme; these were due to lack. of co­
ordination between the Ministry of Finance and the Ministry of Industry. Besides that, banks 
representatives informed the Committee that DIC personnel were very slow in sending 
applications to branches which reached there very late generally after the lapse of six to 
seven months of the financial year and branches were directed to sanction, those applications 
upto by the closing month of the year . for their speedy disposal. Some delay was usually 
observed between sanction of the loan application and its final disbursement because a 
twenty days training was to be,..arranged by DIC during that time. The representatives also 
clarified that in respect of projects that require power supply, the borrower needed time to 
get the power coimection from electricity authorities which was one of the reasons for delay. 
Lease of premises was also a big problem for its smooth operation as borrowers were unable 
to offer the asset to be financed as security after the completion of lease time of premises. 
Bankers were sometimes unable to sanction loans as per the recommendations of the task 

222 
force because the projects recommended for sanctions were repetitive in nature for the 
same locality leading to oversaturation. Banks, representatives demanded that banks 
should have the right to take up, the assets of the defaulter without any legal process as 
it was time consuming and required as unnecessary exercise. Wh~n banks, representatives 
were enquired about the complaints of the collateral security asked by the bankers, they 
denied it and clarified that the banks were working under the guidelines issued by 
Reserve Bank of India from time to time and which they were bound to follow. The 
Sub-Committee was informed that the main reasons for the complaints were non-viability 
of the project, premises and saturation of the similar type of work. When asked about 
the status of loan disbursement of land based programme under Prime Minister Rozgar 
Yojana, the Sub-Committee was informed that banks were working according to the 
potential linked gradation plan sponsored by NABARD. The bankers were of the view 
that such land based industries like poultry, dairy, sericulture, etc .. should be categorised 
by the Government and registration of the land for these industries should be made 
without stamp duty. 
The representatives of the Reserve Bank ·of India, Bangalore informed the Sub­
Committee that strict instructions received from the Finance Ministry were issued to these 
banks from time to time and they were directed not to ask collateral security and also 
advised to sanction 80% of their targets during the current year .. 
Meeting with Management/Workers of CCI, Kurkunta Unit at Bangalore 
The Sub-Committee met with workers and management of Kurkunta unit of Cement 
Corporation of India in Bangalore. The Sub-Committee was informed that CCI was the 
only central public sector undertaking in the cement industry having an installed capacity 
of 42.2 lakh mt. p.a. It had at present 11 units located at Mandhar, Nayagaon and 
Akaltara in Madhya Pradesh, Adilabad, Y erragunta and Tandur in Andhra Pradesh, 
Charkhi Dadri in Haryana, Kurkunta in l_(arnataka, Bokajan in Assam and Rajban in 
Himachal Pradesh and Delhi unit. The Kurkunta unit was the second oldest operating 
unit in Karnataka with an installed capacity to produce 1.98 lakh tonnes of cement per 
annum. The Kurkunta plant was based on the wet process technology and this plant was 
commissioned in October, 1972 at a total cost of Rs. 12 crores. The Sub-Committee was 
informed that this plant had its own captive mine located about 4.5 km. from the unit 
and had its own wagon facility for transportation of the limestone to the factory. 
As regards the quality of cement, this plant had a full fledged laboratory for the 
physical and chemical te.sting. Tests wer~ conducted for raw materials as well as for final 
products and the testing was also done at different stages of manufacturing. At present 
this plant was producing ordinary port land cement of forty three grade. The Committee 
was informed that the unit was facing some. problems which were as under:-
(i) The coal consumption was very high because the plant was very old and was 
based on wet process technology. The coal being used was also of very poor 
quality containing a large percentage of ash. 
(ii) The plant required frequent repairs and maintenance which required high stores 
and spares consumption. 
~ ~ 
(iii) Due to infrastructural constraints, like power, coal ;md wagons, production 
performance of the unit was adversely affected. " 
(iv) Short supply of wagons, against the fixed quota and directional restrictions 
affecting production and despatches. 
(v) The unit required funds for modernisation and exp~~sion whereas the Cement 

223 
Corporation of India did not get any budgetary support and its internal sources 
were also limited; and 
(vi) Due to a cluster of cement plants situated in the unit area, the plant was facing a 
stiff competition. 
The Sub-Committee was also informed that some units of Cement Corporation of India 
were making loss because of non-availability of limestone, Charkhi Dadri unit was one of 
the loss making units because of non-availability of limestone and this plant had to procure 
limestone from Rajasthan which required extra budgetary support. 
On the basis of discussion held with the representatives of Cement Corporation of India, 
the Committee feels that most of the problems of the unit were self created. There is lack of 
foresightedness on the part of the management. The Sub-Committee wonders as to why the 
new technology was not adopted for the last so many years. The Committee observes that 
either the management failed to convince the Government for replacing the technology or 
reluctant to apply the same. At a time when the rapid advancement in technology is taking 
place and the number of competitors increasing considerably. The management has exhibited 
its inefficiency by managing with old technology/machinery at the cost of nation. Whatever 
may the earlier circumstances there is still time to awake. The Sub-Committee would caution 
the present management not to repeat the policies of their predecessors and also not to blame 
them entirely but go ahead with a strong proposal for change in technology. The Government 
should also help the management in finding out the solution and extend the necessary 
budgetary resources to revive the units of Cement Corporation of India. The Government 
should also ensure orders for the units to ensure their survival. 
Meeting with representatives of Vijayanagar Steel Limited 
The representative of Vijayanagar Steel Limited informed the Sub-Committee that 
during December, 1982, an independent company under the name and style Ws. 
Vijayanagar Steel Ltd., was incorporated under the Ministry of Steel and Mines, 
Government of India. A total of 9341 acres of land was acquired (8791 acres for the steel 
plant and 550 acres for the housing colony) near Thorangal forming part of the villages of 
Thorangal, Vaddu, Kurekuppa, Yerabanahalli, Nayakanahalli, Chikanthapur, Kudithini and 
Thimmalpura. Mecon was commissioned to do various pre-feasibility step/reports towards 
setting up of the steel plant. However, due to insufficient budget provision and the lack of 
firm decision as to go ahead with the steel project, the company could not proceed beyond 
the initial steps of land acquisition and pre-feasibility steps/reports preparation upto 1991. 
Subsequent to the economic and industrial liberalisation policy, it was decided by the 
Government of India not to set up any new steel plants in the public sector but to encourage 
private sector investment in various areas including steel plants. Accordingly, several private 
companies/industrial houses of India came forward to set up steel plants in the private sector 
in various parts of the country including Karnataka. A report prepared by the Ministry of 
Steel and Mines, Government of India indicated Mangalore and Hospet-Bellary region as 
two possible locations for setting up of the integrated steel plants in Karnataka. 
The Government of Karnataka, immediately after the economic liberalisation in 1991, 
moved to the Government of India for the transfer of Vijayanagar Steel Limited from 
Government of India to Government of Karnatka and also for the transfer of 9350 acres of 
land acquired for the project to the Government of Karnataka, so that, the Government of 
Karnataka could identify suitable private sector partner and set up the Karnataka 's first 
integrated steel plant at Hospet-Bellary area in the joint sector. Accordingly, a 
III!cl!ssary revenues. 
The Management was of the view that the proposed diversification project of MSTC 
should be given top priority for clearances by the Ministry, Planning Commission, CCEA, 
etc. so as to enable MSTC to create its own productivity and give sufficient business volume 
to its existing infrastructure. 
On enquiring as to what had been the impact of liberation on the Company, the 
Management clarified that liberalisation primarily involved decanalisation of import of scrap, 
reduction of import duties and simplification of systems and procedures in that sphere. 

224 
Memorandum of Undertaking was signed between the Government of India and the 
Government of Karnataka on 23 February, 1994, whereby the share holdings of the 
Government of India in Ws. Vijayanagar Steel Limited were transferred to the Government 
of Karnataka alongwith the land and other assets of the company for setting up the steel 
plant. Subsequently, the deed of conveyance was also executed by the Government of India 
m favour of the Government of Karnataka on 2 January, 1995 in this regard. 
The Government of Karnataka, immediately after signing the Memorandum of 
226 
As a result of these measures, the following developments took place:-
(a) A number of private units including large ,organisations such as Reliance. TISCO, 
lTC, etc. and international traders as well as individual furnace units came into the 
field of import of scrap. This resulted in foreign suppliers taking advantage of a 
number of players in the field and dilution of strict quality criteria followed by MSTC 
during the canalisation period. MSTC also had as a result to follow the suit, since the 
prices offered to MSTC otherwise were unnecessarily higher and as such 
uncompetitive. The mushrooming led to simultaneous import by number of parties and 
avoidable infructuous cost by way of bunching of vessels at ports, demurrage, necessity 
to keep material in stock for extended periods due to inability to sell in advance, 
introduction of extra commercial practice, etc. 
(b) Impact of reduction duties: Import duty on melting scrap had been reduced to 5% 
Simultaneously, duty on finished products had also been reduced. For example, duty 
on billet stood at 20%. As a result, billet was being imported in preference to scrap, 
since billet, produced out of imported scrap after melting in furnaces was costlier than 
the landed cost of imported billet. As a result of this as well as the hike in 
international price of scrap, a large number of furnaces were closed down, resulting in 
gross under-utilisation of investment and capacity build up. 
(c) Irlpact of removal of definition of heavy melting scrap rerollable scrap: Heavy Melting 
Scr~lp (HMS) was defined in the import policy in 1985-88. However, in the last few 
years, that definition had been withdrawn and as a result large scale imports of HMS 
were now being undertaken. Material, actually coming under this category, however, 
was in higher sizes which could only be categorised as rerollable scrap. Duty on 
rerollable scrap ranged upto 40%, whereas duty on HMS was just 5%. This led to 
large scale evasion of duty. MSTC could not obviously be party to such practices and 
was not in a position to import HMS, being otherwise unv~able. 
In the presen~ decanahsatim. regime, HMS was being imported from sources which 
includes zones of conflict. As a result there had been a number of cases of presence of 
explosive:; in such consignment& which led to the loss of life. That was primarily due to non­
enforcement of quality control and the inability of private importers to enforce such a 
discipline on the sellers. 
The Management highlighted the following facts regarding import of scrap after 
decanalisation:-
1. There had been several instances where scrap impc:-t.;-d by private importers had been 
m:companied by explosives and on a few occasions there had been serious casualities. 
2. There had been every possibility of radio-active contamina!.inn being present in such 
material imported. 
3. Due to substantial differential in customs duty between HMS a11d rerollable scrap, 
there was every possibility/tendency on the part of private importers to import 
rerollab!e scrap in the guise of HMS which naturally resulted in revenue bss to the 
country. 
4. Price manipulation through under-invoicing/over invoicing depending upon the 
circumstances could not be ruled out· which evidently was at the cost of revenue and 
loss of foreign exchange to the country. 
On the basis of evidence of the management and the papers/documents submitted before 
it the Sub-Committee feels that though it would be difficult to extend full monopoly to the 
Company in the field of importing scrap but the Government may still help out MSTC by 
rationalising the duty structure for raw materials and to enable it to compete with private 

227 
sector. It should also be well within the consideration of the Government of India to expedite 
clearance of the diversification proposal of the Company. The Ministry of Railways and 
Defence may also be persuaded to prefer MSTC in respect of disposal of their scrap.
